# Medieval Indian History Flashcards

> One flashcard per examinable item in the repository Medieval Indian History owners (basic + advanced), plus a concept card per owner file. IDs: MED-1001 onward by agent block.
> **Verification status:** built from the repository owners with claims checked against Wikipedia and other pages that loaded while building; confirmed items carry a ✅ source tag; unverified details are marked ⚠️. Owner-vs-source conflicts are flagged inside the cards. Time-sensitive facts carry as-of dates from the owner.

---

## MEDIEVAL INDIAN HISTORY — Part 1: Early Sultanate to Sultanate Administration

> Section note: Prefix `MED`; ID block `MED-1001` onward.
> Tags: `(✅ owner NN)` = grounded in the repository owner file (basic/advanced); `(✅ Wikipedia: <page>)` = confirmed on a fetched page on 2026-11-01; `⚠️` = unverified, memory-only, or an owner-vs-source conflict (flagged inside the card).

### GROUP — India on the Eve of the Medieval Age & Arab-Turkish Contacts

### Flashcard MED-1001 — "Eve of the medieval age" as an analytical snapshot
**Front:** What does "India on the eve of the medieval age" mean, and why is it not a claim of all-India collapse?
**Definition (learn):** An analytical snapshot of changing political, agrarian, commercial and cultural structures before the Delhi Sultanate. It is not a civilisational vacuum, a single all-India decline, or a sharp ancient/medieval boundary. (✅ owner 01, §9.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Periodisation options: ancient/medieval (broad comparison), Hindu/Muslim (colonial ruler-religion split, communalising), dynastic (Pratihara, Pala, Rashtrakuta, Rajput, Ghaznavid, Ghurid), and a c. 700/750–1200 transition window (✅ owner 01).
- Regions followed different chronologies: north-west frontier, Sindh, Ganga plain, Rajasthan/Gujarat/Malwa, Bengal/east, Kashmir, Deccan, South (✅ owner 01, §9.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Transition window c. 700/750–1200 (✅ owner 01). Arab Sind conquest 711–12 is the earliest anchor.
**Links:** MED-1002 (Sindh), MED-1008 (successor states), MED-1010 (South), MED-1019 (Mains architecture).
**Trap:** "Eve of conquest" applies to the north-west and Gangetic north, not to the Chola south (⚠️ owner 01, §8.3).
**Mains hook:** Assess whether pre-1200 India was declining or realigning. Qualified answer: political realignment and regional state formation, not civilisational collapse; the north-west frontier was structurally vulnerable.
**Back:** *"Connected, not isolated; realigned, not collapsed."*

### Flashcard MED-1002 — Muhammad bin Qasim and the Arab conquest of Sindh, 711–12
**Front:** Who conquered Sindh in 711–12, under whose authority, and whom did he defeat?
**Definition (learn):** The Arab conquest of Sindh led by Muhammad bin Qasim, acting under Umayyad authority and the governor al-Hajjaj, which defeated Raja Dahir and established control over key towns extending toward Multan. (✅ owner 01, §11.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Causes (layered): maritime insecurity, frontier politics, imperial expansion and fiscal interests. No single pirate episode explains it (✅ owner 01).
- Governance: garrisons, revenue collection and retained local personnel rather than replacing every institution (✅ owner 01, §11.3).
- Limits: control stayed concentrated in Sindh/Multan and did not cross the Ganga plain (✅ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 711–12 (✅ owner 01); Raja Dahir (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MED-1003 (Chachnama), MED-1001, MED-1015 (trap).
**Trap:** Muhammad bin Qasim was not the founder of the Delhi Sultanate; the Sultanate came centuries later through the Turkish-Ghurid route (✅ owner 01, §18.2).
**Mains hook:** Use Sindh as a "conduit", not a bridgehead that inevitably conquered India: a limited frontier province that mattered for commerce and knowledge.
**Back:** *"711–12, Sindh, Dahir down; a frontier, not the Sultanate."*

### Flashcard MED-1003 — Chachnama (source card)
**Front:** Why is the *Chachnama* valuable, and what must not be done with it?
**Definition (learn):** The richest narrative of Chach, Dahir and Muhammad bin Qasim, an early-thirteenth-century Persian work associated with Ali Kufi. It claims an Arabic source that does not survive. (✅ owner 01, §11.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Secure use: remembered conquest and the political world of its own composition (✅ owner 01).
- Limit: history, political advice and literary narrative interact; it is not an eyewitness transcript of eighth-century speeches (✅ owner 01).
- Comparison: Al-Baladhuri and other Arabic traditions (written later, shaped by imperial genres); inscriptions and coins (uneven survival); Debal/Banbhore archaeology (identification debated) (✅ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Early-13th-century Persian; about Sindh's much earlier past (✅ owner 01, §18.2).
**Links:** MED-1002, MED-1017.
**Trap:** The *Chachnama* is not Al-Biruni's work; Al-Biruni wrote *Kitab al-Hind* (MED-1032) (✅ owner 01, §18.2).
**Mains hook:** Discuss how a source written about an earlier conquest reveals its own period's politics. Qualify with source criticism.
**Back:** *"Early-13th-century Persian about 8th-century Sindh: remembered, not recorded."*

### Flashcard MED-1004 — Arab maritime trade and the Malabar settlements
**Front:** What did Arab traders do for India's sea trade before the Turkish conquests, and how did Indian rulers respond?
**Definition (learn):** Arab traders revived and strengthened India's sea trade with West Asia after overland routes were affected; they settled on the Malabar coast, and the Rashtrakuta rulers welcomed them and permitted mosques for worship. (✅ owner 01, §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Channel: Indian Ocean commerce, port communities and negotiated residence (✅ owner 01, §10.1).
- Limit: the evidence shows negotiated commercial presence, not universal tolerance, social equality or inland political control (✅ owner 01, §10).
- Indian merchants also operated around the Persian Gulf and beyond (✅ owner 01 adv).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Malabar settlements; Rashtrakuta permission for mosques (✅ owner 01). Dates for the settlements: 8th–10th centuries (✅ owner 01 adv).
**Links:** MED-1005, MED-1014, MED-1015.
**Trap:** Arab trade is not the same as the Turkish military conquest. Arab merchants did not "displace" Indian traders (✅ owner 01 adv, §5).
**Mains hook:** "Arab-Indian contact was primarily commercial and intellectual, creating corridors that preceded and differed from the later Turkish conquest." Qualify: limited to coastal/commercial spheres.
**Back:** *"Malabar welcomed, Rashtrakutas allowed mosques: trade first, conquest later."*

### Flashcard MED-1005 — Sindh as a conduit for Indian numerals and siddhanta astronomy
**Front:** Which Indian knowledge reached the Arab world through Sindh and Baghdad, and what caution applies?
**Definition (learn):** Indian numerals (the decimal system) and astronomical works such as the *Surya Siddhanta* and Brahmagupta-associated *siddhanta* material entered Arabic learning through translation and adaptation. (✅ owner 01, §1 and §12)
**Breakdown:**
- Numerals: entered Arabic mathematics through multiple stages of translation and adaptation (✅ owner 01, §12).
- Astronomy: *Sindhind* label in Arabic works; attribution and exact translation chains require caution (⚠️ owner 01, §12).
- Medicine: physicians and texts moved through Sanskrit, Middle Persian and Arabic channels (✅ owner 01, §12).
- Nature: translation and reconstruction, not one-directional copying (✅ owner 01, §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Baghdad under the Abbasids; Sindh after 711–12 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MED-1004, MED-1014.
**Trap:** Indo-Arab contact was not only military: science, numerals, astronomy and medicine were key channels. A "one-way civilising mission" is wrong (✅ owner 01, §5).
**Mains hook:** Use the numerals example to argue that pre-Sultanate contact was intellectual exchange, not mere conquest.
**Back:** *"Sindh carried the numerals and siddhantas; Baghdad translated them."*

### Flashcard MED-1006 — Persianised and Islamised Turkish military elites
**Front:** What political culture did Turkish elites bring into India, and why does it matter for the Sultanate?
**Definition (learn):** Turkish military elites of West and Central Asia had been Islamised and Persianised before they entered India. Their courts used Persian language, culture and administrative practice. (✅ owner 01, §2; ✅ owner 01 adv, §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Turkish commanders assimilated Iranian language, culture and administration (✅ owner 01 adv).
- They carried cavalry tactics plus Persianate court culture and institutions (✅ owner 01 adv).
- Arabic and Persian were the languages of ruling classes in West/Central Asia (✅ owner 01 adv).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Context: Abbasid decline in the 9th century allowed Islamised Turkish military elites to rise (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MED-1012 (terminology), MED-1024 (Ghaznavid Persianate court), MED-1043.
**Trap:** Turks were not "culturally pure" Central Asians; Turk does not equal Arab, Afghan or Persian (✅ owner 01, §5 and §17.2).
**Mains hook:** Explain the Sultanate's rapid administrative and literary establishment by pointing to the pre-formed Persianate culture of the Turkish military elite.
**Back:** *"Turks came already Islamised and Persianised, not raw nomads."*

### Flashcard MED-1007 — Loss of the outer bastions: Hindu Shahis, Kabul, Jalalabad, Punjab and Multan
**Front:** Which north-western frontier lands were lost to the Ghaznavids, and why did this matter?
**Definition (learn):** The "outer bastions" (Afghanistan, Zabulistan, Kabul–Jalalabad, Punjab and Multan) held by the Hindu Shahis passed to the Ghaznavids, opening the route for a later Turkish advance into the Gangetic plains. (✅ owner 01, §1; ✅ owner 01 adv, Concept box)
**Breakdown:**
- Hindu Shahi defeat: c. 990–91 led to loss of Kabul and Jalalabad (✅ owner 01 adv).
- Punjab: Lahore was annexed to the Shahi kingdom in 999, shortly before Mahmud's rise (✅ owner 01 adv mini-timeline).
- Gradual loss, c. 990–1015: Kabul, Afghanistan, Punjab and Multan fell in phases, not in one battle (✅ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 990–1015 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MED-1024 (Mahmud), MED-1030, MED-1012.
**Trap:** ⚠️ Owner-vs-source conflict on Punjab. Owner says Punjab fell c. 990–1015; the fetched Wikipedia article on Mahmud dates the formal annexation of the Punjab region to about 1021, when Lahore was besieged and Bhimapala driven out (✅ Wikipedia: Mahmud of Ghazni). Use "gradual, c. 990–1021" with the flag.
**Mains hook:** Discuss why the fall of the north-western frontier mattered more than any single raid. Qualify: loss of depth was gradual.
**Back:** *"Kabul, Punjab, Multan: the outer bastions fell one by one."*

### Flashcard MED-1008 — Successor states after the Gurjara-Pratihara decline
**Front:** Name the regional Rajput and other states that filled north India after the Pratihara decline.
**Definition (learn):** Competing regional states that arose in the 10th–12th centuries from Pratihara decline; the owner list is the Gahadavalas of Kanauj, Paramaras of Malwa, Chauhans of Ajmer, Chalukyas of Gujarat, Chandellas and Tomars. (✅ owner 01, §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Gahadavalas: Kanauj; their defeat at Chandawar in 1194 opened the upper Ganga (✅ owner 01; MED-1040).
- Paramaras: Malwa (✅ owner 01).
- Chauhans: Ajmer/Delhi-Ajmer region; Prithviraj (✅ owner 01).
- Chalukyas: Gujarat (Chaulukyas) (✅ owner 01).
- Chandellas and Tomars (✅ owner 01).
- "Rajput" was not one ethnicity or unified state; lineage-based states with forts, revenue, alliances and rivalries (⚠️ owner 01, §9.2 and §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 10th–12th centuries (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MED-1009, MED-1011, MED-1038, MED-1039.
**Trap:** "Rajput" is a broad label; do not treat it as a single army or ethnicity (✅ owner 01 §9.2).
**Mains hook:** Use the state list to show political fragmentation as a structural factor in frontier loss, not as "lack of patriotism".
**Back:** *"Gahadavala, Paramara, Chauhan, Chaulukya, Chandella, Tomar: rivals, not one Rajput army."*

### Flashcard MED-1009 — The tripartite contest: Pala–Pratihara–Rashtrakuta
**Front:** What was the tripartite contest, and what did its aftermath produce?
**Definition (learn):** The competition for north Indian supremacy among the Palas, Gurjara-Pratiharas and Rashtrakutas, before the eleventh-century invasions. Its decline produced regional successor powers rather than an institutional vacuum. (✅ owner 01, §1; ✅ owner 01 adv §8.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Three powers: Pala (east), Gurjara-Pratihara (north), Rashtrakuta (Deccan) (✅ owner 01).
- Aftermath: competing regional states (MED-1008) (✅ owner 01).
- Chola south: separate powerful state and maritime network; not part of the contest's collapse (✅ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Pre-11th-century (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MED-1008, MED-1010.
**Trap:** The contest produced successor states, not "institutional collapse" (✅ owner 01 §9.2 closing line).
**Mains hook:** Critically evaluate "Indian decline": realignment, not civilisational collapse (verdict scaffold in owner 01 §8.1).
**Back:** *"Pala, Pratihara, Rashtrakuta fought; successors arose; nothing vanished."*

### Flashcard MED-1010 — The Cholas and the south: why the "decline" narrative does not apply
**Front:** Why does the "eve of conquest" narrative not describe the south under the Cholas?
**Definition (learn):** The Chola state was expansive: an agrarian, temple and maritime power with a commercial network. It was not in decline during the period when northern polities were realigning. (✅ owner 01, §8.3 and §9.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Chola agrarian, temple and maritime power (✅ owner 01 §9.2).
- Deccan: Rashtrakuta aftermath, Chalukya and other formations, no universal northern chronology (✅ owner 01 §9.2).
- Counterpoint: "decline" is a north-India story only (⚠️ owner 01 §8.3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Chola maritime-commercial network noted by owner 01 §1 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MED-1001, MED-1009.
**Trap:** Do not generalise a northern decline to all India; periodisation differs by region.
**Mains hook:** Use the south as a counterexample to test any all-India decline thesis.
**Back:** *"The south was expanding; the decline was northern."*

### Flashcard MED-1011 — Rajput political order: capacity without coordination
**Front:** How should the military failure of Rajput states be explained?
**Definition (learn):** A structural, not moral, explanation: Rajput and regional states had forts, agrarian revenue, cavalry, infantry, elephants and local networks, but rivalries and competing interests limited sustained joint command. (⚠️ owner 01, §5 and §16)
**Breakdown:**
- Political capacity: forts, revenue, mixed forces (✅ owner 01 §16).
- Alliances existed: Tarain I involved a coalition (⚠️ owner 02 §8.3).
- Failed coordination: rivalries and competing courts (⚠️ owner 01).
- Counter-evidence: Chaulukya victory over Muizz al-Din at Kasahrada in 1178 and Prithviraj's win at Tarain I in 1191 (✅ owner 01 §16; ✅ Wikipedia: Muhammad of Ghor, Battle of Tarain).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1178 and 1191 counter-evidence (✅ owner 01 and Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1008, MED-1041, MED-1045.
**Trap:** "Rajputs lost because they lacked bravery" is a false answer; the owner treats it as anachronistic (✅ owner 01 §5).
**Mains hook:** Military courage with weak inter-state coordination, plus Ghurid mobility and frontier bases. Avoid racial, communal or "national disunity" monocausality.
**Back:** *"Forts and courage yes; coordination no."*

### Flashcard MED-1012 — Terminology discipline: Arab, Turk, Afghan, Persianate, Islamic
**Front:** Distinguish the terms Arab, Turk, Afghan, Persianate and Islamic as used in medieval Indian history.
**Definition (learn):** These are different ethnic, linguistic, cultural or religious categories and are not synonyms. (✅ owner 01, §17.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Arab: Arabic-speaking/Arabian lineages, merchants or states; not every Muslim (✅ owner 01).
- Turk: Turkic military/political groups; not synonymous with Arab, Afghan or Persian (✅ owner 01).
- Afghan: people/lineages linked to Afghanistan and eastern Iranian frontier histories (✅ owner 01).
- Persianate: a Persian-language courtly-administrative culture, not Persian ethnicity (✅ owner 01).
- Islamic: a religious/civilisational category containing many political and ethnic groups (✅ owner 01).
- Modern borders (India, Pakistan, Afghanistan) are location aids, not medieval nation-states (✅ owner 01 §17.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Table in owner 01 §17.2.
**Links:** MED-1006, MED-1014, MED-1086.
**Trap:** Khaljis were Turkic in origin with Afghan associations; a single ethnic label is misleading (✅ owner 04 §9.1).
**Mains hook:** Use the distinctions to avoid ethnic or communal shorthand in any Sultanate answer.
**Back:** *"Arab, Turk, Afghan, Persian culture, Islam: five different keys."*

### Flashcard MED-1013 — Pre-conquest contact actors: distinguish who did what
**Front:** Which agents and relationships shaped Indo-Arab contact before political conquest?
**Definition (learn):** A table of agents: merchants, settlers/diasporas, raiders, envoys, physicians/scholars/translators, and Arab or Indian states. Each has a distinct historical effect. (✅ owner 01 adv §10.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Merchants: Arabian Sea commerce; not state conquest (✅ owner 01 §10.1).
- Settlers: negotiated residence on the western coast, including Malabar; not loss of sovereignty (✅ owner 01 §10.1).
- Raiders/piracy: episodic violence; not all merchant contact (✅ owner 01 §10.1).
- Envoys: court information and gifts; not permanent alliance (✅ owner 01 §10.1).
- Scholars/translators: movement of medicine and mathematics, not a one-way civilising mission (✅ owner 01 §10.1).
- States: taxation, war, protection, port policy; not one homogeneous religious community (✅ owner 01 §10.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Arab and Indian merchants used western Indian Ocean routes before Muhammad bin Qasim (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MED-1004, MED-1005, MED-1002.
**Trap:** Confusing merchant settlement with conquest is the central error in Arab-contact answers.
**Mains hook:** Build a 15-mark answer by separating route, agent and relationship before analysing the political result.
**Back:** *"Merchants, settlers, raiders, envoys, scholars, states: six agents, six effects."*

### Flashcard MED-1014 — Abbasid-era translation and reconstruction, not copying
**Front:** How did Indian science and medicine reach Abbasid courts, and why is "translation and reconstruction" the right term?
**Definition (learn):** Under the Abbasids, Baghdad and Persian Gulf networks linked Indian merchants, physicians, craftsmen and scholars with Arabic-Persian learning. Texts moved through multiple language stages and adaptations. (✅ owner 01, §12)
**Breakdown:**
- Numerals: multi-stage translation (✅ owner 01 §12).
- Astronomy: Sanskrit *siddhanta* works informed Arabic works labelled *Sindhind* (⚠️ attribution; owner 01 §12).
- Medicine: physicians and texts circulated at courts (✅ owner 01 §12).
- Stories and political wisdom also travelled; the Arabic version differs from its Indian source (✅ owner 01 §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Abbasid period (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MED-1005, MED-1004.
**Trap:** The later Arabic version is not identical to its Indian source; do not assume textual identity.
**Mains hook:** Use "translation and reconstruction" to challenge one-directional diffusion narratives.
**Back:** *"Baghdad rebuilt Indian learning: translated, adapted, never copied whole."*

### Flashcard MED-1015 — Trap pair: Arab conquest of Sindh is not the Delhi Sultanate
**Front:** What examiners often confuse: the Arab conquest of Sindh and the Delhi Sultanate. What is the correct distinction?
**Definition (learn):** The Arab conquest of Sindh (711–12) produced a limited frontier province. The Delhi Sultanate (1206 onward) came later through Turkish-Ghurid expansion. (✅ owner 01, §5 and §18.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Arab conquest of Sindh did not immediately produce all-India rule (✅ owner 01).
- Sindh was a conduit for commerce and knowledge, neither instant creator of the Sultanate nor insignificant (✅ owner 01 §11.3).
- Delhi Sultanate: Aibak's phase from 1206 (MED-1052).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 711–12 vs 1206 (✅ owner 01 and Wikipedia: Qutb al-Din Aibak, 25 June 1206).
**Links:** MED-1002, MED-1052.
**Trap:** Muhammad bin Qasim = Sindh 711–12; not the founder of the Sultanate.
**Mains hook:** Use it as the opener for a "continuity without teleology" answer.
**Back:** *"Sindh 711 is not Delhi 1206."*

### Flashcard MED-1016 — Transition-society terms: samanta, varna-jati and "Indian feudalism"
**Front:** What terms describe the transition society of c. 700/750–1200, and which is debated?
**Definition (learn):** Land grants, **samanta** relations, agrarian expansion, temples and regional courts reconfigured authority. Varna-jati hierarchy and occupational groups shaped society. "Indian feudalism" is a debated model, not an automatic label. (✅ owner 01, §9.3)
**Breakdown:**
- Samanta: intermediary/subordinate relations (✅ owner 01).
- Varna-jati hierarchy: normative texts are not demographic censuses (✅ owner 01).
- Towns, pilgrimage centres, ports and merchants changed unevenly (✅ owner 01).
- "Indian feudalism": grants, intermediaries and agrarian relations, debated (✅ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 700/750–1200 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MED-1001, MED-1076 (iqta).
**Trap:** Iqta is not European feudal ownership; the "feudal" label is contested (✅ owner 07 §14).
**Mains hook:** Critically assess the "feudal" model: useful for grants and intermediaries, but not a universal explanation.
**Back:** *"Samanta, varna-jati, grants: useful terms, debated labels."*

### Flashcard MED-1017 — Mini-chronology ladder: Arab Sindh to the Ghurid turn
**Front:** Give the pre-1206 ladder for Owner 01 in order.
**Definition (learn):** The sequence that frames "eve of the medieval age" (✅ owner 01 mini-timeline).
**Breakdown:**
- 711–12: Muhammad bin Qasim's Sindh conquest (⚠️ owner 01 says "711–12 onward").
- 9th century: Abbasid decline; Islamised Turkish military elites rise (✅ owner 01).
- c. 990–1015: Hindu Shahis lose Afghanistan, Punjab and Multan (✅ owner 01).
- 10th–12th centuries: Pratihara decline produces competing Rajput states (✅ owner 01).
- 1173: Muizzuddin takes Ghazni; Ghurid turn towards India (✅ owner 01).
- c. 1200: North India enters the Sultanate transition (⚠️ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed; 1173 is also confirmed by owner 01 adv and Wikipedia: Muhammad of Ghor (1173 reign start).
**Links:** MED-1052, MED-1036.
**Trap:** c. 1200 is a transition, not a single date for the Sultanate's start (1206).
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder to show that the Sultanate was preceded by a 300-year frontier transformation.
**Back:** *"711, 9th c., 990–1015, 1173, c. 1200: the slow road to 1206."*

### Flashcard MED-1018 — PYQ routing for Owner 01
**Front:** Which PYQs are routed to Owner 01, and which belong elsewhere?
**Definition (learn):** The owner records no verified PYQ routed directly to Topic 01; seven retained application questions test prerequisites or adjacent owners. (✅ owner 01, §18.1)
**Breakdown:**
- 2019 Prelims Q12, Sultanate revenue institutions: Medieval Topic 07 (✅ owner 01).
- 2020 Prelims Q24, Pallava-Pala-Pratihara-Chola chronology: Ancient Topics 22/23/27 (✅ owner 01).
- 2022 Prelims, Nannuka/Jayashakti/Nagabhata/Bhoja pairs: regional-kingdom bridge; direct route not recorded (✅ owner 01).
- 2023 Prelims Q45, Hoysala/Gahadavala/Kakatiya/Yadava chronology: Medieval Topic 08 (✅ owner 01).
- 2018 GS-I Q2, Chinese/Arab travellers: Ancient Sources Topic 02 (✅ owner 01).
- 2020 GS-I Q12, Persian literary sources: Medieval Topic 24 (✅ owner 01).
- 2023 GS-I Q12, Sultanate technology: Medieval Topic 07 (✅ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Official keys not held locally; no answers inferred (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MED-1019, MED-1204 (Owner 07 PYQ card).
**Trap:** Do not treat "Arab contact" PYQs as direct Medieval Topic 01 questions; route them correctly.
**Mains hook:** Use the routing ledger to plan answers around adjacent owners.
**Back:** *"Seven PYQs, none direct: route them, do not assume them."*

### Flashcard MED-1019 — Mains architecture for Owner 01 (10/15/20 marks)
**Front:** What is the answer skeleton for a 10-, 15- or 20-mark question on the eve of the medieval age?
**Definition (learn):** Executable structures from owner 01 §8 and §19.
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks (150 words): thesis → regional political map → frontier depth → maritime contact → one continuity/strength → coordination and contingency → qualified verdict (✅ owner 01 §19).
- 15 marks (250 words): route/agent distinction → Arab maritime commerce and Sindh → knowledge translation and coastal settlements → Ghaznavid Punjab and Ghurid conquest → raid vs durable state → source caution (✅ owner 01 §19).
- 20 marks: full 5-unit evidence set, plus historiographical note on "Indian decline", graded verdict (✅ owner 01 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Evidence units 1–5 (✅ owner 01 §8.2).
**Links:** MED-1001, MED-1013, MED-1020.
**Trap:** Do not use "national disunity" or "caste" as sole explanation (✅ owner 01 §15).
**Mains hook:** Each band must show a qualified verdict, not a single-factor claim.
**Back:** *"10: map; 15: route and raid; 20: graded verdict."*

### Flashcard MED-1020 — Source evidence matrix for early medieval India
**Front:** Which source types serve which purposes for pre-Sultanate history, and what are their limits?
**Definition (learn):** A matrix separating chronicles, inscriptions, coins, archaeology and later epics by their best use and their limit (✅ owner 01 §17.1).
**Breakdown:**
- Arabic/Persian court chronicles: sequence and claims, but patronage and retrospective narration (✅ owner 01).
- Inscriptions: titles, grants, donations; formula and eulogy (✅ owner 01).
- Coins: authority and circulation; presence is not total territorial control (✅ owner 01).
- Archaeology: forts, ports, settlements; rarely supplies motive alone (✅ owner 01).
- Later epics/romances: political memory; late recension and anachronism (✅ owner 01).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Matrix in owner 01 §17.1 (✅ owner 01).
**Links:** MED-1003, MED-1033, MED-1046.
**Trap:** Coin presence is not equal to territorial sovereignty (✅ owner 01 §17.1).
**Mains hook:** Use the matrix to justify source-aware claims in any answer.
**Back:** *"Chronicles claim, inscriptions formalise, coins circulate, sites stay silent, epics remember."*

### GROUP — The Ghaznavids & the Ghurian Invasions

### Flashcard MED-1021 — From raid to conquest: the Ghaznavid-Ghurid transition
**Front:** What is the key distinction between a raid, a tribute, an annexation and a consolidated state?
**Definition (learn):** Raid/plunder/tribute is different from garrison + revenue claim + delegated commander + repeated enforcement, which in turn differs from a fully consolidated territorial state. (✅ owner 02, §11.2 and §16)
**Breakdown:**
- Raid: temporary strike, loot and return (✅ owner 02 §11.2).
- Tribute: recognition/payment without dense administration (✅ owner 02 §11.2).
- Annexation: garrison, governor and recurring revenue claim (✅ owner 02 §11.2).
- Frontier incorporation: roads, forts, intelligence and repeated enforcement (✅ owner 02 §11.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mahmud's durable Indian gain = Punjab/Multan; Ghurid rule expanded through delegated commanders (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MED-1024, MED-1031, MED-1044.
**Trap:** Mahmud's Somnath and Mathura raids demonstrate reach, not Gangetic or Gujarat empire (✅ owner 02 §11.2).
**Mains hook:** Use the four-outcome ladder to argue whether a campaign counted as conquest.
**Back:** *"Raid, tribute, annexation, incorporation: four different things."*

### Flashcard MED-1022 — Alptigin at Ghazni, c. 963
**Front:** Who established a power centre at Ghazni, and when?
**Definition (learn):** Alptigin, a commander who converted a frontier office/base into a regional power at Ghazni in south Zabulistan. (✅ owner 01 adv mini-timeline; ✅ owner 02, §9.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Date: c. 963 (✅ owner 01 §13).
- Nature: background to Indian conquest, not an Indian campaign (✅ owner 01 §13).
- Dynastic foundation remained contested (✅ owner 02 §9.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 963 (✅ owner 01 adv).
**Links:** MED-1023, MED-1024.
**Trap:** Alptigin precedes Sabuktigin; Sabuktigin precedes Mahmud (✅ owner 02 §19.2).
**Mains hook:** Use Ghazni as a frontier base argument.
**Back:** *"963: Alptigin plants Ghazni."*

### Flashcard MED-1023 — Sabuktigin, 977–997, and the Hindu Shahi frontier
**Front:** What did Sabuktigin do against the Hindu Shahis, and how reliable is the account?
**Definition (learn):** Sabuktigin consolidated Ghazni and fought Hindu Shahi Jayapala in 977–997, expanding the frontier. (✅ owner 01 §13; ✅ owner 02 §9.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Campaigns were staged and contested (✅ owner 01 §13).
- Later narratives magnify army and coalition figures (✅ owner 02 §9.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 977–997 (✅ owner 01 §13).
**Links:** MED-1022, MED-1024, MED-1007.
**Trap:** Sabuktigin is not Mahmud; the sequence is Alptigin → Sabuktigin → Mahmud (✅ owner 02 §19.2).
**Mains hook:** Show the frontier war as the bridge from Alptigin's base to Mahmud's raids.
**Back:** *"977–997: Sabuktigin hits Jayapala."*

### Flashcard MED-1024 — Mahmud of Ghazni, 998–1030
**Front:** Summarise Mahmud's reign and his durable Indian achievement.
**Definition (learn):** Ruler of Ghazni who conducted repeated Indian campaigns; his durable gain was Punjab and Multan, not a Gangetic empire. (✅ owner 02 §1; ✅ Wikipedia: Mahmud of Ghazni)
**Breakdown:**
- Reign: March 998 to 30 April 1030; died at Ghazni, buried at Rawza (✅ Wikipedia).
- "Invaded India seventeen times" is the conventional count; counting is not uniform (✅ owner 02 §1; ✅ Wikipedia: "seventeen times").
- Punjab/Multan base; raids into plains (✅ owner 02 §2).
- Patronage of Persian culture including Firdausi (✅ owner 02 adv §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 998–1030 (✅ owner 02; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1025–MED-1031, MED-1034.
**Trap:** Mahmud and Muizzuddin are not interchangeable; Mahmud = Ghaznavid, Muizzuddin = Ghurid (✅ owner 02 §5).
**Mains hook:** Compare Mahmud's raids with Muizzuddin's conquest. Graded contrast (✅ owner 02 §8.1).
**Back:** *"998–1030: deep raids, Punjab kept, Gangetic states not annexed."*

### Flashcard MED-1025 — Jayapala and the 1001 battle near Peshawar
**Front:** What happened in 1001 and what was its consequence?
**Definition (learn):** Mahmud defeated Hindu Shahi Jayapala near Peshawar in 1001; the Shahi capital Waihind was ravaged. (✅ owner 02 mini-timeline; ✅ owner 02 adv §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Shahi defeat, but resistance continued (✅ owner 02 §11.1).
- Frontier defence was active, not passive (✅ owner 02 §8.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1001 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MED-1024, MED-1026.
**Trap:** Jayapala's defeat was not a one-battle collapse of the Shahis (✅ owner 02 §9.1).
**Mains hook:** Use Shahi resistance to refute deterministic collapse narratives.
**Back:** *"1001: Jayapala beaten near Peshawar, Waihind ravaged."*

### Flashcard MED-1026 — Anandapala and the 1009 Indus battle; Nandana overrun
**Front:** What did Mahmud achieve against Anandapala in 1009?
**Definition (learn):** Anandapala was defeated near the Indus in 1009; Nandana in the Salt Range was overrun. (✅ owner 02 mini-timeline; ✅ owner 02 adv)
**Breakdown:**
- Salt Range/Punjab breach deepened (✅ owner 02 §11.1).
- Coalition frontier resistance (✅ owner 02 §11.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1009 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MED-1025, MED-1007.
**Trap:** Do not confuse Anandapala with Jayapala (✅ owner 02 §19.2 traps list).
**Mains hook:** Show the frontier breach as a cumulative process.
**Back:** *"1009: Anandapala, Indus, Nandana fell."*

### Flashcard MED-1027 — Kashmir attempt, 1015 (failure by weather)
**Front:** Why did the 1015 Kashmir attempt fail?
**Definition (learn):** Mahmud's 1015 Kashmir approach failed due to weather and terrain. He had earlier advanced to Lahore in the same year. (✅ owner 02 mini-timeline; ✅ owner 02 §11.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Lahore advance (✅ owner 02).
- Weather/terrain frustrated the Kashmir operation (✅ owner 02 §11.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1015 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MED-1024, MED-1007.
**Trap:** Failure by terrain is not a sign of weak Ghaznavid power (⚠️ owner 02 §11.1).
**Mains hook:** Illustrate how ecology and logistics limited campaigns.
**Back:** *"1015: Lahore reached, Kashmir stopped by weather."*

### Flashcard MED-1028 — Mathura and Kanauj raid, 1018
**Front:** What did the 1018 raid on Mathura and Kanauj achieve?
**Definition (learn):** Mahmud's raid on Mathura and Kanauj in 1018 was the most spectacular Ganga-valley raid; plunder and political demonstration, with no durable Gangetic annexation. (✅ owner 02 §11.1; ✅ owner 01 adv)
**Breakdown:**
- Political demonstration of reach (✅ owner 02 §11.1).
- No Ghaznavid territory beyond Punjab (✅ owner 01 adv §1).
- Upper doab destabilised; coalitions checked (✅ owner 01 adv).
- Wikipedia: Mathura and Kanauj raided 1018–1023 (✅ Wikipedia: Mahmud of Ghazni).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1018 (✅ owner); 1018–1023 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1021, MED-1029.
**Trap:** Raids through the Ganga plain are not annexation (✅ owner 02 §19.2).
**Mains hook:** Use it to distinguish reach from rule.
**Back:** *"1018: Mathura and Kanauj plundered, not ruled."*

### Flashcard MED-1029 — Somnath, 1025–26
**Front:** When was Somnath raided, and what was the outcome?
**Definition (learn):** Mahmud raided the temple at Somnath in 1025–26; he returned to Ghazni with wealth and did not annex Gujarat. (✅ owner 02 §12; ✅ owner 01 adv)
**Breakdown:**
- Date: owner gives 1025 and 1025–26 (✅ owner 02 §11.1 and §12); Wikipedia: attack in 1025, sack dated 8 January 1026 (✅ Wikipedia: Mahmud of Ghazni).
- Return: Mahmud returned via Sind; later punished Jats who harried his return (✅ owner 02 §4).
- Outcome: no permanent Gujarat province (✅ owner 02 §12).
- Memory: later magnified; loot and casualty figures unverified (✅ owner 02 §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1025–26 (✅ owner 02); 8 January 1026 sack date (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1024, MED-1031, MED-1047.
**Trap:** Somnath was not followed by direct Ghaznavid rule over Gujarat (✅ owner 02 §5).
**Mains hook:** Analyse Somnath through source, political economy and later memory without communal celebration or denial (✅ owner 02 §12).
**Back:** *"1025–26: Somnath sacked, Mahmud went home."*

### Flashcard MED-1030 — Punjab: the durable Ghaznavid base
**Front:** Why was Punjab more consequential than Mahmud's distant raids?
**Definition (learn):** Punjab and Multan were incorporated into Ghaznavid control, providing a durable base, supply route and revenue claim. (✅ owner 02 §11.2; ✅ owner 01 adv §13)
**Breakdown:**
- Durable base after Hindu Shahi defeat (✅ owner 01 §13).
- Strategic: shortened supply and intelligence routes (✅ owner 01 §15).
- Wikipedia: formal annexation of Punjab about 1021 (Lahore besieged, Bhimapala driven out) (✅ Wikipedia: Mahmud of Ghazni). Owner dates Punjab loss to c. 990–1015 (⚠️ conflict, see MED-1007).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 990–1015 (owner); c. 1021 (Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1007, MED-1024, MED-1021.
**Trap:** Punjab is a frontier base, not an all-India province.
**Mains hook:** Explain Punjab as the strategic hinge between plunder and rule.
**Back:** *"Punjab kept, plains raided."*

### Flashcard MED-1031 — Mahmud's motives: mixed, not monocausal
**Front:** What motives drove Mahmud's campaigns, and what is the limit of each?
**Definition (learn):** Fiscal/plunder, political, strategic, legitimating and religious motives combined. (✅ owner 02 §11.3)
**Breakdown:**
- Fiscal: bullion financed court, army and western wars (✅ owner 02 §11.3).
- Political: weakened rivals and broke coalitions (✅ owner 02 §11.3).
- Strategic: Punjab, routes and forts (✅ owner 02 §11.3).
- Legitimating: victories enhanced sultanic and caliphal prestige (✅ owner 02 §11.3).
- Religious: ghazi and iconoclastic language mattered but cannot explain every campaign (✅ owner 02 §11.3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Table in owner 02 §11.3.
**Links:** MED-1021, MED-1029, MED-1012.
**Trap:** Religious rhetoric is not a transparent motive diary, and it does not imply mass conversion (✅ owner 02 §11.3).
**Mains hook:** Critically examine religious rhetoric vs political-economic motives in early Turkish invasions (✅ owner 01 adv Mains).
**Back:** *"Loot, power, Punjab, prestige, faith: all five, none alone."*

### Flashcard MED-1032 — Al-Biruni's Kitab al-Hind (source card)
**Front:** Who was Al-Biruni, what did he write, and what are the limits of his account?
**Definition (learn):** Al-Biruni (973–c. 1050), from Khwarazm and active in the Ghaznavid world, learned Sanskrit and wrote a comparative study conventionally called *Kitab al-Hind* (or *Tahqiq ma li'l-Hind*). (✅ owner 02 §14)
**Breakdown:**
- Strengths: linguistic and mathematical competence; comparison; astronomy, calendars, religion and social classification (✅ owner 02 §14).
- Limits: learned Brahmanical informants, north-western elite setting, uneven lower-status and women's knowledge (✅ owner 02 §14).
- Not a neutral camera; not Mahmud's court propagandist (✅ owner 01 §13.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 973–c. 1050 (✅ owner 02 §14).
**Links:** MED-1033 (Al-Utbi), MED-1020, MED-1034.
**Trap:** Al-Biruni wrote *Kitab al-Hind*, not the *Chachnama* (✅ owner 01 §18.2).
**Mains hook:** Use Al-Biruni to test claims about Indian social classification, citing his limits.
**Back:** *"Al-Biruni: Sanskrit learned, Brahmanical sources, north-west view."*

### Flashcard MED-1033 — Al-Utbi's Kitab al-Yamini (source card)
**Front:** What is Al-Utbi's Kitab al-Yamini and why is its ending important?
**Definition (learn):** An Arabic Ghaznavid court panegyric covering Sabuktigin and Mahmud's campaigns and legitimating their rule. (✅ owner 02 §10)
**Breakdown:**
- Secure use: campaigns and legitimating language (✅ owner 02 §10).
- Limit: ornate victory rhetoric, weak geography, ends before Somnath (✅ owner 02 §10 and §19.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ends before 1025–26 Somnath (✅ owner 02 §19.2).
**Links:** MED-1029, MED-1032.
**Trap:** Al-Utbi's account does not cover Somnath.
**Mains hook:** Source-matrix question: panegyric vs independent account.
**Back:** *"Al-Utbi praises, ends before Somnath."*

### Flashcard MED-1034 — Source matrix for the Ghaznavid period
**Front:** Which source suits which claim for Mahmud's period?
**Definition (learn):** A table of Al-Utbi, Al-Biruni, Gardizi, Bayhaqi, Hasan Nizami, Minhaj, Firishta and coins/archaeology, each with a secure use and a limit (✅ owner 02 §10).
**Breakdown:**
- Gardizi: dynastic and ethnographic checks; brief (✅ owner 02).
- Bayhaqi: chancery insider, mainly post-Mahmud; administration (✅ owner 02).
- Hasan Nizami, *Taj al-Ma'asir*: Aibak-era occupation and vassalage (✅ owner 02).
- Minhaj-us-Siraj Juzjani, *Tabaqat-i Nasiri*: Ghurid genealogy and early Sultanate (✅ owner 02).
- Firishta: later memory; cannot authenticate early numbers (✅ owner 02).
- Chahamana inscriptions and *Prithviraja Vijaya*: near-contemporary Sanskrit royal evidence; eulogy (✅ owner 02).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Table in owner 02 §10.
**Links:** MED-1032, MED-1046, MED-1081.
**Trap:** Exact army, casualty and loot numbers are not audited facts (✅ owner 02 §10).
**Mains hook:** Separate contemporary attestation, court rhetoric, later memory and inference (✅ owner 02 §10).
**Back:** *"Every source has a job and a bias."*

### Flashcard MED-1035 — Muizz al-Din Muhammad ibn Sam (Ghurid), 1173–1206
**Front:** Who was Muizz al-Din Muhammad ibn Sam, and how did he die?
**Definition (learn):** The Ghurid ruler (Shansabani house from Ghur) who worked from Ghazni and developed the Indian theatre; reign 1173 to his death in 1206. (✅ owner 02 §15; ✅ Wikipedia: Muhammad of Ghor)
**Breakdown:**
- Ruled from 1173; Ghiyath al-Din Muhammad was the senior brother focusing on western theatres (✅ owner 02 §15).
- Death: assassinated 15 March 1206 at Dhamiak on the Indus during evening prayers; most sources blame Khokhars avenging his suppression of their revolt; Ibn al-Athir names two Muslim assassins, suggesting a Khokhar–Isma'ili attack (✅ Wikipedia: Muhammad of Ghor).
- Buried in Ghazni (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1173; 15 March 1206 (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 01 adv).
**Links:** MED-1036, MED-1038, MED-1052, MED-1047.
**Trap:** "Ghori" is conventional, not an ethnic category (✅ owner 01 §14); Ghurid is not Ghaznavid (✅ owner 02 §15).
**Mains hook:** Compare Muizzuddin's strategy after Gujarat with Mahmud's raids.
**Back:** *"Ghor's Muizz al-Din: 1173 to Dhamiak, 1206."*

### Flashcard MED-1036 — Gujarat defeat, 1178 (Kasahrada near Mount Abu)
**Front:** Where and when did Muizzuddin suffer his major defeat in Gujarat, and what changed?
**Definition (learn):** Muizzuddin's army was routed in 1178 near Mount Abu (Wikipedia: Kasahrada/Gadararaghatta pass) by a Chaulukya-led Rajput coalition under Mularaja II. He was wounded. (✅ Wikipedia: Muhammad of Ghor; ✅ owner 02 §16)
**Breakdown:**
- Strategy changed from the desert-Gujarat route to a Punjab-first route (✅ owner 02 §2).
- Shows conquest was not inevitable (✅ owner 01 §14).
- Campaigns: 1175–78 Multan/Uch then Gujarat (✅ owner 02 mini-timeline).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1178 (✅ owner and Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1035, MED-1037, MED-1011.
**Trap:** Wikipedia's "Kayadara" is not used; the place name is Kasahrada (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** Use 1178 as counter-evidence against inevitability, and a turn to Punjab.
**Back:** *"1178: Gujarat routed, Punjab chosen."*

### Flashcard MED-1037 — Punjab campaign ladder, 1175–86
**Front:** What was Muizzuddin's route from Multan to the Punjab?
**Definition (learn):** A ladder of footholds: Multan and Uch (1175–76), Peshawar, Sialkot and Lahore/Punjab (1179–86). (✅ owner 02 §16; ✅ Wikipedia: Muhammad of Ghor)
**Breakdown:**
- Multan taken from the Qarmatians in 1175 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Peshawar conquered 1179 (✅ Wikipedia; owner gives 1179–86 range).
- Lahore: raids 1180 and 1185; final siege 1186 ended Ghaznavid rule; Khusrau Malik captured (✅ Wikipedia).
- Uch: conflicting dates (infobox 1175; text says after 1179) (⚠️ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1175–86 (✅ owner 02 and Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1036, MED-1021.
**Trap:** Owner says "Ghaznavid remnant removed"; the final siege fell in 1186 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** Show Punjab as the platform for the later Indian campaigns.
**Back:** *"Multan 1175, Peshawar 1179, Lahore 1186."*

### Flashcard MED-1038 — First Battle of Tarain, 14 January 1191
**Front:** Who won the First Battle of Tarain, and what was its significance?
**Definition (learn):** On 14 January 1191 at Tarain (modern Taraori, Haryana), Prithviraj Chauhan defeated Muhammad Ghori. It was a Ghurid defeat. (✅ Wikipedia: Battle of Tarain; ✅ owner 02 mini-timeline)
**Breakdown:**
- Ghurid retreat after the battle (✅ owner 02 §16).
- Prithviraj did not pursue into hostile Punjab (✅ owner 01 adv §1).
- Tabarhinda (owner) triggered the Tarain conflict (✅ owner 01 adv §4). Wikipedia does not mention Tabarhinda (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 14 January 1191 (✅ Wikipedia); 1191 (✅ owner).
**Links:** MED-1039, MED-1041, MED-1011.
**Trap:** Tarain I was not the Turkish victory (✅ owner 02 §5).
**Mains hook:** Tarain I shows Indian states could win field battles; defeats inevitabilist argument.
**Back:** *"1191: Prithviraj wins, Ghuri retreats."*

### Flashcard MED-1039 — Second Battle of Tarain, 8 March 1192
**Front:** Who won the Second Battle of Tarain, and why did it matter?
**Definition (learn):** On 8 March 1192 at Tarain, Muhammad Ghori defeated Prithviraj Chauhan (Wikipedia notes Jaychand's alleged help). It opened the Delhi-Ajmer region. (✅ Wikipedia: Battle of Tarain; ✅ owner 02 §16)
**Breakdown:**
- Mounted archery and movement (✅ owner 01 adv §1).
- Cautious vassal policy afterwards; direct control later under Aibak (✅ owner 01 adv §1).
- Victory did not instantly pacify north India (✅ owner 01 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 8 March 1192 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1038, MED-1040, MED-1052.
**Trap:** Tarain II = Ghurid victory; the Sanyogita legend cannot replace evidence (✅ owner 02 §5 and §17).
**Mains hook:** Assess Tarain II as a turning point: tactics plus political aftermath (✅ owner 01 adv Mains).
**Back:** *"1192: Ghuri returns, Prithviraj falls."*

### Flashcard MED-1040 — Chandawar, 1194: Jayachandra defeated
**Front:** What did the 1194 battle of Chandawar achieve?
**Definition (learn):** The Ghurids under Muhammad Ghori defeated Gahadavala king Jayachandra at Chandawar near Kanauj in 1194; Varanasi was looted (Wikipedia) and the upper Ganga valley opened. (✅ owner 02 §1; ✅ Wikipedia: Muhammad of Ghor)
**Breakdown:**
- Ghurid crossed the Yamuna with about 50,000 horsemen (Wikipedia; a source-stated figure, treat as claim).
- Kanauj annexed in 1198 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Control remained contested and mediated (✅ owner 01 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1194 (✅ owner and Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1008, MED-1041.
**Trap:** Jayachandra's absence at Tarain is only a legend (Sanyogita) and not proven (✅ owner 02 §5).
**Mains hook:** Chandawar as the entry point to the Ganga valley, with long consolidation after.
**Back:** *"1194: Jayachandra falls, Ganga opens."*

### Flashcard MED-1041 — Tarain I vs Tarain II: the pair trap
**Front:** How do Tarain I and Tarain II differ in winner, date and implication?
**Definition (learn):** Two battles at the same place, reversed outcomes. (✅ owner 02 §19.2; ✅ Wikipedia)
**Breakdown:**
- Tarain I: 14 January 1191; Prithviraj wins; Ghurid defeat (✅ Wikipedia).
- Tarain II: 8 March 1192; Muizzuddin wins (✅ Wikipedia).
- Chandawar: 1194, Jayachandra defeated (✅ owner 01 adv and Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1191 / 1192 / 1194 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1038, MED-1039, MED-1040.
**Trap:** Tarain I is not the Turkish victory; Tarain II is (✅ owner 02 §5).
**Mains hook:** "Battle sequence as a turning point requires both battles."
**Back:** *"Ninety-one Prithviraj, ninety-two Ghuri."*

### Flashcard MED-1042 — Qutb al-Din Aibak as delegated commander (1192–1206)
**Front:** What role did Aibak play in the Ghurid conquests before 1206?
**Definition (learn):** Muizzuddin's trusted slave-commander who managed Indian affairs after Tarain and expanded into the doab, operating through garrisons, vassals and revenue claims. (✅ owner 02 §1 and §16)
**Breakdown:**
- Occupied Delhi and Ajmer after resistance (✅ owner 02 §1).
- Lahore was his main royal centre; Delhi strategically important (✅ owner 03 §11).
- Independent Sultanate role belongs to Part 1 Group 03 (MED-1052).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Post-1192 (✅ owner 02 §16).
**Links:** MED-1052, MED-1035, MED-1043.
**Trap:** Aibak's independent sultanate is from 1206, not the Ghurid delegation phase (✅ owner 02 §16).
**Mains hook:** Use Aibak as a bridge from delegated conquest to independent Sultanate.
**Back:** *"Muizz's slave-commander: garrisons, revenue, doab."*

### Flashcard MED-1043 — Bakhtiyar Khalji in Bihar and Bengal
**Front:** What did Bakhtiyar Khalji achieve in the east, and what is the caution?
**Definition (learn):** Bakhtiyar Khalji, a Ghurid slave commander, pushed into Bihar and Bengal around the turn of the 12th–13th century, rapidly destroying major centres. (✅ owner 02 §16; ✅ Wikipedia: Muhammad of Ghor)
**Breakdown:**
- Bihar and Bengal extended Turkish power eastward (✅ owner 02 §1).
- Wikipedia: coinage struck in Muhammad's name in Bengal (1204–06) (✅ Wikipedia).
- Thin initial control; operations partly opportunistic (✅ owner 02 §8.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** late 1190s–early 1200s (✅ owner 02 §16).
**Links:** MED-1042, MED-1064 (Balban Bengal).
**Trap:** Bakhtiyar's eastern thrust is not proof of simultaneous all-India control (✅ owner 01 §14).
**Mains hook:** Show decentralised Turkish initiative: a multi-actor conquest.
**Back:** *"Bihar and Bengal: fast raids, thin rule."*

### Flashcard MED-1044 — Ghurid conquest sequence: victory to uneven state
**Front:** What is the sequence from battle victory to territorial state?
**Definition (learn):** A process chain: victory → fort/garrison → local accommodation or rebellion → revenue assignment → commander network → repeated enforcement → uneven territorial state. (✅ owner 02 §16)
**Breakdown:**
- Each stage can fail: rebellion, revenue dispute or defection (✅ owner 02 §16).
- Post-1192 vassalage, rebellion, siege and occupation varied across Ajmer, Delhi, Bayana and Gwalior (✅ owner 02 §16).
- Institutional implantation differed from battlefield victory (✅ owner 02 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Process chain (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MED-1021, MED-1042.
**Trap:** Battlefield victory is not institutional control.
**Mains hook:** Use the chain in a 15-mark answer on Turkish success.
**Back:** *"Win, garrison, collect, command, enforce: slowly."*

### Flashcard MED-1045 — Why Rajput campaigns were lost: seven factors
**Front:** List the seven factors owner 02 gives for why particular campaigns were lost.
**Definition (learn):** Mounted mobility and archery; reserves and manoeuvre; concentrated command and recruitment; frontier bases, roads and intelligence; fort/siege and garrison strategy; political rivalries; leadership, terrain, timing and contingency. (✅ owner 02 §17)
**Breakdown:**
- Organisation over weapons (✅ owner 01 adv §5).
- No racial, civilisational or technology-alone explanation (✅ owner 02 §17).
- Gujarat 1178 and Tarain I counter-evidence (✅ owner 02 §8.3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Seven-factor list (✅ owner 02 §17).
**Links:** MED-1011, MED-1038.
**Trap:** Sanyogita and betrayal stories do not explain outcome (✅ owner 02 §5).
**Mains hook:** Reject national-disunity, technological and communal monocausality (✅ owner 01 §19).
**Back:** *"Mobility, reserves, command, bases, forts, rivalries, contingency."*

### Flashcard MED-1046 — Prithviraj sources: Prithviraja Vijaya vs Prithviraj Raso
**Front:** Which sources on Prithviraj are closest to his period, and which are later memory?
**Definition (learn):** The Sanskrit *Prithviraja Vijaya* and Chahamana inscriptions are closer to his period but eulogistic; the *Prithviraj Raso* survives in much later recensions and is memory, not contemporary reportage. (✅ owner 01 §16; ✅ owner 02 §10 and §17)
**Breakdown:**
- Inscriptions and coins: near-contemporary (✅ owner 02 §10).
- Persian chronicles: positioned (✅ owner 02 §17).
- Later literary memory: evidence for remembrance (✅ owner 02 §17).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sources listed (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MED-1034, MED-1038.
**Trap:** Sanyogita romance: later literary memory (✅ owner 02 §5).
**Mains hook:** Date and genre separation when using Prithviraj material.
**Back:** *"Vijaya is closer; Raso is later memory."*

### Flashcard MED-1047 — Khokhar resistance in Punjab and Muizz al-Din's death
**Front:** Why did Punjab stay contested even at the end of Muizzuddin's career?
**Definition (learn):** Khokhar resistance in Punjab kept conquest contested through Muizzuddin's final campaign, in which he was killed in 1206. (✅ owner 02 §17; ✅ owner 01 adv mini-timeline)
**Breakdown:**
- Khokhar revolt suppressed before the assassination (✅ owner 01 adv §4).
- Assassination at Dhamiak (MED-1035) (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1206 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1035, MED-1052.
**Trap:** Khokhars are not the same as Mewatis or Sharqis; separate regional resistance (✅ owner 03 §13).
**Mains hook:** Show that conquest faced constant local resistance.
**Back:** *"Khokhars held Punjab; Muizz died there in 1206."*

### Flashcard MED-1048 — Dandanqan (1040) and the Ghaznavid decline
**Front:** What weakened the Ghaznavids after Mahmud?
**Definition (learn):** Succession conflict and fiscal-military pressure weakened Ghaznavid power; Seljuq expansion, especially the defeat at Dandanqan in 1040, reduced western territories, shifting authority to Lahore/Punjab. (✅ owner 02 §15)
**Breakdown:**
- Succession conflict after Mahmud (✅ owner 02 §15).
- Dandanqan defeat (1040) to Seljuqs (✅ owner 02 §15).
- Shift toward Lahore/Punjab (✅ owner 02 §15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1040 (✅ owner 02).
**Links:** MED-1024, MED-1035.
**Trap:** Ghaznavid decline is not the Ghurid rise; the Ghurids were a distinct house from Ghur (✅ owner 02 §15).
**Mains hook:** External Seljuq pressure as a driver of Punjab focus.
**Back:** *"1040: Dandanqan lost, Punjab held."*

### Flashcard MED-1049 — PYQ routing and traps for Owner 02
**Front:** What PYQs route to Owner 02, and what are the Prelims traps?
**Definition (learn):** No verified PYQ is routed directly to Topic 02; three retained adjacent questions (✅ owner 02 §19.1).
**Breakdown:**
- 2022 Prelims Q96, Somnath/Al-Biruni/temple facts: Indian Art and Culture Topic 13 (✅ owner 02).
- 2018 GS-I Q2, Chinese and Arab travellers: Ancient Topic 02 (✅ owner 02).
- 2020 GS-I Q12, Persian literary sources: Medieval Topic 24 (✅ owner 02).
- Traps: Mahmud = Ghaznavid; Muizz al-Din = Ghurid; Tarain I/II; Al-Utbi ends before Somnath; Firishta cannot certify numbers (✅ owner 02 §19.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Official key not held locally (⚠️ owner 02 is silent; treat as owner statement).
**Links:** MED-1041, MED-1034, MED-1050.
**Trap:** Do not assign Somnath PYQ to Medieval Topic 02.
**Mains hook:** Route the Somnath memory question through Art/Culture and Source owners.
**Back:** *"Somnath to Art, Persian to Topic 24: route, don't assume."*

### Flashcard MED-1050 — Mains architecture for Owner 02
**Front:** What is the answer skeleton for 10/15/20-mark questions on Mahmud, Muizzuddin and the campaigns?
**Definition (learn):** Structures from owner 02 §8.4 and §20.
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks: Mahmud/Muizzuddin contrast + 2 evidence units + qualified conclusion (✅ owner 02 §8.4).
- 15 marks: frontier-loss thesis + Tarain comparison + one counterpoint (✅ owner 02 §8.4).
- 20 marks: source matrix (Utbi, Al-Biruni, Hasan Nizami, Juzjani, Firishta); Somnath memory vs event; garrisons, revenue, elite circulation; continuity of local institutions (✅ owner 02 §20).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Evidence units 1–5 (✅ owner 02 §8.2).
**Links:** MED-1021, MED-1044, MED-1034.
**Trap:** Do not claim Mahmud created the Sultanate (✅ owner 02 §5).
**Mains hook:** Graded multi-factor verdict.
**Back:** *"Contrast, two units, graded verdict."*

### GROUP — Foundation of the Delhi Sultanate: The Slave (Mamluk) Dynasty

### Flashcard MED-1051 — "Slave/Mamluk" dynasty: terminology and the Mamluk century
**Front:** Why is "Slave dynasty" a shorthand, and what is the better frame?
**Definition (learn):** "Slave dynasty" is later historiographical shorthand, not the rulers' self-name. A mamluk was an owned military slave, but elite mamluks were trained, converted, promoted and often manumitted. (✅ owner 03 §9.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Patron-client households, marriage, military reputation, manumission and noble choice shaped succession (✅ owner 03 §9.1).
- Better frame: the early Delhi Sultanate's "Mamluk century", in which Ghurid delegated conquest became Delhi-centred (✅ owner 03 §9.1).
- Aibak or Iltutmish should not be imagined as permanently unfree after accession (✅ owner 03 §9.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mamluk period 1206–1290 (✅ owner 03 and Wikipedia: Khalji dynasty listing 1290 end).
**Links:** MED-1052, MED-1073.
**Trap:** The dynasty was not a simple hereditary line (✅ owner 03 §5).
**Mains hook:** Use the terminology caution as the first paragraph of any Mamluk answer.
**Back:** *"Slave is a label; mamluk is a career; the century is the frame."*

### Flashcard MED-1052 — Qutb al-Din Aibak, 1206–1210
**Front:** What did Aibak do in 1206 and how did he die?
**Definition (learn):** Muizzuddin's Turkish slave and commander who became the first independent Turkish ruler in India, ruling from Lahore. He died in 1210 after a fall from his horse while playing polo. (✅ owner 03 §2 and §11; ✅ Wikipedia: Qutb al-Din Aibak)
**Breakdown:**
- Coronation: 25 June 1206 at Qasr-e-Humayun, Lahore; informal ascension (✅ Wikipedia).
- Severed links with Ghazni (✅ owner 03 §1).
- Death: 4 November 1210 in Lahore; fell while playing chaugan (polo); saddle pommel pierced his ribs (✅ Wikipedia).
- Aram Shah then installed by Lahore nobles; Iltutmish took Delhi (✅ owner 03 §11; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 25 June 1206; 4 November 1210 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1053, MED-1054, MED-1055, MED-1057.
**Trap:** Owner spells it "changan" (polo); Wikipedia uses *chaugan* (⚠️ spelling difference, same game).
**Mains hook:** Aibak began the state; Iltutmish consolidated it (✅ owner 03 §22.2).
**Back:** *"1206 Lahore, 1210 polo fall: Aibak began it."*

### Flashcard MED-1053 — Aibak's legitimacy: chatr, investiture and manumission
**Front:** What formal recognition did Aibak receive, and when?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 03 says Aibak received manumission and the *chatr* (ceremonial parasol) from Ghiyasuddin Mahmud, ending Ghazni's legal claim over Hindustan. (✅ owner 03 adv §2; ✅ owner 03 basic §11)
**Breakdown:**
- Wikipedia: 1208–09 Ghiyasuddin Mahmud gave the chatr and a deed of investiture recognising him as ruler of Hindustan; manumission "may have" been issued the same time (✅ Wikipedia: Qutb al-Din Aibak).
- Formal sovereignty was recognised after the fact, not created by the caliph (✅ owner 03 adv §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1208–09 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1052, MED-1060.
**Trap:** Owner's manumission claim is presented as firm; Wikipedia says "may have" (⚠️ conflict in strength of claim).
**Mains hook:** Legal freedom enabled legitimate kingship, but the power came from conquest.
**Back:** *"Chatr and deed in 1208–09: recognised, not created."*

### Flashcard MED-1054 — Aram Shah and the succession of 1210–11
**Front:** Who succeeded Aibak and why did Delhi reject him?
**Definition (learn):** Aram Shah was installed by Lahore's nobles after Aibak's death; Delhi elites invited Iltutmish. (✅ owner 03 §11; ✅ Wikipedia: Iltutmish)
**Breakdown:**
- Aram Shah ruled no more than eight months (✅ Wikipedia).
- Iltutmish, Aibak's son-in-law, beat Aram Shah in 1211 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Ali-yi Ismail backed Iltutmish (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1210–11 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1052, MED-1055.
**Trap:** Aram Shah is not Iltutmish; Iltutmish defeated him in 1211 (✅ Wikipedia: Iltutmish).
**Mains hook:** Succession depended on nobles, not primogeniture.
**Back:** *"Aram Shah: eight months, rejected by Delhi."*

### Flashcard MED-1055 — Iltutmish (c. 1211–1236): the real consolidator
**Front:** Why is Iltutmish called the real consolidator of the Sultanate?
**Definition (learn):** He defeated rivals (Aram Shah, Yildiz, Qubacha), reasserted control over Lahore, Multan, Uch, Bengal/Bihar and Rajput forts, and centred power at Delhi. (✅ owner 03 §1 and §12; ✅ Wikipedia: Iltutmish)
**Breakdown:**
- Reign: June 1211 to 30 April 1236 (✅ Wikipedia); owner says 1210–36 (⚠️ conflict in start year).
- Rival matrix: Aram Shah; Yildiz (Tarain, 1216); Qubacha (Mansura 1217; Uch 1228); Bengal (Iwaz; Lakhnauti 1227) (✅ owner 03 §12; ✅ Wikipedia).
- Delhi as primary political-cultural centre (✅ owner 03 §12.2).
- Iqta system adapted/extended (⚠️ see MED-1076 conflict).
- Qutb Minar completed by him around 1220 (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 03 adv).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1211–1236 (Wikipedia) vs 1210–36 (owner).
**Links:** MED-1054, MED-1056–MED-1061, MED-1076, MED-1078.
**Trap:** Iltutmish consolidated; Aibak began (✅ owner 03 §22.2).
**Mains hook:** "Iltutmish transformed conquest into a Sultanate" (✅ owner 03 §8.1).
**Back:** *"Aram, Yildiz, Qubacha, Bengal, Rajputs: Iltutmish held them all."*

### Flashcard MED-1056 — Taj al-Din Yildiz, defeated at Tarain (1216)
**Front:** Who was Yildiz and how was he removed?
**Definition (learn):** Yildiz, Ghazni's rival claimant, was defeated at Tarain on 25 January 1216 (Third Battle of Tarain in Wikipedia), reducing Ghazni's claim over Delhi; he was killed at Badaun. (✅ owner 03 §12.1; ✅ Wikipedia: Battle of Tarain and Iltutmish)
**Breakdown:**
- Ghazni rival (✅ owner 03 §22.2).
- Sources differ: fled to Hansi or captured wounded (✅ Wikipedia).
- Killed at Badaun (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 25 January 1216 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1055, MED-1041.
**Trap:** Yildiz is not Qubacha; Qubacha is the Multan-Uch/Sindh rival (✅ owner 03 §22.2).
**Mains hook:** Ghazni's claim ended in 1216 through Delhi's own army (✅ owner 03 §12.1).
**Back:** *"Yildiz: Ghazni's man, beaten at Tarain in 1216."*

### Flashcard MED-1057 — Nasir al-Din Qubacha, Multan and Uch (1217–28)
**Front:** Who was Qubacha and when did Iltutmish take Multan and Uch?
**Definition (learn):** Qubacha, Multan-Uch/Sindh rival of Iltutmish, was defeated at Mansura on the Chenab in 1217; Uch fell in 1228 after a siege; he drowned in the Indus on 26 May 1228. (✅ Wikipedia: Iltutmish; ✅ owner 03 §12.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Multan, Uch and Sindh passed to Iltutmish; his rule reached the Arabian Sea (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner says Multan and Uch incorporated by 1228 (✅ owner 03 §12.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1217; 4 May 1228; 26 May 1228 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1055, MED-1056, MED-1037.
**Trap:** Qubacha = Multan-Uch/Sindh; Yildiz = Ghazni (✅ owner 03 §22.2).
**Mains hook:** Qubacha's defeat completed the Indus frontier (✅ owner 01 adv §2).
**Back:** *"Qubacha: Mansura 1217, Uch 1228, Indus drowned."*

### Flashcard MED-1058 — Bengal: Iwaz, Lakhnauti and Balkha Khalji (1225–30)
**Front:** What was the Bengal sequence under Iltutmish?
**Definition (learn):** Iltutmish invaded in 1225; Ghiyasuddin Iwaz accepted suzerainty; Iwaz later reasserted independence. Nasiruddin Mahmud took Lakhnauti in 1227 and executed Iwaz; Bengal coinage then carried Iltutmish's name. (✅ Wikipedia: Iltutmish; ✅ owner 03 §12.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Control remained intermittent (✅ owner 03 §12.1).
- Malik Balkha Khalji usurped after Nasiruddin's death in 1229; Iltutmish defeated him in 1230 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1225; 1227; 1230 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1055, MED-1043.
**Trap:** Bengal under Delhi suzerainty is not the same as direct administration (✅ owner 03 §17.1).
**Mains hook:** Use Bengal to show the limits of Delhi's core.
**Back:** *"Iwaz 1225, Lakhnauti 1227, Balkha 1230: intermittent Bengal."*

### Flashcard MED-1059 — 1221: Mangbarani, Chingiz Khan and Iltutmish's refusal
**Front:** What did Iltutmish decide in 1221 when Jalaluddin Mangbarani fled to Punjab?
**Definition (learn):** Iltutmish refused the alliance and asylum request of Jalal al-Din Mangburni when Chingiz Khan reached the Indus, citing unsuitable climate; he avoided entanglement and kept India outside the Mongol confrontation. (✅ owner 03 §12.1; ✅ Wikipedia: Iltutmish)
**Breakdown:**
- Wikipedia: envoy reportedly killed; Chingiz left via Peshawar (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner: "avoided alliance/asylum commitment" as prudent diplomacy, not victory (✅ owner 03 §12.1).
- Stayed out of Indus affairs until Chingiz died in 1227 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1221 (✅ owner and Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1055, MED-1063.
**Trap:** Chingiz did not invade Delhi; the owner's trap "Mongols crossed and conquered Delhi under Iltutmish" is false (✅ owner 03 §5).
**Mains hook:** Diplomatic realism preserved the new state (✅ owner 03 §8.2).
**Back:** *"1221: Iltutmish said no; the Mongols turned away."*

### Flashcard MED-1060 — Caliphal investiture of 1229
**Front:** What did the 1229 caliphal investiture confer, and what did it not do?
**Definition (learn):** The Abbasid caliph al-Mustansir sent robes and a deed of investiture to Iltutmish, conferring titles "Yamin Khalifat Allah" and "Nasir Amir al-Mu'minin". It arrived in Delhi on 18 February 1229 (Wikipedia). It legitimised after consolidation and did not create power. (✅ owner 03 §12.2; ✅ Wikipedia: Iltutmish)
**Breakdown:**
- Symbolic endorsement, not constitutive sovereignty (✅ owner 03 adv §2).
- Caliph could not enforce authority in India (✅ owner 03 §8.2).
- Owner does not date it; Wikipedia gives the arrival date (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 18 February 1229 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1055, MED-1053.
**Trap:** Caliphal investiture did not create Iltutmish's power; it followed consolidation (✅ owner 03 §5).
**Mains hook:** Legitimacy after the fact, not real control (✅ owner 03 §8.1).
**Back:** *"1229: Caliph's robes followed power, not preceded it."*

### Flashcard MED-1061 — Tanka and jital: the coinage of the Sultanate
**Front:** What were the Sultanate's basic coins, and what do they prove?
**Definition (learn):** The silver tanka (about 11 g per Wikipedia) and copper/billon jital became the basis of Sultanate coinage. Coin presence signals sovereignty claims and monetised exchange, but is not an empire boundary. (✅ owner 03 §9.2 and §18; ✅ Wikipedia: Iltutmish)
**Breakdown:**
- Silver tanka: large silver (✅ owner 03 §9.2).
- Copper jital: small (✅ owner 03 §9.2).
- Tanka/jital aided sovereignty and exchange but no uniform cash economy (✅ owner 03 §18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ~11 g tanka (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1055, MED-1076, MED-1181.
**Trap:** Owner ties tanka/jital to Iltutmish, not Aibak (✅ owner 03 §22.2); Wikipedia attributes the basis to Sultanate coinage under Iltutmish (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** Coinage as sovereignty, not census.
**Back:** *"Silver tanka, copper jital: Iltutmish's coins, not a cash empire."*

### Flashcard MED-1062 — Chihalgani ("Forty"): not literally forty, not a parliament
**Front:** What was the Chihalgani (Turkan-i Chihalgani), and what should it not be taken to mean?
**Definition (learn):** Iltutmish's powerful Shamsi Turkish-mamluk elite, remembered by Barani as the "Forty" (Chihalgani). It was an elite corps, not a body of exactly forty permanent members. (✅ owner 03 §14; ✅ owner 03 adv §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Not exactly forty across all reigns (✅ owner 03 §14).
- Not a representative parliament or one faction (✅ owner 03 §14).
- Not the whole nobility; ruling elite included Khaljis, Tajiks, Indian converts, local chiefs (✅ owner 03 §14).
- Wikipedia: Iltutmish article only links the term; "forty chiefs" backed Muizuddin Bahram (✅ Wikipedia: Iltutmish).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Iltutmish's era (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MED-1055, MED-1063, MED-1064.
**Trap:** "Chihalgani = exactly forty named nobles" is wrong (✅ owner 03 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** The Chihalgani shows the monarchy-nobility dialectic (✅ owner 03 §1).
**Back:** *"Forty is a label for an elite corps, not a headcount."*

### Flashcard MED-1063 — Razia Sultan, 19 November 1236 to 1240
**Front:** When did Razia reign, and what caused her fall?
**Definition (learn):** Iltutmish's daughter Razia reigned from 19 November 1236 to 1240, after the deposition of Ruknuddin Firuz; she was deposed by a noble revolt and killed in October 1240 near Kaithal. (✅ Wikipedia: Razia Sultan; ✅ owner 03 §13)
**Breakdown:**
- Named Jamaluddin Yaqut Amir-i Akhur, angering Turkish nobles (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 03 §13).
- Malik Altunia, governor of Tabarhinda, rebelled; they married Sept 1240 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Muizuddin Bahram defeated them (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner: Yaqut's elevation a focus of resentment; fall not solely gender (✅ owner 03 §13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 19 Nov 1236–20 Apr 1240 deposition; killed 13–15 October 1240 (conflicting in Wikipedia) (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1064, MED-1065, MED-1066.
**Trap:** Owner's 1236–40 vs fetched Wikipedia dates 1236–40; the 13/14/15 Oct killing dates conflict within Wikipedia (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Evaluate Razia's fall as gendered politics and noble oligarchy (✅ owner 03 §7).
**Back:** *"1236 to 1240: a woman on the throne, a noble revolt, Kaithal."*

### Flashcard MED-1064 — Razia's "unveiled" image: source conflict
**Front:** Did Razia discard purdah in public, and what does the source say?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 03 says she "discarded female seclusion", appeared unveiled, and led armies; Isami is cited by Wikipedia as saying she first observed purdah, then wore male clothing (qaba and kulah) and rode elephants. (⚠️ owner 03 §8.2 vs ✅ Wikipedia: Razia Sultan)
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: Minhaj praised her qualities but noted gender prejudice (✅ owner 03 adv §4).
- Wikipedia does not confirm a formal end of the veil (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Isami is later and politically positioned (✅ owner 03 §9.2; ✅ owner 04 §9.3).
**Links:** MED-1063, MED-1065.
**Trap:** A statement "she removed the veil" is an owner claim; present the male-dress/appearance nuance with the source flag.
**Mains hook:** Gender as a lens on legitimacy; source-qualified (✅ owner 03 §8.1).
**Back:** *"Veil questioned; the robes were the statement."*

### Flashcard MED-1065 — Razia's fall: gender, Yaqut or noble oligarchy?
**Front:** Which explanations of Razia's fall are acceptable, and which are inadequate?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 03 treats her fall as a combination of noble factionalism, Yaqut's elevation, Malik Altunia's revolt and gendered expectations; neither gender alone nor one appointment is sufficient. (✅ owner 03 §13 and §8.3)
**Breakdown:**
- Not solely misogyny or romance (✅ owner 03 §13).
- Yaqut's relationship is not a later romance (✅ owner 03 §13).
- Razia's own miscalculations matter (⚠️ owner 03 §8.3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As in MED-1063.
**Links:** MED-1063, MED-1064.
**Trap:** "She failed only because she was a woman" (✅ owner 03 §5).
**Mains hook:** Use the noble-monarchy dialectic to explain her fall.
**Back:** *"Gender mattered; nobles decided."*

### Flashcard MED-1066 — Succession ladder after Iltutmish, 1236–1266
**Front:** Give the succession order after Iltutmish's death.
**Definition (learn):** The sequence of rulers and factional phases (✅ owner 03 §13).
**Breakdown:**
- Ruknuddin Firuz / Shah Turkan: short rule and court opposition (✅ owner 03 §13).
- Razia: 1236–40 (MED-1063).
- Bahram Shah: 1240–42 (✅ owner 03 §13).
- Ala al-Din Masud: 1242–46 (✅ owner 03 §13).
- Nasiruddin Mahmud: 1246–66, Balban rose as naib (✅ owner 03 §13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (✅ owner 03). Iltutmish died 30 April 1236 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1063, MED-1067.
**Trap:** Iltutmish's nomination of Razia did not follow primogeniture (✅ owner 03 §13).
**Mains hook:** Succession as nomination, elite support and force (✅ owner 07 §11).
**Back:** *"Ruknuddin, Razia, Bahram, Masud, Mahmud: five kings, one power."*

### Flashcard MED-1067 — Balban: naib or regent before sultan
**Front:** What was Balban's position under Nasiruddin Mahmud and when did he become sultan?
**Definition (learn):** Balban was the dominant naib (owner) and, per Wikipedia, the vizier and regent of Nasiruddin Mahmud (1246–65); he ruled as sultan from 1266 to 1287. (✅ owner 03 §15; ✅ Wikipedia: Balban)
**Breakdown:**
- Wikipedia: Nasiruddin Mahmud married one of Balban's daughters (✅ Wikipedia).
- Sultan: 18 February 1266 – 13 January 1287 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Death section gives 1265 (⚠️ internal Wikipedia conflict).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1266–87 (✅ owner and Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1066, MED-1068, MED-1069.
**Trap:** "Naib" (owner) vs "vizier/regent" (Wikipedia): use the owner's naib-era label with Wikipedia's office description (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Balban used his regency years to prepare an absolutist monarchy.
**Back:** *"Naib under Mahmud, sultan from 1266."*

### Flashcard MED-1068 — Balban's kingship: zill-i-ilahi, sijda and paibos
**Front:** What court practices and titles marked Balban's monarchy?
**Definition (learn):** Exalted monarchy against noble equality, using Persianate ceremony and controlled access. Key items: zill-i-ilahi (shadow of God), sijda and paibos (prostration and kissing feet), genealogy claims. (✅ owner 03 §15.1; ✅ Wikipedia: Balban)
**Breakdown:**
- Wikipedia: introduced Iranian-style sajda and paibos for the sultan in India (✅ Wikipedia).
- Also called Niyabat-i-Khuda (vice-regency of God) (✅ Wikipedia).
- Did not invent absolutism ex nihilo; Ghaznavid, Ghurid and Persianate precedents (✅ owner 03 §15.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1266–87 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1067, MED-1069.
**Trap:** "Blood and iron" is later shorthand, not his formal programme name (✅ owner 03 §15.2).
**Mains hook:** Was Balban's absolutism necessary centralisation or coercive overreach? (✅ owner 03 §7.)
**Back:** *"Zill-i-ilahi and sijda: kingship over peers."*

### Flashcard MED-1069 — Balban's barid network
**Front:** How did Balban use intelligence, and what were its limits?
**Definition (learn):** Barid (intelligence) agents monitored nobles, officers and provinces, reporting only to the sultan. Wikipedia says the network was modelled on the Umayyad barid, with secret reporters in every department. (✅ owner 03 §15.2; ✅ Wikipedia: Balban)
**Breakdown:**
- Chroniclers magnify royal reach (✅ owner 03 §15.2).
- Harshness was also political theatre (✅ owner 03 §15.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Balban's reign (✅).
**Links:** MED-1174 (barids), MED-1068.
**Trap:** Barids were not invented by Balban; Muhammad bin Tughlaq and Alauddin used them (✅ owner 07 §8.2 and owner 03 §17.2).
**Mains hook:** Coercive information as a state-capacity tool.
**Back:** *"Balban's spies answered only to the sultan."*

### Flashcard MED-1070 — Balban's Mewat campaigns and internal law and order
**Front:** Which internal disorders did Balban suppress, and what measures did he take?
**Definition (learn):** Campaigns against Meo raiders of Mewat, doab robbers and Katehar rebels; he cleared jungles, built outposts, settled soldiers and Afghans, and made roads safe. (✅ owner 03 §15.2 and owner 03 adv §2; ✅ Wikipedia: Balban for Mewat and Awadh)
**Breakdown:**
- Wikipedia: Meo raiders of Mewat; unruly groups in Awadh; Ranthambore failed, Gwalior retaken (✅ Wikipedia).
- Katehar: owner only; not in fetched Wikipedia text (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Balban's reign (✅).
**Links:** MED-1068, MED-1069.
**Trap:** Blood-and-iron applied to internal law and order, not only frontiers (✅ owner 03 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Use internal repression as evidence of coercive state capacity.
**Back:** *"Mewat, doab, Katehar: inside Delhi's arm."*

### Flashcard MED-1071 — Tughril's rebellion in Bengal, 1275–1280
**Front:** How did Balban deal with Bengal's rebellion?
**Definition (learn):** Governor Tughril Tughan Khan renounced Delhi's authority in 1275; after two failed armies, Balban joined a third force, killed Tughril, and his son Bughra Khan became governor. (✅ Wikipedia: Balban; ✅ owner 03 §15.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: suppressed Tughril's rebellion and reasserted sovereignty; distance persisted (✅ owner 03 §15.2).
- Bughra Khan later remained in Bengal (✅ owner 03 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1275 rebellion (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1067, MED-1073.
**Trap:** Delegated Bengal did not mean integrated Bengal (✅ owner 03 §17.1).
**Mains hook:** Distance and delegated rule as recurring limits.
**Back:** *"1275: Tughril rebels, Balban kills him."*

### Flashcard MED-1072 — Prince Muhammad and the Beas battle (9 March 1285)
**Front:** How did Balban's heir die, and what followed?
**Definition (learn):** Balban's heir Prince Muhammad Khan was killed fighting the Mongols on 9 March 1285 (Battle of Beas River, Wikipedia), creating a succession crisis. (✅ owner 03 §15.2; ✅ Wikipedia: Balban)
**Breakdown:**
- Balban named grandson Kaikhasrau heir; nobles chose Qaiqabad (✅ Wikipedia).
- Mongol pressure constrained distant expansion (✅ owner 03 §15.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 9 March 1285 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1073, MED-1066.
**Trap:** Prince Muhammad's death is not the end of Mamluk rule; the Khalji takeover came in 1290 (✅ owner 03 §16).
**Mains hook:** External threat shaped succession.
**Back:** *"1285: the heir dies fighting the Mongols."*

### Flashcard MED-1073 — Qaiqabad and the end of Mamluk primacy, 1290
**Front:** How did the Mamluk dynasty end?
**Definition (learn):** Court factions placed Balban's grandson Muiz al-Din Qaiqabad on the throne; Bughra Khan remained in Bengal; the weakened Balbani household and the growing Khalji group ended Mamluk primacy in 1290. (✅ owner 03 §16)
**Breakdown:**
- Structural and factional change, not one weak ruler or ethnic replacement (✅ owner 03 §16).
- Khalji takeover in 1290 (✅ owner 04 §1; ✅ Wikipedia: Khalji dynasty).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1290 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1087, MED-1072.
**Trap:** Qaiqabad marks the Mamluk/Khalji transition, not a Khalji ruler (✅ owner 03 §22.2).
**Mains hook:** Explain the dynastic end through structure.
**Back:** *"Qaiqabad, Bughra, Khalji: 1290 ends the slave line."*

### Flashcard MED-1074 — Territorial zones: core, frontier, Bengal, Rajput forts, Deccan
**Front:** How should Mamluk-era territorial extent be read?
**Definition (learn):** Authority varied by zone: the Delhi-upper doab core (strongest), the Lahore/Punjab/Multan-Sindh frontier, Bengal/Lakhnauti with intermittent suzerainty, Rajput forts with changing direct control, and the Deccan outside Mamluk control. (✅ owner 03 §17.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Maps must distinguish claim, campaign, tribute, garrison and regular revenue (✅ owner 03 §17.1).
- Deccan outside the Mamluk state; later dynasties own expansion (✅ owner 03 §17.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Zone table (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MED-1021, MED-1058.
**Trap:** A maximal shaded map misstates reach (✅ owner 04 §11.2).
**Mains hook:** Use zones to explain the Sultanate's core-frontier asymmetry.
**Back:** *"Core, frontier, Bengal, Rajput forts, Deccan: five reaches."*

### Flashcard MED-1075 — Administrative vocabulary of the early Sultanate
**Front:** Define the early Sultanate's core administrative terms.
**Definition (learn):** Iqta (revenue assignment linked to service), muqti/wali (holder/governor), army (central, mamluk household and iqta contingents), central departments, justice (sultan, qazi, local), barids (intelligence). (✅ owner 03 §17.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Iqta: not European feudalism or private land ownership (✅ owner 03 §17.2).
- Muqti/wali: title and duties varied (✅ owner 03 §17.2).
- Departments: later full diwan structure must not be back-projected (✅ owner 03 §17.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Table (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MED-1076, MED-1176.
**Trap:** Do not back-project later departmental charts into 1206 (✅ owner 03 §12).
**Mains hook:** Institutional terms need dated qualification.
**Back:** *"Iqta, muqti, barid, departments: each with its date."*

### Flashcard MED-1076 — Iqta is revenue service, not private ownership
**Front:** What is an iqta, and why is "feudal ownership" wrong?
**Definition (learn):** An iqta was an assignment of revenue and associated service/administrative responsibility, not ownership of the soil, cultivators or a European feudal estate. (✅ owner 03 §14; ✅ owner 07 §14)
**Breakdown:**
- Cycle: central assignment → muqti collects → pays troops and administers → submits accounts and remits surplus → centre may transfer, audit, reduce or resume (✅ owner 07 §14).
