- Wikipedia: iqta is a temporary grant of land-tax revenue for service; holders had no claim on peasants beyond assigned tax (✅ Wikipedia: Iqta).
- Conflict: owner 03 says Iltutmish "did not invent it from nothing", while Wikipedia says Iltutmish set up the system; Wikipedia adds that under Iltutmish iqtas were usually hereditary in practice (⚠️ owner 03 §12.2 vs ✅ Wikipedia: Iqta).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Fetched: Alauddin appointed a diwan-i-bajarat to estimate iqta income; Muhammad paid soldiers from the treasury; Balban opposed heredity; Firuz made iqtas hereditary (✅ Wikipedia: Iqta).
**Links:** MED-1075, MED-1055, MED-1179.
**Trap:** Iqta ≠ jagir in every respect (✅ owner 07 §5).
**Mains hook:** Revenue assignment for service, with heredity pressures; transfer and audit determined its nature (✅ owner 07 §8.1).
**Back:** *"Iqta: revenue for service, on loan, not owned."*

### Flashcard MED-1077 — Quwwat-ul-Islam mosque and the Qutb Minar phases
**Front:** What do the Qutb complex's construction phases show?
**Definition (learn):** The Quwwat-ul-Islam mosque was begun under Aibak in 1199 and enlarged later; the Qutb Minar was begun by Aibak and completed by Iltutmish around 1220; Firuz Shah Tughlaq repaired the tower after lightning (1368/1369). (✅ Wikipedia: Qutb Minar; ✅ owner 03 §20)
**Breakdown:**
- Quwwat-ul-Islam: earliest surviving mosque built by the Delhi Sultans (✅ Wikipedia).
- Qutb Minar: 72.5 m; Iltutmish added three storeys; Sikandar Lodi repaired earthquake damage after 1505 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Spolia: temple remains reused in both (✅ Wikipedia: Qutb al-Din Aibak; ✅ owner 03 §20).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1199, c. 1220 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1052, MED-1055, MED-1109.
**Trap:** Qutb Minar is not one-ruler construction (✅ owner 03 §22.2); the text "1199 and 1503" in Wikipedia is an error.
**Mains hook:** Architecture as material evidence of appropriation and hybrid craft (✅ owner 03 §20).
**Back:** *"1199 Aibak, 1220 Iltutmish, many hands after."*

### Flashcard MED-1078 — Sultan Ghari tomb, 1231
**Front:** What is the Sultan Ghari tomb, and what does it show?
**Definition (learn):** Tomb built for Iltutmish's son Nasiruddin Mahmud in 1231, an early monumental funerary patronage. (✅ owner 03 §20)
**Breakdown:**
- Early funerary architecture (✅ owner 03 §20).
- Owner does not provide further architectural sourcing (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1231 (✅ owner 03 §20).
**Links:** MED-1077, MED-1055.
**Trap:** Modern heritage claims do not settle construction phases (✅ owner 03 §20).
**Mains hook:** Use it as evidence of dynastic patronage.
**Back:** *"1231: a prince's tomb, an early Sultanate statement."*

### Flashcard MED-1079 — Balban's tomb and the true arch: qualify "first" claims
**Front:** What is claimed about Balban's tomb, and how should it be qualified?
**Definition (learn):** Balban's tomb is associated with an important true-arch phase in Indian architecture; "first" claims need architectural qualification. (✅ owner 03 §20; ✅ owner 04 §6 for the source placing the first surviving true arch at Balban's tomb)
**Breakdown:**
- Alai Darwaza is a mature arch-and-dome phase, not the first arch (✅ owner 04 §6).
- Lime-mortar and true arch enabled larger domes (✅ owner 07 §8.3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Balban 1266–87 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1109, MED-1187.
**Trap:** Do not call Alai Darwaza India's first true arch (✅ owner 04 §6).
**Mains hook:** Technology in architecture as cumulative diffusion.
**Back:** *"Balban's arch first; Alai's dome mature."*

### Flashcard MED-1080 — Sufis of Delhi and Punjab: Bakhtiyar Kaki and Baba Farid
**Front:** Name two early Sultanate Sufis and state their role.
**Definition (learn):** Delhi and Punjab Sufi networks developed distinct religious authority; Qutb al-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki (Delhi) and Baba Farid (Punjab) are the owner's examples. (✅ owner 03 §19)
**Breakdown:**
- Sufis were not one religious establishment (✅ owner 07 §23).
- Ulama provided legal legitimacy but were not one bloc (✅ owner 03 §19).
- Refugees, scholars and Sufis expanded Delhi's Persianate world under Iltutmish (✅ owner 03 §12.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated in owner.
**Links:** MED-1055, MED-1082.
**Trap:** Ulama did not control policy (✅ owner 03 §19).
**Mains hook:** Religious legitimacy as plural and negotiated.
**Back:** *"Kaki in Delhi, Farid in Punjab: authority without one establishment."*

### Flashcard MED-1081 — Minhaj-i Siraj Juzjani, Tabaqat-i Nasiri (1260)
**Front:** What is the *Tabaqat-i Nasiri*, when was it completed, and what does it cover?
**Definition (learn):** A Persian general history completed in 1260 by Minhaj-i Siraj Juzjani, who had left Khurasan for India in 1227; named after Sultan Nasir-ud-Din (Nasiruddin Mahmud). (✅ Wikipedia: Tabaqat-i-Nasiri; ✅ owner 03 §9.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Covers from Adam through Abbasid decline, Ghaznavids, Ghurids and the Delhi Sultanate; to Mongol history to 1259 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Secure use: Ghurid genealogy, Iltutmish, Razia, nobles (✅ owner 03 §9.2).
- Limit: court perspective, clerical outlook and gendered judgement (✅ owner 03 §9.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1260 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1063, MED-1034, MED-1082.
**Trap:** Tabaqat-i Nasiri ≠ Barani's *Tarikh-i Firuz Shahi* (MED-1103) (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Mains hook:** Judge a chronicle by its patron and date.
**Back:** *"1260: Minhaj writes for Nasir-ud-Din's court."*

### Flashcard MED-1082 — Hasan Nizami, Taj al-Ma'asir (early conquest chronicle)
**Front:** What is the *Taj al-Ma'asir* and what is its best use?
**Definition (learn):** An early Indo-Persian conquest chronicle by Hasan Nizami covering Aibak-era occupation, revolt and legitimacy. (✅ owner 03 §9.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Secure use: occupation and vassalage (✅ owner 03 §9.2).
- Limit: ornate panegyric and selective narrative (✅ owner 03 §9.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Aibak period (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MED-1081, MED-1052.
**Trap:** Do not use Hasan Nizami as an audit of Aibak's revenue (✅ owner 03 §9.2).
**Mains hook:** Panegyric source in conquest context.
**Back:** *"Taj al-Ma'asir: Aibak's conquest, polished."*

### Flashcard MED-1083 — PYQ routing and traps for Owner 03
**Front:** Which PYQs connect to Owner 03, and what are its Prelims traps?
**Definition (learn):** No verified PYQ is routed directly to Topic 03; three retained adjacent applications (✅ owner 03 §22.1).
**Breakdown:**
- 2019 Prelims Q12, Sultanate revenue administration: Medieval Topic 07 (✅ owner 03).
- 2022 Prelims Q57, Mongol invasions: Topic 04 (✅ owner 03).
- 2020 GS-I Q12, Persian literary sources: Topic 24 (✅ owner 03).
- Traps: Aibak begins, Iltutmish consolidates; Yildiz = Ghazni; Qubacha = Multan; tanka/jital are Iltutmish; Chihalgani not forty; iqta not private land; Qutb Minar multi-phase (✅ owner 03 §22.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Official keys not held locally (✅ owner 03).
**Links:** MED-1018, MED-1061, MED-1062, MED-1084.
**Trap:** Balban served as naib before sultan (✅ owner 03 §22.2).
**Mains hook:** Route the Sultanate-revenue question to Topic 07.
**Back:** *"2019 to 07, 2022 to 04, 2020 to 24."*

### Flashcard MED-1084 — Mains architecture for Owner 03
**Front:** What is the answer skeleton for Iltutmish, Razia or Balban questions?
**Definition (learn):** Structures from owner 03 §23.
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks (Iltutmish): Yildiz/Qubacha → Bengal/Rajput frontiers → Mongol prudence → iqta, coinage and Delhi → caliphal recognition as symbolic → verdict (✅ owner 03 §23).
- 15 marks (Razia or Balban): succession/faction → policy and personal authority → noble composition → gender or kingship ideology → military/administrative evidence → source limitation (✅ owner 03 §23).
- 20 marks: terminology and sources; Ghurid-to-independent transition; iqta, army, departments, justice; religious legitimacy; Mongol constraint; factional end (✅ owner 03 §23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** MED-1055, MED-1063, MED-1067.
**Trap:** Do not present Balban's absolutism as unique (⚠️ owner 03 §8.3).
**Mains hook:** Institution-building under structural constraints.
**Back:** *"Ten for Iltutmish, fifteen for Razia or Balban, twenty for the state."*

### Flashcard MED-1085 — Nature of the early Mamluk Sultanate
**Front:** How should the Mamluk Sultanate be characterised?
**Definition (learn):** A military-fiscal monarchy rooted in conquest; a Delhi-centred Persianate court; dependent on mamluk and freeborn elite networks and iqta assignments; constrained by Mongols, distance, rebellion and succession; negotiated through local chiefs and religious institutions; strong in the core, uneven at frontiers. (✅ owner 03 §21)
**Breakdown:**
- Sovereignty claimed through force, coin, khutba, titles, investiture, architecture and justice; none alone made a modern state (✅ owner 03 §21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1206–1290 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1074, MED-1198.
**Trap:** Modern territorial-state terms do not describe it (✅ owner 03 §21).
**Mains hook:** Use the six-part characterisation as an opener.
**Back:** *"Military-fiscal, Delhi-centred, elite-dependent, core-strong."*

### GROUP — The Khaljis: Alauddin Khalji & Market Reforms

### Flashcard MED-1086 — The "Khalji revolution" of 1290
**Front:** What did the 1290 Khalji accession change?
**Definition (learn):** Jalaluddin Firuz Khalji's accession broadened the ruling class beyond the Shamsi-Turkish mamluk monopoly; Satish Chandra treats it as a widening of the political base, not a national or social revolution. (✅ owner 04 §1 and §9.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Khalji origin: Turkic, long associated with Afghan regions; Turks sometimes called them outsiders or "Afghans" (✅ owner 04 §9.1).
- Wikipedia: called Turko-Afghan; Turkish nobles viewed them as non-Turks (✅ Wikipedia: Khalji dynasty).
- Ended Mamluk rule and started the Khalji phase 1290–1320 (✅ Wikipedia: Khalji dynasty).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** January 1290 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1073, MED-1087, MED-1012.
**Trap:** Khalji ≠ slave dynasty; Khalji ≠ Afghan ethnic label (✅ owner 04 §5).
**Mains hook:** Recomposition of elites, not revolution in the modern sense.
**Back:** *"1290: the ruling class widens, not the state overturned."*

### Flashcard MED-1087 — Jalaluddin Firuz Khalji, 1290–96
**Front:** Who was Jalaluddin Firuz Khalji, and what was his reign's tone?
**Definition (learn):** The founder of the Khalji dynasty, who reigned from 1290 to 1296 and sought legitimacy through relative clemency and elite accommodation. (✅ owner 04 §9.2; ✅ Wikipedia: Khalji dynasty)
**Breakdown:**
- About 70 when he took power; described as mild and kind (✅ Wikipedia).
- Old-nobility suspicion of his accession (✅ owner 04 §9.2).
- Killed by Alauddin in 1296 (MED-1088).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1290–96 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1086, MED-1088.
**Trap:** Jalaluddin founded the dynasty; Alauddin seized power (✅ owner 04 §19.2).
**Mains hook:** Clemency as a political tool in a fragile accession.
**Back:** *"Jalaluddin: clement, old, killed by his nephew."*

### Flashcard MED-1088 — Alauddin's seizure of power, 1296
**Front:** How did Alauddin take the throne in 1296?
**Definition (learn):** Alauddin murdered Jalaluddin and seized Delhi using treasure, patronage and military support, after a raid at Devagiri that funded the coup. (✅ owner 04 §9.2; ✅ Wikipedia: Alauddin Khalji)
**Breakdown:**
- Jalaluddin lured across the Ganges at Kara (around 20 July 1296) with about 1,000 soldiers; killed (✅ Wikipedia).
- Crowned in Delhi on 21 October 1296 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Devagiri loot funded the coup (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 20 July 1296 (Kara); 21 Oct 1296 (Delhi) (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1087, MED-1089.
**Trap:** Owner says "1296 seizure" and "Kara to Delhi"; Wikipedia gives the Kara date (✅ both).
**Mains hook:** Violent accession still required recognition and coalition building (✅ owner 04 §9.2).
**Back:** *"1296: Kara lure, Delhi crown, Devagiri gold."*

### Flashcard MED-1089 — Alauddin Khalji, 1296–1316
**Front:** Summarise Alauddin's reign: aims and achievements.
**Definition (learn):** The Khalji ruler who built a stronger, more centralised state, with a large standing army, market control and agrarian reform, and conquests in north and Deccan. (✅ owner 04 §1; ✅ Wikipedia: Alauddin Khalji)
**Breakdown:**
- Reign: 19 July 1296 to 4 January 1316 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Mongol invasions forced a standing army (✅ owner 04 §1).
- Conquests: Gujarat, Chittor 1303, Ranthambore, Malwa; Deccan under Kafur (✅ owner 04 §1).
- Death: 4 January 1316 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** MED-1090–MED-1111.
**Trap:** Alauddin is not Slave-dynasty (✅ owner 04 §5).
**Mains hook:** Alauddin as the Sultanate's first systematic regulator (✅ owner 04 §7).
**Back:** *"1296–1316: army, markets, Chittor, Kafur."*

### Flashcard MED-1090 — Mongol invasions repelled, 1297–1306, and Siri
**Front:** Which Mongol attacks shaped Alauddin's policy, and how did Delhi survive 1303?
**Definition (learn):** Repeated Mongol invasions forced a large standing army and fortified defence. In 1303, Mongols ransacked Delhi while Alauddin was away at Chittor and Warangal, but failed to breach the Siri fort. (✅ Wikipedia: Alauddin Khalji; ✅ owner 04 §10)
**Breakdown:**
- Wikipedia's invasion list: 1297–98, 1298, 1299, 1303, 1305, 1306 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner gives a 1299–1305 frame (⚠️ conflict in range; both name 1303).
- Measures: Siri, frontier forts, standing cavalry, horse branding, roll of soldiers (✅ owner 04 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1303 Siri (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1091, MED-1092, MED-1102.
**Trap:** The 2022 Prelims Mongol PYQ is routed to Topic 04, not to Topic 01 or 02 (✅ owner 04 §19.1).
**Mains hook:** External threat as driver of fiscal-military innovation (✅ owner 04 §7).
**Back:** *"Mongols came, Siri held, Alauddin paid for the army."*

### Flashcard MED-1091 — Siri: fortified capital and standing army base
**Front:** What was Siri and why did it matter?
**Definition (learn):** A fortified Delhi-region centre built under Alauddin for defence, court legitimacy and as a field base against the Mongols. (✅ owner 04 §10 and §17; ✅ Wikipedia: Alauddin Khalji)
**Breakdown:**
- Shelter for the court in 1303 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Urban construction also served legitimacy (✅ owner 04 §10).
- Archaeological survival incomplete (✅ owner 04 §17).
- Standing army of 475,000 horsemen is Firishta's figure (⚠️ later source, unverified).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1303 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1090, MED-1109.
**Trap:** Firishta is much later and cannot certify early army numbers (✅ owner 02 §19.2).
**Mains hook:** Siri as a symbol of defence-led state-building.
**Back:** *"Siri: Delhi's Mongol shield."*

### Flashcard MED-1092 — Chittor, 1303: conquest and the Padmini legend
**Front:** What was the 1303 Chittor conquest, and what is the Padmini story?
**Definition (learn):** Alauddin took Chittor, the Guhila capital under Ratnasimha, after an eight-month siege in 1303; Padmini is a later literary legend most modern historians reject. (✅ Wikipedia: Alauddin Khalji; ✅ owner 04 §11.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Khusrau claims 30,000 Hindus killed (✅ Wikipedia; source claim).
- Padmavat (Jayasi, 16th century) is literary memory (✅ owner 04 §9.3).
- Garrisoned control followed (✅ owner 04 §11.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1303; eight months (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1089, MED-1104.
**Trap:** Padmavat is not a contemporary Chittor chronicle (✅ owner 04 §19.2).
**Mains hook:** Use Chittor as conquest evidence, separating literary romance from record.
**Back:** *"1303 Chittor: eight months, then Padmavat's legend."*

### Flashcard MED-1093 — Gujarat (1299), Ranthambore (1301) and Malwa (c. 1305)
**Front:** Give the campaign ladder for Alauddin's northern and western theatres.
**Definition (learn):** Gujarat (1299) raid/conquest with ports and wealth; Ranthambore (1301) siege and conquest; Malwa (c. 1305) expanded reach (✅ owner 04 §11.1).
**Breakdown:**
- Gujarat: conquest and political incorporation (✅ owner 04 §11.1).
- Ranthambore: siege after resistance (✅ owner 04 §11.1).
- Malwa: extent should not be inferred from a campaign line alone (✅ owner 04 §11.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MED-1092, MED-1094.
**Trap:** A campaign is not automatic annexation (✅ owner 04 §11.2).
**Mains hook:** Graded control across north-west, core and expeditionary zones (✅ owner 04 §11.2).
**Back:** *"Gujarat 1299, Ranthambore 1301, Malwa 1305: campaigns, not maps."*

### Flashcard MED-1094 — Malik Kafur's Deccan expeditions: tribute, not annexation
**Front:** What were Malik Kafur's southern campaigns, and what did they not mean?
**Definition (learn):** Kafur's raids brought tribute and wealth from Devagiri, Warangal, Dwarasamudra and Madurai; they did not initially mean annexation as Delhi provinces. (✅ owner 04 §11.2; ✅ Wikipedia: Alauddin Khalji)
**Breakdown:**
- Devagiri (1308 per Wikipedia; owner gives 1306–11 span) (⚠️ date conflict).
- Warangal (1310): Prataparudra became a tributary (✅ Wikipedia).
- Dwarasamudra (1311): Ballala III became a tributary (✅ Wikipedia).
- Madurai/Pandya raid (1311) (✅ Wikipedia).
- Devagiri again 1313: Wikipedia tags it "citation needed" (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1308, 1310, 1311 (✅ Wikipedia); 1306–11 (owner).
**Links:** MED-1089, MED-1095.
**Trap:** "Kafur annexed the south outright" is wrong (✅ owner 04 §5).
**Mains hook:** Distinguish tribute, garrison and annexation (✅ owner 04 §11.2).
**Back:** *"Kafur took tribute; the south stayed mostly itself."*

### Flashcard MED-1095 — The three Delhi markets
**Front:** Name the three Delhi markets set up by Alauddin.
**Definition (learn):** Alauddin set up three markets at Delhi: (1) foodgrains, (2) costly cloth, (3) horses, slaves and cattle. (✅ owner 04 §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Foodgrains: grain mandi (✅ owner 04 §14.1).
- Cloth and costly goods: Sarai-i-Adl (⚠️ owner 04 §2 says standard name; owner adv §14.1 lists cloth).
- Horses, slaves and cattle: category-specific control (✅ owner 04 §14.1).
- Wider necessities: oil, sugar and other goods under price schedules (✅ owner 04 §14.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Three markets (✅ owner 04 and Wikipedia reports price control on wide goods).
**Links:** MED-1096, MED-1088.
**Trap:** Market control was centred on Delhi, not the whole empire (✅ owner 04 §5).
**Mains hook:** Market control as a system of categories (✅ owner 04 §14.1).
**Back:** *"Grain, cloth, cattle: three Delhi markets."*

### Flashcard MED-1096 — Shahna-i-mandi and Diwan-i-Riyasat: trap pair
**Front:** What is the difference between the shahna-i-mandi and the Diwan-i-Riyasat?
**Definition (learn):** The Diwan-i-Riyasat was the market ministry; the shahna-i-mandi was the market superintendent who kept merchant registers and enforced prices. (✅ owner 04 §1 and §14.2; ✅ owner 04 §19.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Shahna: one per market; merchant register; price enforcement (✅ owner 04 §1).
- Diwan-i-Riyasat: department (✅ owner 04 §14.2).
- Wikipedia: shahana-i-mandi official markets gave Muslim merchants exclusive permits (⚠️ a claim not in owner; Wikipedia: Khalji dynasty).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Shahna and Riyasat as above.
**Links:** MED-1095, MED-1097.
**Trap:** Do not conflate the market department (Riyasat) with the superintendent (shahna) (✅ owner 04 §19.2).
**Mains hook:** Use the shahna and register to argue for bureaucratic reach.
**Back:** *"Riyasat is the ministry; shahna is the superintendent."*

### Flashcard MED-1097 — Munhiyans and barids: the price-enforcement intelligence
**Front:** Who were the munhiyans, and how did they support market control?
**Definition (learn):** Intelligence agents (munhiyans and barids) who reported prices, stocks and violations to the Sultan, enforcing deterrence against hoarding and short-weighing. (✅ owner 04 §1 and §14.2; ✅ Wikipedia: Khalji dynasty for the munhiyan network)
**Breakdown:**
- Reports were coercive and selective (✅ owner 04 §10).
- Wikipedia: market supervisors and spies filed independent reports (✅ Wikipedia: Alauddin Khalji).
- Nothing 'remained hidden from the Sultan' (quoted in owner 04 adv §1 per Satish Chandra).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Alauddin's reign (✅).
**Links:** MED-1096, MED-1069.
**Trap:** Surveillance is coercive, not welfare (✅ owner 04 §15.2).
**Mains hook:** State capacity through intelligence (✅ owner 04 §7).
**Back:** *"Munhiyans: the price police."*

### Flashcard MED-1098 — Banjaras: registered grain carriers
**Front:** Who were the banjaras in Alauddin's grain system?
**Definition (learn):** Grain-carriers who were registered and collectively responsible, bound to bring grain to Delhi at fixed prices. (✅ owner 04 §1; ✅ owner 04 adv §2)
**Breakdown:**
- Registered and collectively responsible (✅ owner 04 §1).
- Wikipedia: transport workers resettled in villages along the Yamuna to speed grain (✅ Wikipedia: Alauddin Khalji).
- Compliance relied on coercion (✅ owner 04 §8.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Alauddin's reign (✅).
**Links:** MED-1099, MED-1100.
**Trap:** Banjaras were not a free market (✅ owner 04 §8.2).
**Mains hook:** The state controlled supply chain, not only the final price (✅ owner 04 §8.2).
**Back:** *"Banjaras: registered, responsible, bringing grain to Delhi."*

### Flashcard MED-1099 — Doab revenue: masahat, half the produce, and curbs on intermediaries
**Front:** What revenue reform sustained Alauddin's grain supply?
**Definition (learn):** In the Ganga-Yamuna doab (Meerut to Kara), the state took revenue directly, assessed by measurement (masahat) at about half the produce, curbing the privileges of khuts, muqaddams and chaudhris. (✅ owner 04 §1, §13.1; ✅ Wikipedia: Alauddin Khalji)
**Breakdown:**
- Iqtas removed in the doab (✅ Wikipedia).
- 50% kharaj, the maximum Hanafi law allows (✅ Wikipedia).
- Revenue calculated in kind, demanded generally in cash; near Delhi some in kind for royal stores (✅ owner 04 §1 and §13).
- Grazing and house taxes added (✅ owner 04 adv §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ½ produce; Meerut–Kara (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MED-1100, MED-1076, MED-1179.
**Trap:** Alauddin did not abolish all intermediaries; he curtailed them (✅ owner 04 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Doab revenue made market control feasible (✅ owner 04 adv §7).
**Back:** *"Measure, half, curb the middlemen."*

### Flashcard MED-1100 — Granaries and anti-hoarding: buffer stocks
**Front:** How did the state hold grain prices in scarcity?
**Definition (learn):** Government granaries stocked the state's share and released it in shortage; hoarding and overcharging faced very harsh punishment. (✅ owner 04 §1; ✅ Wikipedia: Alauddin Khalji)
**Breakdown:**
- Peasants barred from hoarding (✅ Wikipedia).
- Rationing reported for scarcity (✅ owner 04 §14.2).
- Violators (false weights) severely punished (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Alauddin's reign (✅).
**Links:** MED-1099, MED-1098.
**Trap:** Prices stayed fixed in his lifetime, not after (✅ Wikipedia: Mubarak Shah revoked price controls).
**Mains hook:** Storage and punishment as twin instruments.
**Back:** *"Granaries stored, punishment deterred."*

### Flashcard MED-1101 — Dagh (horse-branding) and chehra (descriptive roll)
**Front:** What do dagh and chehra mean, and when were they introduced?
**Definition (learn):** Dagh is horse branding to prevent substitution or fraud; chehra is the descriptive roll identifying soldiers. Both came in under Alauddin for army inspection. (✅ owner 04 §10; ✅ owner 07 §8.4; ✅ Wikipedia describes branding and rolls without the terms)
**Breakdown:**
- Sher Shah revived/adapted them; Akbar systematised within mansab framework (✅ owner 07 §8.4).
- Not a modern biometric bureaucracy (✅ owner 04 §10).
- Dagh = horse branding; chehra = descriptive personnel roll (✅ owner 04 §19.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Alauddin's reign; Sher Shah; Akbar (✅).
**Links:** MED-1090, MED-1179.
**Trap:** Dagh and chehra were not Mughal inventions (✅ owner 07 §8.4).
**Mains hook:** Continuity in military administration without teleology.
**Back:** *"Dagh brands the horse; chehra records the man."*

### Flashcard MED-1102 — Barani's motive chain: Mongol threat to market control
**Front:** What is Barani's explanation for market regulation, and what are its alternatives?
**Definition (learn):** Barani's chain: Mongol threat → large standing army → cash salaries → controlled cost of living and remounts → grain and revenue supply + market regulation. Alternatives: provisioning Delhi, disciplining merchants and nobles, political control. (✅ owner 04 §15.1; ✅ Wikipedia: Alauddin Khalji)
**Breakdown:**
- Wikipedia: Barani says low soldier salaries sufficed for a large army; Khusrau and Hamid Qalandar suggest public welfare (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner: political control as a dimension, not proof of one motive (✅ owner 04 §15.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Alauddin's reign (✅).
**Links:** MED-1090, MED-1103.
**Trap:** Market control was not welfare or socialism (✅ owner 04 §15.2).
**Mains hook:** "Most systematic economic regulation" — mechanism, motives, limits (✅ owner 04 §7).
**Back:** *"Mongols, army, low salaries, fixed prices."*

### Flashcard MED-1103 — Ziauddin Barani and the Tarikh-i Firuz Shahi
**Front:** Who was Barani and why is his account central but biased?
**Definition (learn):** Ziauddin Barani wrote the Tarikh-i Firuz Shahi (dated 1357 in owner 07) decades after Alauddin's reforms; his account of regulations, markets and revenue is indispensable but normative and aristocratic. (✅ owner 04 §9.3; ✅ owner 07 §9)
**Breakdown:**
- Barani's phrasing: "no means of checking the veracity" (✅ owner 04 adv §1).
- Fatwa-i-Jahandari: political theory (MED-1191) (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Wikipedia's Barani page fetch failed; no independent check (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1357 (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MED-1102, MED-1191.
**Trap:** Barani is not a neutral economist (✅ owner 04 §9.3).
**Mains hook:** Evaluate Barani as a source for Sultanate economic history (✅ owner 04 adv §7).
**Back:** *"Barani: essential, opinionated, late."*

### Flashcard MED-1104 — Amir Khusrau and the Khaza'in-ul-Futuh
**Front:** What did Amir Khusrau write for the Khalji period, and what is his bias?
**Definition (learn):** Court poet and historian; the *Khaza'in-ul-Futuh* records campaigns and conquest rhetoric; he also reported the price stability of Alauddin's markets. (✅ owner 04 §9.3; ✅ Wikipedia: Alauddin Khalji)
**Breakdown:**
- Panegyric, literary selection and patron dependence (✅ owner 04 §9.3).
- Reported Chittor massacre claim (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner 07 calls his works earliest Hindavi use (⚠️ owner 07 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Late 13th–early 14th century (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Links:** MED-1092, MED-1102.
**Trap:** Khusrau's praise of Alauddin is not neutral (✅ owner 04 §9.3).
**Mains hook:** Court poetry as evidence of ideology.
**Back:** *"Khusrau: poet-courtier, victories in verse."*

### Flashcard MED-1105 — Collapse of market control after 1316
**Front:** What happened to Alauddin's market system after his death?
**Definition (learn):** The system collapsed soon after 1316 because it depended on Alauddin's personal coercive apparatus; Mubarak Shah revoked the price controls months later. (✅ owner 04 §15.2; ✅ Wikipedia: Alauddin Khalji and Khalji dynasty)
**Breakdown:**
- Prices and wages rose within a few years (✅ Wikipedia: Khalji dynasty).
- Mubarak Shah relaxed several controls (✅ owner 04 adv §18).
- Cause: personal coercion, not institutional continuity (✅ owner 04 §8.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1316; months after (✅).
**Links:** MED-1107, MED-1089.
**Trap:** Market control did not survive him as a permanent institution (✅ owner 04 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Limits of coercion-based state capacity (✅ owner 04 adv §7).
**Back:** *"Alauddin dies, the prices follow."*

### Flashcard MED-1106 — Khalji vs Mamluk: the dynasty distinction
**Front:** Why is Alauddin not a Slave-dynasty ruler?
**Definition (learn):** The Khalji dynasty (1290–1320) was distinct from the Mamluk dynasty, beginning after Qaiqabad's failure. (✅ owner 04 §5; ✅ owner 03 §16; ✅ Wikipedia: Khalji dynasty)
**Breakdown:**
- Khalji origin: Turko-Afghan (✅ Wikipedia).
- Khalji 1290–1320; Mamluk ended 1290 (✅ owner 03).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1290–1320 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1073, MED-1086.
**Trap:** Alauddin belonged to the Khalji house, not the Slave line (✅ owner 04 §5).
**Mains hook:** Dynastic change within the same military-elite universe.
**Back:** *"Khalji is not Mamluk."*

### Flashcard MED-1107 — Succession crisis after Alauddin, 1316–20
**Front:** Give the ladder from Alauddin's death to the Tughlaq founding.
**Definition (learn):** After Alauddin's death on 4 January 1316, Malik Kafur was killed; Qutb al-Din Mubarak Shah ruled 1316–20; Khusrau Khan seized power in 1320; Ghazi Malik defeated him and founded the Tughlaq dynasty as Ghiyasuddin Tughluq. (✅ owner 04 §18; ✅ Wikipedia: Khalji dynasty; ✅ Wikipedia: Alauddin Khalji)
**Breakdown:**
- Malik Kafur's dominance ended violently; Shihabuddin as puppet (✅ Wikipedia).
- Khusrau Khan murdered Mubarak Shah in 1320 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Ghazi Malik beheaded Khusrau Khan (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1316, 1320 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1113, MED-1105.
**Trap:** Khusrau Khan (1320) is not Khusrau of the poetry (MED-1104) (⚠️ same name; check the role).
**Mains hook:** Multicausal collapse, not one ruler (✅ owner 04 §18).
**Back:** *"Kafur, Mubarak, Khusrau, Ghazi: 1316–1320."*

### Flashcard MED-1108 — Military-fiscal or welfare? Interpreting Alauddin's market control
**Front:** Which interpretation of market control does the owner endorse, and why?
**Definition (learn):** Military-fiscal device: fixed prices kept the army cheap to pay; benefits to Delhi consumers were a by-product, not the objective. (✅ owner 04 §5 and §8.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Mongol defence → army → low salaries → fixed prices → agrarian surplus (✅ owner 04 §8.1).
- Not socialism or welfare (✅ owner 04 §15.2).
- Barani's bias requires caution (✅ owner 04 §8.3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** MED-1102, MED-1105.
**Trap:** Alauddin fixed prices to help the poor is the trap (✅ owner 04 §5).
**Mains hook:** Military-fiscal vs welfare, source-qualified.
**Back:** *"Army first, consumers second."*

### Flashcard MED-1109 — Alai Darwaza, 1311: mature arch and dome, not the first
**Front:** What does Alai Darwaza show, and what is the trap?
**Definition (learn):** A royal gateway of 1311 showing mature arch and dome construction, calligraphy and royal gateway design; not the first arch in India. (✅ owner 04 §17; ✅ owner 04 §6)
**Breakdown:**
- Alai Minar: an uncompleted ambition (✅ owner 04 §17).
- Hauz-i Alai: water supply and urban planning (✅ owner 04 §17).
- Siri: archaeological survival incomplete (✅ owner 04 §17).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1311 (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MED-1079, MED-1077.
**Trap:** Alai Darwaza ≠ first true arch; Balban's tomb holds the source's first surviving true arch (✅ owner 04 §6).
**Mains hook:** Architecture as state capacity and ideology.
**Back:** *"Alai 1311: mature dome, not first arch."*

### Flashcard MED-1110 — PYQ routing and traps for Owner 04
**Front:** Which PYQs route to Owner 04, and what are its traps?
**Definition (learn):** One direct routed demand: 2022 Prelims Q57, Mongol invasions under Khalji and Tughlaq rulers; official key unavailable locally; owner infers statement 2 only (✅ owner 04 §19.1).
**Breakdown:**
- 2020 GS-I Q12, Persian literary sources: Topic 24 (✅ owner 04).
- 2023 GS-I Q12, Sultanate technology: Topic 07 (✅ owner 04).
- Traps: Jalaluddin founded dynasty; Alauddin seized power 1296; dagh and chehra; Diwan-i-Riyasat vs shahna; Sarai-i-Adl is a market; Malik Kafur's raids; tribute not annexation; Padmavat late (✅ owner 04 §19.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No key held locally (✅ owner 04).
**Links:** MED-1090, MED-1095, MED-1111.
**Trap:** Sarai-i-Adl is a cloth market here, not a court of justice (✅ owner 04 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Route the Mongol PYQ to Topic 04 and do not infer its key.
**Back:** *"Mongols to Khalji; keys stay unknown."*

### Flashcard MED-1111 — Mains architecture for Owner 04 (market regulation, Mongols, Kafur)
**Front:** Outline the 15-mark answer on market regulation.
**Definition (learn):** Structure from owner 04 §20 and §15.
**Breakdown:**
- Objectives and Barani's source → agrarian/khalisa supply base → markets, prices and officials → transport, storage, intelligence and punishment → effects on army, consumer, merchant and peasant → geographic, duration and sustainability limits (✅ owner 04 §20).
- 10 marks: chronology → Siri and frontier → standing cavalry → dagh/chehra/cash salaries → market-revenue link (✅ owner 04 §20).
- 20 marks: accession and elite change; Mongol reform; core/frontier geography; Deccan tribute vs annexation; sharia and anti-rebellion controls; revenue and urban economy; source limits; succession (✅ owner 04 §20).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** MED-1108, MED-1102.
**Trap:** Do not apply the market system across the whole empire (✅ owner 04 §5).
**Mains hook:** Qualify with duration and coercion.
**Back:** *"Objectives, base, markets, enforcement, effects, limits."*


### GROUP — The Tughlaqs: Muhammad bin Tughlaq & Firuz Shah

### Flashcard MED-1112 — Tughlaq experiments: centralisation without capacity
**Front:** What is the central analytical question about Muhammad bin Tughlaq and Firuz Shah?
**Definition (learn):** The Tughlaq period tests whether an all-India, centralised Sultanate could be administered. Muhammad tried wide centralisation and experiments; Firuz pursued conciliation, welfare and hereditary elite bargains. (✅ owner 05 §1 and §21)
**Breakdown:**
- Muhammad: uniform administration, regulations, bringing distant regions into one system (✅ owner 05 adv §1).
- Firuz: appeasement of nobles, soldiers and theologians; avoided the Deccan (✅ owner 05 §1).
- Each policy must be tested by intent, capacity, timing, ecology and sources (✅ owner 05 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Muhammad 1325–51; Firuz 1351–88 (✅ owner 05; ✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1114, MED-1125, MED-1134.
**Trap:** Muhammad was not simply "mad"; Satish Chandra treats him as intelligent and administratively impatient (✅ owner 05 §5).
**Mains hook:** Muhammad shows the gap between administrative imagination and state capacity; Firuz shows the price of stability (✅ owner 05 §7).
**Back:** *"Muhammad imagined, Firuz conciliated; neither had the capacity."*

### Flashcard MED-1113 — Ghiyasuddin Tughlaq (1320–25)
**Front:** Who founded the Tughlaq dynasty, and what was his short reign like?
**Definition (learn):** Ghiyasuddin (Ghazi Malik) rose from the north-western frontier establishment, overthrew Khusrau Khan in 1320, and ruled with moderation in revenue and concern for cultivation. (✅ owner 05 §9.1; ✅ Wikipedia: Khalji dynasty)
**Breakdown:**
- Tughlaqabad: fortified capital and legitimacy (✅ owner 05 §20).
- Bengal and Warangal campaigns; frontier campaigns (✅ owner 05 §9.1).
- Death in 1325 after a pavilion collapsed at Afghanpur; blame falls on Prince Juna Khan (Muhammad) in some accounts (✅ owner 05 §9.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1320–1325 (✅ owner 05).
**Links:** MED-1107, MED-1114, MED-1137.
**Trap:** The Afghanpur death is contested (accident, negligence or conspiracy) and cannot be resolved as fact (✅ owner 05 §9.1).
**Mains hook:** Use Ghiyasuddin's moderation as the baseline against which Muhammad's experiments look radical.
**Back:** *"1320: Ghazi Malik founds the Tughlaqs; 1325: a pavilion falls."*

### Flashcard MED-1114 — Muhammad bin Tughlaq (1325–51)
**Front:** When did Muhammad bin Tughlaq reign, and what made him notable?
**Definition (learn):** Son of Ghiyasuddin; reigned from 4 February 1325 to 20 March 1351; learned in religion, philosophy and sciences; open to Muslim mystics, Hindu yogis and Jain saints (✅ Wikipedia: Muhammad bin Tughluq; ✅ owner 05 §1).
**Breakdown:**
- Officials appointed by merit, not only noble birth (✅ owner 05 §1).
- Barani calls him a "rationalist" who would not accept claims merely on authority (✅ owner 05 §15).
- Daulatabad, token currency, Doab, Khurasan, Qarachil (MED-1115–MED-1120) (✅ owner 05).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 4 Feb 1325–20 Mar 1351 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1115, MED-1116, MED-1138.
**Trap:** "Mad king", "mad genius" and "complete failure" are stereotypes; test each decision (✅ owner 05 §15).
**Mains hook:** Muhammad as a case study in ambitious reform without administrative instruments (✅ owner 05 §7).
**Back:** *"1325–51: learned, rational, impatient, and ruinous."*

### Flashcard MED-1115 — Daulatabad (Deogir), 1327: the second capital
**Front:** What was the Daulatabad project, and why was it not a capital transfer?
**Definition (learn):** Deogir was renamed Daulatabad and developed as a second capital for better control over the south. Officials, nobles, Sufis, ulama and merchants were pressured to move; Delhi was not emptied. (✅ owner 05 §10; ✅ Wikipedia: Muhammad bin Tughluq)
**Breakdown:**
- Ordered 1327 (✅ Wikipedia); pressure about 1328–29 (✅ owner 05 adv mini-timeline).
- Hardship on the road reported (✅ Wikipedia).
- Plague and revolts; capital moved back to Delhi in 1335 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Barani's claim that Delhi was emptied of every dog and cat is exaggerated (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1327; return 1335 (✅ Wikipedia; owner 05 gives 1327–34/35).
**Links:** MED-1114, MED-1121, MED-1137.
**Trap:** Daulatabad was a second capital, not the only capital, and the migration was selective (✅ owner 05 §5).
**Mains hook:** Rational strategic goal plus coercion and logistics, defeated by regional breakaways (✅ owner 05 §10).
**Back:** *"Daulatabad: second capital, selective, reversed by 1335."*

### Flashcard MED-1116 — Token currency experiment, c. 1329–33
**Front:** What was the token currency, and why did it fail?
**Definition (learn):** Copper and brass coins were assigned the value of silver and gold currency. It failed because counterfeiting flooded the market, minting control was weak, and no verification or redemption system existed. (✅ owner 05 §1 and §11; ✅ Wikipedia: Muhammad bin Tughluq)
**Breakdown:**
- Introduced 1330 (Wikipedia); owner gives c. 1329–33 (⚠️ small date difference).
- Use stopped by 1333 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Forged coins lay in heaps; redemption burdened the treasury (✅ owner 05 adv §4 and §11).
- Chronicle imagery (heaps, "as worthless as stones") is not audited (✅ owner 05 §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1330 (Wikipedia); c. 1329–33 (owner).
**Links:** MED-1114, MED-1135.
**Trap:** Token currency existed elsewhere; the failure was administrative, not conceptual (✅ owner 05 §8.2).
**Mains hook:** Monetary innovation without verification collapses (✅ owner 05 §8.2).
**Back:** *"Copper as gold: forgers won, the treasury paid."*

### Flashcard MED-1117 — Doab revenue crisis and famine (c. 1334–41)
**Front:** Why did the Doab become a crisis under Muhammad?
**Definition (learn):** Revenue in the Ganga-Yamuna Doab was reassessed using standard yields and official prices rather than actual harvests; harsh collection and famine produced distress and an agrarian uprising. (✅ owner 05 §12.1; ✅ owner 05 adv §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Swargadwari camp as relief site (✅ owner 05 adv mini-timeline).
- Famine and policy overlapped; migration and rebellion followed (✅ owner 05 §12.1).
- Relief followed severe crisis; it did not erase the revenue crisis (✅ owner 05 §12.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 1334–41 (✅ owner 05 adv).
**Links:** MED-1118, MED-1139.
**Trap:** Relief camps are not a substitute for analysis of the revenue crisis (✅ owner 05 §12.2).
**Mains hook:** Over-assessment plus ecology as joint cause of peasant revolt (✅ owner 05 §8.3).
**Back:** *"Standard yields, official prices, famine: the Doab revolts."*

### Flashcard MED-1118 — Diwan-i-amir-i-Kohi and sondhar loans
**Front:** What was the Diwan-i-amir-i-Kohi, and why did it fail?
**Definition (learn):** An agricultural development department that gave sondhar loans for seeds, wells and superior crops to extend cultivation; it was an experiment in developmental policy, not mere famine charity. (✅ owner 05 §12.2; ✅ owner 05 §2)
**Breakdown:**
- Unsuitable or uncertain land selection (✅ owner 05 §12.2).
- Corruption and misuse of funds by officials (✅ owner 05 §8.2).
- Monitoring and recovery problems (✅ owner 05 §12.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Muhammad's reign (✅).
**Links:** MED-1117, MED-1128.
**Trap:** "Diwan-i-amir-i-Kohi = agricultural extension and loans", not famine relief (✅ owner 05 §23.2).
**Mains hook:** Early state-directed development without monitoring (✅ owner 05 §8.2).
**Back:** *"Kohi lent seed; officials pocketed it."*

### Flashcard MED-1119 — Khurasan project: planning, recruitment and the disbanded army
**Front:** What was the Khurasan project, and how is it different from the Qarachil expedition?
**Definition (learn):** A planned western campaign amid perceived opportunity, for which a large army was recruited and paid; the campaign was not launched and the army was disbanded in 1329 because it could not be paid. (✅ owner 05 §13; ✅ Wikipedia: Muhammad bin Tughluq)
**Breakdown:**
- Wikipedia: army possibly up to 370,000 (a chronicler's figure; treat as claim) (✅ Wikipedia).
- Troops paid a year in advance and kept idle (✅ Wikipedia).
- Fiscal burden on the treasury (✅ owner 05 §13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 1329 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1120, MED-1114.
**Trap:** Planning, recruitment and execution are separate acts (✅ owner 05 §13).
**Mains hook:** Frontier strategy after Mongol weakness, not world-conquest fantasy (✅ owner 05 adv §1).
**Back:** *"Khurasan: army raised, war never launched, army sent home."*

### Flashcard MED-1120 — Qarachil expedition, 1333: Himalayan not Chinese
**Front:** What was the Qarachil expedition, and was it an invasion of China?
**Definition (learn):** A campaign in 1333 into the Kumaon region of the Himalayan foothills; local resistance and terrain, climate and supply defeated it with severe losses. It was not an invasion of China. (✅ Wikipedia: Muhammad bin Tughluq; ✅ owner 05 §13)
**Breakdown:**
- Badauni and Ferishta say it was originally aimed at crossing the Himalayas toward China (✅ Wikipedia; treat as later chronicle claim).
- Wikipedia: reported losses of nearly all of about 100,000 soldiers (⚠️ chronicle figure).
- Owner: Kumaon-Kangra related (✅ owner 05 adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1333 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1119, MED-1114.
**Trap:** Qarachil ≠ China; Khurasan ≠ Qarachil (✅ owner 05 §23.2).
**Mains hook:** Planning versus execution, fiscal cost of troops, not imagined conquest.
**Back:** *"1333: Qarachil Himalaya, not China."*

### Flashcard MED-1121 — Southern breakaways: Madurai, Ma'bar, Dwarasamudra and Bahmani
**Front:** Which southern and Deccan regions broke from Delhi during Muhammad's reign, and what is the key date?
**Definition (learn):** Ma'bar/Madurai, Dwarasamudra and Warangal separated or rebelled; Jalaluddin Ahsan Khan founded the Madurai Sultanate after a 1334 rebellion; the Bahmani state followed in 1347. (✅ Wikipedia: Muhammad bin Tughluq; ✅ owner 05 §14)
**Breakdown:**
- Warangal conquered after the 1323 siege (✅ Wikipedia).
- Plague at Bidar hit the army on the march to suppress the revolt (✅ Wikipedia).
- Bengal, Gujarat, Sindh and Deccan rebellions or autonomy (✅ owner 05 §14).
- Bahmani 1347 under Ala al-Din Bahman Shah (✅ owner 05 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1323 (Warangal), 1334 (Ma'bar revolt), 1347 (Bahmani).
**Links:** MED-1115, MED-1119, MED-1122.
**Trap:** No single rebellion explains contraction; plague, army losses, fiscal pressure and distance interacted (✅ owner 05 §14).
**Mains hook:** The Sultanate's expansion outran administrative reach (✅ owner 05 §7).
**Back:** *"1323 Warangal, 1334 Ma'bar, 1347 Bahmani: the south slips."*

### Flashcard MED-1122 — Ibn Battuta: eyewitness with conventions
**Front:** What does Ibn Battuta offer on Muhammad's court, and what are his limits?
**Definition (learn):** The Moroccan traveller's *Rihla* provides eyewitness evidence on gifts, punishments, routes and Delhi; he visited Delhi in 1334. (✅ owner 05 §15; ✅ Wikipedia: Muhammad bin Tughluq)
**Breakdown:**
- Criticised excessive gifts and harsh punishments; the Sultan rarely took advice (✅ Wikipedia).
- His journal does not mention token currency (✅ Wikipedia).
- Literary conventions, marvels, composed after travel (✅ owner 05 §15).
- Owner 07: Delhi largest city in the eastern Islamic world; Daulatabad equal (✅ owner 07 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1334 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1114, MED-1182.
**Trap:** Vividness is not statistical representativeness (✅ owner 05 §15).
**Mains hook:** Eyewitness value with genre caution.
**Back:** *"Ibn Battuta saw, but wrote like a traveller."*

### Flashcard MED-1123 — Isami: Deccan displacement and grievance
**Front:** What does Isami's account contribute, and what must be discounted?
**Definition (learn):** Isami's account covers Deccan displacement, Daulatabad and political critique; he wrote from grievance or exile and retrospective memory. (✅ owner 05 §9.2; ✅ owner 04 §9.3)
**Breakdown:**
- Useful for Muhammad's southern and Daulatabad experience (✅ owner 05 §9.2).
- Later narrative of Khalji-Tughlaq transition (✅ owner 04 §9.3).
- Panegyric or grievance bias (✅ owner 05 §9.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Post-1330s (⚠️ date not given by owner).
**Links:** MED-1115, MED-1103.
**Trap:** Isami's suffering stories are literary; check against phased evidence (✅ owner 05 §10).
**Mains hook:** Separate grievance, memory and evidence in source criticism.
**Back:** *"Isami: grievance at a distance."*

### Flashcard MED-1124 — Shams-i Siraj Afif: Firuz's works and slave numbers
**Front:** What does Afif record about Firuz, and what should be checked?
**Definition (learn):** The later Tughlaq chronicler whose account of Firuz's works, administration, slaves and benevolence is celebratory and gives large-number claims. (✅ owner 05 §9.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Source of the 180,000 slave claim (⚠️ owner 05 §1 vs adv §19.1 conflict).
- Celebratory later-Tughlaq lens (✅ owner 05 §9.2).
- Title of Afif's chronicle is not given in owner 05; the standard Firuz-period work is Afif's *Tarikh-i Firuz Shahi* (⚠️ standard knowledge; unverified here).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Firuz's reign (✅).
**Links:** MED-1125, MED-1129.
**Trap:** Do not confuse Afif (Firuz) with Barani (Firuz-era political history) (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Use Afif to show the gap between chronicle claim and audited census.
**Back:** *"Afif praises Firuz; check the numbers."*

### Flashcard MED-1125 — Firuz Shah Tughlaq (1351–88)
**Front:** Summarise Firuz Shah Tughlaq's accession, reign and death.
**Definition (learn):** Firuz accepted power in 1351 on the Sindh banks after Muhammad's death; reigned about 37 years; sought peace, conciliation and public works in the north Indian core. (✅ Wikipedia: Firuz Shah Tughlaq; ✅ owner 05 §16)
**Breakdown:**
- Reign: 23 March 1351 to 20 September 1388 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Abdicated in 1387 in favour of Muhammad Shah III; nobles opposed; Tughluq Khan named (✅ Wikipedia).
- Buried at Hauz Khas beside a madrasa he built in 1352–53 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Accepted limits to reconquering distant provinces (✅ owner 05 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** MED-1126–MED-1133.
**Trap:** Firuz's peace was bought by appeasing elites (✅ owner 05 §5).
**Mains hook:** Welfare-oriented but politically decentralising phase (✅ owner 05 §7).
**Back:** *"1351–88: peace bought, works built, centre weakened."*

### Flashcard MED-1126 — Firuz's Bengal campaigns: treaty, not annexation
**Front:** What happened in Firuz's Bengal campaigns?
**Definition (learn):** Firuz campaigned in Bengal in 1353 and again in 1358; in 1353 he feigned retreat and defeated Ilyas Shah's army, declined to annex the marshy land, and in 1358 a treaty restored Zafar Khan. (✅ Wikipedia: Firuz Shah Tughlaq; ✅ owner 05 §16)
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: "failed to recover Bengal" (✅ owner 05 §1) conflicts with Wikipedia's treaty account (⚠️ conflict).
- Owner adv: mixed military results (✅ owner 05 adv §16).
- Thatta and Jajnagar/Nagarkot also mixed (✅ owner 05 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1353; 1358 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1125, MED-1055 (Bengal under Iltutmish).
**Trap:** Firuz did not annex Bengal; "failed" is too strong (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Use the treaty to show limited military ambition.
**Back:** *"1353 Ilyas flees, 1358 treaty: Bengal stays out."*

### Flashcard MED-1127 — Hisar-Firuzah and Firuzabad: towns of public works
**Front:** Which towns did Firuz found, and what were they for?
**Definition (learn):** Hisar-Firuzah (Haryana) and Firuzabad (on the Yamuna, later Firoz Shah Kotla) were towns linked to canals and cultivation. (✅ owner 05 §1; ✅ Wikipedia: Firuz Shah Tughlaq)
**Breakdown:**
- Hisar built about two years after the 1353 Bengal invasion; Firoz Shah Palace Complex dated 1354 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Firuzabad on the Yamuna before the second Bengal invasion; later stripped for building material (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner: towns, hospitals, public works department (✅ owner 05 adv §18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 1354 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1128, MED-1136.
**Trap:** Do not invent precise counts of towns or gardens (✅ owner 05 adv §18).
**Mains hook:** Public works as a welfare image and an agrarian base.
**Back:** *"Hisar and Firuzabad: towns from canals."*

### Flashcard MED-1128 — Canals and the haqq-i-sharb water charge
**Front:** What canals did Firuz build, and what was the haqq-i-sharb?
**Definition (learn):** Firuz dug canals linking water systems to Hisar and Haryana tracts; benefiting villages paid a water charge, the haqq-i-sharb, owner gives as 10%. (✅ owner 05 adv §18 and §4; ⚠️ Wikipedia excerpt lists five major canals and a Western Yamuna renovation but does not mention haqq-i-sharb)
**Breakdown:**
- Five major canals; Western Yamuna Canal renovated; Sarasvati–Markanda link near Sirhind (✅ Wikipedia).
- Canals supported two crops (✅ owner 07 §4).
- Benefits flowed to nobles and privileged groups because revenue was assigned as iqtas (✅ owner 07 adv §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 10% (owner); not verified by fetch (⚠️).
**Links:** MED-1127, MED-1133, MED-1076.
**Trap:** The Western Yamuna Canal dates to the Prithviraj Chauhan era in Wikipedia; Firuz renovated it (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** Canal policy as welfare plus elite benefit (✅ owner 05 adv §3).
**Back:** *"Five canals, one-tenth charge: benefit for the privileged."*

### Flashcard MED-1129 — Diwan-i-Bandagan and the slave establishment
**Front:** What was the Diwan-i-Bandagan, and how large was Firuz's slave establishment?
**Definition (learn):** The separate department managing bandagan (slaves) serving households, workshops, administration and armed roles. Afif's reported total is about 180,000, with 12,000 trained as artisans (owner adv). (✅ owner 05 adv §19.1 and §4; ⚠️ owner 05 §1 and §19.1 flag the figure as unaudited)
**Breakdown:**
- Basic owner: "treat as chronicler's large administrative claim" (⚠️ owner 05 §1).
- Advanced owner: "about 1,80,000 with 12,000 artisans" (✅ owner 05 adv §4) — a conflict in confidence level.
- Corps became a factional problem after Firuz (✅ owner 05 §19.1).
- Slaves in karkhanas, households and armed corps (✅ owner 07 §21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 180,000 (claim); 12,000 artisans (owner adv).
**Links:** MED-1124, MED-1130.
**Trap:** Do not treat the 180,000 as an audited census (✅ owner 05 §19.1).
**Mains hook:** Coerced labour and the faction risk of an armed slave corps (✅ owner 07 §21).
**Back:** *"Bandagan: one-eighty thousand, claimed; a faction in waiting."*

### Flashcard MED-1130 — Diwan-i-Khairat: dowries for the poor
**Front:** What was the Diwan-i-Khairat?
**Definition (learn):** A department associated with charity, notably dowries or marriage funding for girls from poor families (Muslim women in owner adv). (✅ Wikipedia: Firuz Shah Tughlaq; ✅ owner 05 adv §18)
**Breakdown:**
- Wikipedia: funded marriages for girls from poor families (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner: charity and dowries for needy Muslim women (✅ owner 05 adv §18).
- Employment-office claims should not be inflated into a labour ministry (✅ owner 05 adv §18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Firuz's reign (✅).
**Links:** MED-1125, MED-1132.
**Trap:** Khairat is charity, not a modern welfare ministry (✅ owner 05 adv §18).
**Mains hook:** Welfare as selective, communal and court-sponsored.
**Back:** *"Khairat: dowries for the needy, not a ministry."*

### Flashcard MED-1131 — Ashokan pillars moved to Delhi
**Front:** Which Ashokan pillars did Firuz move, and where did he place one?
**Definition (learn):** Firuz brought two pillars from Meerut and one from Topra (Haryana) to Delhi by bullock-cart trains and re-erected one on the roof of his palace at Firuz Shah Kotla. (✅ Wikipedia: Firuz Shah Tughlaq; ✅ owner 05 adv §19.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Carefully cut, wrapped in silk, transported (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner: inscriptions were not necessarily understood fully (✅ owner 05 §20).
- Orthodoxy coexisted with antiquarian appropriation (✅ owner 05 adv §19.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Meerut; Topra (near Radaur) (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1136, MED-1132.
**Trap:** Ashokan pillars were transported, not carved in Delhi (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** Sovereign display versus religious orthodoxy.
**Back:** *"Meerut and Topra: pillars to Delhi, one on Kotla's roof."*

### Flashcard MED-1132 — Jizya on Brahmans and Firuz's orthodoxy
**Front:** How did Firuz's orthodoxy show in taxation and religious policy?
**Definition (learn):** The owner records that jizya was treated as a distinct levy and extended to Brahmans who previously claimed exemption; Firuz also emphasised taxes he said were sanctioned by Islamic law and removed some cesses. (✅ owner 05 adv §19.2; ⚠️ Wikipedia gives a general "persecution of Hindus" statement without a jizya-on-Brahmans detail)
**Breakdown:**
- Sanskrit works translated; Ashokan pillars moved; older monuments conserved (✅ owner 05 adv §19.2).
- Repair of Surya Kund at Gaya (✅ Wikipedia).
- "Uniform persecution" and "complete tolerance" are both false (✅ owner 05 adv §19.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Reign 1351–88 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1131, MED-1130.
**Trap:** Orthodoxy did not preclude translations and public works (✅ owner 05 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Religious policy as plural, case-specific and political.
**Back:** *"Jizya extended, Sanskrit translated: orthodoxy with antiquarianism."*

### Flashcard MED-1133 — Heredity: iqtas, offices and wajh (soldiers' village grants)
**Front:** Which Firuz policies made elite positions hereditary, and what did they cost?
**Definition (learn):** Firuz made iqtas, offices and soldiers' village assignments (wajh) hereditary and reduced harsh audits, buying peace while weakening central transfer and audit. (✅ owner 05 adv §17; ✅ Wikipedia: Iqta)
**Breakdown:**
- Short-term: reassured nobles; military households stabilised (✅ owner 05 adv §17).
- Long-term: reduced transfer leverage, weaker cash review, inflated assignments (✅ owner 05 adv §17).
- Dagh weakened; high salaries in iqtas (✅ owner 05 adv §2).
- Wikipedia: Firuz revived iqta and made it hereditary (✅ Wikipedia: Iqta).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Firuz's reign.
**Links:** MED-1076, MED-1125, MED-1066.
**Trap:** Hereditary iqta did not become European private property by one decree (✅ owner 05 adv §17).
**Mains hook:** Trading short-term stability for long-term decentralisation (✅ owner 05 §8.1).
**Back:** *"Heredity bought peace and bled the centre."*

### Flashcard MED-1134 — Muhammad vs Firuz: comparison axes
**Front:** Compare Muhammad bin Tughlaq and Firuz Shah on objective, capacity, agrarian policy, military, religion and outcome.
**Definition (learn):** Muhammad sought centralisation and mobility through experiments; Firuz sought conciliation, core development and stability. (✅ owner 05 adv §21)
**Breakdown:**
- Agrarian: Doab crisis and loans vs canals and water levy (✅ owner 05 adv §21).
- Military: large recruitment and campaigns vs reduced distant ambition (✅ owner 05 adv §21).
- Religion: wide contacts plus harsh violence vs orthodoxy plus translations (✅ owner 05 adv §21).
- Outcome: rebellions and contraction vs long peace but weakened centre (✅ owner 05 adv §21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Table (✅).
**Links:** MED-1112, MED-1114, MED-1125.
**Trap:** Neither genius-failure nor benevolent-fanatical binaries are adequate (✅ owner 05 §21).
**Mains hook:** Use as a 15-mark comparative frame (✅ owner 05 §24).
**Back:** *"Muhammad centralised and failed; Firuz conciliated and drifted."*

### Flashcard MED-1135 — Token currency is not fiat money: trap
**Front:** Why is token currency a misleading analogy for modern fiat money?
**Definition (learn):** The Tughlaq tokens were metal, not paper; modern fiat money rests on legal tender, controlled issue, authentication and confidence. The analogy is misleading if applied directly. (✅ owner 05 §11)
**Breakdown:**
- Counterfeiting and inability to distinguish valid issues destroyed confidence (✅ owner 05 §11).
- Redemption cost the treasury (✅ owner 05 §11).
- Fiscal context, trust, minting technology and enforcement all mattered (✅ owner 05 §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As in MED-1116.
**Links:** MED-1116, MED-1196.
**Trap:** Token currency was not paper money (✅ owner 05 §23.2).
**Mains hook:** Use the analogy only with qualifications.
**Back:** *"Metal tokens, not paper: confidence was the currency."*

### Flashcard MED-1136 — Tughlaq architecture: Tughlaqabad, Jahanpanah, Firuz Shah Kotla, Hauz Khas
**Front:** Which Tughlaq monuments serve as evidence, and what is the caution for each?
**Definition (learn):** Tughlaqabad (Ghiyasuddin's fortified capital), Jahanpanah/Adilabad (Muhammad's defence), Firuz Shah Kotla (court, mosque, palace and Ashokan pillar) and Hauz Khas (water, madrasa, funerary patronage). (✅ owner 05 adv §20)
**Breakdown:**
- Tughlaqabad: death story contested (✅ owner 05 adv §20).
- Jahanpanah: construction phases need archaeology (✅ owner 05 adv §20).
- Firuz Shah Kotla: ruins incomplete (✅ owner 05 adv §20).
- Hauz Khas: earlier Khalji reservoir vs later Tughlaq work (✅ owner 05 adv §20).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** MED-1127, MED-1131.
**Trap:** Architecture is evidence for resources and ideology, not a measure of prosperity (✅ owner 05 adv §20).
**Mains hook:** Use monuments with phased caution.
**Back:** *"Tughlaqabad, Jahanpanah, Kotla, Hauz Khas: four stones, four cautions."*

### Flashcard MED-1137 — Ghiyasuddin's death at Afghanpur: contested accounts
**Front:** How did Ghiyasuddin die, and why can it not be settled?
**Definition (learn):** He died in 1325 after the collapse of a pavilion at Afghanpur; Barani, Ibn Battuta and later traditions give different accounts of accident, negligence or conspiracy by Prince Juna Khan (Muhammad). (✅ owner 05 §9.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Barani, Ibn Battuta and later traditions differ (✅ owner 05 §9.1).
- Accident, negligence and conspiracy cannot be resolved as audited fact (✅ owner 05 §9.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1325.
**Links:** MED-1113, MED-1114.
**Trap:** Do not state the death as a settled murder (✅ owner 05 §9.1).
**Mains hook:** Source reliability in succession narratives.
**Back:** *"1325: a pavilion, three stories, no verdict."*

### Flashcard MED-1138 — Muhammad's religion and the ulama: openness with violence
**Front:** How did Muhammad's religious and intellectual openness coexist with harsh punishment?
**Definition (learn):** Muhammad was learned in philosophy, logic, medicine and religious debate; he engaged ulama, Sufis, Jain teachers and Hindu yogis, but also used harsh punishment and political violence. (✅ owner 05 §15)
**Breakdown:**
- Barani criticised him as a rationalist (✅ owner 05 §15).
- Conflicts with some ulama and Sufis did not make him anti-religious (✅ owner 05 §15).
- Caliphal recognition and Islamic legitimacy still mattered (✅ owner 05 §15).
- Ibn Battuta criticised harsh punishments (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Reign 1325–51.
**Links:** MED-1114, MED-1122.
**Trap:** "Mad" or "ahead of his time" are stereotypes (✅ owner 05 §15).
**Mains hook:** Intellectual openness is not liberalism.
**Back:** *"Rationalist, learned, and violent."*

### Flashcard MED-1139 — Doab assessment: Alauddin vs Muhammad
**Front:** How did Muhammad's Doab assessment differ from Alauddin's?
**Definition (learn):** Both raised the Doab burden. Alauddin used measurement (masahat) at half of produce with curbs on intermediaries; Muhammad used standard yields and official prices rather than actual produce, with harsh collection. (✅ owner 04 §13.1; ✅ owner 05 §12.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Alauddin's system was supported by coercion and supply to Delhi (✅ owner 05 §8.3).
- Muhammad's yields and prices were disconnected from harvests (✅ owner 05 §12.1).
- Alauddin's policy was not uniquely gentler (⚠️ owner 05 §8.3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Alauddin 1296–1316; Muhammad 1325–51.
**Links:** MED-1099, MED-1117.
**Trap:** "Alauddin harsh but successful, Muhammad harsh and failed" needs the institutional difference (✅ owner 05 §8.3).
**Mains hook:** Coercive apparatus, not policy alone, decided outcome.
**Back:** *"Alauddin measured the crop; Muhammad guessed the yield."*

### Flashcard MED-1140 — PYQ routing and traps for Owner 05
**Front:** Which PYQs route to Owner 05, and what are its traps?
**Definition (learn):** 2021 Prelims Q38 (medieval rulers matched with events) is the direct Topic 05 route; the official key is unavailable locally and no answer is inferred. (✅ owner 05 §23.1)
**Breakdown:**
- 2022 Prelims Q57 (Mongols): Topic 04 (✅ owner 05).
- 2020 GS-I Q12 (Persian sources): Topic 24 (✅ owner 05).
- 2023 GS-I Q12 (Sultanate technology): Topic 07 (✅ owner 05).
- Traps: Daulatabad second capital; token currency copper/brass; Diwan-i-amir-i-Kohi loans; Khurasan ≠ Qarachil; Qarachil Himalayan; Firuz's canals, haqq-i-sharb, Diwan-i-Bandagan, Diwan-i-Khairat; Timur post-Firuz (✅ owner 05 §23.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 Q38 (✅ owner 05 adv ledger).
**Links:** MED-1083, MED-1141.
**Trap:** Timur's invasion was after Firuz, not under Muhammad (✅ owner 05 §23.2).
**Mains hook:** Route each PYQ by owner, not by keyword.
**Back:** *"2021 Q38 is ours; keys stay blank."*

### Flashcard MED-1141 — Mains architecture for Owner 05
**Front:** What are the 10-, 15- and 20-mark skeletons for Tughlaq questions?
**Definition (learn):** Structures from owner 05 §24.
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks (one experiment): intent → instrument → implementation → regional/ecological context → outcome → source limit → state-capacity verdict (✅ owner 05 §24).
- 15 marks (Muhammad vs Firuz): inherited crises → centralisation vs conciliation → fiscal and military tools → religion and elite management → short-term outcomes → long-term costs (✅ owner 05 §24).
- 20 marks: sources and stereotypes; empire geography; Daulatabad, currency, Doab, Kohi, Khurasan/Qarachil; breakaways; Firuz's canals, heredity, slavery, orthodoxy; succession and Timur; multicausal verdict (✅ owner 05 §24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** MED-1134, MED-1140.
**Trap:** Do not reduce the Tughlaqs to "failure vs welfare" (✅ owner 05 §5).
**Mains hook:** Sultanate oscillated between over-centralisation and devolution (✅ owner 05 §8.4).
**Back:** *"Intent, instrument, outcome, source, verdict."*

### GROUP — Decline of the Sultanate: Timur, Sayyids & Lodis

### Flashcard MED-1142 — Decline as a process, not an event
**Front:** How should the decline of the Delhi Sultanate be framed?
**Definition (learn):** A process: succession conflict, noble and slave factionalism, Firuz's devolutionary concessions, provincial state formation and communication limits, with Timur as a shock that exploited existing weakness. (✅ owner 06 §1 and §17)
**Breakdown:**
- Structural factors: open succession, elite faction, fiscal-military constraint, provincial dynamics, geography (✅ owner 06 §9).
- Regional state formation as political reorganisation, not darkness (✅ owner 06 §1).
- No single ruler, Timur or artillery explains decline (✅ owner 06 §17).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1388 to 1526 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1143, MED-1146, MED-1164.
**Trap:** Timur did not cause decline alone (✅ owner 06 §5).
**Mains hook:** Assess the decline as a process with a catalyst, not a sole cause (✅ owner 06 §8.1).
**Back:** *"Decline: a slow sickness, then a shock."*

### Flashcard MED-1143 — Post-Firuz succession chaos and the "Delhi to Palam" joke
**Front:** What was the state of the Delhi Sultanate before Timur, and what does the Palam joke show?
**Definition (learn):** After Firuz's death in 1388, nobles, rajas and Firuzi slaves intervened in succession politics; the sultan's real authority shrank so far that the joke ran that the "Lord of the Universe" ruled only from Delhi to Palam. (✅ owner 06 §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Two rival kings had already appeared in Delhi and Firuzabad before 1398 (✅ owner 06 adv §1).
- Provincial kingdoms broke away (✅ owner 06 adv §1).
- Palam joke is rhetorical image, not a surveyed boundary (✅ owner 06 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1388 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1142, MED-1144.
**Trap:** The Palam joke may be literary exaggeration (✅ owner 06 §8.2).
**Mains hook:** Central collapse preceded the external shock.
**Back:** *"Delhi to Palam: the writ shrank before Timur came."*

### Flashcard MED-1144 — Timur's 1398 invasion: route, sack and the date
**Front:** When did Timur invade and sack Delhi, and what was his route?
**Definition (learn):** Timur crossed the Indus on 30 September 1398 with a force of about 90,000 (Wikipedia figure) and won the battle and sack of Delhi on 17 December 1398. (✅ Wikipedia: Timur; ✅ owner 06 §10.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Route: Central Asian base → Indus → Punjab towns → Delhi → return north-west with booty (✅ owner 06 §10.1).
- Wikipedia: Timur reportedly killed about 100,000 captured slaves before the battle, from fear of revolt (⚠️ chronicle claim).
- Motives: plunder, prestige, frontier strategy, army reward, religious self-justification (✅ owner 06 §10.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 30 Sept 1398; 17 Dec 1398 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1145, MED-1146.
**Trap:** Timur did not establish an Indian dynasty; he left (✅ owner 06 §5, §10.3).
**Mains hook:** External shock exploiting internal weakness (✅ owner 06 §7).
**Back:** *"1398: Indus in September, Delhi in December."*

### Flashcard MED-1145 — Timur's sack and the artisans: source conflict
**Front:** What did Timur's sack do to Delhi, and what is the artisan claim?
**Definition (learn):** Delhi suffered mass violence, enslavement, looting and economic disruption; owner 06 says Indian artisans were carried toward Samarqand, which is not confirmed by the fetched Wikipedia excerpt. (✅ owner 06 §10.3; ⚠️ Wikipedia's artisan deportation statement concerns Aleppo and Damascus)
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: artisans and captives taken toward Samarqand (✅ owner 06 §10.3).
- Wikipedia: the deportation of artisans is recorded for Syria (Aleppo and Damascus) (✅ Wikipedia: Timur).
- Recovery and habitation continued after the sack (✅ owner 06 §10.3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Exact captive and loot numbers unverified (✅ owner 06 §10.3).
**Links:** MED-1144, MED-1146.
**Trap:** Do not attribute the Syrian artisan deportation to Delhi without a source (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Separate the sack's human cost from its administrative effect.
**Back:** *"Delhi sacked; artisans taken, the source claim needs care."*

### Flashcard MED-1146 — Timur as catalyst, not sole cause
**Front:** Is Timur the cause of the Sultanate's decline?
**Definition (learn):** Timur accelerated fragmentation; he did not solely cause it. Regional state formation continued after 1398. (✅ owner 06 §8.1 and §10.3)
**Breakdown:**
- Pre-existing fragmentation (✅ owner 06 §9).
- Timur as external shock (✅ owner 06 §7).
- Regional states Jaunpur, Bengal, Gujarat, Malwa, Mewar and others (✅ owner 06 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1398 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1142, MED-1144.
**Trap:** "Timur's invasion alone caused the decline" (✅ owner 06 §5).
**Mains hook:** Catalyst-not-cause verdict (✅ owner 06 §8.1).
**Back:** *"Timur hastened the fall; he did not write it."*

### Flashcard MED-1147 — Khizr Khan (1414–21): deputy of Timur
**Front:** Who was Khizr Khan, and how did he take Delhi?
**Definition (learn):** Timur appointed Khizr Khan as deputy of Multan, with charge including Lahore, Dipalpur, Multan and upper Sindh; he entered Delhi on 28 May 1414 without taking the title of sultan. (✅ Wikipedia: Sayyid dynasty; ✅ owner 06 §11.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Rayat-i-Ala, vassal, to Timur and later Shah Rukh (✅ Wikipedia).
- Shah Rukh's name in khutba; coins kept Tughlaq names (✅ Wikipedia).
- Sayyid descent is a legitimating tradition, not proven (✅ owner 06 §11.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1414–21; 28 May 1414 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1151, MED-1148.
**Trap:** Sayyids were not direct Timurid governors in a simple sense (✅ owner 06 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Restricted sovereignty through tribute and noble cooperation.
**Back:** *"Khizr Khan: Timur's deputy, Rayat-i-Ala, no sultan title."*

### Flashcard MED-1148 — Mubarak Shah (1421–34): ending nominal Timurid allegiance
**Front:** What did Mubarak Shah change, and what happened to him?
**Definition (learn):** Khizr Khan's son; he ended nominal allegiance to Timur, used the title shah and professed allegiance to the caliph alone; coins name him Muizz-ud-Din Mubarak Shah. (✅ Wikipedia: Sayyid dynasty; ✅ owner 06 §11.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Beat Hoshang Shah of Malwa, who paid tribute (✅ Wikipedia).
- Campaigns against Khokhars and Mewatis; put down Jasrat's rebellion (✅ owner 06 §11.2; ✅ Wikipedia).
- Murdered (owner; not in fetched excerpt) (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1421–34 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1147, MED-1149.
**Trap:** Mubarak Shah is a Sayyid, not a Lodi (✅ owner 06 §19).
**Mains hook:** Sovereign titulature as a measure of autonomy.
**Back:** *"Mubarak Shah dropped Timur's name and was killed."*

### Flashcard MED-1149 — Muhammad Shah (1434–45): vizier politics and Multan
**Front:** Who came to power with Muhammad Shah, and what was the cost?
**Definition (learn):** Muhammad Shah, Mubarak's nephew, came to power with vizier Sarwar ul-Mulk's help, then used Kamal ul-Mulk to free himself; his reign saw rebellions and Multan became independent under the Langahs. (✅ Wikipedia: Sayyid dynasty; ✅ owner 06 §11.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Wikipedia infobox 1434–43; text 1434–45 (⚠️ internal conflict; owner uses 1434–45).
- Factional pressure and shrinking authority (✅ owner 06 §11.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1434–45 (owner).
**Links:** MED-1148, MED-1150.
**Trap:** Multan under the Langahs was not Sayyid-controlled (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** Factional control of the sultan.
**Back:** *"Muhammad Shah: vizier-managed, Multan lost."*

### Flashcard MED-1150 — Alam Shah (1445–51): withdrawal and Bahlul's entry
**Front:** How did the Sayyid line end?
**Definition (learn):** Alam Shah, recalled from Badaun, proved ineffective and left duties to minister Hamid Khan; he appointed Bahlul Lodi governor of Sirhind; in April 1451 he abdicated voluntarily to Bahlul and withdrew to Badaun, where he died in 1478. (✅ Wikipedia: Sayyid dynasty; ✅ owner 06 §11.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Bahlul grew strong enough to attack Delhi (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner: Delhi yielded to Bahlul (✅ owner 06 §11.2).
- Abdication date: 19 April (text) or 20 April (infobox) (⚠️ Wikipedia internal).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1445–51; 1478 death (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1154, MED-1149.
**Trap:** Alam Shah is a Sayyid; Bahlul is the Lodi (✅ owner 06 §19).
**Mains hook:** Negotiated transfer, not conquest.
**Back:** *"Alam Shah stepped down for Bahlul in 1451."*

### Flashcard MED-1151 — Sayyid claim and legitimacy
**Front:** What is the basis of the Sayyid dynasty's claimed descent, and how should it be treated?
**Definition (learn):** The Sayyid title claimed descent from the Prophet; Yahya Sirhindi reports Syed Jalaluddin Bukhari established the Syed identity of Khizr Khan's father, Malik Sulaiman; modern historians doubt it and some suggest an Arab family long settled at Multan. (✅ Wikipedia: Sayyid dynasty; ✅ owner 06 §11.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: legitimating tradition, not securely proven (✅ owner 06 §11.1).
- Some scholars suggest Khizr Khan was a Punjabi chieftain of Khokhar clan (✅ Wikipedia).
- Dynasty: 1414–51, the fourth dynasty of the Sultanate (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1414–51 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1147, MED-1163.
**Trap:** The Sayyid label is a claim of descent, not a verified genealogy (✅ owner 06 §11.1).
**Mains hook:** Legitimacy by lineage claim under weak power.
**Back:** *"Sayyid: a claimed lineage, a restricted realm."*

### Flashcard MED-1152 — Malik Sarwar and the Sharqi kingdom
**Front:** Who founded the Sharqi kingdom of Jaunpur, and what did its name come from?
**Definition (learn):** Malik Sarwar, a eunuch slave and former wazir, held the title Malik-us-Sharq ("Master of the East") from 1394 as governor of Jaunpur, from which the Sharqis take their name. (✅ Wikipedia: Sharqi dynasty; ✅ owner 06 adv §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Earlier title Khwaja-i-Jahan (1389) (✅ Wikipedia).
- Later styled himself Atabak-i-Azam after asserting independence (✅ Wikipedia).
- Ruled 1394–99 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Mubarak Shah, Malik Sarwar's adopted son, later ruler (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1394 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1153, MED-1155.
**Trap:** Sharqi = Jaunpur kingdom, not a Delhi dynasty (✅ owner 06 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Regional state formation in the 15th century.
**Back:** *"Malik-us-Sharq 1394: Jaunpur becomes Sharqi."*

### Flashcard MED-1153 — Jaunpur: "Shiraz of the East" and its architecture
**Front:** Why was Jaunpur called the "Shiraz of the East", and which monuments show its style?
**Definition (learn):** Jaunpur was a cultural centre with a distinctive Sharqi architectural style; owner calls it "Shiraz of the East". Wikipedia calls it "Shiraz of India". (✅ owner 06 §2; ⚠️ conflict in the nickname's wording)
**Breakdown:**
- Atala Masjid: foundation laid 1376 under Firuz Shah Tughlaq; finished 1408 by Ibrahim Shah (✅ Wikipedia).
- Jhanjhari Masjid: 1430 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Lal Darwaza Mosque: 1450 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Jama Masjid: 1470 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1376, 1408, 1430, 1450, 1470 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1152, MED-1154.
**Trap:** Do not claim Jaunpur was insignificant; it was a major state (✅ owner 06 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Culture and patronage in regional states.
**Back:** *"Jaunpur: learning, lofty gates, Atala 1376 to 1408."*

### Flashcard MED-1154 — Bahlul Lodi (1451–89): the first Lodi
**Front:** Who was Bahlul Lodi, and what did he achieve?
**Definition (learn):** An Afghan leader from Punjab and Sirhind who took Delhi on 19 April 1451 after Alam Shah's abdication; he invited Afghans from the Roh region and fought the Sharqis. (✅ Wikipedia: Lodi dynasty; ✅ owner 06 §13)
**Breakdown:**
- Alliance, kinship, bargaining, grants and military pressure (✅ owner 06 §13).
- Lodi-Sharqi war ran 1452–1500 (✅ Wikipedia: Sharqi).
- Mahmud Shah's 1452 siege of Delhi repulsed by Bahlul (✅ Wikipedia: Sharqi).
- Divided kingdom among sons and relations (✅ owner 06 adv §13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1451 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1150, MED-1155, MED-1158.
**Trap:** Bahlul was an Afghan, not a Turk (✅ owner 06 §5).
**Mains hook:** Afghan mobilisation revived Delhi's upper-Ganga reach.
**Back:** *"Bahlul: Afghan, from Sirhind, Delhi in 1451."*

### Flashcard MED-1155 — Jaunpur's annexation: conflicting dates
**Front:** When did Bahlul annex Jaunpur, and why is the answer contested?
**Definition (learn):** Owner says 1484; Wikipedia's Lodi page gives Sikandar II's completion as 1479 and says Bahlul placed son Barbak on the Jaunpur throne in 1486; Wikipedia's Sharqi page says the state ended with Bahlul's defeat of Hussain Shah in 1494 at Benares, with other internal dates conflicting. (⚠️ owner-vs-source conflict; ✅ Wikipedia: Lodi dynasty; ✅ Wikipedia: Sharqi dynasty)
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: "annexes Jaunpur in 1484" (✅ owner 06 basic §1 and adv).
- Wikipedia: Barbak installed 1486 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Wikipedia lead: Hussain Shah defeated 1494; family tree gives Hussain 1458–79; one category says disestablished 1493 (⚠️ internal conflicts).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1484 (owner); 1479 / 1486 / 1494 (Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1152, MED-1154.
**Trap:** Quote the owner date only with the conflict flagged; do not treat 1484 as settled (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Source-aware chronology for regional annexations.
**Back:** *"1484 says the owner; 1479 and 1494 say the wiki."*

### Flashcard MED-1156 — Sikandar Lodi (1489–1517): administrator and founder of Agra
**Front:** What did Sikandar Lodi do to make the Lodis administratively strong?
**Definition (learn):** Strongest Lodi administrator: disciplined Afghan nobles, improved justice, made roads safer, moved the capital to Agra, and patronised Persian learning. (✅ owner 06 §14; ✅ Wikipedia: Lodi dynasty)
**Breakdown:**
- Crowned 15 July 1489 (✅ Wikipedia).
- Agra: re-founded as Muslim city in 1504 (Wikipedia); owner says 1506 (⚠️ conflict).
- Nobles submitted accounts to state audit (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner: nobles stood before him, honoured farmans, submitted jagir accounts (✅ owner 06 adv §1).
- Grain octroi abolition, rent rolls, gaz-i-Sikandari (owner only; see MED-1157).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1489–1517 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1157, MED-1158, MED-1159.
**Trap:** Sikandar was orthodox and also an efficient administrator, not only one or the other (✅ owner 06 §5).
**Mains hook:** Discipline of Afghan chiefs as a limit of monarchy (✅ owner 06 §7).
**Back:** *"Sikandar: audits, roads, Agra."*

### Flashcard MED-1157 — Gaz-i-Sikandari and the grain octroi
**Front:** What measurement standard and tax reform does the owner credit to Sikandar Lodi?
**Definition (learn):** The gaz-i-Sikandari, a measurement standard associated with revenue; and abolition of octroi duty on grain, which supported grain movement. (✅ owner 06 §14.1 and §4; ⚠️ not confirmed by the fetched Wikipedia Lodi article)
**Breakdown:**
- Application not necessarily uniform (✅ owner 06 §14.1).
- Rent and revenue rolls improved claims (✅ owner 06 §14.1).
- Grain octroi abolition does not prove empire-wide prosperity (✅ owner 06 §14.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (owner).
**Links:** MED-1156, MED-1099.
**Trap:** Gaz-i-Sikandari is not Akbar's gaz-i-ilahi (⚠️ standard knowledge; not in owner).
**Mains hook:** Measurement lineage: Alauddin, Sikandar, Sher Shah (✅ owner 04 adv §3).
**Back:** *"Gaz-i-Sikandari: measure and free the grain."*

### Flashcard MED-1158 — Afghan kingship: bargaining among equals, not democracy
**Front:** How did Afghan political culture differ from Turkish kingship?
**Definition (learn):** Afghan kingship involved clan and noble consultation and relative equality among armed chiefs, which made Sikandar's demands for deference and accounts notable. It was bargaining among noble lineages, not political equality for subjects. (✅ owner 06 §13 and adv Concept box; ⚠️ owner's "first among equals" framing)
**Breakdown:**
