- Turkish rulers stressed awe and hierarchy; Lodi politics had a "first among equals" element (✅ owner 06 adv Concept).
- Afghan ideas of partition and noble autonomy persisted (✅ owner 06 adv §1).
- Not democracy and not decentralisation by nature (✅ owner 06 §13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Lodi period.
**Links:** MED-1154, MED-1156, MED-1159.
**Trap:** Do not romanticise "Afghan tribal equality" (✅ owner 06 adv §1).
**Mains hook:** Lodi revival limited by noble autonomy.
**Back:** *"Afghan equals bargained; they did not vote."*

### Flashcard MED-1159 — Ibrahim Lodi (1517–26): centralisation and revolt
**Front:** Why did Ibrahim Lodi lose the support of his nobles?
**Definition (learn):** The last Lodi sultan, eldest son of Sikandar; he attempted stronger centralisation over Afghan chiefs accustomed to consultation; revolts followed from his brother Jalaluddin and from Pashtun nobles; his uncle Alam Khan sided with Babur. (✅ Wikipedia: Lodi dynasty; ✅ owner 06 §15)
**Breakdown:**
- Removal and punishment of nobles intensified revolt (✅ owner 06 §15).
- Daulat Khan Lodi in Punjab sought outside support (✅ owner 06 §15).
- Eastern Afghan chiefs pursued their own interests (✅ owner 06 §15).
- Rana Sanga pressure in Mewar-Malwa (✅ owner 06 §15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1517–26 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1156, MED-1160, MED-1161.
**Trap:** Ibrahim, not Sikandar, faced Babur at Panipat (✅ owner 06 §19).
**Mains hook:** Crown-chief conflict as cause of dynastic fracture (✅ owner 06 §8.1).
**Back:** *"Ibrahim tried to rule Afghans; they chose Babur."*

### Flashcard MED-1160 — Rana Sanga and the Babur communication question
**Front:** Who was Rana Sanga, and what is the debate about his dealings with Babur?
**Definition (learn):** Rana Sanga was the Rajput ruler of Mewar (r. 1509–26) who expanded into Malwa and fought the Lodi sultan. Babur claimed Sanga invited him; scholars reject or doubt this. (✅ Wikipedia: Lodi dynasty; ✅ owner 06 §15)
**Breakdown:**
- Expansion towards Malwa and Agra pressured Lodi power (✅ owner 06 adv §2).
- Exact communications debated (✅ owner 06 §15).
- Mahmud Lodi supplied about 4,000 Afghan soldiers to Sanga at Khanwa in 1527 (✅ Wikipedia: Lodi dynasty).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mewar 1509–26 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1159, MED-1161, MED-1164.
**Trap:** Babur entered an active coalition field, not an empty India (✅ owner 06 §15).
**Mains hook:** A field of competing powers, not a vacuum.
**Back:** *"Sanga's invite is claimed, not proven."*

### Flashcard MED-1161 — First Battle of Panipat, 21 April 1526
**Front:** What were the forces and outcome of the First Battle of Panipat?
**Definition (learn):** Babur (about 24,000 with muskets and artillery) defeated Ibrahim Lodi (about 100,000 and 1,000 elephants, without guns) on 21 April 1526; Ibrahim died on the field. (✅ Wikipedia: Lodi dynasty; ✅ owner 06 §16)
**Breakdown:**
- Carts (araba) and barriers; tulughma flanking (✅ owner 06 §16.1; memoir-derived).
- Firearms and field artillery: not the sole cause (✅ owner 06 §16.2).
- Ottoman-associated specialists (owner; not in fetched text) (⚠️).
- Cavalry, mounted archery, intelligence (✅ owner 06 §16.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 21 April 1526 (✅ Wikipedia). Numbers are chronicle-derived (⚠️).
**Links:** MED-1159, MED-1162.
**Trap:** Artillery alone did not decide the battle (✅ owner 06 §16.2).
**Mains hook:** Battle as integrated system of tactics, command and politics.
**Back:** *"1526: guns, carts and cavalry; Ibrahim fell."*

### Flashcard MED-1162 — Panipat's aftermath: not instant Mughal consolidation
**Front:** What followed Panipat, and why was it not the end of the story?
**Definition (learn):** Panipat ended Lodi rule but did not instantly create a consolidated Mughal empire; Khanwa, Chanderi, Ghagra and later struggles followed. (✅ owner 06 §16.2 and §18)
**Breakdown:**
- Khanwa (1527) against Rana Sanga (✅ owner 06 §16.2; ✅ Wikipedia).
- Mughal rulers adapted existing revenue, elite, language and urban practices (✅ owner 06 §18).
- 1526 was not total institutional replacement (✅ owner 06 §18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1526, 1527 (✅).
**Links:** MED-1161, MED-1160.
**Trap:** Do not write continuity teleologically as if the Sultanate existed only to prepare Mughal rule (✅ owner 06 §18).
**Mains hook:** Continuity without teleology (✅ owner 06 §18).
**Back:** *"Panipat ended the Lodis, not the Sultanate's institutions."*

### Flashcard MED-1163 — Khokhars, Mewatis and Roh: frontier and recruitment pressures
**Front:** Which groups pressed the Sayyids and Lodis, and how should each be treated?
**Definition (learn):** Khokhars in Punjab (frontier resistance and recruitment), Mewatis near Delhi (persistent local pressure), and the Roh-origin Afghans who were recruited by Bahlul. (✅ owner 06 §11.2, §12 and §13)
**Breakdown:**
- Khokhars: Sarang Khan recovered Lahore from Shaikha Khokhar (✅ Wikipedia: Sayyid dynasty).
- Mewat: persistent pressure near Delhi (✅ owner 06 §12).
- Roh: Afghan networks linking Punjab and armed lineages (✅ owner 06 §13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Fifteenth century.
**Links:** MED-1151, MED-1154.
**Trap:** Khokhars, Mewatis and Sharqis are not one enemy (✅ owner 06 adv §4).
**Mains hook:** Frontier politics as a constant drain.
**Back:** *"Khokhars in Punjab, Mewatis near Delhi, Roh for Bahlul."*

### Flashcard MED-1164 — Balance of power in the fifteenth century
**Front:** Why is the fifteenth century a regional-state system and not a vacuum?
**Definition (learn):** Bengal, Gujarat, Malwa, Jaunpur, Mewar, Kashmir and the Deccan polities formed a balance of power after Delhi's collapse; Malwa became a cockpit of north Indian supremacy. (✅ owner 06 adv §1 and §2; ✅ owner 06 §12)
**Breakdown:**
- Bengal: independent eastern sultanate (✅ owner 06 §12).
- Gujarat: wealthy western sultanate and commercial power (✅ owner 06 §12).
- Malwa: strategic central Indian state (✅ owner 06 §12).
- Mewar: expanding Rajput power (✅ owner 06 §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Fifteenth century (✅).
**Links:** MED-1142, MED-1152, MED-1160.
**Trap:** Do not call the fifteenth century a blank between Sultanate and Mughal (✅ owner 06 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Assess it as a balance-of-power system, not a dark age (✅ owner 06 adv §7).
**Back:** *"Five kingdoms, one balance: Malwa the cockpit."*

### Flashcard MED-1165 — PYQ routing and traps for Owner 06
**Front:** Which PYQs connect to Owner 06, and what are its traps?
**Definition (learn):** No verified PYQ routes directly to Topic 06; four retained applications: 2021 Q38 (Topic 05), 2022 Q57 (Topic 04), 2020 GS-I Q12 (Topic 24), 2023 GS-I Q12 (Topic 07). (✅ owner 06 §19)
**Breakdown:**
- Timur = 1398, after Muhammad and Firuz; not founder of the Sayyid dynasty (✅ owner 06 §19).
- Khizr Khan begins Sayyids; Bahlul begins Lodis (✅ owner 06 §19).
- Mubarak Shah, Muhammad Shah and Alam Shah are Sayyids (✅ owner 06 §19).
- Sikandar founded or developed Agra and used gaz-i-Sikandari (✅ owner 06 §19).
- Ibrahim, not Sikandar, faced Babur (✅ owner 06 §19).
- Yahya Sirhindi wrote *Tarikh-i Mubarak Shahi* (✅ owner 06 §19; ✅ Wikipedia: Sayyid dynasty for Sirhindi).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Route only (✅).
**Links:** MED-1144, MED-1156, MED-1161.
**Trap:** Lodi tombs and coinage must match dynasty and site evidence (✅ owner 06 §19).
**Mains hook:** Route each demand by owner.
**Back:** *"Four routes, no direct hit; Ibrahim fought Babur."*

### Flashcard MED-1166 — Mains architecture for Owner 06
**Front:** What are the 10-, 15- and 20-mark skeletons for decline questions?
**Definition (learn):** Structures from owner 06 §20.
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks (Timur as catalyst): pre-existing fragmentation → route and motives → Delhi sack → human, economic and symbolic effects → regional continuity → catalyst verdict (✅ owner 06 §20).
- 15 marks (Sayyid-Lodi transition): restricted Sayyid sovereignty → regional balance → Bahlul and Jaunpur → Sikandar and Agra → Ibrahim's crown-chief conflict → contingent Panipat (✅ owner 06 §20).
- 20 marks: post-Firuz succession; fiscal-military limits; provincial state formation; Timur shock; Sayyid recovery; Lodi bargains; Babur's battlefield system; continuities after 1526; non-teleological verdict (✅ owner 06 §20).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** MED-1142, MED-1146, MED-1161.
**Trap:** Do not claim the Lodi revival was seamless (✅ owner 06 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Process, not event; catalyst not cause (✅ owner 06 §7).
**Back:** *"Process, catalyst, balance, bargain, battle, continuity."*


### GROUP — Administration, Economy & Society under the Sultanate

### Flashcard MED-1167 — Sultanate administration: centralised at the apex, negotiated below
**Front:** How should the Sultanate's administration be characterised in one line?
**Definition (learn):** A powerful, centralised state at the top (sultan, wazir, diwan-i-arz), dependent below on local intermediaries, village structures and negotiation; a negotiated sovereignty, not absolute control. (✅ owner 07 §1 and §8.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Apex: sultan with supreme political, military and legal authority (✅ owner 07 §1).
- Middle: iqta assignments, muqtis and walis (✅ owner 07 §13).
- Base: pargana under amil; village khuts, muqaddams and patwaris (✅ owner 07 §2).
- Continuity: local machinery remained largely unchanged (✅ owner 07 §8.5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1206 onward (✅).
**Links:** MED-1168, MED-1176, MED-1177, MED-1085.
**Trap:** "Centralised" is relative; village governance was not replaced (⚠️ owner 07 §8.5).
**Mains hook:** Use the apex-base split as the thesis for any Sultanate administration answer.
**Back:** *"Apex strong, base negotiated."*

### Flashcard MED-1168 — The sultan's authority and the absence of settled succession
**Front:** What powers did the sultan hold, and how was succession decided?
**Definition (learn):** The sultan held supreme political, military and legal authority, acted as commander-in-chief and could hear appeals against officials. Succession had no settled rule: all sons had claims, nomination needed noble acceptance, and force often decided. (✅ owner 07 §1 and §11)
**Breakdown:**
- Commander-in-chief role: retained even when the ariz managed the army (✅ owner 07 §5).
- Appeals: sultan could hear appeals against officials (✅ owner 07 §1).
- Succession drivers: nomination, elite support, household networks and force (✅ owner 07 §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period (✅).
**Links:** MED-1066, MED-1170, MED-1167.
**Trap:** The sultan did not delegate supreme command to the ariz (✅ owner 07 §5).
**Mains hook:** Monarchy without primogeniture produced recurrent succession crises (✅ owner 06 §17).
**Back:** *"Sultan commands and judges; succession is won, not inherited."*

### Flashcard MED-1169 — Wazir: the revenue and expenditure department
**Front:** What did the wazir and the Diwan-i-Wizarat handle, and what is the trap?
**Definition (learn):** The wazir headed finance: revenue and income-expenditure. (✅ owner 07 §2 and §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Diwan-i-Wizarat / wazir = finance (✅ owner 07 §12).
- Authority changed with the ruler and the office-holder (✅ owner 07 §12).
- Owner trap: Mir Bakshi is a Mughal office, not Khalji (✅ owner 07 §26.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period (✅).
**Links:** MED-1170, MED-1167.
**Trap:** Wazir (finance) is not the ariz-i-mamalik (military) (✅ owner 07 §26.2).
**Mains hook:** Finance and military separated in the Sultanate, but not into a permanent bureaucracy.
**Back:** *"Wazir: revenue ledger; ariz: army ledger."*

### Flashcard MED-1170 — Diwan-i-arz / ariz-i-mamalik: the military department
**Front:** What did the diwan-i-arz (ariz-i-mamalik) do, and how did it link to dagh and chehra?
**Definition (learn):** The military department handled recruitment, equipment, pay and inspection of the army; the sultan remained commander-in-chief; dagh and chehra intensified under Alauddin. (✅ owner 07 §2, §4 and §12)
**Breakdown:**
- Recruitment, equipment and pay (✅ owner 07 §2).
- Dagh (horse branding) and chehra (descriptive roll) for inspection (✅ owner 07 §12; MED-1101).
- Ariz is not the commander-in-chief (✅ owner 07 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Alauddin's reign for dagh and chehra (✅ owner 07 §4).
**Links:** MED-1101, MED-1168, MED-1197.
**Trap:** Wazir = finance; ariz-i-mamalik/diwan-i-arz = military (✅ owner 07 §26.2).
**Mains hook:** Military administration as the Sultanate's fiscal-military base.
**Back:** *"Ariz pays and inspects the army; the sultan leads it."*

### Flashcard MED-1171 — Diwan-i-insha: royal correspondence
**Front:** What was the diwan-i-insha?
**Definition (learn):** The department for official and diplomatic correspondence and chancery work. (✅ owner 07 §1, §2 and §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Securely handled royal correspondence (✅ owner 07 §1).
- Documentary survival is uneven (✅ owner 07 §12).
- Persian as the main chancery language (✅ owner 07 §24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period (✅).
**Links:** MED-1194, MED-1167.
**Trap:** Diwan-i-insha = correspondence (✅ owner 07 §26.2).
**Mains hook:** Persian chancery as a vehicle of Persianate administration.
**Back:** *"Insha writes the sultan's letters."*

### Flashcard MED-1172 — Diwan-i-risalat: a debated portfolio
**Front:** What did the diwan-i-risalat do, and why is it debated?
**Definition (learn):** Satish Chandra links it to religious matters and pious grants; other reconstructions assign diplomatic functions. The owner says to state which reconstruction is followed. (✅ owner 07 §2, §12 and adv §2)
**Breakdown:**
- Chandra's reconstruction: religious matters and grants (✅ owner 07 §1).
- Other scholars: diplomatic function (✅ owner 07 adv §2).
- Owner's caution: do not present a debated portfolio as settled (✅ owner 07 adv §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period.
**Links:** MED-1173, MED-1167.
**Trap:** Do not treat the diwan-i-risalat as a fixed department with a single function (✅ owner 07 §26.2).
**Mains hook:** Source-aware institutional description.
**Back:** *"Risalat: religion for Chandra, diplomacy for others."*

### Flashcard MED-1173 — Sadr and qazi: religious and judicial offices
**Front:** What did the sadr and the qazi do, and how did local justice persist?
**Definition (learn):** The sadr handled religious grants and learned appointments in many contexts; the qazi held the Islamic judicial office for Muslims in civil justice; qazi jurisdiction did not erase local or customary forums. (✅ owner 07 §2, §12 and §13)
**Breakdown:**
- Sadr: portfolio overlap varied (✅ owner 07 §12).
- Qazi: civil justice for Muslims (✅ owner 07 §2).
- Justice operated at sultan, qazi and local levels (✅ owner 03 §17.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period.
**Links:** MED-1172, MED-1193.
**Trap:** Normative law is not complete practice (✅ owner 03 §17.2).
**Mains hook:** Law as ideal and practice as plural.
**Back:** *"Sadr for grants; qazi for Muslim justice; custom went on."*

### Flashcard MED-1174 — Barids: intelligence and communications
**Front:** What were the barids, and how did they operate?
**Definition (learn):** Intelligence agents and messengers who reported on nobles, officers and provinces, reaching as far as roads, officers and the ruler allowed. Wikipedia says Balban's network was modelled on the Umayyad barid, with secret reporters in every department. (✅ owner 07 §1 and §12; ✅ Wikipedia: Balban)
**Breakdown:**
- Barids were intelligence agents (✅ owner 07 §4).
- Reach depended on roads, officers and ruler (✅ owner 07 §12).
- Most visible under Balban but not invented from nothing (✅ owner 03 §17.2).
- Used by Alauddin for munhiyans (MED-1097).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Balban (1266–87) as best-known example (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-1069, MED-1097.
**Trap:** Barids are not the same as the Mughal waqai-navis (⚠️ standard knowledge; not in owner).
**Mains hook:** Information as the currency of state capacity.
**Back:** *"Barids: the sultan's eyes and ears."*

### Flashcard MED-1175 — Iqta vs jagir: the comparison bank
**Front:** Compare the Sultanate iqta and the Mughal jagir on tenure, method, obligation and accountability.
**Definition (learn):** Both were transferable revenue assignments, but the Mughal system was more codified: jagir valuation linked to jama, zat/sawar obligations, and annual review. (✅ owner 07 §8.4)
**Breakdown:**
- Tenure: transferable in both; Sultanate tenure depended on sultanic strength (✅ owner 07 §8.4).
- Method: Sultanate lump assignment; Mughal valuation tied to measured jama (✅ owner 07 §8.4).
- Obligation: muqti maintained troops broadly; mansab fixed zat/sawar (✅ owner 07 §8.4).
- Accountability: Sultanate audit depended on energetic centre; Mughal review systematic (✅ owner 07 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Table (✅).
**Links:** MED-1076, MED-1101, MED-1205.
**Trap:** Do not flatten Sultanate and Mughal systems into one (✅ owner 07 §8.5).
**Mains hook:** Best comparative axis for 15-mark answers: obligation and accountability (✅ owner 07 §8.4).
**Back:** *"Iqta lent revenue loosely; jagir was counted."*

### Flashcard MED-1176 — Local administration ladder: wali, shiq, pargana, amil, village
**Front:** Give the provincial and local administrative ladder of the Sultanate.
**Definition (learn):** Wali/muqti at the provincial and iqta level; shiq/shiqdar as territorial-policing unit; pargana grouping headed by the amil for revenue; village as cultivation and local assessment unit. (✅ owner 07 §2 and §13)
**Breakdown:**
- Wali/muqti: provincial and military-fiscal authority (✅ owner 07 §13).
- Shiq/shiqdar: later Sultanate usage, not equally visible in all reigns (✅ owner 07 §13).
- Pargana/amil: revenue collection; form clearer over time (✅ owner 07 §13).
- Village: not autonomous from state or local elites (✅ owner 07 §13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period.
**Links:** MED-1075, MED-1177, MED-1167.
**Trap:** Amil = revenue collection; not a Mughal mansab (✅ owner 07 §26.2).
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder to show how central claims were superimposed on local institutions (✅ owner 07 §13).
**Back:** *"Muqti, shiq, amil, village: each a rung down."*

### Flashcard MED-1177 — Rural intermediaries: khut, muqaddam, chaudhuri and patwari
**Front:** Who were the rural intermediaries, and how did Alauddin treat them?
**Definition (learn):** Khuts, muqaddams and chaudhuris were rural headmen and revenue mediators who could collect, shift burdens and hold privileges; patwaris and local scribes kept records in many later contexts. (✅ owner 07 §13 and §17; ✅ owner 04 §13.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Alauddin curtailed their privileges and burden-shifting, not all rural elites (✅ owner 04 adv §5).
- Labels, privileges and reach varied by region (✅ owner 07 §13).
- Evidence on patwaris is uneven (✅ owner 07 §13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Alauddin's Doab measures (✅).
**Links:** MED-1099, MED-1176.
**Trap:** Do not assume Alauddin abolished intermediaries (✅ owner 04 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Intermediaries as the hinge between state claim and peasant burden.
**Back:** *"Khut, muqaddam, chaudhuri: the middle of the village."*

### Flashcard MED-1178 — Zamindar in the early fourteenth century
**Front:** When does the term zamindar first appear, and what did it mean?
**Definition (learn):** The term begins to appear by the early fourteenth century for hereditary intermediaries, and later widens under the Mughals. (✅ owner 07 adv §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Hereditary intermediary, not a full landowner (⚠️ owner 07 adv §1 is compressed).
- Widening under the Mughals (✅ owner 07 adv §1).
- Compare zamindari in colonial revenue (⚠️ standard knowledge; not in owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Early 14th century (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MED-1177, MED-1076.
**Trap:** Zamindar is not the same as iqta-holder (✅ owner 07 §14).
**Mains hook:** Intermediary term changes over time.
**Back:** *"Zamindar: an early 14th-century hereditary middleman."*

### Flashcard MED-1179 — Revenue categories: kharaj, ushr, zakat, jizya, khams and cesses
**Front:** Distinguish kharaj, ushr, zakat, jizya, khams and cesses, and state the practice caution.
**Definition (learn):** Normative categories of Islamic law applied unevenly in India: kharaj (land revenue), ushr (tithe-like levy), zakat (alms on Muslim wealth), jizya (poll tax on non-Muslims), khams (state fifth of specified booty), cesses (house, grazing, market and irrigation). (✅ owner 07 §16)
**Breakdown:**
- Kharaj: practice varied by reign, crop and locality (✅ owner 07 §16).
- Ushr: legal category does not map onto all Indian land (✅ owner 07 §16).
- Jizya: rates and exemptions varied (✅ owner 07 §16).
- Cesses: rulers abolished, revived or renamed selectively (✅ owner 07 §16).
- Alauddin's half-produce demand is a core-policy setting, not a norm (✅ owner 07 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Table (✅).
**Links:** MED-1099, MED-1132, MED-1076.
**Trap:** Kharaj, ushr, zakat, jizya and khams are not interchangeable (✅ owner 07 §26.2).
**Mains hook:** No universal tax proportion covered every Sultanate region (✅ owner 07 §16).
**Back:** *"Kharaj land, ushr tithe, zakat alms, jizya poll, khams fifth, cesses extra."*

### Flashcard MED-1180 — Fanam: a southern coin, not a Delhi revenue term
**Front:** What is the fanam, and why is it on the Sultanate PYQ list?
**Definition (learn):** A small gold coin of south Indian and coastal circulation, especially in Vijayanagara–Malabar trade; not a standard Delhi Sultanate revenue or tanka-jital term. (✅ owner 07 §4 and §26.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Routed as 2022 Prelims Q52 to Topic 07; exact local wording unavailable (✅ owner 07 §26.1).
- Tanka and jital are the Sultanate anchors (✅ owner 07 §26.2).
- Fanam is mainly southern and coastal (✅ owner 07 §26.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Coastal and southern (✅).
**Links:** MED-1061, MED-1181, MED-1204.
**Trap:** Do not assign fanam to Delhi revenue (✅ owner 07 §4).
**Mains hook:** Monetary diversity across regions.
**Back:** *"Fanam: southern gold, not Delhi's tanka."*

### Flashcard MED-1181 — Hundis, Multanis, Sahs and brokers: credit and money transfer
**Front:** What financial instruments and merchants financed trade and nobles?
**Definition (learn):** Hundis (bills of exchange) financed long-distance trade and money transfer; Multanis and Sahs financed nobles; brokers (dallals) and credit networks supported merchant finance. (✅ owner 07 adv §1, §4 and §19)
**Breakdown:**
- Merchant groups: Gujaratis, Marwaris, Jains, Bohras, Multanis, Khurasanis (✅ owner 07 §1).
- Credit: hundis, sahs, brokers and dallals (✅ owner 07 §19).
- Money: tanka, jital, regional coins, bullion (✅ owner 07 §19).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period.
**Links:** MED-1061, MED-1183, MED-1196.
**Trap:** Merchants operated semi-independently of the state (✅ owner 07 §8.2).
**Mains hook:** Credit as a bridge between elite consumption and trade.
**Back:** *"Hundi moves money; Multani lends to lords."*

### Flashcard MED-1182 — Ibn Battuta on Delhi and Daulatabad: city size claims
**Front:** What did Ibn Battuta say about the size of Delhi and Daulatabad?
**Definition (learn):** He called Delhi the largest city in the eastern Islamic world and said Daulatabad equalled it in size. (✅ owner 07 §1 and §8.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Urbanisation real and documented by a contemporary (✅ owner 07 §8.2).
- Owner caution: he is sometimes unreliable on numbers and distances (✅ owner 07 §8.2).
- Ibn Battuta also visited Delhi in 1334 (✅ Wikipedia: Muhammad bin Tughluq).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Delhi and Daulatabad (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MED-1122, MED-1183.
**Trap:** Treat "equalled Delhi" as a claim, not a census (✅ owner 07 §8.2).
**Mains hook:** Urban growth corroborated by a traveller with caution.
**Back:** *"Ibn Battuta: Delhi the greatest, Daulatabad its equal."*

### Flashcard MED-1183 — Sultanate towns: a ladder of centres
**Front:** Which towns were important in the Sultanate, and why did they grow?
**Definition (learn):** Delhi, Daulatabad, Lahore, Multan, Uch, Kara, Lakhnauti, Anhilwara/Patan, Cambay and Khambayat (Khambayat/Cambay) were the main centres, growing from garrisons, courts, trade and crafts. (✅ owner 07 §1 and adv §2)
**Breakdown:**
- Garrison and court demand (✅ owner 07 §5 and §19).
- Ports: Gujarat/Cambay, Bengal/Sonargaon (✅ owner 07 §19).
- Urbanisation was regional, not universal (✅ owner 07 §19).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Town list (✅ owner 07).
**Links:** MED-1182, MED-1184, MED-1196.
**Trap:** Sultanate towns were not only military camps (✅ owner 07 §5).
**Mains hook:** Urbanisation driven by garrisons, currency, crafts and trade, with limits.
**Back:** *"Delhi, Lahore, Multan, Cambay: garrison, court, trade."*

### Flashcard MED-1184 — Textiles and ports: India's manufacturing strength
**Front:** Which textiles and ports made India prominent in Sultanate-era trade?
**Definition (learn):** Cambay and Sonargaon were famous for textiles; Bengal for raw silk and fine cotton; Gujarat for textiles and gold and silver work; patolas, zardozi and muslin were prominent; Indian textiles reached the Red Sea, Persian Gulf and China. (✅ owner 07 §1, §4 and adv §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Coarse cloth, calico, muslin, zardozi, painted and printed cloth, Gujarat patolas, Bengal fine cotton and silk (✅ owner 07 adv §1).
- Village industries: oil-pressing, jaggery, weaving and dyeing (✅ owner 07 §1).
- Crops: rice, sugarcane, wheat, oilseeds, sesame, indigo and cotton (✅ owner 07 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Table (✅).
**Links:** MED-1183, MED-1185, MED-1181.
**Trap:** Do not present Indian textiles as a colonial creation (⚠️ owner 07 adv Concept).
**Mains hook:** India as a manufacturing hub before European dominance (⚠️ owner 07 adv Concept).
**Back:** *"Cambay cloth, Bengal silk, Gujarat patola: the world's clothes."*

### Flashcard MED-1185 — Spinning wheel, cotton-carder's bow and paper
**Front:** Which production technologies diffused in the Sultanate period, and what do they do?
**Definition (learn):** The spinning wheel and cotton-carder's bow raised yarn preparation and spinning efficiency; paper-making became better known and more widely used under the Turks. (✅ owner 07 §8.3 and §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Spinning wheel: raised spinning efficiency; drew women into village production (✅ owner 07 §8.3).
- Cotton-carder's bow: sped cleaning of cotton (✅ owner 07 adv §4).
- Paper: cheaper record-keeping and manuscript culture (✅ owner 07 §8.3).
- Dating caution: no single foreign inventor or arrival date (⚠️ owner 07 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period (✅).
**Links:** MED-1186, MED-1187, MED-1204.
**Trap:** Do not assign each technology one foreign inventor or arrival date (✅ owner 07 §4).
**Mains hook:** Technology diffusion as necessary but insufficient (✅ owner 07 §8.3).
**Back:** *"Wheel spins, bow cleans, paper records."*

### Flashcard MED-1186 — Iron stirrup, looms and the Persian wheel: diffusion with limits
**Front:** What did the iron stirrup, improved looms and the Persian wheel contribute, and what limits apply?
**Definition (learn):** The iron stirrup improved cavalry balance and shock; improved looms supported finer cloth; the Persian wheel and allied water-lifting devices widened irrigation where attested. (✅ owner 07 §8.3; ⚠️ attribution and dating)
**Breakdown:**
- Stirrup advantage depended on horse supply, training and command (✅ owner 07 §8.3).
- Looms benefited merchants and state-linked towns (✅ owner 07 §8.3).
- Persian wheel adoption regionally uneven (✅ owner 07 §8.3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period (✅).
**Links:** MED-1185, MED-1197.
**Trap:** The stirrup alone did not win battles (✅ owner 07 §15).
**Mains hook:** Military technology as necessary, not sufficient.
**Back:** *"Stirrup, loom, wheel: tools, not destiny."*

### Flashcard MED-1187 — Lime mortar and the true arch: construction technology
**Front:** What did lime mortar and the true arch enable, and what are their limits?
**Definition (learn):** Lime mortar and true-arch vaulting enabled larger domes, stronger vaulting and durable construction, expanding monumental architecture and some hydraulic works. (✅ owner 07 §8.3; ✅ Wikipedia: Qutb Minar for construction phases)
**Breakdown:**
- Monumental display and city-building (✅ owner 07 §8.3).
- Did not automatically improve rural welfare (✅ owner 07 §8.3).
- Balban's tomb and Alai Darwaza as evidence (MED-1079, MED-1109).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period (✅).
**Links:** MED-1079, MED-1109, MED-1185.
**Trap:** "First arch" claims need qualification (✅ owner 04 adv §6).
**Mains hook:** Technology served imperial display (✅ owner 07 §8.3).
**Back:** *"Mortar and arch: domes for the city, not the village."*

### Flashcard MED-1188 — Social stratification: nobles, merchants, peasants and artisans
**Front:** How was Sultanate society stratified?
**Definition (learn):** Society was sharply stratified: nobles and wealthy merchants accumulated wealth and lived ostentatiously, while peasants, artisans, labourers, domestic servants and slaves lived in insecurity and low wages. (✅ owner 07 §1 and adv §1)
**Breakdown:**
- Political and military elites: mamluks, freeborn Turks, Khaljis, Afghans, Tajiks, Indian Muslims, local chiefs (✅ owner 07 §20).
- Peasants and intermediaries: rights, caste and locality varied (✅ owner 07 §20).
- Mobility existed but hierarchy and exclusion persisted (✅ owner 07 §20).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Table (✅).
**Links:** MED-1189, MED-1190, MED-1201.
**Trap:** Society cannot be divided into sealed Hindu and Muslim blocs (✅ owner 07 §20).
**Mains hook:** Elite consumption coexisted with peasant and artisan hardship (✅ owner 07 §7).
**Back:** *"Nobles ostentatious, peasants insecure: stratified, not sealed."*

### Flashcard MED-1189 — Slavery and bandagan: forms and limits
**Front:** What forms of slavery existed under the Sultanate, and what must not be assumed?
**Definition (learn):** War captives, purchased domestic servants, military mamluks, royal bandagan, karkhana labour and other dependent forms. Legal status, training and mobility varied greatly. (✅ owner 07 §21)
**Breakdown:**
- Elite military slavery could enable extraordinary rise (✅ owner 07 §21).
- Mass captives and karkhana slaves experienced coercion (✅ owner 07 §21).
- Slavery could depress free labour and create factions (✅ owner 07 §21).
- Firuz's 1,80,000 is a chronicler claim (✅ owner 07 §21; see MED-1129).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period.
**Links:** MED-1129, MED-1200, MED-1168.
**Trap:** No form equals modern Atlantic racial chattel slavery (✅ owner 07 §21).
**Mains hook:** Slavery as a ladder and a constraint at once.
**Back:** *"Mamluk rises, captive labours, none is Atlantic."*

### Flashcard MED-1190 — Gender, household and property
**Front:** How should women's power and constraint be read in the Sultanate?
**Definition (learn):** Elite women could influence succession and household politics; Razia is a case, not proof of general equality. Common women worked in agriculture, spinning, food, service, crafts and markets, under-recorded. Purdah spread among some elites only. (✅ owner 07 §22)
**Breakdown:**
- Marriage, inheritance and dower followed multiple legal and customary systems (✅ owner 07 §22).
- Enslaved women need separate analysis (✅ owner 07 §22).
- Archive privileges male courts and normative texts (✅ owner 07 §22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period.
**Links:** MED-1063, MED-1064, MED-1189.
**Trap:** Razia does not prove general female equality (✅ owner 07 §22).
**Mains hook:** Source-qualified gender analysis.
**Back:** *"Elite women influenced; common women laboured; sources were male."*

### Flashcard MED-1191 — Fatwa-i-Jahandari: Barani's political theory
**Front:** What is the Fatwa-i-Jahandari, and how should it be read?
**Definition (learn):** Barani's political-theory and advice text, from the Tughlaq period, idealising statecraft, the ruler's duty and social hierarchy. (✅ owner 07 §9)
**Breakdown:**
- Reveals elite political ideology (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Prescriptive, not descriptive (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Reflects the author's frustration and bias (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Tughlaq period (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Links:** MED-1103, MED-1192.
**Trap:** Normative political texts are not administrative practice (✅ owner 07 §10.1).
**Mains hook:** Ideal kingship versus practice.
**Back:** *"Fatwa: how a king should act, not how he did."*

### Flashcard MED-1192 — Persian sources: genre distinctions
**Front:** Why must the genre of a Persian source be identified?
**Definition (learn):** Official histories (Akbarnama), private critiques (Badauni), memoirs (Baburnama, Tuzuk) and Sufi malfuzat differ in purpose and reliability; their distinctions must be made before using them. (✅ owner 07 §9)
**Breakdown:**
- Court and elite bias: most chronicles from literate courtly elites (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Patronage dependency: praise and suppression of failure (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Cross-verification: Abul Fazl vs Badauni; Barani vs administrative outcomes (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Framework (✅).
**Links:** MED-1202, MED-1214.
**Trap:** Official history ≠ critique ≠ memoir ≠ malfuzat (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Mains hook:** Source-matrix paragraph for a 20-mark answer.
**Back:** *"Court, critic, memoir, saint: four genres, four biases."*

### Flashcard MED-1193 — Religious legitimacy: sharia, ulama, zawabit and Sufis
**Front:** How did religion legitimate the Sultanate, and what limits applied?
**Definition (learn):** Sharia provided legal norms interpreted by ulama; zawabit (state regulations) served political necessity and sultanic discretion; Sufis and ulama formed no single establishment. (✅ owner 07 §11 and §23)
**Breakdown:**
- Ulama were neither sole policy-makers nor irrelevant (✅ owner 04 adv §12).
- Caliphal investiture as legitimacy, not practical sovereignty (✅ owner 07 §11).
- Religious policy varied by ruler, war, locality and purpose (✅ owner 07 §23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period.
**Links:** MED-1080, MED-1060, MED-1201.
**Trap:** Neither uniform forced conversion nor conflict-free composite society (✅ owner 07 §23).
**Mains hook:** Negotiated religious legitimacy.
**Back:** *"Sharia the ideal, zawabit the practice, ulama the voice."*

### Flashcard MED-1194 — Language and intellectual culture: Persian, Arabic, Sanskrit, Hindavi
**Front:** Which languages carried the Sultanate's culture, and what is Hindavi?
**Definition (learn):** Persian became the principal court, chancery and literary language; Arabic remained important for theology and law; Sanskrit and regional languages continued; Hindavi developed through urban, military, devotional and everyday contact. (✅ owner 07 §24)
**Breakdown:**
- Translation, bilingual administration, Sufi literature (✅ owner 07 §24).
- Persianate means a courtly-administrative culture, not Persian ethnicity (✅ owner 07 §24).
- Amir Khusrau and earliest Hindavi use (⚠️ owner 07 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period.
**Links:** MED-1171, MED-1012, MED-1104.
**Trap:** Persianate ≠ Persian ethnicity (✅ owner 07 §24).
**Mains hook:** Language as a bridge and a boundary.
**Back:** *"Persian ruled, Arabic judged, Sanskrit remembered, Hindavi spoke."*

### Flashcard MED-1195 — Sovereignty claims: coin, khutba, titles, investiture and justice
**Front:** By what means did the Sultans claim sovereignty?
**Definition (learn):** Conquest, coin, khutba, titles, court, justice, appointments and sometimes caliphal symbolism. None alone made a modern territorially uniform state. (✅ owner 07 §11 and owner 03 §21)
**Breakdown:**
- Coin: sovereignty and monetisation, not a border (✅ owner 07 §10.1).
- Khutba: public recitation of the ruler's name (✅ owner 07 §11).
- Caliphal investiture: legitimacy, not dependence (✅ owner 07 §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period.
**Links:** MED-1060, MED-1061, MED-1085.
**Trap:** Coins are not a census or border (✅ owner 07 §10.1).
**Mains hook:** Multiple markers of sovereignty; none sufficient alone.
**Back:** *"Coin, khutba, title, investiture, justice: none enough alone."*

### Flashcard MED-1196 — Monetisation and urbanisation: the qualified urban revolution
**Front:** How far did the Sultanate monetise and urbanise, and what is the qualification?
**Definition (learn):** Cash revenue, silver tanka, salaries, merchant finance and trade tied countryside and towns; the "urban revolution" thesis highlights garrisons, surplus, currency and craft demand, but is regional and qualified. (✅ owner 07 adv §1 and §19)
**Breakdown:**
- Tanka and jital aided exchange but did not produce a uniform cash economy (✅ owner 07 §19).
- Towns depended on agrarian hinterlands (✅ owner 07 §19).
- Alauddin's market regulation as a high-intensity episode, not the norm (✅ owner 07 §19).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period.
**Links:** MED-1183, MED-1181, MED-1135.
**Trap:** Monetisation was uneven and village economy partly barter-based (✅ owner 07 §8.2).
**Mains hook:** Growth and inequality advanced together (✅ owner 07 adv §5).
**Back:** *"Money grew in towns; the village kept barter."*

### Flashcard MED-1197 — Sultanate armies: composition and limits
**Front:** Who made up the Sultanate army, and what really determined its effectiveness?
**Definition (learn):** Mamluk and household forces, free cavalry, iqta contingents, infantry, elephants and auxiliaries; effectiveness depended on remounts, fodder, pay, roads, training and command, not stirrups alone. (✅ owner 07 §15)
**Breakdown:**
- Horses came through domestic breeding and imports (✅ owner 07 §15).
- Forts, garrisons and barid intelligence against Mongols and rebels (✅ owner 07 §15).
- Firearms appeared gradually; Panipat's field system should not be projected back (✅ owner 07 §15).
- Military slavery was one recruitment form, not the whole army (✅ owner 07 §15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period.
**Links:** MED-1170, MED-1186, MED-1161.
**Trap:** Stirrups and firearms do not explain outcomes alone (✅ owner 07 §15).
**Mains hook:** Command, logistics and recruitment as military base.
**Back:** *"Mamluks, free horse, iqta, elephants: command decides."*

### Flashcard MED-1198 — Structural continuity without teleology
**Front:** What did Sultanate institutions pass on, and why must continuity not be written as teleology?
**Definition (learn):** Sultanate institutions influenced regional states and Mughal practice (revenue assignments, offices, chancery, coins, roads, forts, markets, intermediaries), but later rulers adapted rather than inherited a fixed system; the Sultanate did not exist to prepare the Mughals. (✅ owner 07 §25)
**Breakdown:**
- Transmission: revenue assignment, offices, Persian chancery, coins, roads (✅ owner 07 §25).
- Adaptation, not identical transmission (✅ owner 07 §25).
- Compare dagh/chehra with Akbar's mansab framework (✅ owner 07 §8.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1206–1526 with 1526 as the end.
**Links:** MED-1074, MED-1162, MED-1175.
**Trap:** Later similarity does not prove direct unchanged transmission (✅ owner 07 §25).
**Mains hook:** Continuity and change without Mughal teleology.
**Back:** *"Institutions carried forward, not carried whole."*

### Flashcard MED-1199 — Dynasty-change matrix: Mamluk to Lodi
**Front:** What institutional tendency did each dynasty show?
**Definition (learn):** Mamluk: iqta and household elite, Delhi core, gradual departments; Khalji: tighter army, revenue, intelligence and market control; Muhammad Tughlaq: wider centralisation and experiments; Firuz Tughlaq: conciliation, heredity, canals and karkhanas; Sayyid: restricted tribute state; Lodi: Afghan bargaining and Agra. (✅ owner 07 §10.2)
**Breakdown:**
- Each row has a limit: uneven frontier, ruler-dependent core, communications, weaker audit, thin evidence, crown-chief tension (✅ owner 07 §10.2).
- Matches the iqta timeline in owner 07 §14 (✅ owner 07 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Matrix (✅).
**Links:** MED-1085, MED-1106, MED-1133, MED-1158.
**Trap:** Do not impose a single institutional template across all six phases (✅ owner 07 §10.2).
**Mains hook:** Use the matrix to structure a long answer on continuity and change.
**Back:** *"Six dynasties, six tendencies, six limits."*

### Flashcard MED-1200 — Karkhanas: royal workshops and storage
**Front:** What were karkhanas, and who worked in them?
**Definition (learn):** Royal workshops and storage for luxury, military and household goods, which employed artisans, servants and sometimes slaves. (✅ owner 07 §18; ✅ owner 05 adv §19.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Firuz's karkhanas and slave department (✅ owner 05 §18).
- Employed artisans, servants and slaves (✅ owner 07 §18).
- Luxury and military demand (✅ owner 07 §18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Firuz's reign (✅).
**Links:** MED-1129, MED-1189.
**Trap:** Karkhanas are not private manufactories (⚠️ owner 07 §18 implies royal ownership).
**Mains hook:** State production as a form of extraction and capacity.
**Back:** *"Karkhana: royal workshop, artisans and slaves."*

### Flashcard MED-1201 — Conversion and accommodation: no single model
**Front:** How should conversion and accommodation under the Sultanate be explained?
**Definition (learn):** Conversion could involve conviction, patronage, mobility, local networks, status or coercion; there was no single model; the sources do not support mass forced conversion as a universal explanation. (✅ owner 07 §23; ✅ owner 07 adv §5)
**Breakdown:**
- Hindu and Muslim merchants, officials, artisans and soldiers interacted (✅ owner 07 §23).
- Temple destruction, reuse and patronage varied by case (✅ owner 07 §23).
- Jizya and grants varied by ruler (✅ owner 07 §23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sultanate period.
**Links:** MED-1193, MED-1188.
**Trap:** "Conversion happened mainly by sword" is wrong (✅ owner 07 adv §5).
**Mains hook:** Plural, case-specific explanation of religious change.
**Back:** *"No single road to conversion."*

### Flashcard MED-1202 — Evidence hierarchy for the Sultanate
**Front:** Which evidence type serves which purpose for Sultanate economy and administration?
**Definition (learn):** Court chronicles (rulers, offices, conflicts), normative texts (ideals), farmans and inscriptions (dated acts), coins (titles, mints, circulation), archaeology (towns, forts, workshops), travellers (routes, products, customs), later reconstructions (synthesis). (✅ owner 07 §10.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Each has a secure use and a limit (✅ owner 07 §10.1).
- One Barani passage cannot produce a timeless system (✅ owner 07 §10.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Table.
**Links:** MED-1020, MED-1192, MED-1214.
**Trap:** Coin presence is not a full census (✅ owner 07 §10.1).
**Mains hook:** State the evidence base before stating a system.
**Back:** *"Chronicle, text, deed, coin, site, traveller, synthesis."*

### Flashcard MED-1203 — The industrial-hub concept: India before European dominance
**Front:** What does owner 07 argue about India's manufacturing before the Portuguese?
**Definition (learn):** Indian textiles and merchant networks were strong before Portuguese intrusion; India functioned as a manufacturing hub between West Asia and Southeast Asia. (⚠️ owner 07 adv Concept box, flagged as inference)
**Breakdown:**
- Textile exports to the Red Sea, Persian Gulf and China (✅ owner 07 §8.2).
- Merchant networks and credit (✅ owner 07 §19).
- Used to challenge colonial-era assumptions (⚠️ owner 07 adv Concept).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Pre-European (⚠️).
**Links:** MED-1184, MED-1181.
**Trap:** "Industrial" is an anachronistic label; use "manufacturing hub" with care (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Use to challenge colonial narratives, with evidence.
**Back:** *"Before the Portuguese, India wove the world."*

### Flashcard MED-1204 — PYQ routing and traps for Owner 07
**Front:** Which PYQs route to Owner 07, and what are the traps?
**Definition (learn):** Three direct routes: 2019 Prelims Q12 (revenue institutions), 2022 Prelims Q52 (fanam terminology), 2023 GS-I Q12 (Sultanate technology and society, 15 marks). Two adjacent: 2022 Q57 (Topic 04), 2020 GS-I Q12 (Topic 24). (✅ owner 07 §26.1 and adv PYQ ledger)
**Breakdown:**
- 2019 Q12 revenue institutions: iqta, muqti, amil and departments (✅ owner 07 §26).
- 2022 Q52 fanam: local wording unavailable (✅ owner 07 §26.1).
- 2023 GS-I Q12: 15 marks, 250 words; technology and society (✅ owner 07 adv PYQ).
- Official keys not held locally; no answers inferred (✅ owner 07 §26).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** MED-1180, MED-1205, MED-1206.
**Trap:** Amil = revenue collection; Mir Bakshi = Mughal (✅ owner 07 §26.2).
**Mains hook:** Route the 2023 technology question through the technology engine (✅ owner 07 §8.3).
**Back:** *"2019 revenue, 2022 fanam, 2023 technology: direct routes."*

### Flashcard MED-1205 — Trap list: what the Sultanate was not
**Front:** List the high-frequency misconceptions about Sultanate administration and economy.
**Definition (learn):** Iqta is not identical to the later jagir; the sultan did not delegate command to the ariz; Hindus were not excluded from administration; towns were not merely camps; villages were not fully autonomous; slavery was not harmless; conversion was not mainly by sword. (✅ owner 07 §5; ✅ owner 07 adv §5)
**Breakdown:**
- Iqta ≠ jagir in every respect (✅ owner 07 §5).
- Hindus served as managers, soldiers and officials (✅ owner 07 §5).
- Villages were not untouched from Alauddin onward (✅ owner 07 adv §5).
- Slavery depressed free labour status (✅ owner 07 adv §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Table.
**Links:** MED-1175, MED-1168, MED-1177, MED-1189, MED-1201.
**Trap:** Each misconception above is a close-option distractor in MCQs (⚠️ exam-pattern judgement).
**Mains hook:** Use the corrections as the opening paragraph of any administration answer.
**Back:** *"Not identical, not commanded away, not excluded, not camps."*

### Flashcard MED-1206 — Mains architecture for Owner 07 (iqta, technology, cross-dynastic system)
**Front:** What are the 10-, 15- and 20-mark skeletons for administration and society questions?
**Definition (learn):** Structures from owner 07 §27.
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks (iqta): definition → assignment flow → service, audit and transfer → dynasty change → regional enforcement limit → not-property verdict (✅ owner 07 §27).
- 15 marks (technology and society): source/dating caution → textile, paper, water, construction and military technologies → state, craft and trade mechanisms → urban and agrarian effects → gender, class and regional inequality → diffusion verdict (✅ owner 07 §27).
- 20 marks (cross-dynastic system): source and legal/practice distinction; sovereignty and departments; provincial institutions; iqta/revenue/army; agrarian extraction; craft, technology and credit; social composition, slavery and gender; religion and language; Mamluk-to-Lodi change; continuity without teleology (✅ owner 07 §27).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** MED-1175, MED-1186, MED-1204.
**Trap:** Do not use technology alone to explain economic change (✅ owner 07 §8.3).
**Mains hook:** Technology necessary but insufficient; benefits uneven.
**Back:** *"Iqta in ten, technology in fifteen, the system in twenty."*

### Flashcard MED-1207 — Baburnama: Babur's memoir (source card)
**Front:** What is the *Baburnama*, and what does it offer and withhold?
**Definition (learn):** Babur's first-person memoir, written in the early sixteenth century; a candid Central Asian and Indian account of campaigns, geography and personal observations. (✅ owner 07 §9)
**Breakdown:**
- Unique first-person account of the first Mughal campaigns (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Limited Indian depth; military and nature focus (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Cross-link: Topic 12 (✅ owner 07 §9 cross-links).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Early 16th century (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Links:** MED-1161 (Panipat), MED-1192.
**Trap:** Memoir is self-representation, not neutral audit (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Mains hook:** Babur's account as the basis for Panipat's field system, with caution (✅ owner 06 §16).
**Back:** *"Babur's own pen: candid, military, first-hand."*

### Flashcard MED-1208 — Akbarnama and Ain-i Akbari: official history and gazetteer
**Front:** What do the *Akbarnama* and *Ain-i Akbari* offer, and what is their bias?
**Definition (learn):** Abul Fazl's official history and administrative gazetteer of Akbar's reign (1590s), the most comprehensive Mughal administrative source with rich statistics; court-sponsored hagiography favouring Akbar's policies. (✅ owner 07 §9)
**Breakdown:**
- Administration, revenue, society and culture (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Statistical detail (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Cross-link: Topic 24 (✅ owner 07 §9 cross-links).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1590s (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Links:** MED-1209, MED-1192.
**Trap:** Abul Fazl's statistics still need cross-verification (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Mains hook:** Official gazetteer as a source for jama and mansab.
**Back:** *"Abul Fazl: Akbar's history and the gazetteer."*

### Flashcard MED-1209 — Muntakhab-ut-Tawarikh: Badauni's critical history
**Front:** What does Badauni's *Muntakhab-ut-Tawarikh* correct, and what is its bias?
**Definition (learn):** A private, critical history of Akbar's reign by Abdul Qadir Badauni, recording orthodox dissent against Akbar's religious experiments. (✅ owner 07 §9)
**Breakdown:**
- Corrective to Abul Fazl (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Bitter personal bias against Akbar's liberalism (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Cross-check genre: private critique vs official history (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Akbar's reign (✅).
**Links:** MED-1208, MED-1192.
**Trap:** Badauni is not a neutral alternative (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Mains hook:** Genre contrast between official and critical history.
**Back:** *"Badauni: the orthodox dissenter's diary."*

### Flashcard MED-1210 — Tuzuk-i Jahangiri: Jahangir's memoir
**Front:** What is the *Tuzuk-i Jahangiri* and what should be discounted?
**Definition (learn):** The first-person Mughal memoir of Jahangir (1605–27) on art, nature, justice and personal life. (✅ owner 07 §9)
**Breakdown:**
- Candid on taste and personality (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Limited administrative detail (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Self-serving (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1605–1627 (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Links:** MED-1207, MED-1192.
**Trap:** A memoir is not a neutral administrative audit (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Mains hook:** Use Tuzuk for art and personality, not for revenue figures.
**Back:** *"Jahangir's own pen: art, nature, self-regard."*

### Flashcard MED-1211 — Padshahnama: Shah Jahan's official history
**Front:** What does the *Padshahnama* cover, and what does it minimise?
**Definition (learn):** The official court history of Shah Jahan's reign (1628–58) by Abdul Hamid Lahori and others; key on campaigns, the Deccan, architecture and administration; court panegyric that minimises failures. (✅ owner 07 §9)
**Breakdown:**
- Key source for 1628–58 political and military history (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Panegyric and glorification (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1628–58 (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Links:** MED-1208, MED-1192.
**Trap:** Official history ≠ independent audit (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Mains hook:** Use with a critical source of the same period.
**Back:** *"Padshahnama: Shah Jahan's glorious record."*

### Flashcard MED-1212 — Humayun-nama: Gulbadan Begum's memoir
**Front:** What does Gulbadan Begum's *Humayun-nama* offer that other sources do not?
**Definition (learn):** A mid-sixteenth-century memoir of Humayun's life from the women's quarters; rare female voice; family dynamics and exile. (✅ owner 07 §9)
**Breakdown:**
- Domestic focus, limited political scope (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Incomplete text (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mid-16th century (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Links:** MED-1207, MED-1192.
**Trap:** Rare voice, narrow scope (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Mains hook:** Gendered sources in the Mughal period.
**Back:** *"Gulbadan: the women's quarters speak."*

### Flashcard MED-1213 — Malfuzat: Sufi conversations (Fawaid-ul-Fuad)
**Front:** What is the Malfuzat genre, and what is its value and bias?
**Definition (learn):** Records of Sufi discourses; *Fawaid-ul-Fuad* by Amir Hasan Sijzi records the conversations of Nizamuddin Auliya; the owner marks this row as ⚠️. (✅ owner 07 §9; ⚠️ owner marker)
**Breakdown:**
- Non-court perspective on daily life, ethics and Hindu-Muslim interaction (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Hagiographic intent; may idealise the saint (✅ owner 07 §9).
- 13th–14th century (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 13th–14th century (⚠️ owner marker).
**Links:** MED-1080, MED-1192.
**Trap:** Sufi malfuzat is not court annals (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Mains hook:** Use malfuzat for ordinary social worlds, with hagiography caution.
**Back:** *"Malfuzat: the saint talks, the disciple records."*

### Flashcard MED-1214 — Source analysis framework: bias, patronage and cross-verification
**Front:** What four checks should every Persian-source claim pass?
**Definition (learn):** Court and elite bias; patronage dependency; genre distinction; cross-verification. Also note what the sources cannot tell us: everyday peasant life, most women, oral traditions, lower artisans and much non-Muslim practice. (✅ owner 07 §9)
**Breakdown:**
- Court/elite bias: peasants, lower castes and most women are marginal (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Patronage: incentives to praise and suppress failure (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Genre: court history ≠ critique ≠ memoir ≠ malfuzat (✅ owner 07 §9).
- Cross-verify: compare Abul Fazl with Badauni; Barani with outcomes (✅ owner 07 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Framework (✅).
**Links:** MED-1192, MED-1202, MED-1020.
**Trap:** Single-source claims are not proof of system (✅ owner 07 §10.1).
**Mains hook:** Open any source-based answer with this framework.
**Back:** *"Bias, patron, genre, check."*

## MEDIEVAL INDIAN HISTORY — Part 2: Regional Kingdoms, Bhakti-Sufi, Architecture, Babur to Sher Shah

### GROUP — 08 Provincial & Regional Kingdoms (Bengal, Gujarat, Malwa, Jaunpur, Kashmir)

### Flashcard MED-2001 — Regional kingdoms as state formation, not "decline"
**Front:** Why are Bengal, Gujarat, Malwa, Jaunpur and Kashmir treated as regional states rather than footnotes to Delhi's decline?
**Definition (learn):** Regional kingdoms that consolidated after Delhi's contraction (c. 1338 onward), each with its own capital, revenue base, army, trade routes and cultural patronage.
**Breakdown:**
- Timur's invasion and Delhi's weakness let governors and local powers assert independence (✅ owner 08 §1).
- Balance of power: western triangle Gujarat–Malwa–Mewar; eastern check Bengal–Orissa–Jaunpur; Kashmir separate (✅ owner 08 §1; adv. 08 §1).
- Consolidation sequence: agrarian/port/route resources → capitals and forts → military-revenue networks → legitimacy through architecture and language → alliances with elites and merchants (✅ owner 08 §19).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mini-timeline 1338 (Bengal breaks away) → 1342 (Ilyas Shah) → 1407 (Gujarat) → 1420–70 (Zainul Abidin) → 1459–1511 (Mahmud Begarha) → 1484 (Jaunpur annexed by Bahlul Lodi) (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MED-2002 (Bengal 1338), MED-2008 (Gujarat 1407), MED-2016 (Jaunpur), MED-2018 (Kashmir), MED-2022 (balance of power), MED-2023 (state-capacity matrix).
**Trap:** Regional autonomy is not total independence. Delhi-era campaigns, tribute and gift exchanges continued (Bengal after Firuz Tughlaq). Equally, "decline of Delhi" is only one driver; the states also created their own prosperity.
**Mains hook:** "Regional kingdoms were laboratories of cultural synthesis." Qualified answer: they were creative and autonomous, but unequal in scale and vulnerable to absorption by Lodi and Mughal expansion.
**Back:** *"Delhi slipped, five states stood: land, route, fort, coin, patron."*

### Flashcard MED-2002 — Bengal breaks from Delhi (1338)
**Front:** When and under which Delhi ruler did Bengal again break away, and what was the political context?
**Definition (learn):** Bengal's renewed independent trajectory after the Delhi Sultanate's administrative control weakened during Muhammad bin Tughlaq's troubled reign.
**Breakdown:**
- Break from Delhi: 1338 (✅ owner 08 mini-timeline).
- Bengal's autonomy rested on distance, climate and waterways (✅ owner 08 §4; adv. 08 §1 adds difficult land/water communications and a climate unsuitable for many north-western troops).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1338 — Bengal breaks away (✅ owner 08). Firuz Tughlaq's later campaign fixed the Kosi as a boundary (see MED-2034).
**Links:** MED-2003 (Ilyas Shah 1342), MED-2006 (geography of autonomy), MED-2034 (Kosi boundary), MED-2026 (non-subordination trap).
**Trap:** Bengal "rebelled" and was not "crushed" in 1338; do not infer that Delhi kept direct administration after 1338 — the owner gives no basis for any claim about Delhi's direct control after 1338, so do not assert one (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Use Bengal to test the claim that geography determines autonomy: the owner says geography "favoured autonomy but did not determine it automatically" (✅ owner 08 §11.1).
**Back:** *"Thirteen thirty-eight, Bengal waits: the Delhi tether snaps."*

### Flashcard MED-2003 — Shamsuddin Ilyas Shah and Bengal's unification (1342)
**Front:** Who captured Lakhnauti and Sonargaon in 1342 and what did he unify?
**Definition (learn):** Ilyas Khan, who as Sultan Shamsuddin Ilyas Shah united Lakhnauti and Sonargaon zones into a wider Bengal sultanate.
**Breakdown:**
- 1342: captures Lakhnauti and Sonargaon (✅ owner 08 mini-timeline).
- Restored Ilyas Shahi phase later, giving continuity amid court and regional changes (✅ owner 08 §11.1).
- Ilyas Shah remains a Bengal dynastic anchor; Ilyas Shahi line gives the "riverine autonomy" model in the advanced owner (✅ adv. 08 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1342 (✅ owner 08). Note: the advanced owner calls the capturer "Ilyas Khan/Sultan Shamsuddin Ilyas Shah" (✅ adv. 08 mini-timeline).
**Links:** MED-2002, MED-2005 (Husain Shah of the Ilyas line), MED-2006.
**Trap:** Ilyas Shah (Bengal) is not the same as Ilyas Shah of other regions; and Ilyas Shahi ≠ Husain Shahi (a separate dynasty identity). Also: "Ilyas Shah/Husain Shah = Bengal; Sharqis = Jaunpur" (✅ owner 08 §20.2).
**Mains hook:** Use 1342 to show that Bengal's coherence came from state capacity over several river-linked centres, not from a single geographic inevitability.
**Back:** *"Forty-two, Ilyas binds Lakhnauti to Sonargaon: two ends, one sultan."*

### Flashcard MED-2004 — Ghiyasuddin Azam Shah (1389–1409)
**Front:** What is Ghiyasuddin Azam Shah of Bengal remembered for?
**Definition (learn):** Bengal sultan (1389–1409) remembered for justice traditions and diplomatic-literary contacts with Hafiz and China.
**Breakdown:**
- Justice tradition (✅ owner 08 §4).
- Contacts with the poet Hafiz and with China (✅ owner 08 mini-timeline; adv. 08 mini-timeline).
- Bengal's overseas trade and re-export of Chinese goods belong to the same trade orbit (MED-2033).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1389–1409 (✅ owner 08). Use "justice + diplomacy" as the two-word recall.
**Links:** MED-2003, MED-2005, MED-2033.
**Trap:** Do not confuse Ghiyasuddin Azam Shah (Bengal, 1389–1409) with the Delhi sultans named Ghiyasuddin (Balban, Tughlaq). The Bengal ruler's "China" link is diplomatic-trade in the owner, not a Mongol-era military episode.
**Mains hook:** Cite him as an example of regional kingship using justice, diplomacy and literature to project legitimacy beyond the Gangetic plain.
**Back:** *"Azam for Justice, Hafiz for poems, China for trade."*

### Flashcard MED-2005 — Alauddin Husain Shah (1494–1519) and Bengal's consolidation
**Front:** Who was Alauddin Husain Shah and what did he achieve in Bengal?
**Definition (learn):** Bengal sultan (1494–1519) who presided over the major consolidation, territorial expansion and Bengali/Persianate court culture of the sultanate.
**Breakdown:**
- Major consolidation and territorial expansion (✅ owner 08 §11.1).
- Hindu officials and literary patrons at court, including Rupa and Sanatan (✅ owner 08 §1).
- Patronage of Bengali language and literature (✅ owner 08 §1).
- Court inclusion is not social equality or absence of conflict (✅ owner 08 §11.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1494–1519 (✅ owner 08 §11.1). Some sources give 1494–1518 — ⚠️ the owner uses 1494–1519, flagged as the repository reading.
**Links:** MED-2003, MED-2007 (Gaur/Pandua architecture), MED-2004.
**Trap:** Husain Shah's Hindu officials show inclusion at court, not universal equality. Do not say "Bengali patronage meant Hindus were equal under the sultanate".
**Mains hook:** Husain Shah's court shows how "composite culture" can be evidenced in office and patronage without claiming harmony.
**Back:** *"Husain pays Rupa and Sanatan: office for Hindus, no equality claim."*

### Flashcard MED-2006 — Bengal's autonomy: distance, climate and waterways
**Front:** Which factors explain Bengal's autonomy from Delhi, and what is the owner's limit on them?
**Definition (learn):** Geography that made Bengal hard to control from Delhi: distance, climate and a water-based communication network.
**Breakdown:**
- Distance and difficult land communications (✅ owner 08 §4; adv. 08 §1).
- Water-based communications favoured autonomy (✅ owner 08 §1).
- Climate unsuitable for many north-western troops (✅ adv. 08 §1).
- Limit: geography "favoured autonomy but did not determine it automatically" (✅ owner 08 §11.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Riverine delta; cities Pandua and Gaur as changing capitals (✅ owner 08 §11.1).
**Links:** MED-2002, MED-2033 (trade), MED-2023 (state-capacity matrix).
**Trap:** Geography enables autonomy but needs state capacity. Do not present Bengal's autonomy as purely natural. Ports and trade matter too (MED-2033).
**Mains hook:** "Bengal's autonomy was ecological but not automatic." Use Bengal and Gujarat to argue that material base plus institutions produced durable regional states.
**Back:** *"Far, wet, hot: Bengal's walls were water, not stone."*

### Flashcard MED-2007 — Gaur and Pandua: Bengal's brick architecture
**Front:** What were the capitals of the Bengal sultanate and what architectural vocabulary did they develop?
**Definition (learn):** Pandua and Gaur, the changing royal-urban centres of Bengal, whose architecture adapted brick, terracotta, local roof forms and transregional mosque vocabularies.
**Breakdown:**
- Capitals: Pandua and Gaur (✅ owner 08 §11.1).
- Materials: brick, mortar, terracotta; drop arches; curvilinear roofs; lotus and swan motifs (✅ adv. 08 §1).
- Adina Mosque at Pandua and a "Dakhil Darwaza" type gateway — local forms plus Hindu decorative motifs (✅ adv. 08 §4 for type; ⚠️ builder and date of the Adina Mosque not stated in the owner — standard knowledge gives Sikandar Shah c. 1364, not verified here).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Brick and terracotta; drop arch; lotus/swan; Gaur-Pandua (✅ adv. 08).
**Links:** MED-2005, MED-2013 (Gujarat's style), MED-2015 (Mandu), MED-2017 (Jaunpur).
**Trap:** Bengal's architecture is not a copy of Delhi. The owner says Gaur/Pandua adapted local forms; the advanced owner stresses brick and terracotta rather than the Delhi stone-and-mortar mode.
**Mains hook:** Architecture as statecraft: Bengal's buildings show a regional idiom derived from materials, climate and transregional forms.
**Back:** *"Brick, terracotta, drop arch, lotus: Gaur speaks Bengal."*

### Flashcard MED-2008 — Gujarat's independence under Zafar Khan / Muzaffar Shah (1407)
**Front:** Who formally proclaimed Gujarat independent in 1407 and under what regnal name?
**Definition (learn):** Zafar Khan, the Delhi-appointed governor, who proclaimed Gujarat independent as Sultan Muzaffar Shah.
**Breakdown:**
- 1407: formal proclamation of independence (✅ owner 08 mini-timeline).
- Founded the Gujarat sultanate line that Ahmad Shah I consolidated (MED-2009).
- Owner chronology: Muzaffar Shah → Ahmad Shah I → Mahmud Begarha → Bahadur Shah (MED-2012).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1407 (✅ owner 08). Advanced owner: "Gujarat formally independent under Muzaffar Shah" (✅ adv. 08 mini-timeline).
**Links:** MED-2009, MED-2012, MED-2001.
**Trap:** Muzaffar Shah is a regnal name; Zafar Khan is his earlier identity. Ahmad Shah I is not Muzaffar Shah, and the 1407 proclamation is not the foundation of Ahmedabad.
**Mains hook:** Use 1407 to show that a provincial governor's proclamation can produce a durable port-based state when the hinterland and trade are strong.
**Back:** *"Zafar becomes Muzaffar in 1407: governor to sultan in one stroke."*

### Flashcard MED-2009 — Ahmad Shah I and Ahmedabad (1411–42)
**Front:** Who consolidated Gujarat between 1411 and 1442 and founded Ahmedabad?
**Definition (learn):** Ahmad Shah I, Gujarat sultan (1411–42), who consolidated the state and founded Ahmedabad.
**Breakdown:**
- Consolidation and founding of Ahmedabad (✅ owner 08 mini-timeline).
- Gujarat style drew on Jain architectural traditions (✅ owner 08 §1; adv. 08 §1: slender turrets, stone carving, ornate brackets).
- Foundation date: the owner's timeline gives "1411–42" for the reign and "1413" as the assigned source's foundation date; §12.1 gives "conventionally c. 1411–13".
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1411–42 reign (✅ owner 08). Foundation: 1413 (✅ owner 08 mini-timeline) vs c. 1411–13 (✅ owner 08 §12.1). ⚠️ The two owner dates are a repository inconsistency; state "c. 1411–13" in answers unless the source is checked.
**Links:** MED-2008, MED-2013 (Jain-influenced architecture), MED-2010.
**Trap:** The founding year is not settled in one number. Do not write 1413 as an unqualified exam fact without the "conventionally" qualifier.
**Mains hook:** Ahmedabad shows architecture as composite statecraft: local craft and Jain idiom in a Sultanate city.
**Back:** *"Ahmad founds Ahmedabad in the Eleven-to-Thirteen window."*

### Flashcard MED-2010 — Mahmud Begarha (1459–1511): Girnar, Champaner and the Portuguese
**Front:** What did Mahmud Begarha capture and how did Gujarat relate to the Portuguese during his reign?
**Definition (learn):** Gujarat sultan (1459–1511) who captured Girnar and Champaner and resisted early Portuguese intrusion on the Gujarat coast.
**Breakdown:**
- Captured Girnar (Junagadh) and Champaner (✅ owner 08 mini-timeline).
- Resisted Portuguese intrusion on the Gujarat coast (✅ owner 08 §4).
- Gujarat reached maximum power and prosperity under him (✅ adv. 08 §4).
- Champaner and Jama Masjid show fine stone work (✅ adv. 08 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1459–1511 (✅ owner 08). Girnar and Champaner (✅ owner 08). "Begarha" name etymology is ⚠️ (standard knowledge; not in owner).
**Links:** MED-2011 (Bahadur Shah and Bassein/Diu), MED-2012 (ladder), MED-2013 (architecture).
**Trap:** The Portuguese coastal resistance belongs to Mahmud Begarha; the pressured Bassein–Diu concession belongs to Bahadur Shah (✅ owner 08 §5). Do not merge them.
**Mains hook:** Use Mahmud Begarha's resistance to show an indigenous Indian Ocean power before the Mughal–Portuguese convergence of the 1520s–30s.
**Back:** *"Mahmud takes Girnar and Champaner; Portugal meets Mahmud's resistance."*

### Flashcard MED-2011 — Bahadur Shah and the Bassein–Diu concession (1526–37)
**Front:** Which Gujarat sultan conceded Bassein and Portuguese rights at Diu, and why?
**Definition (learn):** Bahadur Shah of Gujarat (r. 1526–37), who under pressure from Humayun's threat conceded Bassein and Portuguese positions at Diu.
**Breakdown:**
- Reign 1526–37 (✅ owner 08 §4, §12.1).
- Conflict with Humayun; pressured Portuguese agreements at Bassein and Diu (✅ owner 08 §12.1).
- The owner says "exact treaty wording varies across sources; the pressured concession is the safe exam point" (✅ owner 08 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1526–37 (✅ owner 08). Bassein and Diu (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MED-2010, MED-2012, MED-2026 (trap).
**Trap:** Answer "Bahadur Shah conceded Bassein and Diu-related rights under compulsion", not "Mahmud Begarha conceded". Do not write a precise treaty date or text unless verified.
**Mains hook:** Gujarat's ports made it a target of Portuguese and Mughal pressure; the 1530s concession shows how dynastic chronology converts into a cause-effect answer.
**Back:** *"Bahadur bends at Bassein and Diu: pressure beats Begarha's resistance."*

### Flashcard MED-2012 — Gujarat succession ladder
**Front:** What is the exam-safe Gujarat sultan sequence?
**Definition (learn):** The ordered line of Gujarat sultans used in UPSC chronology questions.
**Breakdown:**
- Zafar Khan / Muzaffar Shah (c. 1407) → Ahmad Shah I (1411–42) → Mahmud Begarha (1459–1511) → Bahadur Shah (1526–37) (✅ owner 08 §4, §12.1).
- Each rung links to a card: MED-2008, MED-2009, MED-2010, MED-2011.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** The four rungs above (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MED-2008, MED-2009, MED-2010, MED-2011.
**Trap:** Do not place Mahmud Begarha before Ahmad Shah I. Also: Bahadur Shah is 1526–37, after Mahmud's 1459–1511 — there is a long gap, so do not treat them as consecutive reigns.
**Mains hook:** Convert a ladder into cause-effect: port growth, then Portuguese naval pressure, then Mughal pressure under Humayun.
**Back:** *"Muzaffar, Ahmad, Mahmud, Bahadur: forty-odd years between the first and the last."*

### Flashcard MED-2013 — Gujarat architecture: Jain-influenced regional style
**Front:** What architectural traditions shaped Gujarat's regional style at Ahmedabad and Champaner?
**Definition (learn):** A Gujarati idiom that used local stone craft, brackets, screens and Jain building traditions rather than copying Delhi.
**Breakdown:**
- Jain architectural traditions in Ahmedabad (✅ owner 08 §1).
- Slender turrets, stone carving, ornate brackets (✅ adv. 08 §1).
- Champaner and Ahmedabad used local stone craft, brackets, screens and earlier building traditions (✅ owner 08 §12.2).
- Champaner (Mahmud Begarha) and Jama Masjid show fine stone work (✅ adv. 08 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ahmedabad (Ahmad Shah I); Champaner (Mahmud Begarha) (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MED-2009, MED-2010, MED-2007 (Bengal idiom).
**Trap:** "Gujarat architecture simply copied Delhi" is wrong (✅ owner 08 §5). Also avoid calling it a "fixed communal style": the owner says it was not a fixed communal style.
**Mains hook:** Use Gujarat's stone and bracket work to argue that regional architecture carried composite identity without requiring a single religious style.
**Back:** *"Gujarat carves Jain brackets into Islamic cities: a local grammar."*

### Flashcard MED-2014 — Malwa's dynastic frame: Ghuris and Khaljis
**Front:** Name Malwa's dynastic phases in order.
**Definition (learn):** The Malwa sultanate's sequence from autonomy under late Tughlaq Delhi to the Khalji phase.
**Breakdown:**
- Dilawar Khan Ghuri — established autonomy from late Tughlaq Delhi (✅ owner 08 §13.1).
- Hushang Shah Ghuri — consolidated Malwa and Mandu (✅ owner 08 §13.1).
- Khalji dynasty of Malwa, especially Mahmud Khalji — expansion, rivalry and contested court (✅ owner 08 §13.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No exact dates for Dilawar Khan or Hushang Shah in the owner; ⚠️ do not invent years.
**Links:** MED-2015 (Mandu), MED-2022 (balance of power), MED-2029 (PYQ routes).
**Trap:** Dilawar Khan Ghuri and Hushang Shah Ghuri precede the Malwa Khaljis (✅ owner 08 §20.2). Do not confuse Malwa's Khalji with Alauddin Khalji of Delhi.
**Mains hook:** Malwa's rulers show how regional Sultanate dynasties were recombined by rivals; use it to test the "dynastic continuity" assumption.
**Back:** *"Dilawar, Hushang, then the Khaljis: Ghuri first, Khalji later."*

### Flashcard MED-2015 — Mandu: massive Malwa architecture
**Front:** What defines Mandu's architecture and which buildings belong to Malwa's patronage?
**Definition (learn):** Malwa's plateau capital, with massive plinths, coloured/glazed tiles and monumental buildings from different patronage phases.
**Breakdown:**
- Style: massive architecture, high plinths, coloured/glazed tiles (✅ owner 08 §4; adv. 08 §4).
- Jama Masjid; Hushang Shah's tomb; Hindola Mahal; Jahaz Mahal (✅ owner 08 §13.2; adv. 08 §4).
- Different buildings belong to different construction phases (✅ owner 08 §13.2). Builder and date per building are not in the owner; ⚠️ do not assign dates without checking.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mandu = Malwa (✅ owner 08 §20.2).
**Links:** MED-2014, MED-2007 (Bengal idiom), MED-2017 (Jaunpur).
**Trap:** Mandu is Malwa; Champaner is Gujarat; Atala Mosque is Jaunpur (✅ owner 08 §20.2). Do not mix the three monument identities.
**Mains hook:** Mandu's plateau setting made it both a monument and a defensive node; use it to argue that geography shaped architecture as well as politics.
**Back:** *"Mandu: plinth high, tile bright, Hindola swings, Jahaz sails."*

### Flashcard MED-2016 — Jaunpur and the Sharqi sultans
**Front:** What was Jaunpur's political and cultural identity under the Sharqis, and how did it end?
**Definition (learn):** The Sharqi kingdom in the eastern Ganga valley, known as the "Shiraz of the East", absorbed by Bahlul Lodi in 1484.
**Breakdown:**
- Name: Malik Sarwar's title "Malik-us-Sharq" is associated with the Sharqi line (✅ owner 08 §14).
- "Shiraz of the East": learning, Sufis, Persianate and regional traditions (✅ owner 08 §1, §14).
- Political: rivalry with Delhi/Lodis and interaction with Bengal (✅ owner 08 §14).
- End: Bahlul Lodi annexes Jaunpur 1484 (✅ owner 08 mini-timeline).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1484 (✅ owner 08). Sharqis = Jaunpur (✅ owner 08 §20.2).
**Links:** MED-2017 (Atala Mosque), MED-2022, MED-2026.
**Trap:** Absorption did not erase Jaunpur's architectural and cultural legacy (✅ owner 08 §14). "Shiraz of the East" is a learning-and-culture label, not a Persian-era political claim.
**Mains hook:** Jaunpur: cultural achievement did not guarantee political survival (✅ owner 08 §8.2 evidence unit 4).
**Back:** *"Sharqi Shiraz falls to Bahlul in 1484: learning survives the throne."*

### Flashcard MED-2017 — Atala Mosque and Jaunpur's monumental gateways
**Front:** Which monument and form define Jaunpur's regional architecture?
**Definition (learn):** The Atala Mosque and the lofty gateways and huge arches that created Jaunpur's regional idiom.
**Breakdown:**
- Atala Mosque (✅ owner 08 §14).
- Lofty gates and huge arches (✅ owner 08 §1, §4).
- Builder and date of the Atala Mosque are not stated in the owner; ⚠️ standard knowledge attributes it to the Sharqi period — verify before naming a sultan.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sharqi architecture, Jaunpur (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MED-2016, MED-2015, MED-2007.
**Trap:** Jaunpur's gateway architecture is not the same as Mandu's plinth-and-tile architecture. Match "lofty gates and arches" to Jaunpur only.
**Mains hook:** Jaunpur's arches show regional architecture as a legitimation device in a city that lost political primacy.
**Back:** *"Atala and arches: Jaunpur's gate speaks the Sharqi idiom."*

### Flashcard MED-2018 — Kashmir: Shah Mir dynasty and the Sikandar problem
**Front:** Who founded the Shah Mir sultanate and what is the owner's caution about Sikandar's reign?
**Definition (learn):** The Shah Mir sultanate in the valley of Kashmir, founded in the fourteenth century, whose early rulers include Sikandar.
**Breakdown:**
- Shah Mir established the Shah Mir sultanate in the fourteenth century (✅ owner 08 §15.1).
- Sikandar's reign is associated in chronicles with sharper religious policies and temple actions; "details require source caution" (✅ owner 08 §15.1).
- Kashmir's Islamisation was not uniform and peaceful; both saintly influence and coercion are noted (✅ adv. 08 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dynasty founder: Shah Mir; persecution then reversal under Zainul Abidin (✅ owner 08 §15, §20.2).
**Links:** MED-2019 (Zainul Abidin), MED-2020 (comparison pair), MED-2035 (Rishi-Sufi milieu).
**Trap:** "Shah Mir = dynasty founder" (✅ owner 08 §20.2). Do not assign Sikandar's temple actions to Zainul Abidin, or vice versa.
**Mains hook:** Kashmir shows a regional ruler's religious policy can swing sharply; avoid one-word labels for any ruler.
**Back:** *"Shah Mir founds, Sikandar strikes, Zainul heals."*

### Flashcard MED-2019 — Zainul Abidin (Bud Shah), 1420–70: restorative toleration
**Front:** What did Zainul Abidin (Bud Shah, 1420–70) do to reverse earlier persecution in Kashmir?
**Definition (learn):** Kashmir's sultan (1420–70), called Bud Shah, who pursued reconciliation and cultural revival.
**Breakdown:**
- Cancelled anti-Hindu orders; abolished jizyah and cow slaughter; restored temples; welcomed back non-Muslims (✅ owner 08 §1).
- Restored grants and institutions; encouraged return of displaced groups; patronised learning, crafts and translation (✅ owner 08 §15.1).
- High offices for Hindus; restored libraries and land grants (✅ adv. 08 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1420–70 (✅ owner 08). Bud Shah = Zainul Abidin (✅ adv. 08 §4).
**Links:** MED-2018, MED-2020, MED-2035.
**Trap:** Zainul Abidin should not be reduced to "tolerant king" alone. The owner: "court chronicles and later regional memory require comparison" (✅ owner 08 §15.2).
**Mains hook:** Kashmir shows that a regional ruler could reverse persecution and build a plural court, but the policy was event-specific and enabled partly by valley isolation (✅ owner 08 §8.2 evidence unit 2).
**Back:** *"Bud Shah ends the jizyah and rebuilds the temples."*

### Flashcard MED-2020 — Comparison pair: Sikandar Shah vs Zainul Abidin (Kashmir)
**Front:** How do Sikandar Shah and Zainul Abidin differ in Kashmir's religious policy?
**Definition (learn):** A comparison pair for the two Kashmir rulers that examiners use to test against one-word labels.
**Breakdown:**
- Sikandar: sharper religious policies and temple actions in chronicles (✅ owner 08 §15.1).
- Zainul Abidin: cancelled those orders; broad toleration; temples restored (✅ owner 08 §5).
- Caution: owner says details require source caution for Sikandar.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Zainul Abidin 1420–70 (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MED-2018, MED-2019.
**Trap:** "Zainul Abidin continued Sikandar's persecution" is wrong (✅ owner 08 §5). He cancelled those orders.
**Mains hook:** Use the pair to show variation in Sultanate religious policy; universal tolerance or persecution describes neither ruler.
**Back:** *"Sikandar struck, Zainul struck back at the strike: he reversed it."*

### Flashcard MED-2021 — Fanam: a small gold coin, not a Sultanate term
**Front:** What was the fanam and why is it a trap in Medieval coin questions?
**Definition (learn):** A small gold coin of south Indian and coastal trade, especially associated with Vijayanagara and Malabar circulation.
**Breakdown:**
- Small gold coin (✅ owner 08 §4).
- Vijayanagara and Malabar circulation (✅ owner 08 §4).
- Not a standard Delhi Sultanate monetary term or office (✅ owner 08 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gold; south Indian/coastal (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MED-2065 (Vijayanagara gold varaha/pagoda coinage), MED-2031 (Bengal trade and re-export).
**Trap:** Fanam ≠ silver coin of north India; fanam ≠ Delhi office. This is exactly the owner's "not a standard Delhi term" trap.
**Mains hook:** Coins show sovereignty and monetisation, but coin circulation does not map exact borders (✅ owner 08 §18, §10).
**Back:** *"Fanam: small, gold, southern coast — never a Delhi office."*

### Flashcard MED-2022 — Balance of power: Gujarat–Malwa–Mewar and Bengal–Orissa–Jaunpur
