**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1510 (Merv); 1514 (✅ owner 12).
**Links:** MED-2180 (Safavid), MED-2181 (Chaldiran).
**Trap:** Shaibani's death at Merv opened a Safavid opening, not an Uzbek defeat (✅ owner 12 §12).
**Mains hook:** Uzbek consolidation as the key external pressure.
**Back:** *"Shaibani pushes Babur out; Merv 1510 kills him; Uzbeks still dominate."*

### Flashcard MED-2180 — Safavid Shah Ismail and the temporary opening after Merv (1510)
**Front:** How did the Safavid victory at Merv affect Babur, and what complicated it?
**Definition (learn):** Shah Ismail's victory at Merv (1510) created a temporary return to Samarkand; Safavid aid, Shia symbols and khutba-sikka demands complicated it.
**Breakdown:**
- 1510: Safavid victory at Merv temporarily reopens Samarkand hopes (✅ adv. 12 mini-timeline).
- Safavid aid, Shia symbols and khutba-sikka demands, and Sunni response complicated the position (✅ owner 12 §12).
- Babur could not sustain Samarkand with Iranian help (✅ owner 12 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1510 (✅ owner 12; adv. 12).
**Links:** MED-2179, MED-2181.
**Trap:** Safavid help did not make Babur a Safavid client (⚠️ owner-implied).
**Mains hook:** Religious symbols as political constraints in Central Asia.
**Back:** *"Merv 1510: Safavid help opens a door, and asks for the khutba."*

### Flashcard MED-2181 — Chaldiran (1514) and the balance of power
**Front:** What did the 1514 Chaldiran victory change and what did it not do?
**Definition (learn):** The Ottoman victory over Shah Ismail at Chaldiran (1514) shifted the balance; Uzbeks regained Trans-Oxiana dominance.
**Breakdown:**
- Ottoman victory over Shah Ismail (✅ owner 12 mini-timeline; adv. 12 mini-timeline).
- Uzbeks regain Trans-Oxiana dominance (✅ adv. 12 mini-timeline).
- Chaldiran altered Ottoman-Safavid power but did not mechanically send Babur to India (✅ owner 12 §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1514 (✅ owner 12).
**Links:** MED-2179, MED-2180, MED-2175.
**Trap:** "Chaldiran sent Babur to India" is a mechanical causation error (✅ owner 12 §12).
**Mains hook:** The Ottoman-Safavid balance as external context.
**Back:** *"Fourteen-fourteen, Chaldiran shifts the balance but doesn't send Babur."*

### Flashcard MED-2182 — Kabul (1504): refuge, base, junction and market
**Front:** What functions did Kabul serve for Babur?
**Definition (learn):** Kabul was a refuge, a base for a mobile army and court, a route junction, a market, and a cultural environment.
**Breakdown:**
- Refuge beyond immediate Uzbek reach (✅ owner 12 §13).
- Base for a mobile army and Timurid court (✅ owner 12 §13).
- Junction among Hindustan, Khurasan and Transoxiana routes (✅ owner 12 §13).
- Market for horses, textiles, fruits and other goods (✅ owner 12 §13).
- Cultural environment for Chagatai/Persianate literature, gardens and recruitment (✅ owner 12 §13).
- Revenue insufficient for growing begs and forces (✅ owner 12 §13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1504 (✅ owner 12 mini-timeline).
**Links:** MED-2183, MED-2175, MED-2184.
**Trap:** Kabul was not merely poor; routes, agriculture and prestige gave it value (✅ owner 12 §13).
**Mains hook:** Kabul as a strategic node rather than a poverty trap.
**Back:** *"Kabul: refuge, base, junction, market, and a Persianate stage."*

### Flashcard MED-2183 — Kabul and Qandahar: strategic shield and trade-marts
**Front:** Why did incorporating Kabul and Qandahar matter for the Indian empire?
**Definition (learn):** Kabul and Qandahar gave a north Indian empire a strategic shield and a stake in Central Asia; they were trade-marts linking Hindustan with Khurasan, China, Turkistan and West Asia.
**Breakdown:**
- Strategic shield against invasions; stake in Central Asian politics (✅ adv. 12 §1).
- Trade-marts linking Hindustan with Khurasan, China, Turkistan and West Asia (✅ adv. 12 §1).
- Increased India's share in trans-Asian trade (✅ adv. 12 §1; owner §4 for trade access).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2182, MED-2184.
**Trap:** Qandahar's later Mughal-Persian contest belongs to Topic 19 (✅ owner 12 §13).
**Mains hook:** Strategic geography: Kabul-Qandahar, trade and external security.
**Back:** *"Kabul and Qandahar: a shield, a bridge, a trade-mart."*

### Flashcard MED-2184 — Qandahar before the final Indian invasion
**Front:** What did securing Qandahar do for the final Indian campaign?
**Definition (learn):** Babur later secured Qandahar, strengthening the rear and flank and the Iran–Central Asia–Hindustan linkage before the final invasion.
**Breakdown:**
- Secured later, before the final Indian invasion (✅ owner 12 §13).
- Rear/flank strengthened (✅ owner 12 §13).
- Date of capture not given in the owner (⚠️ not stated).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Date not in owner (⚠️).
**Links:** MED-2183, MED-2185.
**Trap:** Do not invent a date for Qandahar.
**Mains hook:** Rear security as a precondition of the Indian advance.
**Back:** *"Qandahar guards the rear before Hindustan."*

### Flashcard MED-2185 — Babur's Indian campaign chronology before 1526
**Front:** What is the pre-Panipat campaign ladder?
**Definition (learn):** 1519 Bajaur/Bhera–Bhira probes; 1519–24 repeated Indus and Punjab advances (Sialkot, Lahore–Dipalpur); 1524 intervention amid Lodi rivalries; 1525 rear secured and final march from Kabul; December 1525–April 1526 Indus crossing and Panipat approach.
**Breakdown:**
- 1519: Bajaur/Bhera-Bhira and north-west probes (✅ owner 12 §14).
- 1519–24: Indus/Punjab advances (✅ owner 12 §14).
- 1524: intervention amid Ibrahim, Daulat Khan and Alam Khan rivalry (✅ owner 12 §14).
- 1525: rear/flank secured; final march from Kabul (✅ owner 12 §14).
- December 1525–April 1526: Indus crossing; Punjab control; Panipat approach (✅ owner 12 §14; adv. 12 §1 says army crossed the Indus by mid-December).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** See above (✅ owner 12 §14; adv. 12 §1).
**Links:** MED-2186, MED-2187, MED-2188.
**Trap:** Do not present Panipat as a sudden invasion; it was the end of a Punjab contest (✅ adv. 12 §1).
**Mains hook:** Panipat as culmination of a longer contest.
**Back:** *"Nineteen to twenty-four: probes. Twenty-five: Kabul's final march. Twenty-six: Panipat."*

### Flashcard MED-2186 — Daulat Khan Lodi and Alam Khan: the invitation context
**Front:** Who invited Babur and how did they behave?
**Definition (learn):** Daulat Khan Lodi (Punjab) and Alam Khan (who sought Delhi with Babur's help) invited or sought Babur's help; their behaviour was opportunistic.
**Breakdown:**
- Daulat Khan alternated between resistance, alliance and surrender (✅ owner 12 §14).
- Alam Khan sought Delhi with Babur's help (✅ owner 12 §14).
- Babur sought Punjab and then wider sovereignty, not merely fulfilment of their invitations (✅ owner 12 §14).
- Invitations do not cancel conquest (✅ owner 12 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1524–25 (✅ owner 12 §14, adv. 12 §1).
**Links:** MED-2185, MED-2187, MED-2204.
**Trap:** "Babur came only because Indian nobles invited him" is wrong (✅ owner 12 §5).
**Mains hook:** Internal Lodi fissures enabled external conquest (✅ owner 12 §8.2 unit 5).
**Back:** *"Daulat wavers, Alam asks: invitations, but Babur wanted more."*

### Flashcard MED-2187 — Babur's talks with Rana Sanga: an uncertain claim
**Front:** What do the sources say about Babur's negotiations with Rana Sanga before Panipat?
**Definition (learn):** Babur reports negotiations with Rana Sanga against Ibrahim; the terms, initiative and intended partition remain uncertain. His later accusation of breach is a participant's claim.
**Breakdown:**
- Babur reports negotiations with Rana Sanga against Ibrahim (✅ owner 12 §14).
- Exact terms, initiative and intended partition uncertain (✅ owner 12 §14).
- Later accusation of breach = participant's claim (✅ owner 12 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2186, MED-2195.
**Trap:** Do not state a partition agreement as established.
**Mains hook:** Participant's memoir and the uncertainty of diplomacy.
**Back:** *"Babur talks to Sanga; the terms, and the breach, are his claim."*

### Flashcard MED-2188 — Panipat I (1526): 20 or 21 April
**Front:** What is the date of the First Battle of Panipat and what is secure?
**Definition (learn):** The First Battle of Panipat, 1526, between Babur and Ibrahim Lodi; the year and combatants are secure, but the day is disputed (20 or 21 April).
**Breakdown:**
- Year 1526; Babur vs Ibrahim Lodi (✅ owner 12 §4; adv. 12 mini-timeline).
- Part II gives 20 April 1526; many standard chronologies give 21 April (⚠️ owner-flagged discrepancy).
- Army totals, elephant counts and casualties are memoir/chronicle claims (✅ owner 12 §15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1526; 20/21 April (⚠️ day disputed). Wikipedia (First Battle of Panipat) gives 21 April 1526 without naming the calendar (✅ wiki).
**Links:** MED-2189, MED-2193, MED-2214.
**Trap:** "The day is trivial but frequently tested" (✅ owner 12 §8.3); qualify the day if used.
**Mains hook:** Source discrepancies must be stated, not hidden.
**Back:** *"Twenty-six, the April days: year is firm, day is qualified."*

### Flashcard MED-2189 — Panipat I field system: araba, raw-hide links, tulughma, matchlocks
**Front:** What were the combined-arms elements at Panipat?
**Definition (learn):** A narrow defended front of carts (araba) joined by raw-hide ropes, breastworks and firing gaps for matchlockmen and field artillery, with mounted archers, a cavalry reserve and tulughma flanking parties.
**Breakdown:**
- Right anchored near the Panipat settlement (✅ owner 12 §15).
- Cart line (araba) + raw-hide links + breastworks + firing gaps (✅ owner 12 §15; adv. 12 §1).
- Matchlockmen and field artillery under Ustad Ali and Mustafa (✅ owner 12 §15).
- Mounted archers and cavalry reserve; tulughma flanking parties; left protected by ditch (✅ owner 12 §15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2190, MED-2191, MED-2216, MED-2193.
**Trap:** Artillery alone did not win (✅ owner 12 §5).
**Mains hook:** Tactical innovation against a numerically superior army (✅ owner 12 §8.2 unit 2).
**Back:** *"Carts, guns, bowmen, wheeling flanks: the combined field."*

### Flashcard MED-2190 — Araba and tulughma: definitions and the artillery trap
**Front:** What do araba and tulughma mean and why is confusing them a trap?
**Definition (learn):** Araba means carts or wagons in this battlefield context; tulughma (tulghuma) describes wheeling or flanking group tactics known from Central Asian warfare.
**Breakdown:**
- Araba = carts/wagons (✅ owner 12 §15, §23).
- Tulughma = wheeling/flanking group tactics; not another word for artillery (✅ owner 12 §15).
- Babur called the cart-artillery arrangement Rumi/Ottoman-inspired (✅ owner 12 §15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2189, MED-2216.
**Trap:** Tulughma ≠ artillery; araba ≠ tulughma.
**Mains hook:** Use the terms precisely.
**Back:** *"Araba carts; tulughma wheels: not guns."*

### Flashcard MED-2191 — Ustad Ali and Mustafa: master gunners
**Front:** Who were Babur's master gunners at Panipat?
**Definition (learn):** Ustad Ali and Mustafa, Ottoman or Rumi gunners who commanded the field artillery.
**Breakdown:**
- Master-gunners Ustad Ali and Mustafa (✅ owner 12 §4; adv. 12 §4).
- Ottoman-style carts and field defence (✅ owner 12 §4).
- Ottoman/Rumi gunners (✅ owner 12 §23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2189, MED-2190.
**Trap:** Ustad Ali and Mustafa ≠ Ibrahim's commanders.
**Mains hook:** Technology and personnel transfer.
**Back:** *"Ali and Mustafa: Rumi gunners on Babur's line."*

### Flashcard MED-2192 — Ibrahim Lodi: died fighting, buried with honour
**Front:** How did Ibrahim Lodi die at Panipat and how did Babur treat him?
**Definition (learn):** Ibrahim Lodi fought bravely and died on the field; Babur buried him with honour.
**Breakdown:**
- Fought bravely; died on the field (✅ adv. 12 §4).
- Babur buried him with honour (✅ adv. 12 §4).
- Ibrahim's poor leadership also contributed (✅ owner 12 §8.2 unit 2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1526 (✅ owner 12).
**Links:** MED-2188, MED-2189.
**Trap:** Ibrahim's death is not the end of Afghan opposition (✅ adv. 12 §5).
**Mains hook:** Individual failure and battlefield collapse as two sides.
**Back:** *"Ibrahim fights and falls; Babur buries him with honour."*

### Flashcard MED-2193 — Panipat's immediate effect: opening Delhi–Agra, not a consolidated empire
**Front:** What did Panipat achieve and what did it not achieve?
**Definition (learn):** Panipat destroyed Ibrahim's field army and opened Delhi–Agra; it did not create a consolidated empire in one day.
**Breakdown:**
- Destroyed Ibrahim's field army; opened Delhi–Agra (✅ owner 12 §15).
- Khanwa and Ghagra were still needed (✅ adv. 12 §5).
- Post-Panipat: eastern Afghans and fortified towns remained (✅ owner 12 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2194, MED-2195, MED-2198.
**Trap:** "Panipat's political effect was complete and immediate" is wrong (✅ adv. 12 §5).
**Mains hook:** Panipat as opening, not closure.
**Back:** *"Panipat opens the road; Khanwa and Ghagra make the empire."*

### Flashcard MED-2194 — After Panipat: fragile control
**Front:** What tools did Babur use to bind followers, and what strained his rule?
**Definition (learn):** Babur occupied Delhi and Agra and bound followers through khutba, treasure distribution, gifts, offices and assignments, while begs wished to return to Kabul and local forts resisted.
**Breakdown:**
- Khutba, treasure, gifts, offices and assignments (✅ owner 12 §16).
- Begs wished to return to Kabul; climate, food, fodder shortage and local hostility (✅ owner 12 §16).
- Fortified towns and local commanders did not submit (✅ owner 12 §16).
- Eastern Afghan chiefs remained organised; roads and revenue insecure (✅ owner 12 §16).
- Unconquered territories assigned to begs, using existing pargana/local personnel (✅ owner 12 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1526–30 (✅ owner 12 §22).
**Links:** MED-2193, MED-2204, MED-2210.
**Trap:** Control was uneven and negotiated; not instant.
**Mains hook:** Early Mughal rule as conquest garrisons plus local continuities.
**Back:** *"Khutba binds, begs grumble, forts resist, Afghans hold the east."*

### Flashcard MED-2195 — Khanwa (1527): Rana Sanga's coalition and Babur's revised formation
**Front:** What was the Khanwa coalition and what did Babur change?
**Definition (learn):** Rana Sanga's Rajput-Afghan coalition, including Mahmud Lodi and Hasan Khan Mewati, defeated at Khanwa (1527) against a revised cart system with chains/tripods, matchlocks, artillery, cavalry and flanking groups.
**Breakdown:**
- Coalition included Rajput rulers and Afghan figures Mahmud Lodi and Hasan Khan Mewati (✅ owner 12 §17; §4).
- Sanga's ambitions around Mewar, Malwa, Bayana and Agra interacted with Babur's decision to stay (✅ owner 12 §17).
- Revised cart system: chains/tripods, matchlocks, artillery, cavalry, flanking (✅ owner 12 §17).
- Results: checked the coalition; Delhi-Agra remained fragile (✅ owner 12 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1527 (✅ owner 12).
**Links:** MED-2196, MED-2187, MED-2211.
**Trap:** Khanwa was not only a Hindu-Muslim war; Afghans joined Rana Sanga (✅ owner 12 §5).
**Mains hook:** A political-military coalition, not modern nationalism (✅ owner 12 §17).
**Back:** *"Khanwa 1527: Sanga and Afghans lose to a revised cart line."*

### Flashcard MED-2196 — Ghazi and jihad rhetoric in the Baburnama
**Front:** What religious language did the Baburnama use around Khanwa, and how should it be read?
**Definition (learn):** The Baburnama presents jihad, Quran oaths, wine renunciation, tamgha remission and the title ghazi as mobilising language.
**Breakdown:**
- Jihad, Quran oaths, wine renunciation, tamgha remission, ghazi (✅ owner 12 §17).
- Records piety, legitimacy and morale; does not exhaust territorial, dynastic and military motives (✅ owner 12 §17).
- Jihad rhetoric is political mobilisation (✅ adv. 12 §5).
- Ghazi rhetoric is not the sole cause of Khanwa (✅ owner 12 §23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2195, MED-2209.
**Trap:** "Babur's jihad rhetoric proves a primarily religious war" is wrong (✅ adv. 12 §5).
**Mains hook:** Separate private devotion, public rhetoric, diplomacy, violence and later memory (✅ owner 12 §21).
**Back:** *"Jihad sounds at Khanwa, but it is mobilisation, not the whole war."*

### Flashcard MED-2197 — Chanderi (1528): campaign against Medini Rai
**Front:** What was the Chanderi campaign of 1528?
**Definition (learn):** Babur's campaign against Medini Rai, following Khanwa, against a strategic Rajput-held centre.
**Breakdown:**
- 1528, Chanderi; campaign against Medini Rai (✅ owner 12 §17).
- Siege, violence and religious rhetoric require source caution (✅ owner 12 §17).
- Not the conquest of all Rajput polities (✅ owner 12 §17).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1528 (✅ owner 12 §23).
**Links:** MED-2195, MED-2198.
**Trap:** Do not present Chanderi as the conquest of Rajputana.
**Mains hook:** Campaign violence within a political frame.
**Back:** *"Chanderi 1528: Medini Rai's fort, not all of Rajputana."*

### Flashcard MED-2198 — Ghaghra / Ghagra (5 May 1529): eastern Afghans and Bengal
**Front:** What did the Ghagra battle of 1529 achieve?
**Definition (learn):** Babur's eastern campaign against Afghan power in the Ganga region, involving relations with Bengal's Nusrat Shah; it checked an eastern coalition and produced an accommodation or boundary with Bengal, not an annexation.
**Breakdown:**
- 5 May 1529 (✅ adv. 12 mini-timeline and §4).
- Afghan rebels and Nusrat Shah's Bengal forces (✅ adv. 12 §4; owner §17).
- Accommodation/boundary with Bengal; not annexation (✅ owner 12 §17).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 5 May 1529 (✅ adv. 12 §4; basic owner gives 1529 only).
**Links:** MED-2194, MED-2199.
**Trap:** Ghaghra did not mean annexation of Bengal (✅ owner 12 §23).
**Mains hook:** Eastern Afghan power as the main post-Panipat challenge.
**Back:** *"Ghagra 1529: Afghans and Nusrat Shah checked, Bengal not taken."*

### Flashcard MED-2199 — Baburnama (Tuzuk-i Baburi): source method
**Front:** How should Babur's memoir be used as a source?
**Definition (learn):** The Baburnama, known as Tuzuk-i Baburi, is a selective ruler's memoir of campaigns, landscapes, people and self-presentation, composed over time with gaps and retrospective passages.
**Breakdown:**
- Campaigns, landscapes, people, court relations, emotions, flora/fauna and self-presentation (✅ owner 12 §11).
- Selective memoir, composed over time with gaps (✅ owner 12 §11).
- Ask: when written, what Babur wished to justify, what observed directly, what needs corroboration (✅ owner 12 §11).
- Use carefully: vivid but a ruler's self-representation (✅ adv. 12 historiography note).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2200, MED-2201, MED-2216.
**Trap:** The memoir is neither a neutral daily diary nor useless (✅ owner 12 §11).
**Mains hook:** Source criticism and corroboration.
**Back:** *"Babur's memoir: vivid, selective, corroborate it."*

### Flashcard MED-2200 — Trap: Chagatai Turkish original, not a Persian diary
**Front:** What language was the Baburnama originally written in?
**Definition (learn):** The Baburnama was written in Chagatai Turkish, later translated into Persian.
**Breakdown:**
- Chagatai Turkish original (✅ owner 12 §23; adv. 12 §4 "Chaghtai Turkish prose").
- Persian translation under Akbar, associated with Abd al-Rahim Khan-i-Khanan (✅ owner 12 §11).
- Translation is not identical in nuance (✅ owner 12 §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Akbar-era Persian translation (✅ owner 12 §11).
**Links:** MED-2199, MED-2201.
**Trap:** "Persian diary" is wrong (✅ owner 12 §23).
**Mains hook:** Language and translation as source issues.
**Back:** *"Chagatai first, Persian later: not a Persian original."*

### Flashcard MED-2201 — Persian translation under Akbar (Abd al-Rahim Khan-i-Khanan)
**Front:** Who is associated with the Persian translation of the Baburnama and why does it matter?
**Definition (learn):** The Persian translation under Akbar, associated with Abd al-Rahim Khan-i-Khanan, shows later imperial circulation and reception.
**Breakdown:**
- Associated with Abd al-Rahim Khan-i-Khanan (✅ owner 12 §11).
- Later imperial circulation (✅ owner 12 §11).
- Not identical in nuance to Chagatai (✅ owner 12 §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Akbar's period (✅ owner 12 §11).
**Links:** MED-2199, MED-2200.
**Trap:** The translator is not the author.
**Mains hook:** Reception history of a Central Asian memoir in the Mughal court.
**Back:** *"Khan-i-Khanan's Persian: Akbar's reading of Babur."*

### Flashcard MED-2202 — Genealogy and legitimacy: Timurid father, Chagatai-Chingizid mother
**Front:** What was Babur's genealogical claim and why does "Mughal" mask complexity?
**Definition (learn):** Babur's paternal line claimed Timurid descent and his maternal line a Chagatai-Chingizid connection; "Mughal" is a later label covering a Timurid, Chagatai, Turkic, Mongol and Persianate inheritance.
**Breakdown:**
- Timurid paternal claim (✅ owner 12 §10; §4).
- Chagatai-Chingizid maternal connection (✅ owner 12 §4).
- "Mughal" = Persian-derived root of Mongol (✅ owner 12 §10).
- Turco-Mongol and Persianate inheritance (✅ adv. 12 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2203, MED-2204.
**Trap:** Genealogy is a political claim; it does not make a biological ethnicity test (✅ owner 12 §10).
**Mains hook:** Legitimacy through lineage and marriage politics.
**Back:** *"Timur on the father's side, Chingiz on the mother's: 'Mughal' is the later name."*

### Flashcard MED-2203 — Labels: Timurid, Chagatai, Mongol, Turkic and Mughal
**Front:** What do the five identity labels mean in exam answers?
**Definition (learn):** Timurid: dynastic-political claim through the paternal line to Timur. Chagatai: maternal Chingizid connection and literary language. Mongol: ancestry tradition and the root of "Mughal". Turkic: language, military and Barlas culture. Mughal: later Indian dynastic label.
**Breakdown:**
- Timurid (✅ owner 12 §10).
- Chagatai/Chaghatay (✅ owner 12 §10).
- Mongol (✅ owner 12 §10).
- Turkic, including Barlas context (✅ owner 12 §10).
- Mughal: later label (✅ owner 12 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2202.
**Trap:** None of these is a modern ethnicity test (✅ owner 12 §10).
**Mains hook:** Use the label table in a 10-mark answer on identity.
**Back:** *"Timurid claims, Chagatai voice, Mongol root, Turkic army, Mughal name."*

### Flashcard MED-2204 — Legitimacy tools: padshah, khutba, sikka, grants, victory
**Front:** Which tools built Babur's authority?
**Definition (learn):** Padshah title, Timurid prestige, Chingizid connection, khutba (Friday prayer), sikka (coinage), victory, grants and court loyalty.
**Breakdown:**
- Padshah and Timurid prestige (✅ owner 12 §10).
- Chingizid connection (✅ owner 12 §10).
- Khutba and sikka (✅ owner 12 §10; §16).
- Treasure distribution, robes, gifts, pargana/jagir-like assignments (✅ owner 12 §19).
- Victory and court loyalty (✅ owner 12 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2194, MED-2202, MED-2205.
**Trap:** Coins and khutba do not prove uniform territorial control (✅ owner 12 §11).
**Mains hook:** Authority as negotiated and symbolic.
**Back:** *"Padshah, khutba, sikka, gifts: victory becomes authority."*

### Flashcard MED-2205 — Military organisation and logistics: the gunpowder-empire caveat
**Front:** What were the components of Babur's military system and what limits applied?
**Definition (learn):** Begs and personal followings; cavalry and mounted archery; matchlocks and artillery; carts, chains and field works; elephants; reconnaissance; supply and fodder; forts and garrisons.
**Breakdown:**
- Begs and followings: cohesion depended on reward and persuasion (✅ owner 12 §18).
- Cavalry: mobility and flanking (✅ owner 12 §18).
- Matchlocks/artillery: shock and fire; rate and accuracy limited (✅ owner 12 §18).
- Carts, chains and field works (✅ owner 12 §18).
- Elephants: numbers cautioned (✅ owner 12 §18).
- Supply and fodder constrained operations (✅ owner 12 §18).
- Forts: victory needed follow-up possession (✅ owner 12 §18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2189, MED-2206.
**Trap:** "Gunpowder empire" is useful only when technology is embedded in finance, logistics, command and alliances (✅ owner 12 §18).
**Mains hook:** The gunpowder empire concept as a conditional label.
**Back:** *"Guns need carts, horses, food, and forts to become an empire."*

### Flashcard MED-2206 — Kingship and court: councils and personal monarchy
**Front:** How did Babur's kingship combine personal monarchy and consultation?
**Definition (learn):** Babur used Timurid charisma and padshah claims while bargaining with begs whose service depended on reward, honour, kinship and success; councils coexisted with personal monarchy.
**Breakdown:**
- Timurid dynastic charisma and padshah claims (✅ owner 12 §19).
- Bargaining with begs (✅ owner 12 §19).
- Councils and elite consultation coexisted with personal monarchy (✅ owner 12 §19).
- Afghan clan/noble bargaining; neither "Afghan equality" nor "Timurid absolutism" was fixed (✅ owner 12 §19).
- Early Indian court remained mobile and Central Asian in personnel and culture (✅ owner 12 §19).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2205, MED-2218.
**Trap:** Do not characterise Babur's kingship as absolute or as Afghan-style equality (✅ owner 12 §19).
**Mains hook:** Kingship as negotiated personal monarchy.
**Back:** *"Councils talk, begs bargain, the padshah rules: negotiated, not absolute."*

### Flashcard MED-2207 — Bagh-e Babur and gardens in Kabul and north India
**Front:** What does Bagh-e Babur preserve and what is the caution?
**Definition (learn):** Gardens joined water, terrace or axis, leisure, burial, memory and royal ordering of landscape; Bagh-e Babur preserves phases from Babur, Jahangir and Shah Jahan.
**Breakdown:**
- Gardens joined water, terrace/axis, leisure, burial, memory, and royal ordering (✅ owner 12 §20).
- Bagh-e Babur preserves Babur, Jahangir, Shah Jahan and later phases (✅ owner 12 §20).
- The present reconstructed garden is not a wholly unchanged 1530 landscape (✅ owner 12 §20).
- Early Agra garden names and attributions must be tied to text or fabric (✅ owner 12 §20).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2208, MED-2199.
**Trap:** Do not treat the present garden as the 1530 landscape; gardens do not prove a secular personality (✅ owner 12 §20).
**Mains hook:** Garden culture as political order.
**Back:** *"Bagh-e Babur keeps many hands: Babur, Jahangir, Shah Jahan, and restorers."*

### Flashcard MED-2208 — Babur as a writer and natural observer
**Front:** How did Babur describe India and what is the caution?
**Definition (learn):** The Baburnama records terrain, rivers, climate, fruits, plants, animals, towns and customs through a comparative Central Asian gaze; he wrote Chagatai prose and poetry.
**Breakdown:**
- Natural and cultural observation (✅ owner 12 §20).
- Chagatai prose and poetry in a Persianate literary world (✅ owner 12 §20).
- Description may classify Hindustan by preference, nostalgia and rulership (✅ owner 12 §20).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2199, MED-2207.
**Trap:** Natural description is valuable but not neutral.
**Mains hook:** Use Babur's observations as a historical source with bias.
**Back:** *"Babur sees India with a Central Asian eye: vivid, comparative, partial."*

### Flashcard MED-2209 — Religious language: five separations
**Front:** How should Babur's religious language be analysed?
**Definition (learn):** Separate five things: private or devotional expression; public victory rhetoric; diplomacy and alliance; campaign violence; later secular or fanatic memory.
**Breakdown:**
- Private/devotional expression (✅ owner 12 §21).
- Public victory rhetoric (✅ owner 12 §21).
- Diplomacy and alliance (✅ owner 12 §21).
- Campaign violence (✅ owner 12 §21).
- Later secular or fanatic memory (✅ owner 12 §21).
- Sparse temple references do not establish complete tolerance or uniform fanaticism (✅ owner 12 §21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2196, MED-2208.
**Trap:** Babur's religious language is not a single position.
**Mains hook:** Analyse rhetoric alongside territory, coalition, morale and legitimacy.
**Back:** *"Five boxes for faith: private, public, diplomacy, violence, memory."*

### Flashcard MED-2210 — Founder or fragile conqueror: the verdict
**Front:** Was Babur a founder of the Mughal empire?
**Definition (learn):** Babur was a conqueror and dynastic founder of a fragile imperial project, not the creator of the fully consolidated Mughal empire later associated with Akbar.
**Breakdown:**
- Founder case: durable dynastic claim; Delhi-Agra-Kabul axis; three major campaigns; decision to remain; combined-arms repertoire; literary model (✅ owner 12 §22).
- Fragility case: 1526–30 north Indian rule; eastern Afghans; no standardised administration; Humayun's loss (✅ owner 12 §22).
- Sultanate revenue and Timurid practices continued selectively (✅ owner 12 §22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1526–30 (✅ owner 12 §22).
**Links:** MED-2194, MED-2211, MED-2212.
**Trap:** "Babur created the Mughal empire in full" is wrong (✅ owner 12 §22).
**Mains hook:** Conqueror-founder debate with a graded verdict.
**Back:** *"Founder, yes; consolidator, no: fragile for four years."*

### Flashcard MED-2211 — Babur's death (30 December 1530, Agra) and Humayun's succession
**Front:** When and where did Babur die, and who succeeded him?
**Definition (learn):** Babur died at Agra on 30 December 1530; Humayun succeeded.
**Breakdown:**
- Death 30 December 1530 at Agra (✅ adv. 12 mini-timeline).
- Humayun succeeded (✅ owner 12 §22).
- The succession struggle beyond the transition belongs to Topic 13 (✅ owner 12 §22).
- Succession did not prove complete consolidation (✅ owner 12 §23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 30 December 1530 (✅ adv. 12; basic owner gives 1530 only).
**Links:** MED-2210, MED-2212.
**Trap:** 1530 succession ≠ complete consolidation (✅ owner 12 §23).
**Mains hook:** Succession as a test of institutional strength.
**Back:** *"Thirty December 1530: Babur dies in Agra; Humayun inherits a fragile state."*

### Flashcard MED-2212 — PYQ routes: zero direct PYQs for Babur; adjacent routes
**Front:** Which PYQs are routed to this topic and which belong elsewhere?
**Definition (learn):** The repository routes zero direct UPSC CSE PYQs to Topic 12 for 2018–2026. The 2024 Bhatkal question belongs to Topic 09; the 2025 Akbar religious question belongs to Topic 17.
**Breakdown:**
- Zero direct UPSC CSE PYQs (✅ owner 12 §23).
- 2024 Bhatkal: Topic 09 (MED-2052) (✅ owner 12 §23).
- 2025 Akbar religious question: Topic 17 (outside this fragment) (✅ owner 12 §23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2052, MED-2210.
**Trap:** Do not invent a Babur-owned PYQ (✅ owner 12 §23).
**Mains hook:** Use the Babur material for Mains answers rather than invented PYQs.
**Back:** *"No Babur PYQ: Bhatkal is Vijayanagara's, Akbar's religion is later."*

### Flashcard MED-2213 — Babur's chronology ladder: 1504, 1526, 1527, 1528, 1529
**Front:** What is the exam-safe Babur chronology?
**Definition (learn):** Kabul 1504; Panipat 1526; Khanwa 1527; Chanderi 1528; Ghaghra 1529; death 1530.
**Breakdown:**
- 1504 Kabul (✅ owner 12 §23).
- 1526 Panipat (✅ owner 12 §23).
- 1527 Khanwa (✅ owner 12 §23).
- 1528 Chanderi (✅ owner 12 §23).
- 1529 Ghaghra (✅ owner 12 §23).
- 1530 death (✅ owner 12 §23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed.
**Links:** MED-2182, MED-2188, MED-2195, MED-2197, MED-2198, MED-2211.
**Trap:** Panipat's day (20 or 21 April) must be qualified (✅ owner 12 §23).
**Mains hook:** The ladder converts into a cause-effect narrative.
**Back:** *"Four-oh-four, Twenty-six, Twenty-seven, Twenty-eight, Twenty-nine, thirty."*

### Flashcard MED-2214 — Mains thesis: Babur's Central Asia and Panipat (10/15/20 marks)
**Front:** How should a Babur answer be built at 10, 15 and 20 marks?
**Definition (learn):** Executable scaffolds from the owner's answer architecture.
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks: Central Asian necessity + Panipat + the staying decision; conclusion: strategic, not adventurist (✅ owner 12 §8.4).
- 15 marks: add Khanwa and Daulat Khan / Alam Khan; conclusion: conquest joined external pressure (✅ owner 12 §8.4).
- 20 marks: five-unit integration; compare with Mahmud and Muizzuddin; "gunpowder empire" note (✅ owner 12 §8.4).
- Thesis: "strategic choice, not destiny" (✅ owner 12 §8.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 10 marks/150 words; 15 and 20 marks/250 words (✅ owner 12 §24).
**Links:** MED-2175, MED-2188, MED-2195, MED-2205, MED-2210.
**Trap:** Avoid a Panipat-only essay.
**Mains hook:** Build the thesis through the narrowing-options argument.
**Back:** *"Ten: necessity and Panipat. Fifteen: add Khanwa. Twenty: add the verdict."*

### Flashcard MED-2215 — Advanced: Punjab as the trigger for Panipat
**Front:** Why was Panipat not a sudden invasion?
**Definition (learn):** Panipat was the culmination of a longer contest over Punjab: Babur's claims, Daulat Khan's oscillation, Alam Khan's ambitions and Ibrahim Lodi's refusal to surrender Punjab.
**Breakdown:**
- Babur's claims expanded from frontier tracts to the whole Punjab (✅ adv. 12 §2).
- Daulat Khan's oscillation (✅ adv. 12 §1).
- Alam Khan's ambitions (✅ adv. 12 §1).
- Ibrahim's refusal to surrender Punjab (✅ adv. 12 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2185, MED-2186, MED-2175.
**Trap:** Avoid the "sudden invasion" simplification (✅ adv. 12 §2).
**Mains hook:** The Lodi decline and Punjab as an advanced trigger.
**Back:** *"Punjab first, Panipat second: a contest, not a surprise."*

### Flashcard MED-2216 — Advanced: tulughma and cavalry as important as artillery
**Front:** Why does Satish Chandra warn against reducing Panipat to guns?
**Definition (learn):** Babur's tulughma flanking parties and mounted archery were as important as artillery; the advanced owner warns against a guns-only explanation.
**Breakdown:**
- Tulughma flanking and mounted archery (✅ adv. 12 §1).
- Artillery was one element (✅ adv. 12 §1).
- The combined effect of carts, guns, cavalry, bowmen and flanking (✅ owner 12 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2189, MED-2190.
**Trap:** "Artillery alone won Panipat" is wrong (✅ owner 12 §5).
**Mains hook:** Generalship, organisation and tactics, not a single technological cause (✅ adv. 12 §5).
**Back:** *"Guns plus flanks plus archers: the whole field won Panipat."*

### Flashcard MED-2217 — Advanced: the Baburnama as self-representation
**Front:** How should the Baburnama's vivid detail be used with caution?
**Definition (learn):** The memoir is unusually vivid but still a ruler's self-representation; pair military details with caution on exaggerated army figures.
**Breakdown:**
- Unusually vivid (✅ adv. 12 historiography note).
- Ruler's self-representation (✅ adv. 12 historiography note).
- Army figures exaggerated (✅ adv. 12 historiography note).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2199, MED-2188.
**Trap:** Do not quote army totals from the memoir as audited statistics (✅ owner 12 §15).
**Mains hook:** Source limits in military history.
**Back:** *"Vivid memoir, self-portrait; numbers need a second witness."*

### Flashcard MED-2218 — Advanced: restoring monarchy's prestige against Afghan equality
**Front:** How did Babur restore the prestige of monarchy against Afghan tribal ideas?
**Definition (learn):** Babur restored the prestige of monarchy against Afghan tribal ideas of equality; his Timurid-Chingizid prestige prevented begs from claiming equality.
**Breakdown:**
- Afghan tribal ideas of equality (✅ adv. 12 §1).
- Timurid-Chingizid prestige (✅ adv. 12 §1).
- Begs could not claim equality (✅ adv. 12 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2206, MED-2204.
**Trap:** Afghan equality was not one fixed system (✅ owner 12 §19).
**Mains hook:** Monarchy's prestige as statecraft.
**Back:** *"Timur and Chingiz outrank tribal equality: monarchy restored."*

### Flashcard MED-2219 — Advanced: Babur's state as bridge
**Front:** What does "Babur's state as a bridge" mean?
**Definition (learn):** Central Asian court culture combined with Indian territorial resources and revenue, scribal and local military practices.
**Breakdown:**
- Central Asian court culture (✅ adv. 12 §1).
- Indian territorial resources (✅ adv. 12 §1).
- Absorbed Indian revenue, scribal and local military practices (✅ owner 12 §19).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2175, MED-2206.
**Trap:** The bridge was negotiated, not seamless (✅ owner 12 §16).
**Mains hook:** Babur's state as a hybrid.
**Back:** *"Central Asian court on Indian land: a bridge, not a copy."*

### GROUP — 13 Struggle for Empire: Humayun & the Afghans

### Flashcard MED-2220 — Humayun's inherited unfinished empire
**Front:** What did Babur leave Humayun in 1530?
**Definition (learn):** An unfinished empire with weak finances, unconsolidated administration, unsubdued Afghans and the Timurid problem of brothers claiming shares.
**Breakdown:**
- Weak finances after conquest rewards (✅ owner 13 §11).
- Uneven Delhi–Agra territorial control (✅ owner 13 §11).
- Unconquered eastern Afghan networks (✅ owner 13 §11).
- A small Central Asian/Mughal ruling group without deep provincial roots (✅ owner 13 §11).
- Timurid expectations that brothers and kinsmen receive territories and commands (✅ owner 13 §11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** December 1530 (✅ owner 13 §11). Adv.: 30 December 1530 (✅ adv. 13 mini-timeline).
**Links:** MED-2221, MED-2222, MED-2237, MED-2254.
**Trap:** Humayun's failure was not only personal; it was structural (✅ owner 13 §8.1).
**Mains hook:** "Humayun inherited an unfinished empire" — his failures were structural as much as personal.
**Back:** *"Thirty: a fragile inheritance, broke, divided, unfinished."*

### Flashcard MED-2221 — Humayun's accession at age 23
**Front:** When did Humayun succeed Babur and at what age?
**Definition (learn):** Humayun succeeded Babur in December 1530 at age 23.
**Breakdown:**
- Age 23 (✅ owner 13 mini-timeline).
- December 1530; 30 December 1530 in the advanced owner (✅ owner 13 §4; adv. 13 mini-timeline).
- Babur died at Agra on 30 December 1530 (✅ adv. 12 mini-timeline).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1530 (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** MED-2220, MED-2211.
**Trap:** Do not confuse Humayun's accession with his Delhi restoration in 1555.
**Mains hook:** Accession as the start of the test.
**Back:** *"Thirty, December: twenty-three, Humayun takes the throne."*

### Flashcard MED-2222 — Kamran: Lahore and Multan
**Front:** What did Kamran hold, and why did Humayun accept it?
**Definition (learn):** Kamran occupied Lahore and Multan; Humayun accepted the arrangement to avoid civil war and focus east.
**Breakdown:**
- Kamran's Punjab/Lahore–Multan power reduced Humayun's recruitment and revenue base (✅ owner 13 §11).
- Humayun accepted to avoid civil war (✅ owner 13 §8.2 unit 1).
- Humayun's tolerance may also reflect political calculation (✅ owner 13 §8.2).
- Kamran's non-cooperation mattered especially after Chausa (✅ adv. 13 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2223, MED-2257, MED-2241.
**Trap:** "Kamran caused the entire failure from the start" is wrong (✅ adv. 13 §5).
**Mains hook:** Timurid succession norms weakened unified command (✅ owner 13 §8.2 unit 1).
**Back:** *"Kamran holds Lahore and Multan: Humayun accepts to avoid a civil war."*

### Flashcard MED-2223 — Askari and Hindal: delegated command and fraternal uncertainty
**Front:** What roles did Askari and Hindal play?
**Definition (learn):** Askari and Hindal alternated between delegated command, rivalry and cooperation; Hindal's actions around Agra worsened the rear.
**Breakdown:**
- Askari and Hindal alternated between delegated command, rivalry and cooperation (✅ owner 13 §11).
- Askari's position became untenable after the Gujarat campaign (✅ owner 13 §14).
- Hindal's actions around Agra and fraternal uncertainty worsened the rear during the Bengal campaign (✅ owner 13 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2222, MED-2228, MED-2233.
**Trap:** Do not turn each brother into a timeless villain (✅ owner 13 §11).
**Mains hook:** Brothers as one factor among several.
**Back:** *"Askari commands, Hindal frets, Kamran holds: brothers, not villains."*

### Flashcard MED-2224 — Dadrah / Daurah and the Chunar siege (1531 or 1532)
**Front:** What happened at Dadrah and why is its date disputed?
**Definition (learn):** Humayun defeated Afghans at Dadrah/Daurah and then besieged Chunar. The date is 1531 or 1532 across accounts; Nizamuddin Ahmad places Dadrah in 1531.
**Breakdown:**
- Humayun defeated Afghans at Dadrah (✅ owner 13 §1).
- Besieged Chunar, the gateway to eastern India (✅ owner 13 §1).
- Dadrah/Daurah dated 1531 or 1532 across accounts (✅ owner 13 §13).
- Adv.: the reconstruction used by Chandra places the decisive encounter late in 1532 (✅ adv. 13 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1531/1532 (⚠️ source disagreement).
**Links:** MED-2225, MED-2220.
**Trap:** Do not manufacture precision; state the secure sequence (✅ owner 13 §10).
**Mains hook:** Strategic interpretation is firmer than exact dating (⚠️ owner 13 §1).
**Back:** *"Dadrah and Chunar: thirty-one or thirty-two, the sequence matters more."*

### Flashcard MED-2225 — Chunar: gateway fort, time and route
**Front:** Why was Chunar strategically important?
**Definition (learn):** Chunar controlled the land and river communications between Agra–Allahabad and Bihar/Bengal, the gateway of eastern India.
**Breakdown:**
- Land and river route control between Agra and the east (✅ owner 13 §2).
- Held by Sher Khan; called a gateway of eastern India (✅ owner 13 §4).
- Six-month siege in 1537–38 (✅ owner 13 §13; basic mini-timeline "Chunar takes six months").
- Time, route, river and communications (✅ owner 13 §13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Six months (✅ owner 13 §8.2 unit 2, reported duration).
**Links:** MED-2224, MED-2230, MED-2233.
**Trap:** Chunar was not merely a fort; it consumed campaign time (✅ owner 13 §13).
**Mains hook:** Strategic forts shaped Gangetic logistics (✅ owner 13 §8.2).
**Back:** *"Chunar is the eastern gate, and the siege costs six months."*

### Flashcard MED-2226 — Malwa and Gujarat campaign (1535): Mandu, Champaner and Ahmadabad
**Front:** What did Humayun take in 1535 and why was it not a durable conquest?
**Definition (learn):** Humayun took Mandu, Champaner and Ahmadabad in the Malwa–Gujarat campaign of 1535; the occupation lacked durable administration.
**Breakdown:**
- 1535: Malwa-Gujarat campaign; Mandu, Champaner and Ahmadabad taken (✅ owner 13 mini-timeline; adv. 13 mini-timeline).
- Demonstrated Mughal operational capacity (✅ owner 13 §14).
- Ahmedabad/Gujarat occupation lacked durable local administration, supply and provincial personnel (✅ owner 13 §14).
- Campaign military success but administrative failure (✅ owner 13 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1535 (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** MED-2227, MED-2228, MED-2010, MED-2013.
**Trap:** "The Gujarat campaign achieved nothing" is wrong; it removed the major threat from Bahadur Shah (✅ owner 13 §5).
**Mains hook:** Gujarat as a test of early Mughal administrative capacity (✅ adv. 13 §7).
**Back:** *"Thirty-five: Mandu and Champaner fall; Ahmedabad slips away."*

### Flashcard MED-2227 — Bahadur Shah of Gujarat and his death near Diu
**Front:** Who was Humayun's western rival and how did Bahadur Shah end?
**Definition (learn):** Bahadur Shah of Gujarat absorbed Malwa, pressured Mewar and used artillery and Portuguese relations; he was killed in a scuffle with the Portuguese after the Gujarat episode.
**Breakdown:**
- Absorbed Malwa; pressured Mewar/Chittor (✅ owner 13 §14).
- Artillery and west-coast/Portuguese relations (✅ owner 13 §14).
- Killed in a scuffle with the Portuguese near Diu (✅ owner 13 §4, §14).
- Bassein and Diu concession (MED-2011) (✅ owner 08 §12.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1526–37 reign (✅ owner 08).
**Links:** MED-2011, MED-2226, MED-2228.
**Trap:** Humayun faced a genuine western rival, not a diversion invented by personality (✅ owner 13 §14).
**Mains hook:** Gujarat as a western theatre.
**Back:** *"Bahadur Shah falls near Diu after Humayun's western campaign."*

### Flashcard MED-2228 — Gujarat campaign logic: manoeuvre, Mandu, Ahmedabad, Askari
**Front:** What were the five stages of the Gujarat campaign?
**Definition (learn):** Humayun moved while Bahadur was engaged around Chittor; manoeuvre and supply pressure around Mandsaur weakened Bahadur; Mandu and Champaner showed capacity; the Ahmedabad occupation lacked durable administration; Askari's position became untenable and Bahadur recovered territory.
**Breakdown:**
- Humayun moved while Bahadur was engaged around Chittor (✅ owner 13 §14).
- Manoeuvre and supply around Mandsaur (✅ owner 13 §14).
- Mandu and Champaner demonstrated operational capacity (✅ owner 13 §14).
- Ahmedabad lacked administration, supply and personnel (✅ owner 13 §14).
- Askari's position untenable; Bahadur recovered territory (✅ owner 13 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1535 (✅ owner 13 §14).
**Links:** MED-2226, MED-2227, MED-2223.
**Trap:** Military success was not administrative success (✅ owner 13 §14).
**Mains hook:** Ambition outrunning logistics (✅ owner 13 §8.1).
**Back:** *"Manoeuvre wins, administration loses: Gujarat in five steps."*

### Flashcard MED-2229 — Din Panah (Dinpanah): Humayun's Delhi capital project
**Front:** What was Din Panah and why is it a trap?
**Definition (learn):** Din Panah ("refuge of religion/faith") was an imperial-capital project in Delhi's Purana Qila zone, built by Humayun in 1533–34.
**Breakdown:**
- Built by Humayun at Delhi, 1533–34 (✅ adv. 13 mini-timeline).
- "Refuge of religion" (✅ owner 13 §22).
- Purana Qila fabric is multi-phase; one-patron attribution is unsafe (✅ owner 13 §22).
- Delhi's prestige made a new capital politically intelligible (✅ adv. 13 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1533–34 (✅ adv. 13 mini-timeline).
**Links:** MED-2259 (trap), MED-2246.
**Trap:** Dinpanah = Humayun; Purana Qila is multi-phase (✅ owner 13 §25).
**Mains hook:** A capital as legitimacy and an unrealised ambition.
**Back:** *"Din Panah, refuge of faith, Humayun's Delhi, Purana Qila's many phases."*

### Flashcard MED-2230 — Sher Khan's rise: from Bihar to Gaur
**Front:** What stages marked Sher Khan's rise?
**Definition (learn):** Farid/Sher Khan built power through service, local knowledge, Bihar estates, Afghan alliances, forts and conflict with Bengal: Bihar consolidation, Chunar, Surajgarh (1534), Bengal campaigns, Gaur (1538), Rohtas.
**Breakdown:**
- Bihar consolidation: revenue, chiefs and military following (✅ owner 13 §15).
- Chunar: route-fort and bargaining asset (✅ owner 13 §15).
- Surajgarh, 1534: victory over Bengal forces (✅ owner 13 §15).
- Bengal campaigns: tribute then territorial pressure (✅ owner 13 §15).
- Gaur, 1538 (✅ owner 13 §15).
- Rohtas: protected families and cut Mughal return routes (✅ owner 13 §15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1534 Surajgarh; 1538 Gaur (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** MED-2231, MED-2232, MED-2243, MED-2249.
**Trap:** Sher Khan's rise is contingent, not inevitable (✅ owner 13 §15).
**Mains hook:** Afghan leadership as coalition-building (✅ owner 13 §12).
**Back:** *"Bihar, Chunar, Surajgarh, Gaur, Rohtas: the Afghan ladder."*

### Flashcard MED-2231 — Surajgarh (1534): Sher Khan defeats Bengal forces
**Front:** What was the significance of Surajgarh?
**Definition (learn):** Sher Khan's victory over Bengal forces at Surajgarh (1534), which raised his prestige and resources and preceded his Bengal dominance.
**Breakdown:**
- 1534 victory over Bengal forces (✅ owner 13 §15).
- Raised prestige and resources (✅ owner 13 §15).
- Precedes Sher Khan's Bengal dominance (✅ owner 13 §4 trap list; adv. 13 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1534 (✅ owner 13 §15).
**Links:** MED-2230, MED-2232.
**Trap:** Surajgarh precedes Bengal dominance; do not place it after Gaur (✅ owner 13 §25).
**Mains hook:** A regional victory turned into a larger base.
**Back:** *"Surajgarh 1534: Sher Khan beats Bengal, then reaches for Gaur."*

### Flashcard MED-2232 — Gaur (1538): Sher Khan takes Bengal's capital
**Front:** What did Sher Khan do when he took Gaur?
**Definition (learn):** Sher Khan took Gaur, Bengal's capital, in 1538, removing treasure and resources strategically and avoiding being trapped by Humayun.
**Breakdown:**
- 1538, Gaur captured (✅ owner 13 mini-timeline; §15).
- Removed treasure and resources; avoided entrapment (✅ owner 13 §15).
- Evacuated and stripped Gaur strategically; targeted communications (✅ owner 13 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1538 (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** MED-2230, MED-2233.
**Trap:** Sher Khan captured Gaur; Humayun's occupation did not create revenue control (✅ owner 13 §16).
**Mains hook:** Capture is not governance.
**Back:** *"Gaur 1538: the capital taken, the treasure gone, the trap avoided."*

### Flashcard MED-2233 — Humayun in Bengal (Oct 1538–Jan 1539): winning a capital is not governing it
**Front:** Why did Humayun's Bengal campaign fail to produce control?
**Definition (learn):** Humayun's Bengal objective was imperial authority over a wealthy region; the long Chunar delay, Gaur's stripping, monsoon, rivers, distance and fraternal uncertainty isolated the Mughal army.
**Breakdown:**
- Humayun at Gaur/Bengal Oct 1538–Jan 1539 (✅ adv. 13 mini-timeline).
- Chunar delay gave Sher Khan initiative (✅ owner 13 §16).
- Monsoon, rivers, distance, fodder and supply complicated occupation (✅ owner 13 §16).
- Humayun's stay at Gaur did not create revenue or administrative control (✅ owner 13 §16).
- The army became isolated from Delhi–Agra (✅ owner 13 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Oct 1538–Jan 1539 (✅ adv. 13 mini-timeline).
**Links:** MED-2225, MED-2232, MED-2234.
**Trap:** "Humayun ignored Sher Khan from the beginning" is wrong; he misread Sher Khan's strength (✅ owner 13 §5).
**Mains hook:** Bengal as a test of state capacity (✅ adv. 13 §7).
**Back:** *"Bengal conquered for a season, never governed."*

### Flashcard MED-2234 — Chausa (26 June 1539): Humayun's surprise defeat
**Front:** What happened at Chausa and what did it mean?
**Definition (learn):** At Chausa (26 June 1539), Sher Khan surprised Humayun, who barely escaped; Humayun had placed the river Karmnasa at his back.
**Breakdown:**
- 26 June 1539 (✅ owner 13 §17; adv. 13 §4).
- Exposed camp, river position, monsoon and broken communications (✅ owner 13 §17; §8.2 unit 3).
- Humayun placed the river Karmnasa at his back (✅ adv. 13 §1).
- Chausa weakened the regime; Kanauj expelled it (✅ owner 13 §25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 26 June 1539 (✅ owner 13; adv. 13).
**Links:** MED-2235, MED-2236, MED-2237.
**Trap:** Do not reduce Chausa to one river error (✅ owner 13 §17).
**Mains hook:** Generalship, logistics and contingency (✅ owner 13 §8.2 unit 3).
**Back:** *"Chausa 1539: river at his back, surprise in front; Humayun escapes."*

### Flashcard MED-2235 — Kanauj / Bilgram (17 May 1540): Mughal expulsion
**Front:** What did the Kanauj (Bilgram) battle decide?
**Definition (learn):** The larger set battle of 17 May 1540 at Kanauj/Bilgram, where weak cohesion, command and reinforcement led to decisive Mughal expulsion from north India.
**Breakdown:**
- 17 May 1540 (✅ owner 13 §17; adv. 13 mini-timeline).
- Larger set battle; weak cohesion, command and reinforcement (✅ owner 13 §17).
- Kanauj was bitterly fought; Humayun's hastily assembled army lacked Kamran's hardened troops (✅ adv. 13 §1).
- Humayun lost north India (✅ owner 13 mini-timeline).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 17 May 1540 (✅ owner 13 §17; adv. 13).
**Links:** MED-2234, MED-2236, MED-2237.
**Trap:** Do not reduce Kanauj to Kamran alone (✅ owner 13 §17).
**Mains hook:** Field defeat became regime collapse because supports were weak (✅ owner 13 §8.2 unit 4).
**Back:** *"Kanauj 1540: the set battle, the Mughal exit from north India."*

### Flashcard MED-2236 — Chausa vs Kanauj: the decisive contrast
**Front:** What is the difference between Chausa and Kanauj?
**Definition (learn):** Chausa weakened Humayun; Kanauj expelled him. Chausa was a surprise on an exposed camp; Kanauj was a larger set battle.
**Breakdown:**
- Chausa weakened the regime (✅ owner 13 §25).
- Kanauj expelled it (✅ owner 13 §25).
- Chausa: surprise and exposed position; Kanauj: set battle and cohesion (✅ owner 13 §17).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1539; 1540 (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** MED-2234, MED-2235.
**Trap:** "Chausa alone ended Mughal power" is wrong; Kanauj decided it (✅ owner 13 §5).
**Mains hook:** Two battles, two different effects.
**Back:** *"Chausa wounds, Kanauj expels: thirty-nine then forty."*

### Flashcard MED-2237 — Causes of defeat: weighted matrix
**Front:** Which factors weighed most in Humayun's defeat?
**Definition (learn):** Inherited fragility; strategic overreach; Afghan alternative; brothers and nobles; logistics; operational decisions; contingency; personality, weighed together.
**Breakdown:**
- Inherited fragility: unfinished Babur conquest (✅ owner 13 §18).
- Strategic overreach: Gujarat and Bengal exceeded follow-up (✅ owner 13 §18).
- Afghan alternative: Sher Khan linked chiefs, forts, revenue and Bengal (✅ owner 13 §18).
- Brothers and nobles: fragmented recruitment and command (✅ owner 13 §18).
- Logistics: rivers, monsoon, distance, food and fodder (✅ owner 13 §18).
- Operational decisions: Chunar delays, Gaur occupation (✅ owner 13 §18).
- Contingency: surprise, weather, defections (✅ owner 13 §18).
- Personality: real choices, but not luck or opium as total cause (✅ owner 13 §18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2220, MED-2234, MED-2235, MED-2253.
**Trap:** "Humayun failed because of opium or laziness" is not the owner's explanation (✅ owner 13 §8.3; adv. 13 §5).
**Mains hook:** Use a weighted matrix in a 15 or 20-mark answer.
**Back:** *"Fragile inheritance, overreach, Afghan alternative, brothers, logistics, timing, personality: weighed."*

### Flashcard MED-2238 — Afghan networks: Lodi remnants, Mahmud Lodi and chiefs
**Front:** What kind of force were the Afghans in the Ganga valley?
**Definition (learn):** Afghan political society survived the Lodi defeat: chiefs, soldiers, families, forts and revenue ties across east UP, Bihar and Bengal.
**Breakdown:**
- Chiefs, soldiers, families, forts and revenue ties (✅ owner 13 §12).
- Mahmud Lodi remained a claimant and symbol (✅ owner 13 §12; §8.2).
- Afghan chiefs did not act as one permanent tribe; they could compete, submit or unite under a leader (✅ owner 13 §12).
- Sher Khan's achievement was coalition and state-building, not inevitable ethnic unity (✅ owner 13 §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2230, MED-2239, MED-2243.
**Trap:** "Afghan" is not one permanently cohesive bloc (✅ owner 13 §8.1).
**Mains hook:** Discuss the Afghan challenge as social-military, not dynastic rivalry (✅ owner 13 §7).
**Back:** *"Afghans compete, submit, or unite: never one tribe."*

### Flashcard MED-2239 — Rajput polities after Rana Sanga and the Karnavati-rakhi story
**Front:** What was the Rajput political field after Sanga, and what is the Karnavati-rakhi caution?
**Definition (learn):** Mewar faced succession and court conflict after Rana Sanga; Chittor, Marwar and other houses pursued distinct interests. The Karnavati-rakhi story is a later tradition, not a secure diplomatic document.
**Breakdown:**
- Mewar after Rana Sanga: succession and court conflict (✅ owner 13 §12).
- Rajput houses pursued distinct interests (✅ owner 13 §12).
- Rajput rulers could resist Gujarat, negotiate with Mughals, aid exiles or pursue regional rivalries (✅ owner 13 §12).
- The Karnavati-rakhi story is later tradition (✅ owner 13 §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2195, MED-2227, MED-2238.
**Trap:** "Rajput bloc" is not an adequate explanation (✅ owner 13 §12).
**Mains hook:** Coalitions as changing networks, not fixed civilisational camps (✅ owner 13 §9).
**Back:** *"Mewar fractures after Sanga; the rakhi is legend, not a treaty."*

### Flashcard MED-2240 — Exile: Punjab, Sindh, Amarkot and Akbar's birth (1542)
**Front:** Where was Humayun in exile and where was Akbar born?
**Definition (learn):** After 1540 Humayun moved through Punjab and Sindh, negotiating with regional rulers; Akbar was born at Amarkot (Umarkot) in 1542 under local protection.
**Breakdown:**
- Exile through Punjab and Sindh; difficult negotiations (✅ owner 13 §19).
- Marwar/Rajasthan and Sindh actors followed their own interests (✅ owner 13 §19).
- Akbar born at Amarkot/Umarkot in 1542 (✅ owner 13 §19).
- Household women, kin, servants and patrons sustained continuity (✅ owner 13 §19).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1542 (✅ owner 13 §19).
**Links:** MED-2241, MED-2252.
**Trap:** Exile routes were not a single refuge corridor (✅ owner 13 §19).
**Mains hook:** Household networks sustaining dynastic continuity.
**Back:** *"Forty-two: Akbar born in Amarkot, in exile."*

### Flashcard MED-2241 — Shah Tahmasp, Qandahar (1545) and Kabul
**Front:** How did Humayun recover Qandahar and Kabul?
**Definition (learn):** Humayun reached the Safavid court of Shah Tahmasp and sought military aid; with Safavid help he recovered Qandahar in 1545 and contested Kabul with Kamran.
**Breakdown:**
- Reached Shah Tahmasp's court and sought aid (✅ owner 13 §19).
- Qandahar recovered in 1545 (✅ owner 13 §20).
- Contested Kabul with Kamran (✅ owner 13 §20).
- Qandahar was transferred or retained through negotiation and force (✅ owner 13 §20).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1545 (✅ owner 13 mini-timeline; §20).
**Links:** MED-2242, MED-2222, MED-2257.
**Trap:** The exact Qandahar bargain must be source-cited (✅ owner 13 §20).
**Mains hook:** External alliance compensated for exile weakness (✅ owner 13 §8.2 unit 5).
**Back:** *"Forty-five: Tahmasp's aid retakes Qandahar; Kabul is contested."*

### Flashcard MED-2242 — Safavid assistance: ceremony, Shia conformity and the "Persianised Mughals" caution
**Front:** Did Safavid aid Persianise the Mughals?
**Definition (learn):** Safavid assistance involved ceremony, religious presentation and conditions; accounts of Shia conformity are diplomatic or source claims, not proof of total conversion or Persianisation.
**Breakdown:**
- Ceremony, religious presentation and the Qandahar question (✅ owner 13 §19).
- Accounts of Shia conformity and conditions treated as source claims (✅ owner 13 §19).
- Artists and practices associated with Iran later contributed to Mughal culture (✅ owner 13 §20).
- "Safavid aid Persianized the Mughals" is a false single-import story (✅ owner 13 §20).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2241, MED-2220.
**Trap:** Single-import explanation is wrong (✅ owner 13 §20).
**Mains hook:** Persianate culture already formed part of Mughal inheritance (✅ owner 13 §20).
**Back:** *"Iranian help, Persian culture, not a single import: the false story."*

### Flashcard MED-2243 — Sur fragmentation: Islam Shah, Adil, Sikandar and Ibrahim
**Front:** Who were the claimants after Sher Shah's death, and what does the Sur disintegration explain?
**Definition (learn):** Sher Shah died in 1545; Islam Shah tried to maintain control; succession conflict followed with the rival claims of Adil Shah, Sikandar Sur and Ibrahim Sur, fragmenting Afghan command and territory.
**Breakdown:**
- Sher Shah's death 1545 as transition point (✅ owner 13 §21).
- Islam Shah's attempt to maintain control (✅ owner 13 §21).
- Rival claims of Adil Shah, Sikandar Sur and Ibrahim Sur (✅ owner 13 §21).
- Fragmentation of Afghan command and territory (✅ owner 13 §21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1545 (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** MED-2244, MED-2230.
**Trap:** Sur administration belongs to Topic 14, not here (✅ owner 13 §21).
**Mains hook:** Sur disintegration created the window for Humayun's return.
**Back:** *"Forty-five: Sher dies, Islam tries, Adil, Sikandar, Ibrahim fight."*

### Flashcard MED-2244 — Reconquest 1555: Machhiwara, Sirhind, Bairam Khan and Delhi
**Front:** How did Humayun recover Delhi in 1555?
**Definition (learn):** Humayun returned through Punjab as Sur rivals weakened one another; Mughal forces, with Bairam Khan prominent, won at Machhiwara and Sirhind, displacing Sikandar Sur, and Delhi was recovered in 1555.
**Breakdown:**
- Return through Punjab as Sur rivals weakened (✅ owner 13 §21).
- Lahore and the Punjab route recovered (✅ owner 13 §21).
- Machhiwara and Sirhind won, with Bairam Khan prominent (✅ owner 13 §21).
- Sikandar Sur displaced (✅ owner 13 §21).
- Delhi recovered in 1555 (✅ owner 13 mini-timeline).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1555 (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** MED-2243, MED-2245.
**Trap:** Machhiwara/Sirhind and Bairam Khan belong to the reconquest (✅ owner 13 §25).
**Mains hook:** Restored dynastic sovereignty was not completed consolidation (✅ owner 13 §21).
**Back:** *"Fifty-five: Machhiwara, Sirhind, Delhi back: restoration, not consolidation."*

### Flashcard MED-2245 — Humayun's death (1556) and Akbar's succession
**Front:** How and when did Humayun die, and who succeeded him?
**Definition (learn):** Humayun died in 1556 after a fall associated with his Delhi library building; the young Akbar succeeded with Bairam Khan central.
**Breakdown:**
- Death 1556 after a fall from his library (✅ owner 13 §24; basic mini-timeline).
- The exact anecdote is chronicle memory (✅ owner 13 §24).
- Akbar succeeds; Bairam Khan central (✅ owner 13 §24; adv. 13 mini-timeline).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1556 (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** MED-2244, MED-2246, MED-2254.
**Trap:** Humayun did not "never recover" — he recovered Delhi in 1555 (✅ owner 13 §5).
**Mains hook:** Continuity into Akbar real but not inevitable (✅ owner 13 §24).
**Back:** *"Fifty-six: the library fall; Akbar with Bairam, a restored but young regime."*

### Flashcard MED-2246 — Humayun's Tomb: built in the 1560s, not a campaign narrative
**Front:** When was Humayun's Tomb built and what can it not prove?
**Definition (learn):** Humayun's Tomb was built in the 1560s under Akbar-era patronage with Persian and Indian craftsmen; it cannot narrate Humayun's campaigns.
**Breakdown:**
- Built in the 1560s under Akbar-era patronage (✅ owner 13 §10, §23).
- Persian and Indian craftsmen (✅ owner 13 §23).
- Belongs here only as later dynastic memory; full architecture is a later owner (✅ owner 13 §23).
- Humayun's Tomb post-dates Humayun and cannot narrate campaigns (✅ owner 13 §25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1560s (✅ owner 13).
**Links:** MED-2229, MED-2245.
**Trap:** Tomb ≠ campaign record (✅ owner 13 §25).
**Mains hook:** Dynastic memory through monuments.
**Back:** *"Humayun's Tomb: built after him, remembers him, cannot fight for him."*

### Flashcard MED-2247 — Gulbadan Begum and the Humayun-nama
**Front:** Who wrote the Humayun-nama and what can it and can't it do?
**Definition (learn):** Gulbadan Begum, a Timurid princess and participant-observer, wrote the Humayun-nama, illuminating kinship, travel, conflict, reconciliation and women.
**Breakdown:**
- Timurid princess and participant-observer (✅ owner 13 §23).
- Written later under Akbar (✅ owner 13 §10).
- Selective, retrospective, household-centred memoir (✅ owner 13 §23).
- Not a neutral comprehensive biography (✅ owner 13 §23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2248, MED-2252.
**Trap:** Do not use the memoir as a complete biography.
**Mains hook:** Women and household politics as sources.
**Back:** *"Gulbadan records the household, not the whole reign."*

### Flashcard MED-2248 — Abu'l Fazl's Akbarnama: official chronology
**Front:** What is the Akbarnama's use and limit?
**Definition (learn):** Abu'l Fazl's official dynastic chronology covering restoration and the Akbar succession frame; it carries imperial teleology and patronage.
**Breakdown:**
- Official dynastic chronology; restoration and Akbar succession frame (✅ owner 13 §10).
- Imperial teleology and patronage (✅ owner 13 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2247, MED-2249.
**Trap:** The Akbarnama's teleology is a limit, not a neutral chronicle.
**Mains hook:** Source criticism for official histories.
**Back:** *"Abu'l Fazl writes Akbar's history: official, teleological."*

### Flashcard MED-2249 — Haidar Dughlat's Tarikh-i Rashidi and Central Asian relations
**Front:** What does Mirza Haidar Dughlat's Tarikh-i Rashidi offer and what must be discounted?
**Definition (learn):** The Tarikh-i Rashidi is a Timurid/Mughal Central Asian source on relationships and political culture, with the author's position, region and loyalties.
**Breakdown:**
- Timurid/Mughal Central Asian relationships and political culture (✅ owner 13 §10).
- Author's position, region and personal loyalties (✅ owner 13 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2248, MED-2250.
**Trap:** The author's loyalties must be checked against the claims.
**Mains hook:** Central Asian context through a regional author.
**Back:** *"Dughlat's Rashidi: Central Asian lens, author's loyalties."*

### Flashcard MED-2250 — Nizamuddin Ahmad's Tabaqat-i Akbari and the Dadrah date
**Front:** What is the Tabaqat-i Akbari's use and where does it conflict with the Chunar sequence?
**Definition (learn):** Nizamuddin Ahmad's Tabaqat-i Akbari is a chronology and administrative-dynastic synthesis, but dates disagree; it places Dadrah in 1531 and omits Chunar from that sequence.
**Breakdown:**
- Chronology and administrative-dynastic synthesis (✅ owner 13 §10).
- Later compilation; date disagreements remain (✅ owner 13 §10).
- Places Dadrah/Daurah in 1531 and omits Chunar from the sequence (✅ adv. 13 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1531 (per Nizamuddin, ✅ adv. 13 §1).
**Links:** MED-2224, MED-2251.
**Trap:** The Tabaqat's Dadrah date differs from the reconstruction (✅ adv. 13 §1).
**Mains hook:** Source conflict requires the minimum secure sequence.
**Back:** *"Nizamuddin's Tabaqat: thirty-one for Dadrah, Chunar left out."*

### Flashcard MED-2251 — Abbas Sarwani's Tarikh-i Sher Shahi
**Front:** What does Abbas Sarwani's Tarikh-i Sher Shahi offer as an Afghan chronicle?
**Definition (learn):** An Afghan chronicle of Sher Khan and Sur memory, Afghan chiefs and campaigns, written under later Mughal patronage.
**Breakdown:**
- Sher Khan and Sur memory; Afghan chiefs and campaigns (✅ owner 13 §10).
- Retrospective and written under later Mughal patronage (✅ owner 13 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2230, MED-2250.
**Trap:** Afghan chronicle ≠ neutral Afghan view.
**Mains hook:** Afghan memory under Mughal patronage.
**Back:** *"Sarwani's Sher Shahi: Afghan memory, Mughal pen."*

### Flashcard MED-2252 — Hamida Banu Begum and dynastic survival
**Front:** What role did Hamida Banu Begum play?
**Definition (learn):** Hamida Banu Begum married Humayun in exile and travelled within the royal household's political network; she was Akbar's mother.
**Breakdown:**
- Married Humayun during exile (✅ owner 13 §19).
- Travelled within the household network (✅ owner 13 §19).
- Akbar's mother; part of dynastic survival (✅ owner 13 §23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2240, MED-2247.
**Trap:** Royal women mediated kinship and refuge (✅ owner 13 §23); not every non-elite woman's life is visible.
**Mains hook:** Royal women and dynastic continuity.
**Back:** *"Hamida in exile: wife, traveller, Akbar's mother."*

### Flashcard MED-2253 — Learned cosmology: astrology and ceremony, not eccentricity
**Front:** How should Humayun's interest in astrology and ceremony be read?
**Definition (learn):** Chroniclers describe his interest in astrology, planetary timing, colour and ceremonial classification; these belong to learned courtly cosmology and political performance, not proof of irrational eccentricity or military failure.
**Breakdown:**
- Astrology, planetary timing, colour and ceremonial classification (✅ owner 13 §22).
- Learned courtly cosmology and political performance (✅ owner 13 §22).
- Not proof of eccentricity or failure (✅ owner 13 §22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2237, MED-2254.
**Trap:** "Humayun failed because of opium or eccentricity" is not a sufficient explanation (✅ owner 13 §18).
**Mains hook:** Cultural history as political performance.
**Back:** *"Stars and colours, not eccentricity: courtly cosmology."*

### Flashcard MED-2254 — Humayun's assessment: failures and capabilities
**Front:** What were Humayun's failures and capabilities?
**Definition (learn):** Failures: overextension, weak provincial settlement, misread Afghan scale, communications loss and uneven coordination. Capabilities: energetic Gujarat operations, persistence in exile, Safavid diplomacy, Kabul recovery and successful reconquest.
**Breakdown:**
- Failures listed (✅ owner 13 §24).
- Capabilities listed (✅ owner 13 §24).
- Verdict: a ruler of a structurally shallow empire who preserved a dynastic network and restored it in 1555 (✅ owner 13 §24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2237, MED-2245.
**Trap:** Humayun was neither wholly incompetent nor absolved (✅ owner 13 §18).
**Mains hook:** Graded verdict.
**Back:** *"Overreach and shallow roots, but exile, Persia and 1555 restored the line."*

### Flashcard MED-2255 — Advanced: Humayun's eastern campaign aimed at Bengal, not rescue
**Front:** Why did Part II say Humayun's eastern campaign was larger than punishing Sher Khan?
**Definition (learn):** The campaign was driven by the ambition to bring Bengal under a north Indian imperial aegis, not only to punish Sher Khan or rescue Bengal's ruler.
**Breakdown:**
- Bengal as the main prize (✅ adv. 13 §2).
- Ambition of north Indian unity (✅ adv. 13 §2).
- Not merely rescue of Bengal's ruler (✅ adv. 13 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2233, MED-2220.
**Trap:** Bengal campaign ≠ rescue mission.
**Mains hook:** Imperial ambition outrunning logistics (✅ owner 13 §8.1).
**Back:** *"Bengal was the prize, not the rescue."*

### Flashcard MED-2256 — Advanced: Sher Khan was an independent ruler by Bengal
**Front:** Why is "Sher Khan was just a rebel jagirdar" a trap?
**Definition (learn):** By the time of Bengal, Sher Khan behaved as an independent ruler; he had virtual control of Bihar and defeated Bengal at Surajgarh.
**Breakdown:**
- Virtual control of Bihar (✅ adv. 13 §1).
- Defeated Bengal at Surajgarh (✅ adv. 13 §1).
- Captured Gaur; no longer a mere subordinate sardar (✅ adv. 13 §1).
- Offered to leave Bihar and pay tribute if Bengal were confirmed to him; Humayun's ambition made compromise unlikely (✅ adv. 13 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2230, MED-2231, MED-2232.
**Trap:** "Rebel jagirdar" understates his state-building (✅ adv. 13 §5).
**Mains hook:** Sher Khan executed the north Indian empire better (✅ adv. 13 §7).
**Back:** *"Sher Khan: no mere jagirdar, an independent ruler by Bengal."*

### Flashcard MED-2257 — Advanced: Kamran's non-cooperation and the Kabul contest
**Front:** Why did Kamran's non-cooperation matter after Chausa?
**Definition (learn):** Kamran's decisive non-cooperation mattered especially after Chausa; his hardened troops were absent at Kanauj.
**Breakdown:**
- Non-cooperation after Chausa (✅ adv. 13 §5).
- Kamran's hardened troops absent at Kanauj (✅ adv. 13 §1).
- Kabul contest with Kamran (✅ owner 13 §20).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2222, MED-2235, MED-2241.
**Trap:** Kamran did not cause the entire failure from the start (✅ adv. 13 §5).
**Mains hook:** Brothers as a later and partial cause.
**Back:** *"Kamran's silence after Chausa: a partial cause, not the whole failure."*

### Flashcard MED-2258 — PYQ routes for Humayun and Afghans
**Front:** Which PYQ is adjacent to this topic and what is the owner's PYQ status?
**Definition (learn):** The repository contains zero direct Topic-13 UPSC CSE PYQs for 2018–2026. The 2023 Prelims Q49 (Gujarat–Portuguese–Diu) is adjacent and belongs to Topic 08 for Bahadur Shah context.
**Breakdown:**
- Zero direct Topic-13 UPSC CSE PYQs (✅ owner 13 §25).
- 2023 Prelims Q49: adjacent, Topic 08 (✅ owner 13 §25; MED-2028).
- Original Humayun/Sher Khan questions are practice only (✅ owner 13 §25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2028, MED-2227.
**Trap:** Do not relabel practice questions as PYQs (✅ owner 13 §25).
**Mains hook:** Use the practice question bank rather than an invented PYQ.
**Back:** *"No Humayun PYQ; Q49 is Gujarat's, not Humayun's."*

### Flashcard MED-2259 — Trap: Dinpanah = Humayun; Purana Qila is multi-phase
**Front:** Why should Purana Qila not be attributed to one patron?
**Definition (learn):** Dinpanah was Humayun's imperial-capital project in the Purana Qila zone, but archaeological phases and later rebuilding make one-patron attribution unsafe.
**Breakdown:**
- Dinpanah = Humayun (✅ owner 13 §25).
- Purana Qila fabric is multi-phase (✅ owner 13 §25).
- Simple one-patron attribution is unsafe (✅ owner 13 §22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2229.
**Trap:** Do not attribute the whole Purana Qila to Humayun.
**Mains hook:** Monument biography as process.
**Back:** *"Din Panah is Humayun's; Purana Qila is many hands."*

### Flashcard MED-2260 — Mains thesis: Humayun's failure and restoration (10/15/20 marks)
**Front:** How should a Humayun answer be built at 10, 15 and 20 marks?
**Definition (learn):** Executable scaffolds from the owner's answer architecture.
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks: structural inheritance + Chausa/Kanauj; conclusion: premature expansion without institutional base (✅ owner 13 §8.4).
- 15 marks: add Kamran, Chunar and Iranian recovery; conclusion: restoration required internal resilience and external alliance (✅ owner 13 §8.4).
- 20 marks: full five units + Afghan social-military analysis + comparison with Akbar (✅ owner 13 §8.4).
- Thesis: structural as much as personal (✅ owner 13 §8.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 10 marks/150 words; 15 and 20 marks/250 words (✅ owner 13 §26).
**Links:** MED-2220, MED-2237, MED-2244, MED-2245.
**Trap:** Avoid a personality-only answer.
**Mains hook:** Premature expansion vs institutional depth.
**Back:** *"Ten: inheritance and two battles. Fifteen: add brothers and Iran. Twenty: the full case."*

### Flashcard MED-2261 — Humayun's chronology ladder: 1530 to 1556
**Front:** What is the exam-safe Humayun ladder?
**Definition (learn):** 1530 accession; 1531–32 Dadrah/Chunar; 1535 Malwa–Gujarat; 1537–38 Chunar siege and east; 1538 Gaur; 1539 Chausa; 1540 Kanauj; 1545 Qandahar and Kabul; 1555 Delhi; 1556 death.
**Breakdown:**
- 1530 accession (✅ owner 13 mini-timeline).
- 1531–32 Dadrah/Chunar phase (⚠️ owner-flagged).
- 1535 Malwa–Gujarat (✅ owner 13 mini-timeline).
- 1537–38 Chunar siege (✅ owner 13 mini-timeline).
- 1538 Gaur captured by Sher Khan (✅ owner 13 mini-timeline).
- 1539 Chausa (✅ owner 13 mini-timeline).
- 1540 Kanauj (✅ owner 13 mini-timeline).
- 1545 Qandahar and Kabul recovered (✅ owner 13 mini-timeline).
- 1555 Delhi recovered (✅ owner 13 mini-timeline).
- 1556 death (✅ owner 13 mini-timeline).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As above.
**Links:** MED-2224, MED-2226, MED-2232, MED-2234, MED-2235, MED-2241, MED-2244, MED-2245.
**Trap:** Chausa precedes Kanauj (✅ owner 13 §25).
**Mains hook:** Use the ladder to show cause and effect.
**Back:** *"Thirty, thirty-one, thirty-five, thirty-nine, forty, forty-five, fifty-five, fifty-six."*

### Flashcard MED-2262 — Advanced historiography: rehabilitating Humayun
**Front:** What does Part II's historiography do with Humayun's reputation?
**Definition (learn):** Part II rehabilitates Humayun from moralising accounts: faults existed, but the decisive explanation is the mismatch between imperial ambition and Mughal institutional depth.
**Breakdown:**
- Rehabilitates from moralising accounts (✅ adv. 13 historiography note).
- Faults existed (✅ adv. 13 historiography note).
- Decisive explanation: ambition vs institutional depth (✅ adv. 13 historiography note).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2237, MED-2253.
**Trap:** Do not use the moralising frame or ignore the faults.
**Mains hook:** Institutional depth as the explanatory key.
**Back:** *"Humayun's fault is real; his failure was institutional depth."*

### Flashcard MED-2263 — Concept: Rajput, Afghan and Mughal as changing coalitions
**Front:** Why are "Afghan", "Rajput" and "Mughal" not fixed camps in this period?
**Definition (learn):** Rajputs, Afghans and Mughals were changing coalitions and political networks, not fixed ethnic or civilisational camps.
**Breakdown:**
- Changing coalitions and networks (✅ owner 13 §9).
- Afghan chiefs compete, submit or unite (✅ owner 13 §12).
- Rajput houses pursued distinct interests (✅ owner 13 §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2238, MED-2239, MED-2195.
**Trap:** "Rajput bloc" and "Afghan tribe" are not explanations.
**Mains hook:** Coalitions as political networks.
**Back:** *"Changing coalitions, not fixed camps."*

### GROUP — 14 Sher Shah & the Sur Empire

### Flashcard MED-2264 — Sur state: process and precedent, not a one-man miracle
**Front:** Why does the owner refuse to treat Sher Shah as an isolated genius?
**Definition (learn):** The Sur state continued a half-century process of north Indian unification begun under Sikandar Lodi, Babur and Humayun, and it set administrative precedents Akbar adopted and improved.
**Breakdown:**
- Empire emerged from processes under Sikandar Lodi, Babur and Humayun (✅ adv. 14 §1).
- The old balance of power was broken by Babur, Humayun's Malwa–Gujarat campaigns and Sher Khan's Bengal/Bihar successes (✅ adv. 14 §1).
- The Sur phase created precedents Akbar adopted and improved (⚠️ owner 14 §1).
- Achievement: turning opportunity into a functioning empire (⚠️ adv. 14 §1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sur sovereignty 1540–55 (✅ owner 14 §8.3). Wikipedia gives Sur Hindustan reign from 17 May 1540 to 22 May 1545 (✅ wiki: Sher Shah Suri).
**Links:** MED-2265, MED-2289, MED-2306, MED-2307.
**Trap:** "Sher Shah invented the empire from nothing" is wrong (✅ adv. 14 §2 "avoid one-man miracle").
**Mains hook:** Sher Shah as a builder of state capacity, but within a longer process (✅ owner 14 §7).
**Back:** *"Process first, Sher Shah second: precedent, not miracle."*

### Flashcard MED-2265 — Farid, Sher Khan and Sher Shah: one person, three career stages
**Front:** Who were Farid, Sher Khan and Sher Shah?
**Definition (learn):** The same person at different stages: Farid (birth name), Sher Khan (Bihar and Bengal leader), and Sher Shah (ruler from 1540).
**Breakdown:**
- Original name Farid (✅ owner 14 §4).
- Sher Khan: leader in Bihar and Bengal, victor at Chausa and Kanauj (✅ owner 14 §10).
- Sher Shah: the title after Kanauj, 1540 (✅ owner 14 §10; adv. 14 mini-timeline).
- Trap: the Sur sovereign was also known as Sher Khan before the coronation (✅ owner 14 §25).
