**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sher Shah formally crowned 1540 (✅ adv. 14 mini-timeline). Wikipedia: two coronations (1538 and 1540) and a disputed founding date (✅ wiki: Sher Shah Suri).
**Links:** MED-2266, MED-2268, MED-2230 (Humayun-side rise).
**Trap:** "Farid/Sher Khan/Sher Shah are the same person at different career stages" means one identity, not three rulers (✅ owner 14 §25).
**Mains hook:** Use the name sequence to explain why the rise appears contested in sources.
**Back:** *"Farid began it, Sher Khan fought it, Sher Shah ruled it."*

### Flashcard MED-2266 — Farid's early career in Sasaram and Bihar
**Front:** What training did Farid receive before his rise?
**Definition (learn):** Farid was born into a Sur Afghan family with jagir and service interests around Sasaram; he gained practical administrative training in his father's parganas and service in Bihar.
**Breakdown:**
- Sur Afghan family with jagir and service interests around Sasaram (✅ owner 14 §10).
- Practical administrative training in his father's parganas (✅ adv. 14 §4).
- Service in Bihar and management of family lands: knowledge of revenue, chiefs and military households (✅ owner 14 §10).
- Childhood anecdotes are shaped by later biography (✅ owner 14 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2267, MED-2265.
**Trap:** Do not present childhood anecdotes as verified biography (✅ owner 14 §10).
**Mains hook:** Local revenue experience as the base for state capacity.
**Back:** *"Sasaram boy learns the parganas before he rules them."*

### Flashcard MED-2267 — Hasan Sur's iqta and Farid's early administrative exposure (c. 1510)
**Front:** What early iqta gave Farid administrative exposure?
**Definition (learn):** Hasan Sur received the Sahsaram/Khawaspur-Tanda iqta around 1510, which gave Farid administrative exposure.
**Breakdown:**
- Hasan Sur received the Sahsaram/Khawaspur-Tanda iqta, c. 1510 (✅ adv. 14 mini-timeline).
- Farid gained administrative exposure through this iqta (✅ adv. 14 mini-timeline).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 1510 (✅ adv. 14).
**Links:** MED-2266, MED-2268.
**Trap:** Hasan Sur is the father, not the ruler Sher Shah; do not confuse the two (⚠️ owner note).
**Mains hook:** Iqta-based administration gave a training ground.
**Back:** *"Ten-ten: an iqta teaches Farid the land."*

### Flashcard MED-2268 — Sher Shah's reign and the Sur sovereignty dates
**Front:** What are the key dates of Sher Shah's rule?
**Definition (learn):** Sher Shah ruled as a Bihar sovereign from 1530 (Hazrat-i-Ala), Bengal and Bihar from 1538, Hindustan from 1540 to his death in 1545.
**Breakdown:**
- Bihar, 1530–38 (✅ wiki: Sher Shah Suri).
- Bengal and Bihar, from 6 April 1538 (✅ wiki).
- Sur Hindustan from 17 May 1540 (✅ wiki; owner: 1540 Kanauj and Sher Shah crowned, adv. 14 mini-timeline).
- Death 22 May 1545 at Kalinjar (✅ wiki; owner: May 1545 in adv. 14 mini-timeline; basic says 1545).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner reign 1540–45 (✅ owner 14 §4). Wikipedia dates above (✅ wiki Sher Shah Suri).
**Links:** MED-2265, MED-2272, MED-2296.
**Trap:** The 1538 Bengal date and the 1540 Hindustan date are different; do not merge them. The owner gives "1540–45" as the reign.
**Mains hook:** The short reign's visible achievements.
**Back:** *"Thirty-eight Bengal, forty Hindustan, forty-five Kalinjar."*

### Flashcard MED-2269 — Samel / Sammel (1544): Maldeo's Rajput forces
**Front:** What was the Samel (Sammel) battle of 1544?
**Definition (learn):** Sher Shah defeated Maldeo's Rajput forces at Samel (Marwar), a hard contest whose forged-letter narrative is mediated by Sarwani.
**Breakdown:**
- 1544: Sher Shah defeats Maldeo's Rajput forces (✅ owner 14 mini-timeline).
- Marwar/Sammel: hard contest (✅ owner 14 §10).
- Forged-letter narrative is Sarwani-mediated (✅ owner 14 §10).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1544 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** MED-2270, MED-2271, MED-2272.
**Trap:** Campaign numbers and dramatic treachery claims require a source label (✅ owner 14 §10).
**Mains hook:** Rajasthan and the Sur frontier.
**Back:** *"Forty-four: Samel, Maldeo, and a forged letter in the chronicle."*

### Flashcard MED-2270 — Raisen / Puran Mal (1543): safe-conduct and killing
**Front:** What is the Raisen–Puran Mal episode of 1543 and why is it a legitimacy problem?
**Definition (learn):** Puran Mal surrendered at Raisen under promise of safe-conduct; accusations and killing after surrender are remembered in the sources; it cannot be reduced either to neutral realpolitik or uniform persecution.
**Breakdown:**
- 1543: Raisen/Puran Mal (✅ owner 14 §10).
- Promises or safe-conduct, accusations and killing after surrender (✅ owner 14 §21).
- Political act given a religious gloss (✅ adv. 14 §2, §5).
- Use three levels: claim, surrender and coercion, wider evidence (✅ owner 14 §21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1543 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** MED-2292, MED-2271.
**Trap:** "Puran Mal proves uniform religious persecution" is wrong (✅ adv. 14 §5).
**Mains hook:** Legitimacy loss within a capable state.
**Back:** *"Raisen 1543: a surrender that ends in killing: a stain, not a policy."*

### Flashcard MED-2271 — Malwa (c. 1542): expanded central Indian control
**Front:** What did the Malwa campaign of c. 1542 achieve?
**Definition (learn):** Sher Shah conquered Malwa around 1542, expanding central Indian control.
**Breakdown:**
- Malwa conquered (✅ adv. 14 mini-timeline, 1542).
- Expanded central Indian control (✅ owner 14 §10).
- Multan, upper Sindh and Rajasthan campaigns in 1543–44 (✅ adv. 14 mini-timeline).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1542 (✅ adv. 14).
**Links:** MED-2226 (Malwa under Humayun), MED-2269.
**Trap:** Sur Malwa (1542) is not Humayun's 1535 Mandu conquest (✅ owner 13 §14).
**Mains hook:** Central India as a Sur extension.
**Back:** *"Forty-two: Malwa taken, Mandu no longer Mughal."*

### Flashcard MED-2272 — Kalinjar (1545): siege and gunpowder explosion
**Front:** How did Sher Shah die?
**Definition (learn):** Sher Shah died during the siege of Kalinjar in 1545; the cause was a gunpowder explosion during the siege.
**Breakdown:**
- Siege of Kalinjar, Bundelkhand (✅ owner 14 §10; adv. 14 mini-timeline).
- Gunpowder explosion: the cause (✅ owner 14 §10; Wikipedia says accounts differ on how it happened).
- Wikipedia: siege from 1544 lasting seven months; death 22 May 1545 (✅ wiki Sher Shah Suri).
- Kalinjar also demonstrates the risk and accident of artillery (✅ owner 14 §18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1545; 22 May 1545 (✅ wiki).
**Links:** MED-2268, MED-2269, MED-2243.
**Trap:** Death in battle is not an assassination; the owner's cause is a gunpowder explosion (✅ owner 14 §4).
**Mains hook:** Artillery as a risk in a siege.
**Back:** *"Forty-five, Kalinjar: a gunpowder explosion, not a sword."*

### Flashcard MED-2273 — Grand Trunk Road: restored and extended, not built from nothing
**Front:** What did Sher Shah do to the Grand Trunk Road and what is the label's status?
**Definition (learn):** Sher Shah restored and extended older imperial, market and pilgrimage corridors. The name "Grand Trunk Road" is a later label.
**Breakdown:**
- Restored the old imperial road later called the GT Road, Indus to Sonargaon (✅ owner 14 §1).
- Repaired, realigned, extended and policed older corridors (✅ owner 14 §16).
- The label is later; colonial narratives can convert repair into invention (✅ owner 14 §9, §16).
- Wikipedia (Sher Shah Suri): rebuilt and modernised the road, extended from Chittagong to Kabul; conflicts with the owner's Sonargaon–Indus framing, flagged (⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner: Indus–Sonargaon. Wikipedia: Chittagong–Kabul. The endpoints conflict; the owner is the repository reading (⚠️ flagged).
**Links:** MED-2274, MED-2275, MED-2306.
**Trap:** "Sher Shah invented the GT Road from nothing" is wrong (✅ owner 14 §5).
**Mains hook:** Roads as state infrastructure; not a modern highway.
**Back:** *"Old road, new Sur hands: restored, extended, policed, renamed later."*

### Flashcard MED-2274 — Sarais: two karoh intervals, news and dak stages
**Front:** What were the sarais and what functions did they serve?
**Definition (learn):** Inns built at intervals of two karohs (about four miles in the owner's wording), serving as lodging, markets, news and dak stages.
**Breakdown:**
- Built at regular intervals of two karohs (✅ owner 14 §1; adv. 14 §1).
- Lodging, animals, food, security, markets, officials and communication stages (✅ owner 14 §16).
- Separate Hindu and Muslim lodging arrangements, where reported (✅ owner 14 §1; §16).
- Fortified inns, markets, dak-chowkis (✅ adv. 14 §2).
- Served surveillance as much as welfare (✅ owner 14 §8.2 unit 1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Two karohs (✅ owner 14 §4).
**Links:** MED-2273, MED-2275, MED-2302.
**Trap:** "Sarais were only welfare shelters" is wrong (✅ owner 14 §5).
**Mains hook:** Sarais as the communications network of a state.
**Back:** *"Two karohs, one sarai: rest, news, guard, and tax."*

### Flashcard MED-2275 — Road corridors: Agra–Jodhpur–Chittor, Lahore–Multan, Agra–Burhanpur
**Front:** Which corridors did Sher Shah's roads connect?
**Definition (learn):** The source tradition names the Sonargaon–Indus spine, Agra–Burhanpur, Agra–Jodhpur–Chittor and Lahore–Multan.
**Breakdown:**
- Sonargaon–Bengal to the Indus: imperial east–west spine (✅ owner 14 §16).
- Agra–Burhanpur: Malwa and Deccan link (✅ owner 14 §16; adv. 14 §4).
- Agra–Jodhpur/Chittor: Rajasthan route (✅ owner 14 §16).
- Lahore–Multan: Punjab-frontier route (✅ owner 14 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** The corridors above (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** MED-2273, MED-2274.
**Trap:** Keep these as corridors, not modern highway alignments (✅ owner 14 §6).
**Mains hook:** Roads served trade, military movement and control of the countryside (✅ adv. 14 §2).
**Back:** *"Bengal to Indus, Agra to Burhanpur and Jodhpur, Lahore to Multan."*

### Flashcard MED-2276 — Customs: entry and sale duties, no arbitrary levies
**Front:** What did Sher Shah's customs system look like?
**Definition (learn):** Duties were charged at entry and sale points; arbitrary levies on roads, ferries and towns were removed in the ideal account.
**Breakdown:**
- Duties at entry and sale (✅ owner 14 §2).
- No arbitrary road, ferry or town levies (✅ owner 14 §2).
- Local collection practice varied (✅ owner 14 §17).
- Customs simplification associated with controlled points (✅ owner 14 §17).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2274, MED-2277.
**Trap:** Do not assume uniform collection practice (✅ owner 14 §17).
**Mains hook:** Trade and coercion are two sides of route control.
**Back:** *"Duty at the gate and the sale: no road tolls on top."*

### Flashcard MED-2277 — Currency: rupiya, dam and gold; standardisation not invention
**Front:** What currency did Sher Shah standardise and what did he not invent?
**Definition (learn):** Standardised gold, silver and copper coins; the silver rupiya (rupee) and copper dam became reference units. He standardised weight and fineness; he did not invent money or the rupee.
**Breakdown:**
- Fine gold, silver and copper coins (✅ owner 14 §2).
- Silver rupiya and copper dam as reference units (✅ owner 14 §15).
- Tri-metallic standardisation (✅ owner 14 §15).
- Earlier silver currencies and the idea of the rupee pre-dated him (✅ owner 14 §15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2276, MED-2300, MED-2307.
**Trap:** "He invented the rupee" is wrong (✅ owner 14 §25).
**Mains hook:** Currency as an instrument of revenue, army pay and trade, but not complete monetisation (✅ owner 14 §15).
**Back:** *"Rupiya, dam, gold: a standard, not the first money."*

### Flashcard MED-2278 — Revenue: zabt, measurement, rai and one-third norm
**Front:** How did Sher Shah's revenue system work?
**Definition (learn):** Measurement of sown land, classification into good, middling and bad, average yield and crop-price schedule (rai), state demand commonly one-third in core descriptions.
**Breakdown:**
- Field measurement (jarib/bigha; Sikandari-gaz tradition) (✅ owner 14 §14).
- Classification: good, middling, bad (✅ owner 14 §14; adv. 14 §1).
- Average produce and crop-price schedule (rai) (✅ owner 14 §14).
- State demand commonly one-third; qualified (✅ owner 14 §14; §4).
- Sarwani's idealised account cannot prove annual measurement everywhere (✅ owner 14 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** One-third norm (✅ owner 14 §4).
**Links:** MED-2279, MED-2293, MED-2294.
**Trap:** One-third is a common source norm, not identical collection everywhere (✅ owner 14 §4).
**Mains hook:** Revenue as legible demand; implementation varied (✅ owner 14 §7).
**Back:** *"Measure, grade, average, a third: the Sur zabt in outline."*

### Flashcard MED-2279 — Patta and qabuliyat: the written agreement with cultivators
**Front:** What did the patta and qabuliyat do?
**Definition (learn):** Patta recorded the area, crop and amount due from each peasant; qabuliyat is the cultivator's acceptance of the demand.
**Breakdown:**
- Patta: area, crop and amount due (✅ owner 14 §4).
- Qabuliyat: acceptance (✅ owner 14 §14).
- Written patta could clarify demand, not guarantee bargaining equality (✅ owner 14 §20).
- Patta had Sultanate precedents (✅ owner 14 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2278, MED-2280.
**Trap:** Patta does not mean direct modern peasant-state settlement or ryotwari (✅ adv. 14 §5; owner §25).
**Mains hook:** Written records as state legibility, not peasant empowerment.
**Back:** *"Patta writes what's due; qabuliyat says you accept."*

### Flashcard MED-2280 — Army control: chehra (descriptive roll) and dagh (horse branding)
**Front:** How did Sher Shah control his army?
**Definition (learn):** Direct recruitment, a descriptive roll of each soldier (chehra) and horse branding (dagh) to assert individual accountability.
**Breakdown:**
- Direct recruitment; cash or assignment payment (✅ owner 14 §18).
- Chehra: descriptive roll; dagh: horse branding (✅ owner 14 §2, §4).
- Alauddin-era precedents for dagh and chehra (✅ owner 14 §18).
- Did not eliminate noble contingents (✅ owner 14 §18).
- Mughal dagh under Akbar as later development (✅ owner 14 §24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2281, MED-2294, MED-2300.
**Trap:** Dagh/chehra had Sultanate precedents (✅ owner 14 §25).
**Mains hook:** Systematisation rather than invention.
**Back:** *"Chehra names the man, dagh brands the horse: the army's ledger."*

### Flashcard MED-2281 — Law and order: stern action and collective responsibility
**Front:** How did Sher Shah secure roads and villages?
**Definition (learn):** Stern action against robbers, dacoits and disobedient zamindars; the administration used local headmen and zamindars, collective responsibility, patrols and severe punishments.
**Breakdown:**
- Stern action against robbers and dacoits (✅ owner 14 §2).
- Local headmen and zamindars, collective responsibility, patrols (✅ owner 14 §19).
- Equal accountability rhetoric could restrain elite impunity (✅ owner 14 §19).
- Collective responsibility could punish communities beyond guilt (✅ owner 14 §19).
- "No theft occurred" is not a statistical claim (✅ owner 14 §19).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2276, MED-2282.
**Trap:** Impartial justice rests on Sarwani's ideal stories (✅ owner 14 §19).
**Mains hook:** Justice linked to coercive capacity.
**Back:** *"Order by the stick and the village: justice with collective teeth."*

### Flashcard MED-2282 — Central, sarkar/shiq, pargana and village administration
**Front:** What was the territorial and local hierarchy?
**Definition (learn):** Centre: sultan with finance (wizarat), military (arz), chancery (insha) and religious/judicial portfolios. Sarkar/shiq: military-police and revenue supervision. Pargana: shiqdar, amin/munsif, qanungo, treasurer/fotedar. Village: muqaddam/headman, patwari, panchayat.
**Breakdown:**
- Centre: wizarat, arz, insha (✅ owner 14 §13).
- Sarkar/shiq: chief shiqdar and munsif traditions (✅ owner 14 §13).
- Pargana: shiqdar, amin/munsif, qanungo, treasurer/fotedar (✅ owner 14 §13).
- Village: muqaddam, patwari, panchayat (✅ owner 14 §13).
- Sarkars/shiqs and parganas remained the important units (✅ owner 14 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2283, MED-2299 (provinces trap).
**Trap:** Terms and portfolios must be tied to Sarwani and later reconstruction (✅ owner 14 §13).
**Mains hook:** Continuity from Sultanate to Sur to Mughal (✅ owner 14 §24).
**Back:** *"Centre, sarkar, pargana, village: the Sur ladder of offices."*

### Flashcard MED-2283 — Sher Shah's tomb, Sasaram: octagonal lake mausoleum
**Front:** What is the Sasaram tomb and what does its building history show?
**Definition (learn):** A monumental octagonal mausoleum in a lake at Sasaram, built across the Sher Shah and Islam Shah phases and inscriptionally completed after Sher's death.
**Breakdown:**
- Monumental octagonal lake mausoleum (✅ owner 14 §22).
- Built across Sher and Islam Shah phases (✅ owner 14 §22; §25).
- Designed by Aliwal Khan tradition (✅ owner 14 §22).
- "Pathan style" labels are later reception (✅ owner 14 §22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sher and Islam Shah phases (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** MED-2282, MED-2285.
**Trap:** Sasaram tomb spans Sher and Islam Shah phases (✅ owner 14 §25).
**Mains hook:** Architecture shows legitimacy, labour, water and frontier strategy (✅ owner 14 §22).
**Back:** *"Sasaram's lake tomb: Sher begins, Islam Shah builds on."*

### Flashcard MED-2284 — Rohtas (Pakistan) vs Rohtasgarh (Bihar): two different forts
**Front:** Why must Rohtas and Rohtasgarh be kept apart?
**Definition (learn):** Rohtas, in present-day Pakistan, guarded the north-west and Mughal return routes against the Gakhhars; Rohtasgarh is a distinct Bihar fort and resource setting.
**Breakdown:**
- Rohtas in present-day Pakistan: north-west frontier, Gakhhar and Mughal-return strategy (✅ owner 14 §18, §22).
- Rohtas construction entrusted to Todar Mal Khatri (✅ adv. 14 §4).
- Rohtasgarh in Bihar: distinct fort and resource setting (✅ owner 14 §22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2285, MED-2286.
**Trap:** Rohtas ≠ Rohtasgarh (✅ owner 14 §25).
**Mains hook:** Frontier fort as state control.
**Back:** *"Rohtas guards the north-west; Rohtasgarh guards Bihar."*

### Flashcard MED-2285 — Purana Qila, Dinpanah/Shergarh and Qila-i-Kuhna mosque
**Front:** What does the Purana Qila zone's multi-phase history show?
**Definition (learn):** Purana Qila (Dinpanah/Shergarh) is a multi-phase Humayun, Sur and Mughal urban-fort landscape; the Qila-i-Kuhna mosque is commonly attributed to Sher Shah.
**Breakdown:**
- Purana Qila: multi-phase, no one-patron attribution (✅ owner 14 §22).
- Qila-i-Kuhna mosque: commonly attributed to Sher Shah; dated fabric and inscription control (✅ owner 14 §22).
- Dinpanah (Humayun) and Shergarh (Sur) are phases (✅ owner 14 §22; MED-2229).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2229, MED-2259, MED-2283.
**Trap:** Purana Qila is not Humayun's alone; the Qila-i-Kuhna attribution is "commonly attributed" (✅ owner 14 §22).
**Mains hook:** Monument biography for Mughal and Sur layers.
**Back:** *"Purana Qila has many hands: Humayun, Sher Shah, Mughals, and later works."*

### Flashcard MED-2286 — Afghan kingship: personal monarchy through clans and nobles
**Front:** How did Sher Shah rule through Afghan clans and nobles?
**Definition (learn):** Sher Shah built a strong personal monarchy but governed through Afghan clans and nobles, household followers, commanders, local chiefs, zamindars, scribes and revenue officers.
**Breakdown:**
- Strong personal monarchy (✅ owner 14 §12).
- Governance through clans, nobles, followers, chiefs, zamindars, scribes and revenue officers (✅ owner 14 §12).
- Nobles expected honour and assignments; ruler sought discipline (✅ owner 14 §12).
- Iqta/jagir-like assignments, cash payment and direct recruitment coexisted (✅ owner 14 §12).
- Afghan identity did not create automatic democracy or equality (✅ owner 14 §12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2287, MED-2305.
**Trap:** Not a pure Turkish monarchy, and not Afghan tribal democracy (✅ adv. 14 §2; owner §8.3).
**Mains hook:** Personal supervision gave rapid coordination but made succession fragile (✅ owner 14 §12).
**Back:** *"Clans bargain, the king supervises, assignments and cash coexist."*

### Flashcard MED-2287 — Islam Shah (1545–53): monarchical discipline and noble resentment
**Front:** What did Islam Shah do and why did it matter?
**Definition (learn):** Islam Shah (Jalal Khan) continued the roads, revenue and order structures, tightened monarchical discipline, regulated nobles and camps, and tried to contain Afghan chiefs.
**Breakdown:**
- Reign 1545–53 (✅ owner 14 §23; wiki: died 1553).
- Continued roads, revenue and order (✅ owner 14 §23).
- Tightened discipline; regulated nobles and camps (✅ owner 14 §12, §23).
- Resentment as a cause of later fragmentation (✅ adv. 14 §4).
- Not an empty postscript (✅ owner 14 §23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1545–53 (✅ owner 14 mini-timeline).
**Links:** MED-2286, MED-2288.
**Trap:** "Islam Shah's reign was a postscript" is wrong (✅ owner 14 §23).
**Mains hook:** Continuity and resentment as a single dynamic.
**Back:** *"Islam Shah tightens the screws: continuity with resentment."*

### Flashcard MED-2288 — Sur fragmentation after 1553: Firuz, Adil Shah, Sikandar and Ibrahim
**Front:** Who fought over the Sur inheritance after Islam Shah?
**Definition (learn):** After Islam Shah's death the young Firuz was killed; Muhammad Adil Shah faced rivals; Sikandar Sur and Ibrahim Sur claimed separate power; Hemu became Adil Shah's leading military-administrative figure; Humayun exploited the division.
**Breakdown:**
- Young Firuz killed (✅ owner 14 §23).
- Muhammad Adil Shah faced rivals (✅ owner 14 §23).
- Sikandar Sur and Ibrahim Sur claimed separate power (✅ owner 14 §23).
- Hemu as Adil Shah's leading military-administrative figure (✅ owner 14 §23).
- Humayun exploited the division to recover Delhi in 1555 (✅ owner 14 §23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Islam Shah died 1553 (✅ owner 14 mini-timeline).
**Links:** MED-2243, MED-2244, MED-2287.
**Trap:** Rapid collapse was not an inherent Afghan incapacity (✅ owner 14 §23).
**Mains hook:** Succession, personalised control and noble faction explain fragmentation.
**Back:** *"Fifty-three: Firuz dies, Adil and Hemu, Sikandar and Ibrahim; Humayun returns."*

### Flashcard MED-2289 — Continuity ladder: Sultanate/Lodi, Sur and Mughal
**Front:** What did the Sur state inherit, adapt and pass to the Mughals?
**Definition (learn):** Sultanate/Lodi: sarkar and pargana, assignments, measurement and crop schedules, tanka coins, roads and dagh/chehra. Sur: tighter supervision, patta and rai, rupiya-dam-gold standards, route and security network, claimed dagh and chehra revival. Mughal: suba framework, zabt/dahsala, imperial mint and mansabdari-dagh.
**Breakdown:**
- Sultanate/Lodi precedents: sarkar/pargana, assignments, crop schedules, tanka (✅ owner 14 §24).
- Sur adaptations: tighter supervision, patta/qabuliyat, rupiya-dam-gold, road and sarai security (✅ owner 14 §24).
- Mughal developments: suba framework, zabt/dahsala, imperial mint, mansabdari-dagh (✅ owner 14 §24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2264, MED-2294, MED-2306.
**Trap:** Akbar did not receive a complete Sur blueprint (✅ owner 14 §24).
**Mains hook:** Three-level continuity table in a 15-mark answer.
**Back:** *"Sultanate sets, Sur shapes, Mughal adapts: no blueprint."*

### Flashcard MED-2290 — Abbas Khan Sarwani's Tarikh-i Sher Shahi: ruler ideal and romanticism
**Front:** What is the Tarikh-i Sher Shahi's value and limit?
**Definition (learn):** The Tarikh-i Sher Shahi, by Abbas Khan Sarwani, is the key source on Sher Shah's rise, campaigns, revenue, roads, justice and ruler ideal; written decades later under Akbar, it romanticises.
**Breakdown:**
- Rise, campaigns, revenue, roads, justice, ruler ideal (✅ owner 14 §9).
- Written decades later under Akbar; praise, anecdote and idealised system (✅ owner 14 §9).
- Romanticises Sher Shah (✅ adv. 14 historiography note).
- Separate the ideal from coin and building evidence and implementation inference (✅ owner 14 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2278, MED-2281, MED-2291.
**Trap:** Sarwani's ideal ≠ measured reality (✅ owner 14 §14).
**Mains hook:** Admire results; do not ignore duplicity and limits (✅ adv. 14 historiography note).
**Back:** *"Sarwani praises the ideal; check the coin and the field."*

### Flashcard MED-2291 — Sur sources: Nizamuddin, inscriptions, coins, colonial labels
**Front:** Which source types support the Sur state and which need caution?
**Definition (learn):** Nizamuddin Ahmad's Tabaqat-i Akbari (chronology), Mughal chronicles (Sur–Mughal comparison), inscriptions, coins, architecture and archaeology (Sasaram, Delhi, Rohtas), and colonial GT Road narratives.
**Breakdown:**
- Nizamuddin Ahmad: chronology, later Mughal compilation (✅ owner 14 §9).
- Mughal chronicles: Akbar-centred teleology (✅ owner 14 §9).
- Inscriptions and coins: dated patronage; names, mints, metals, weight (✅ owner 14 §9).
- Architecture: Sasaram, Delhi, Rohtas; multi-phase (✅ owner 14 §9).
- Colonial GT Road narratives can convert repair into invention (✅ owner 14 §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2290, MED-2273, MED-2250.
**Trap:** Coins do not map complete monetisation (✅ owner 14 §9).
**Mains hook:** Source ladder: claim, genre, corroboration, limit.
**Back:** *"Nizamuddin dates, coins prove names, colonial labels mislead."*

### Flashcard MED-2292 — Religion and Puran Mal: Sunni ruler, political act with religious gloss
**Front:** How should Sher Shah's religious policy be stated?
**Definition (learn):** Sher Shah was a Sunni Muslim ruler who used Islamic legitimacy and dealt with diverse officials, chiefs and subjects; the Raisen/Puran Mal episode was a political act with a religious gloss.
**Breakdown:**
- Sunni Muslim ruler using Islamic legitimacy (✅ owner 14 §21).
- Dealt with diverse officials, chiefs and subjects (✅ owner 14 §21).
- Orthodox but not generally bigoted (✅ adv. 14 §2).
- Puran Mal political with religious gloss; temple destruction not a general pattern (✅ adv. 14 §5).
- Inclusion does not prove equal status or total tolerance (✅ owner 14 §20).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2270, MED-2288.
**Trap:** Avoid a communal label or a tolerance label for the whole reign (✅ adv. 14 §2).
**Mains hook:** Religious language authorises politics; coercive episodes damage trust.
**Back:** *"Sunni in faith, political in Raisen: no uniform persecution."*

### Flashcard MED-2293 — Zamindars, muqaddams and patta: controlled, not abolished
**Front:** What happened to zamindars and intermediaries under Sher Shah?
**Definition (learn):** Local headmen and zamindars remained central to assessment and collection; they were controlled and used, not abolished; this was not British ryotwari.
**Breakdown:**
- Headmen and zamindars remained central (✅ adv. 14 §1).
- Controlled and used, not abolished (✅ owner 14 §20).
- Zamindars, muqaddams, qanungos and cultivators remained central (✅ owner 14 §14).
- Multan and frontier, forest and hill zones show regional exceptions (✅ owner 14 §14; adv. 14 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2278, MED-2279.
**Trap:** "Sher Shah abolished zamindars" is wrong (✅ owner 14 §5).
**Mains hook:** The state worked through local elites, not around them.
**Back:** *"Zamindars controlled, muqaddams used, Multan the exception."*

### Flashcard MED-2294 — Zabt and dahsala: the Sur–Akbar link
**Front:** How does Sher Shah's revenue system connect to Akbar's zabt/dahsala?
**Definition (learn):** Sher Shah's measurement and crop-rate system was a predecessor of Akbar's zabt/dahsala, which developed under different evidence and scale.
**Breakdown:**
- Sher Shah's measurement and crop-rate system (✅ owner 14 §5, §24).
- Akbar's zabt/dahsala developed later (✅ owner 14 §24).
- Sher Shah's experiments, Islam Shah's continuation and Akbar's development must be separated (✅ owner 14 §14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2278, MED-2289, MED-2307.
**Trap:** "Akbar's revenue system had no precedent" is wrong (✅ owner 14 §5).
**Mains hook:** Continuity and precedent in revenue.
**Back:** *"Sur measures, Akbar refines: zabt and dahsala grow from a predecessor."*

### Flashcard MED-2295 — Advanced: over-centralisation as the Sur weakness
**Front:** What was the advanced owner's view of the Sur system's weakness?
**Definition (learn):** Sher Shah's personal over-centralisation was a weakness; after a masterful sovereign disappeared the system strained.
**Breakdown:**
- Personal over-centralisation (✅ adv. 14 §1).
- System strain after the sovereign's death (✅ adv. 14 §1).
- Excessive personal supervision and fragility after Sher Shah and Islam Shah (✅ adv. 14 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2286, MED-2288.
**Trap:** Do not equate personal efficiency with institutional durability (✅ owner 14 §8.3).
**Mains hook:** Limits of personal despotism in sustaining an empire (✅ owner 14 §7).
**Back:** *"A master's system falters when the master is gone."*

### Flashcard MED-2296 — Sur dynasty's length: 1540–55, about fifteen years
**Front:** How long did the Sur empire last?
**Definition (learn):** The Sur empire's period runs from 1540 to the Mughal reconquest in 1555, about fifteen years.
**Breakdown:**
- Sur phase 1540–55 (✅ owner 14 §8.3).
- Mughal recovery of Delhi 1555 (✅ owner 14 mini-timeline).
- Wikipedia: founding date disputed because of two coronations (1538 and 1540) (✅ wiki Sher Shah Suri).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1540–55 (✅ owner 14).
**Links:** MED-2268, MED-2244, MED-2297.
**Trap:** Institutional longevity is unproven; the Sur empire was short (✅ owner 14 §8.3).
**Mains hook:** A short-lived state with a long reputation.
**Back:** *"Forty to fifty-five: fifteen years, long memory."*

### Flashcard MED-2297 — Assessment: Sher Shah as an effective short-reign state-builder
**Front:** What is the owner's verdict on Sher Shah?
**Definition (learn):** An effective short-reign state-builder whose interlocking revenue, circulation, currency, military and order measures deserve attention; Sarwani's idealisation, implementation gaps, coercive episodes and personalised control limit a "modern administrator" or one-man-genius verdict.
**Breakdown:**
- Revenue, circulation, currency, military and order as interlocking measures (✅ owner 14 §24).
- Sarwani idealisation (✅ owner 14 §24).
- Regional implementation gaps (✅ owner 14 §24).
- Coercive episodes and personalised control (✅ owner 14 §24).
- Rapid Sur fragmentation (✅ owner 14 §24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2264, MED-2294, MED-2307.
**Trap:** Avoid "modern administrator" and "one-man genius" verdicts (✅ owner 14 §24).
**Mains hook:** Innovation vs systematisation.
**Back:** *"Effective, not modern; systematic, not a lone genius."*

### Flashcard MED-2298 — PYQ route for Sur administration: zero direct; Topic 16 adjacent
**Front:** Which PYQ is adjacent to Sur administration and what is the owner's status?
**Definition (learn):** The repository routes zero direct Topic-14 UPSC CSE PYQs for 2018–2026. A neutral pargana–sarkar–suba hierarchy route belongs to Topic 16 and is adjacent only.
**Breakdown:**
- Zero direct Topic-14 UPSC CSE PYQs (✅ owner 14 §25).
- Pargana–sarkar–suba hierarchy: Topic 16 adjacent (✅ owner 14 §25).
- No exact official wording reproduced (✅ owner 14 §25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2282, MED-2289.
**Trap:** Do not invent a Sur PYQ (✅ owner 14 §25).
**Mains hook:** Use the hierarchy as a comparative administrative frame.
**Back:** *"No Sur PYQ; the hierarchy belongs to Topic 16."*

### Flashcard MED-2299 — Trap: Sher Shah did not create provinces like Akbar
**Front:** Why is "Sher Shah created provinces like Akbar" wrong?
**Definition (learn):** Under Sher Shah, sarkars and shiqs remained the key units; provincial governments evolved under the Mughals.
**Breakdown:**
- Sarkars and shiqs remained key units (✅ adv. 14 §5).
- Provincial government evolved under the Mughals (✅ adv. 14 §5).
- Sher Shah did not create a modern provincial structure (✅ owner 14 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2282, MED-2289.
**Trap:** Suba is a Mughal unit, not Sur (✅ owner 14 §13).
**Mains hook:** Precision about territorial units.
**Back:** *"Sarkar and pargana, not suba: Akbar's province is later."*

### Flashcard MED-2300 — Trap: Sher Shah did not end jagirs or invent measurement
**Front:** Why are "Sher Shah ended jagirs" and "Sher Shah invented measurement" traps?
**Definition (learn):** Nobles and soldiers continued to be paid through assignments; measurement, patta and chehra/dagh had precedents.
**Breakdown:**
- Nobles and soldiers continued to be paid through assignments (✅ adv. 14 §5).
- Measurement and crop schedules had Sultanate precedents (✅ owner 14 §14).
- Sher Shah systematised; did not invent every component (✅ owner 14 §8.3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2278, MED-2280, MED-2286.
**Trap:** Systematisation, not invention (✅ owner 14 §8.3).
**Mains hook:** Innovation vs systematisation.
**Back:** *"Assignments stay; measurement was systematised, not invented."*

### Flashcard MED-2301 — Trap: patta is not direct peasant-state settlement
**Front:** What does the patta trap look like in the owner?
**Definition (learn):** Patta does not create a direct modern peasant-state settlement; local headmen and zamindars remained central.
**Breakdown:**
- Patta is not direct modern settlement (✅ adv. 14 §5).
- Local headmen and zamindars central to assessment and collection (✅ adv. 14 §5).
- Patta and qabuliyat did not create ryotwari or abolish zamindars (✅ owner 14 §25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2279, MED-2293.
**Trap:** "Patta = ryotwari" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** Written demand, local intermediaries.
**Back:** *"Patta is not ryotwari: intermediaries stay."*

### Flashcard MED-2302 — Advanced: sarais at two karohs (about four miles) as a historical unit
**Front:** How should the sarai interval be expressed?
**Definition (learn):** Part II places sarais at two karohs, about four miles in Part II's wording; keep the historical unit where precision matters.
**Breakdown:**
- Two karohs (✅ adv. 14 §1; §4).
- About four miles in Part II wording (✅ adv. 14 §4).
- The basic owner says the two-karoh interval is an idealised unit, not proof of identical spacing (✅ owner 14 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Two karohs (✅ owner 14 §4).
**Links:** MED-2274.
**Trap:** Do not convert karoh to a modern kilometre equivalent without caution.
**Mains hook:** Unit precision in a historical answer.
**Back:** *"Two karohs, about four miles: the unit, not a modern metric."*

### Flashcard MED-2303 — Mains thesis: Sher Shah (10/15/20 marks)
**Front:** How should a Sher Shah answer be built at 10, 15 and 20 marks?
**Definition (learn):** Executable scaffolds from the owner's answer architecture.
**Breakdown:**
- 10 marks: source caveat, three linked reforms (roads and revenue), one coercion limit; practical legacy greater than dynastic (✅ owner 14 §8.4).
- 15 marks: add currency, army control and continuity; the Sur link in the Sultanate–Mughal chain (✅ owner 14 §8.4).
- 20 marks: full five units, counterpoints and graded verdict on innovation vs systematisation (✅ owner 14 §8.4).
- Thesis: linked measured revenue, currency, routes and order; Mughals adapted precedents (✅ owner 14 §8.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 10 marks/150 words; 15 and 20 marks/250 words (✅ owner 14 §26).
**Links:** MED-2264, MED-2289, MED-2297.
**Trap:** Avoid a simple "invented" thesis.
**Mains hook:** State capacity through roads, revenue and law.
**Back:** *"Ten: roads and revenue. Fifteen: add currency and army. Twenty: the full verdict."*

### Flashcard MED-2304 — Advanced: Sher Shah as part of a process; the historiography caution
**Front:** How does the advanced owner caution against adulation?
**Definition (learn):** Admire the administrative results but do not ignore political duplicity and structural limits; Sarwani romanticises.
**Breakdown:**
- Sher Shah as a process figure, not a genius (✅ adv. 14 §1).
- Sarwani romanticises the ruler (✅ adv. 14 historiography note).
- Political duplicity and structural limits (✅ adv. 14 historiography note).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2290, MED-2264.
**Trap:** Uncritical praise is a trap (✅ owner 14 §8.3).
**Mains hook:** Graded verdict.
**Back:** *"Admire the results, doubt the romance."*

### Flashcard MED-2305 — Advanced: despotic king, not pure Turkish monarchy, not Lodi equality
**Front:** How does the advanced owner describe the Afghan state?
**Definition (learn):** A despotic king with nobles retaining iqta and jagir expectations; not a pure Turkish monarchy, and not a Lodi-style tribal equality.
**Breakdown:**
- Despotic king (✅ adv. 14 §2).
- Nobles retaining iqta and jagir expectations (✅ adv. 14 §2).
- Not pure Turkish monarchy (✅ adv. 14 §2).
- Contrast with Lodi tribal equality is a study link (✅ owner 14 §2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2286, MED-2287.
**Trap:** Afghan identity did not create automatic democracy or equality (✅ owner 14 §12).
**Mains hook:** Afghan political culture as negotiated.
**Back:** *"Despotic king, bargaining nobles: neither Turk nor tribal republic."*

### Flashcard MED-2306 — Sher Shah as a bridge between Sultanate and Mughal governance
**Front:** How does the Sur state bridge the Sultanate and the Mughals?
**Definition (learn):** The Sur state linked Sultanate precedents with later Mughal institutions through measured revenue, currency, roads and military records.
**Breakdown:**
- Sultanate precedents (✅ owner 14 §24).
- Sur adaptations (✅ owner 14 §24).
- Mughal developments (✅ owner 14 §24).
- Bridge, not a blueprint (✅ owner 14 §24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2289, MED-2294.
**Trap:** "Mughals inherited a finished Sur blueprint" is wrong (✅ owner 14 §24).
**Mains hook:** Reform as bridge; the reform's limits.
**Back:** *"Bridge, not blueprint: the Sur hands Akbar tools, not a finished plan."*

### Flashcard MED-2307 — Innovation vs systematisation: the central Sur debate
**Front:** Was Sher Shah an innovator or a systematiser?
**Definition (learn):** He systematised existing precedents in measurement, chehra/dagh, roads and currency, and gave them a coherent form, rather than inventing them.
**Breakdown:**
- Systematisation of measurement and crop schedules (✅ owner 14 §8.3).
- Chehra and dagh had Sultanate precedents (✅ owner 14 §18).
- Currency standardised, not invented (✅ owner 14 §15).
- Roads restored and extended (✅ owner 14 §16).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None further.
**Links:** MED-2297, MED-2294, MED-2300.
**Trap:** Innovation is a loaded word; use systematisation with evidence.
**Mains hook:** Graded verdict on "innovation vs systematisation" (✅ owner 14 §8.4).
**Back:** *"Systematise, don't invent: Sher Shah's real achievement."*

## MEDIEVAL INDIAN HISTORY — Part 3: Akbar, Deccan, Foreign Policy and Jahangir

> Section note: Prefix MED, ID block MED-3001 to MED-3155.
> Grounding: owner files `upsc-ai-kit/knowledge/Medieval-Indian-History/basic/` and `advanced/` 15–20 (read in full). Fetched sources used for 11 claims (Wikipedia pages listed on each card as ✅ Wikipedia). Where the owner and a fetched page disagree, the card says so.

### GROUP — Akbar: Consolidation & Expansion of the Empire

### Flashcard MED-3001 — Concept: Akbar reconquered before he could rule
**Front:** Why did Akbar's consolidation require conquest first, and what did conquest need in order to last?
**Definition (learn):** After Humayun's death (1556) the Mughal state was a small coalition in a hostile field: Afghans (Sikandar Sur, Hemu), rebellious nobles and Uzbeks, and threatened Kabul. Consolidation meant (a) military reconquest, (b) suppression of noble and Uzbek factions, and (c) integration of regional elites.
**Breakdown:**
- Stage 1: Panipat II (1556) and regency (1556–60) restored Delhi–Agra.
- Stage 2: removal of Bairam Khan's dominance (1560); Uzbek revolt crushed (1567).
- Stage 3: conquest of Malwa, Gujarat, Bengal, Rajasthan, then Kashmir, Sindh, Qandahar, Deccan frontier (1561–1601), with Rajput and service integration as the glue.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mughal reconquest 1556–76 (Panipat II to Bengal); Chittor 1568; Gujarat 1572–73; Bengal 1576 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** MED-3002 (Panipat II), MED-3011 (Uzbek revolt), MED-3017 (Rajput policy), MED-3023 (negotiated sovereignty), MED-3024 (state under Akbar).
**Trap:** Panipat II did not end Afghan resistance; Afghans stayed active in the east for years (✅ owner 15).
**Mains hook:** "Akbar converted a fragile restoration into a durable but uneven empire through conquest plus negotiated elite integration, not benevolence or one policy alone." Qualify with massacre at Chittor and uneven Bengal control.
**Back:** *"Reconquer, then reconcile: Panipat, purge the nobles, then pay the rajas."*

### Flashcard MED-3002 — Second Battle of Panipat (1556)
**Front:** Date, combatants and outcome of the Second Battle of Panipat.
**Definition (learn):** The battle in which the Mughal forces under the young Akbar, with Bairam Khan's command, defeated Hemu's army and recovered Delhi and Agra.
**Breakdown:**
- Hemu had captured Agra and Delhi and was marching with cavalry, elephants and artillery.
- Hemu's artillery was seized before the decisive fight (✅ Wikipedia: Ali Quli Khan Shaibani's advance cavalry captured the lightly guarded guns).
- Hemu was struck in the eye by a chance arrow and collapsed; his army then broke (✅ Wikipedia).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 5 November 1556 (✅ Wikipedia: "Second Battle of Panipat was fought on 5 November 1556"). Akbar was crowned at Kalanaur earlier in 1556 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** MED-3003 (Hemu), MED-3004 (accession), MED-3005 (Bairam Khan).
**Trap:** Hemu's wound was a contingency inside a wider contest of command, artillery, cavalry and elephants; the arrow alone is not the whole cause. Victory ended Hemu's Delhi regime but not Afghan resistance (✅ owner 15).
**Mains hook:** Use Panipat II as one unit of the reconquest argument, then qualify: contingency and command, not a civilisational war.
**Back:** *"Five November, fifty-six: arrow, artillery lost, Hemu down, Delhi back."*

### Flashcard MED-3003 — Hemu (Hemchandra): Afghan general, not a Hindu avatar
**Front:** Who was Hemu and why must he not be read as a purely Hindu anti-Mughal leader?
**Definition (learn):** A Sur-Afghan military commander who rose through the Sur/Adil Shah system and captured Agra and Delhi in 1556.
**Breakdown:**
- Earlier superintendent of markets under Islam Shah; later Adil Shah's general/wazir (✅ owner 15).
- His army was largely Afghan; his rise reflects Afghan political openness (✅ owner 15 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Defeated at Panipat II, 5 November 1556 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-3002, MED-3001.
**Trap:** "Hemu proves a Hindu resurgence" is a trap; the owner stresses Afghan composition and social mobility. Also note that the Wikipedia infobox treats him as "king Hemu" while the Mughal court narrative sees him as a usurper.
**Mains hook:** Use Hemu to show social mobility in the Afghan system; avoid communal framing of 1556 (✅ owner 15 historiography note).
**Back:** *"Market man, Adil's general, Afghan army: not a Hindu crusade."*

### Flashcard MED-3004 — Akbar's accession at Kalanaur (1556)
**Front:** Where and at what age was Akbar crowned, and what was his position?
**Definition (learn):** Akbar succeeded Humayun in 1556 at Kalanaur and was a minor under the regency of Bairam Khan.
**Breakdown:**
- Age at coronation: thirteen years and four months (✅ owner 15).
- Mughal coalition was small; Kabul and Mirza threats were live (✅ owner 15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Kalanaur, 1556; born Amarkot, 1542 (✅ owner 15; Akbar born 15 Oct 1542 per owner 15 advanced).
**Links:** MED-3002, MED-3005.
**Trap:** Akbar was born at Amarkot (1542), not Kalanaur; Kalanaur is the coronation site.
**Mains hook:** The minority accession explains why regency and noble factions shaped the first phase.
**Back:** *"Thirteen at Kalanaur, Amarkot-born, Bairam beside."*

### Flashcard MED-3005 — Bairam Khan: wakil mutlaq and regent (1556–60)
**Front:** Who was Bairam Khan, what titles did he hold, and what was his period of power?
**Definition (learn):** Bairam Khan was Akbar's regent (wakil mutlaq), and Khan-i-Khanan, who controlled the nobility and rallied Mughal forces during the minority.
**Breakdown:**
- Stabilised the empire after Panipat II.
- His dominance provoked noble jealousy and Akbar's wish to rule directly (✅ owner 15 advanced).
- Regency approximately four years, 1556–60 (✅ owner 15 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dismissed 1560; assassinated at Patan near Ahmedabad on the way to Mecca (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** MED-3006 (fall), MED-3016 (Haldighati), MED-3041 (vakil and central offices).
**Trap:** Bairam's fall was not explained by his Shia identity alone (✅ owner 15 advanced trap). Rebellion, pardon and assassination are distinct events.
**Mains hook:** Regency vs kingship: the regency gave stability, but its success created the reason for dismissal.
**Back:** *"Bairam the regent, Patan the end: stability brought, royalty withheld."*

### Flashcard MED-3006 — Bairam Khan's fall (1560)
**Front:** Why did Akbar remove Bairam Khan in 1560?
**Definition (learn):** Akbar dismissed Bairam Khan to assert personal rule and break court factionalism.
**Breakdown:**
- Causes: court factions, appointments and authority, Akbar's desire to rule directly (✅ owner 15).
- Not reducible to sect (✅ owner 15 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1560 (✅ owner 15 and 15 advanced).
**Links:** MED-3005, MED-3007.
**Trap:** Dismissal ≠ execution. Bairam was allowed to go on pilgrimage and was later killed at Patan.
**Mains hook:** Use to show the transition from regency to personal rule.
**Back:** *"Sixty dismissal, Patan later: personal rule replaced the regent."*

### Flashcard MED-3007 — Atka Khan's murder and Adham Khan's execution (1562)
**Front:** Which household-faction episode in 1562 asserted Akbar's personal sovereignty?
**Definition (learn):** Adham Khan, a household-connected commander (⚠️ foster-kin link is standard knowledge, not in owner text), killed Atka Khan; Akbar had Adham executed, which asserted personal sovereignty.
**Breakdown:**
- Household factions (Maham Anaga, Adham Khan, Atka Khan) illustrate access, patronage and armed followers (✅ owner 15).
- Malwa exposed Adham/Pir Muhammad violence and limits of delegated command (✅ owner 15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1562 (✅ owner 15 advanced timeline).
**Links:** MED-3009 (Malwa), MED-3011 (Uzbek revolt), MED-3023 (household and noble control).
**Trap:** "Petticoat government" is a misogynistic gossip label, not analysis (✅ owner 15).
**Mains hook:** Household patronage vs royal authority; show how the emperor reasserted control by executions and pardons.
**Back:** *"Atka dead, Adham dead: a king's household answers to the king."*

### Flashcard MED-3008 — Bhara Mal of Amber: first Rajput alliance (1562)
**Front:** Which Rajput chief entered alliance first, and when?
**Definition (learn):** Bhara Mal, ruler of Amber, submitted and entered alliance with Akbar in 1562, before the Chittor campaign.
**Breakdown:**
- Matrimonial ties were one part of the alliance (✅ owner 15).
- His successors Bhagwant Das and Man Singh rose to high command (✅ owner 15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1562 (✅ owner 15 advanced and basic section 14).
**Links:** MED-3012 (Chittor), MED-3017 (Rajput policy).
**Trap:** "Rajput policy began after Chittor" is wrong; Bhara Mal's alliance preceded Chittor.
**Mains hook:** Alliance as integration, not conquest-first.
**Back:** *"Sixty-two: Amber first, Chittor after."*

### Flashcard MED-3009 — Malwa and Baz Bahadur (1561)
**Front:** What happened in Malwa in 1561?
**Definition (learn):** Mughal expedition against Baz Bahadur, the Malwa ruler, who was defeated.
**Breakdown:**
- Baz Bahadur was later enrolled as a Mughal mansabdar and reached 2000 rank (✅ owner 15 advanced).
- Malwa exposed the violence of Adham/Pir Muhammad (✅ owner 15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1561 (✅ owner 15 basic mini-timeline).
**Links:** MED-3007, MED-3014 (Gujarat).
**Trap:** Malwa's conquest did not end the misrule and revolt that followed; provincial governance was contested.
**Mains hook:** Malwa shows that conquest needed governors who did not overreach.
**Back:** *"Sixty-one Malwa: Baz beaten, then Baz became a mansabdar."*

### Flashcard MED-3010 — Garh-Katanga and Rani Durgavati (1564)
**Front:** What was the outcome of Garh-Katanga (1564) and who resisted?
**Definition (learn):** Garh-Katanga (Gondwana) campaign in which Rani Durgavati resisted and died; Mughal plunder followed.
**Breakdown:**
- Conquest plus plunder: qualifies triumphalist accounts of integration (✅ owner 15).
- Regional control was not an uncomplicated integration (✅ owner 15 table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1564 (✅ owner 15 section 13).
**Links:** MED-3012 (Chittor massacre), MED-3001.
**Trap:** Do not present Garh-Katanga as a peaceful integration case; Durgavati's death and plunder are the examinable point.
**Mains hook:** Coercion as part of the same policy as alliance.
**Back:** *"Sixty-four Gondwana: Durgavati fell, plunder followed."*

### Flashcard MED-3011 — Uzbek/Turani revolt and Khan-i-Zaman (defeat June 1567)
**Front:** What was the Uzbek rebellion and when was it defeated?
**Definition (learn):** A rebellion of older Turani/Uzbek nobles against Akbar's centralising authority; defeated in June 1567, opening the road to systematic expansion.
**Breakdown:**
- Involved jagir and command, regional bases and central control (✅ owner 15 advanced).
- Khan-i-Zaman was a principal leader (✅ owner 15 section 12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** June 1567 (✅ owner 15 advanced).
**Links:** MED-3001 (concept), MED-3012 (Chittor 1568).
**Trap:** Defeat did not eliminate faction (✅ owner 15).
**Mains hook:** Noble resistance was part of state formation.
**Back:** *"Sixty-seven June: Uzbek old guard broken, road to expansion opened."*

### Flashcard MED-3012 — Chittor (1568): siege, massacre and symbol
**Front:** Why was Chittor important and what did its fall show?
**Definition (learn):** Chittor, a symbolic Mewar fort controlling routes to Gujarat and Malwa, fell to Akbar in 1568 after siege and massacre.
**Breakdown:**
- Symbolised Rajput resistance and controlled routes to Gujarat (✅ owner 15).
- Massacre qualifies "integrationist" narratives (✅ owner 15 advanced).
- Udai Singh retained the Mewar line (✅ owner 15 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Chittor 1568 (March 1568 per owner 15 advanced timeline).
**Links:** MED-3008, MED-3017, MED-3016 (Haldighati).
**Trap:** Chittor's fall was not a total surrender of Mewar; resistance continued.
**Mains hook:** Force and alliance coexisted: the massacre shows coercion behind accommodation.
**Back:** *"Sixty-eight Chittor: the fort fell, the massacre stayed."*

### Flashcard MED-3013 — Ranthambhor and Kalinjar (1569)
**Front:** What did Ranthambhor and Kalinjar contribute to Akbar's Rajasthan strategy?
**Definition (learn):** Fort submissions/conquests in 1569 strengthened route security in Rajasthan.
**Breakdown:**
- Ranthambhor: Surjan Hada served without a matrimonial link (✅ owner 15 advanced).
- Rajasthan was a security flank and route to Gujarat and Malwa (✅ owner 15 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1569 (✅ owner 15 section 13).
**Links:** MED-3012, MED-3017.
**Trap:** Rajput alliance was not only marriage: Surjan Hada's example.
**Mains hook:** Route security as a non-territorial motive.
**Back:** *"Sixty-nine: Ranthambhor and Kalinjar kept the roads open."*

### Flashcard MED-3014 — Gujarat conquest (1572–73) and the sea at Cambay
**Front:** Why did Gujarat matter, and what did Akbar's rapid return march show?
**Definition (learn):** Conquest of Gujarat in 1572–73 gave Akbar fertile soil, crafts, ports and maritime trade; he reached the sea at Cambay for the first time.
**Breakdown:**
- Economic motive: ports and trade (✅ owner 15).
- A rapid second march broke the revolt (✅ owner 15 advanced).
- Gujarat required repeated suppression of revolts (✅ owner 15 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1572–73; Cambay (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** MED-3015 (Bengal), MED-3025 (dahsala after Gujarat-era revenue reform).
**Trap:** Gujarat was not conquered for glory alone; its trade and revenue made it strategic. Rapid march shows mobility, not miraculous speed.
**Mains hook:** Contrast Akbar's Gujarat management with Humayun's earlier failure to hold Gujarat (✅ owner 15 study link).
**Back:** *"Seventy-two-three: ports and sea, Cambay tasted, Gujarat held by speed."*

### Flashcard MED-3015 — Bengal: Daud Khan Karrani defeated (1576)
**Front:** When and where was Daud Khan Karrani defeated, and what did it end?
**Definition (learn):** Daud Khan Karrani, the last independent Afghan ruler in Bengal, was defeated at Tukaroi/Rajmahal in 1576.
**Breakdown:**
- Ended the independent Karrani state in Bengal (✅ owner 15).
- Did not end all Afghan resistance or eastern frontier instability (✅ owner 15).
- Required boats, garrisons, appointments, riverine logistics (✅ owner 15 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1576 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** MED-3014, MED-3018 (eastern revolt 1580–81), MED-3023.
**Trap:** Daud's defeat ≠ end of Afghan resistance; eastern consolidation was uneven.
**Mains hook:** Military victory needed revenue personnel, zamindars and local alliances.
**Back:** *"Seventy-six Bengal: Daud down, Afghans not out."*

### Flashcard MED-3016 — Haldighati (18 June 1576)
**Front:** Who fought at Haldighati, and what did the battle not achieve?
**Definition (learn):** Battle between Man Singh's Mughal army and Maharana Pratap of Mewar.
**Breakdown:**
- Mughal commander: Man Singh of Amber (✅ Wikipedia).
- Mewar side included Afghans under Hakim Khan Sur, who was killed (✅ Wikipedia and owner 15 advanced).
- Bhils also fought with Pratap (✅ owner 15 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 18 June 1576 (✅ Wikipedia; a footnote gives Badauni's date 21 June 1576).
**Links:** MED-3012 (Chittor), MED-3008 (Amber alliance).
**Trap:** Haldighati was a political-military contest, not a Hindu-Muslim battle; it did not capture Pratap or end resistance (✅ owner 15).
**Mains hook:** Mixed coalitions on both sides refute communal framing.
**Back:** *"Seventy-six June eighteen: Man Singh won the field, Pratap won the war of attrition."*

### Flashcard MED-3017 — Rajput policy: three phases of differentiated coalition
**Front:** What were the phases of Akbar's Rajput policy?
**Definition (learn):** Part II's three-phase account: early loyal allies; Rajputs as the sword-arm of the empire during Gujarat; partners in the kingdom after 1578.
**Breakdown:**
- Incorporation: Bhara Mal, Bhagwant Das, Man Singh (high command, mansab, governorship).
- Coercion: Chittor, Ranthambhor, Haldighati.
- Mewar was the outlier: Udai Singh's line retained rule; Pratap refused personal submission (✅ owner 15 advanced; owner 15 basic section 14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Phases roughly 1560s, 1570s, post-1578 (✅ owner 15 advanced).
**Links:** MED-3008, MED-3012, MED-3016, MED-3139 (Mewar 1615 settlement).
**Trap:** Akbar's Rajput policy was graded: not a simple story of forced marriages (✅ owner 15 advanced).
**Mains hook:** Alliance transformed the ruling class into a composite elite; test against Mewar's stubbornness.
**Back:** *"Allies, sword-arm, partners: three stages, one composite nobility."*

### Flashcard MED-3018 — Mirza Hakim and the 1580–81 crisis; court shifts to Lahore (1585)
**Front:** What was the 1580–81 crisis and how did Akbar respond on the north-west frontier?
**Definition (learn):** A Bihar-Bengal rebellion coincided with Mirza Hakim's Kabul challenge; Akbar reconsolidated and later moved the court to Lahore (1585–98) for north-west supervision.
**Breakdown:**
- Eastern rebellion and Mirza Hakim crisis (1580–81) (✅ owner 15 advanced).
- Lahore court 1585–98 (✅ owner 15 section 23).
- Mirza Hakim died in 1585; Akbar annexed Kabul (✅ owner 19 basic and owner 15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1580–81; 1585; Lahore 1585–98.
**Links:** MED-3112 (Balkh 1572–83), MED-3114 (Kabul annexed), MED-3019.
**Trap:** Fatehpur Sikri's decline after 1585 has several explanations; "water ran out" is too simple (✅ owner 15 section 23).
**Mains hook:** Simultaneous centre-and-frontier pressure shaped the imperial geography.
**Back:** *"Eighty-five: Mirza gone, Kabul annexed, court to Lahore."*

### Flashcard MED-3019 — The frontier ladder: Kashmir 1586, Sindh 1591, Qandahar 1595
**Front:** Give the dated frontier campaigns of Akbar's later reign.
**Definition (learn):** Different frontier problems rather than one line: Kashmir (1586), Sindh/Thatta (1591), Odisha (c. 1592), Baluchistan/Makran (1590s), Qandahar (1595).
**Breakdown:**
- Kashmir: annexation after intervention and factional conflict; valley control remained specific.
- Sindh/Thatta: submission expanded the lower-Indus reach.
- Qandahar: diplomatic-political acquisition from a contested Safavid frontier setting.
- Odisha: Afghan and regional resistance reduced; incorporation remained uneven.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Kashmir 1586; Sindh 1591; Odisha c.1592; Qandahar 1595 (✅ owner 15 section 13).
**Links:** MED-3018, MED-3020, MED-3116 (Qandahar 1595 in foreign policy).
**Trap:** Kashmir 1586, Sindh 1591 and Qandahar 1595 are not one wave; each used different combinations of force, faction and negotiation (✅ owner 15).
**Mains hook:** Map claims ≠ control; use control types.
**Back:** *"Eighty-six Kashmir, ninety-one Sindh, ninety-five Qandahar: three tools, three outcomes."*

### Flashcard MED-3020 — Roshanai movement and Bayazid Ansari
**Front:** What was the Roshanai movement and why is it not a simple foreign invasion?
**Definition (learn):** A Pashtun frontier movement associated with Bayazid Ansari that combined religious, social and political dimensions.
**Breakdown:**
- Yusufzai and other Pashtun communities were not one timeless "tribal threat" (✅ owner 15 basic section 16).
- Uzbek encouragement did not erase frontier agency (✅ owner 19 basic section 7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Associated with Akbar's north-west crisis (1580s) (✅ owner 15 and 19).
**Links:** MED-3018, MED-3114 (Kabul), MED-3115 (Hindukush arrangement).
**Trap:** "Roshanai was a religious war" is a trap; treat it as a mixed local movement.
**Mains hook:** Route control on the frontier needed local negotiation, not only diplomacy with Bukhara.
**Back:** *"Bayazid's Roshanai: religion, society, route, all at once."*

### Flashcard MED-3021 — Late Deccan opening: Ahmadnagar, Berar, Asirgarh, Khandesh (1595–1601)
**Front:** What did Akbar gain in the Deccan before his death in 1605?
**Definition (learn):** Akbar's late Deccan gains: Berar ceded by Ahmadnagar (1596); Ahmadnagar fort taken (1600); Asirgarh and Khandesh annexed (1601).
**Breakdown:**
- Chand Bibi defended Ahmadnagar (1595) and a treaty ceded Berar (✅ owner 15 advanced; owner 18 basic).
- Ahmadnagar fort fell 1600; the Nizam Shahi state and resistance continued (✅ owner 15).
- Khandesh and Asirgarh 1601: surrender narratives require caution (✅ owner 15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1595–96 Berar; 1600 Ahmadnagar fort; 1601 Asirgarh/Khandesh.
**Links:** MED-3078 (Chand Bibi), MED-3080, MED-3081 (in Topic 18 group).
**Trap:** Mughal possession of the fort did not mean control of the Nizam Shahi remnant (✅ owner 15 section 17).
**Mains hook:** Deccan frontier unfinished at Akbar's death.
**Back:** *"Ninety-six Berar, nought Ahmadnagar, one Asirgarh: Deccan begun, not done."*

### Flashcard MED-3022 — Salim's rival court and Abu'l Fazl's killing (1602)
**Front:** What did Prince Salim do between 1599 and 1605, and how was Abu'l Fazl killed?
**Definition (learn):** Salim maintained a separate political base at Allahabad (about 1599–1604), issued coins and appointments, and built a noble network; Abu'l Fazl was killed in 1602 by Bir Singh Deo Bundela in an action linked to Salim.
**Breakdown:**
- Allahabad establishment: autonomous appointments and coinage (✅ owner 15 section 24; owner 20 basic section 2).
- Akbar's reconciliation with Salim shaped 1605 succession (✅ owner 15 section 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Allahabad c.1599–1604; Abu'l Fazl killed 1602 (✅ owner 15 and owner 20).
**Links:** MED-3023 (negotiated sovereignty), MED-3133 (Jahangir's accession bargain).
**Trap:** Abu'l Fazl's killing belongs to the succession conflict, not to Akbar's campaigns.
**Mains hook:** Succession was negotiated, not automatic.
**Back:** *"Ninety-nine to 1604 Allahabad, 1602 Fazl dead: Salim's rival court."*

### Flashcard MED-3023 — Negotiated sovereignty: the repertoire of imperial control
**Front:** What tools did Akbar combine to convert conquest into durable control?
**Definition (learn):** A set of tools: alliance, incorporation, coercion, pardon, marriage, rank, watan/jagir, hostages, tribute and recognition.
**Breakdown:**
- Imperial core (Agra–Delhi–Ganga) → suba claims → allied/watan rulers → fort/route frontiers → campaign zones (✅ owner 15 section 18).
- Control was denser around courts, roads, revenue centres and loyal elites; thinner in mountains, forests, deltas, frontiers (✅ owner 15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Used across the reign 1556–1605 (✅ owner 15).
**Links:** MED-3001, MED-3017, MED-3024, MED-3044 (faujdar).
**Trap:** "Conquest = control" is wrong. Map claim ≠ control.
**Mains hook:** Uneven control explains why later Mughal rule needed repeated adaptation.
**Back:** *"Alliance, rank, pardon, fort: the Akbar toolkit, not one blow."*

### GROUP — State & Government under Akbar (Mansabdari, Dahsala)

### Flashcard MED-3024 — Concept: Akbar's state and government (administrative ideal vs practice)
**Front:** What is the analytical distinction for Akbar's administrative system?
**Definition (learn):** Distinguish the ideal office chart (Ain-i Akbari), assessed figures, actual realisation, regional practice and later reconstruction.
**Breakdown:**
- Akbar's padshahship centred appointment, transfer, justice, command and access on the ruler (✅ owner 16 advanced section 11).
- Checks meant overlapping centrally appointed counterweights, not separation of powers.
- Mansabdari, zabt-dahsala, and suba structure were institutions that evolved (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dahsala 1580; twelve subas 1580; zat-sawar 1595–96 (see cards below).
**Links:** MED-3025 onward (group 16 cards).
**Trap:** Do not project the later jagirdari crisis backward into Akbar's reign (✅ owner 16 advanced section 22).
**Mains hook:** The best Akbar-era answer distinguishes ideal, assessed and realised.
**Back:** *"Ideal, assessed, realised: three columns of the same Akbar state."*

### Flashcard MED-3025 — Dahsala (c. 1580): ten-year averages for annual cash rates
**Front:** What was the dahsala, when was it introduced, and what share did it fix?
**Definition (learn):** A revenue schedule introduced around 1580 that used ten years of produce and price data to fix annual cash rates within measurement-based zabt assessment.
**Breakdown:**
- Ten-year average of produce and prices, grouped into regional schedules (dastur) (✅ owner 16 basic and advanced).
- State demand framed near one-third of average produce, stated in cash (✅ owner 16).
- Implemented by a team: Muzaffar Khan Turbati, Todar Mal and Shah Mansur (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1580; one-third norm (⚠️ a norm, not uniform realisation). Wikipedia dahsala page returned 404; not fetch-verified (✅ owner 16 only).
**Links:** MED-3026 (zabti), MED-3027 (Todar Mal), MED-3038 (dastur), MED-3028–3030 (alternatives).
**Trap:** Dahsala is not a permanent ten-year settlement, and it is not a ten-year tax paid at once. The state could modify rates. Dahsala ≠ zabt ≠ batai (✅ owner 16 advanced concept note).
**Mains hook:** Dahsala was fiscal engineering that made demand predictable while remaining mediated by local prices and realisation gaps.
**Back:** *"Eighty: ten years averaged, one-third in cash, annual rate set."*

### Flashcard MED-3026 — Zabt (zabti): measurement-based assessment
**Front:** What is zabt (zabti) and what measurement technique supported it?
**Definition (learn):** Field measurement followed by a scheduled cash demand; the measurement-based base on which dahsala rates were applied.
**Breakdown:**
- Standardised Ilahi gaz and bigha traditions (✅ owner 16 advanced).
- Bamboo jarib joined with iron rings to reduce stretching (✅ owner 16 basic and advanced).
- Applied in the settled core: Lahore to Allahabad, Malwa and Gujarat (✅ owner 16 basic table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Associated with the 1570s–1580 measurement reform (✅ owner 16 advanced, section 15).
**Links:** MED-3025, MED-3028 (batai), MED-3029 (kankut), MED-3030 (nasaq), MED-3027 (Todar Mal).
**Trap:** Zabt probably did not cover every field even in a nominal zabt province (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Mains hook:** Measurement reduced concealment by intermediaries but needed trained staff, unavailable in forest, hill and frontier tracts.
**Back:** *"Zabt: bamboo measured, iron-ringed, cash demanded."*

### Flashcard MED-3027 — Todar Mal: organiser, not sole inventor
**Front:** What was Todar Mal's role in Akbar's revenue reform?
**Definition (learn):** A major revenue official and organiser of the zabt-dahsala system, working in a team.
**Breakdown:**
- Known as Todar Mal's bandobast, but Chandra stresses team effort (✅ owner 16 basic and advanced).
- Also: mushrif-i-diwan in 1575 (✅ owner 16 advanced timeline).
- Hindu officer in the high service, cited with Birbal (✅ owner 17 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mushrif-i-diwan 1575; dahsala c.1580 (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** MED-3025, MED-3026, MED-3051 (sulh-i-kul inclusion).
**Trap:** "Todar Mal alone created the system" is wrong (✅ owner 16 trap).
**Mains hook:** Credit systemic reforms to officials and precedents rather than one personality.
**Back:** *"Todar Mal led, a team measured."*

### Flashcard MED-3028 — Batai (ghalla-bakhshi): crop-sharing
**Front:** What is batai and when was it used?
**Definition (learn):** Crop-sharing: produce divided in a fixed proportion after harvest, used where measurement or cash demand was unsuitable.
**Breakdown:**
- Also called ghalla-bakhshi (✅ owner 16 basic table).
- Variants differed in the timing of division (✅ owner 16 advanced section 17).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Alternative to zabt; no single date (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** MED-3025, MED-3026, MED-3029, MED-3030.
**Trap:** Batai ≠ dahsala ≠ zabt (✅ owner 16 advanced concept note).
**Mains hook:** Alternative methods show the state adapted revenue to crop and terrain.
**Back:** *"Batai: sharing the sheaf, not the cash."*

### Flashcard MED-3029 — Kankut: appraisement of standing crop
**Front:** What is kankut?
**Definition (learn):** Assessment by measurement plus inspection or estimate of the standing crop.
**Breakdown:**
- Sits between full measurement and crop-sharing (✅ owner 16 basic table).
- Used where measurement was difficult or crops failed (✅ owner 16 basic section 4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No single date (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** MED-3026, MED-3028, MED-3030.
**Trap:** Kankut is not zabt; it estimates the crop.
**Mains hook:** Shows the limits of centralised measurement.
**Back:** *"Kankut: eye the standing crop."*

### Flashcard MED-3030 — Nasaq: estimate from previous assessment
**Front:** What is nasaq?
**Definition (learn):** An estimate based substantially on previous assessment or payment rather than fresh full measurement.
**Breakdown:**
- Avoids new measurement (✅ owner 16 basic table).
- Used where local rates or past payments provided a baseline (✅ owner 16 advanced table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No single date (✅ owner 16).
**Links:** MED-3026, MED-3028, MED-3029.
**Trap:** Nasaq ≠ zabt; it reuses past assessment.
**Mains hook:** A sign of uneven state capacity across terrain and crops.
**Back:** *"Nasaq: last year's number, carried forward."*

### Flashcard MED-3031 — Mansab: rank, salary and service obligation
**Front:** What was a mansab and what did it fix?
**Definition (learn):** A numerical rank in the imperial service hierarchy that fixed status, salary and service obligation.
**Breakdown:**
- Ladder from low ranks to high; Wikipedia says commands above 5000 were reserved for princes, and the ceiling later rose (✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar).
- Man Singh and Aziz Koka reached 7000 under Akbar (✅ owner 16 advanced).
- Numerical ranks generally associated with the later 1560s (✅ owner 16 advanced timeline: 1567, 11th regnal year).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ladder 10 to 5000 (owner); 7000 for top nobles (owner); Wikipedia: Habib gives 7000 zat and 7000 sawar for non-royal top ranks.
**Links:** MED-3032 (zat-sawar), MED-3033 (dagh), MED-3034 (jagir), MED-3045 (ahadis).
**Trap:** Mansab ≠ land grant; mansabdar ≠ salaried civil servant only (✅ owner 16).
**Mains hook:** Mansabdari as a single civil-military service; qualify with patronage and hierarchy.
**Back:** *"Mansab: rank first, pay and horse second."*

### Flashcard MED-3032 — Zat and sawar: the dual rank of 1595–96
**Front:** What do zat and sawar mean and when did the dual rank appear?
**Definition (learn):** Zat was personal rank and pay; sawar was the number of horsemen to be maintained. From about 1595 a mansab was stated as two numbers.
**Breakdown:**
- Standard example: 4000/3000 means 4000 zat and 3000 sawar (✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar).
- Late-Akbar introduction: 40th regnal year, 1595–96 (✅ owner 16 advanced).
- Dating varies: Aziz identifies a second sawar rank in the 41st regnal year; Streusand dates the separation 1596–97 (✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar). ⚠️ Owner 16 uses 1595–96; sources differ by a year.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 40th regnal year (1595–96) per owner; 41st per Aziz; 1596–97 per Streusand.
**Links:** MED-3031, MED-3033.
**Trap:** Zat-sawar is late-Akbar, not timeless; earlier meanings cannot be read through the later dual formula (✅ owner 16 advanced trap).
**Mains hook:** The split tied salary to verifiable cavalry obligation.
**Back:** *"Ninety-five Zat for the person, Sawar for the horse."*

### Flashcard MED-3033 — Dagh (horse branding) and chehra (soldier description)
**Front:** What were dagh and chehra and why were they introduced?
**Definition (learn):** Dagh branded horses for verification; chehra was a descriptive roll of soldiers. Both checked paper cavalry against real musters.
**Breakdown:**
- Dagh introduced in the 18th regnal year, 1573–74 (✅ owner 16 advanced).
- Introduced because paper numbers and real musters diverged, causing noble resistance (✅ owner 16 advanced).
- Enforcement weakened later when inspections became lax (✅ owner 16 advanced table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1573–74 (18th regnal year).
**Links:** MED-3031, MED-3032, MED-3045.
**Trap:** Dagh was not a general mansab promotion; it was verification. Dagh did not make paper cavalry disappear.
**Mains hook:** Administrative thoroughness versus implementation.
**Back:** *"Seventy-three branded horse, chehra soldier: paper troops, checked."*

### Flashcard MED-3034 — Jagir: revenue assignment, not ownership
**Front:** What is a jagir and what is it not?
**Definition (learn):** An assignment of state revenue demand (land revenue due from an area) to a mansabdar in lieu of salary; not ownership of soil or peasants.
**Breakdown:**
- Tankhwah jagir: assignment in lieu of salary (✅ owner 16 advanced section 14).
- Transferable; transfers limited hereditary entrenchment but encouraged short-horizon extraction (✅ owner 16 advanced).
- Later conditional and hereditary developments belong to later reigns (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mansabdars usually paid through jagirs (✅ owner 16 basic).
**Links:** MED-3031, MED-3035 (jama-hasil), MED-3036 (khalisa), MED-3018, MED-3091 (Deccan jagirs).
**Trap:** Jagir ≠ ownership; zamindar ≠ jagirdar (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Mains hook:** Jagir transferability created extraction incentives that worsened under later rulers.
**Back:** *"Jagir: the revenue is lent, the land is not given."*

### Flashcard MED-3035 — Jama and hasil: paper demand vs realised revenue
**Front:** Define jama and hasil and explain the gap.
**Definition (learn):** Jama is assessed or expected revenue on paper; hasil is the amount actually realised.
**Breakdown:**
- A high-paper jama could fail to meet salary if hasil was lower (✅ owner 16 advanced section 14).
- Jama-hasil gap is a structural tension of the Akbar period (✅ owner 16 advanced section 22).
- Deccan jagirs often had inflated jama and poor hasil (✅ owner 18 advanced table; owner 18 basic section 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No single date.
**Links:** MED-3034, MED-3099 (Deccan deficit).
**Trap:** Jama ≠ hasil ≠ tax actually paid by peasants after cesses and transport (✅ owner 16 advanced counterpoints).
**Mains hook:** Paper revenue is not the same as state capacity.
**Back:** *"Jama is promised, hasil is paid."*

### Flashcard MED-3036 — Khalisa, madad-i-maash and inam
**Front:** What do khalisa, madad-i-maash and inam mean?
**Definition (learn):** Khalisa: revenue reserved for direct imperial expenditure. Madad-i-maash and inam: revenue-free or charitable grants under separate supervision.
**Breakdown:**
- Khalisa: direct central expenditure (✅ owner 16 advanced table).
- Madad-i-maash/inam: revenue-free and charitable categories (✅ owner 16 advanced).
- Religious grants are linked to the sadr office (✅ owner 16 advanced section 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated in owner.
**Links:** MED-3034, MED-3041 (sadr-us-sudur), MED-3069 (religious patronage and welfare establishments).
**Trap:** Khalisa is not a jagir; inam is not a jagir.
**Mains hook:** Distinguishes state revenue reserved for the centre from revenue assigned to elites.
**Back:** *"Khalisa is the king's cash, inam is the charity's."*

### Flashcard MED-3037 — Polaj, parauti, chachar and banjar: cultivation categories
**Front:** Name the four land categories and their meanings.
**Definition (learn):** Cultivation categories used in assessment: polaj (regularly cultivated), parauti/parati (temporarily fallow), chachar (fallow several years), banjar (long uncultivated).
**Breakdown:**
- Polaj: regularly cultivated (✅ owner 16 advanced section 15).
- Parauti/parati: temporarily fallow (✅ owner 16 advanced).
- Chachar: fallow for several years; banjar: long uncultivated (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3026 (zabt), MED-3025 (dahsala).
**Trap:** These are cultivation categories, not social classes (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Mains hook:** Measurement categories show how the state read the land.
**Back:** *"Polaj ploughed, parauti pausing, chachar chilling, banjar gone."*

### Flashcard MED-3038 — Dastur: regional crop cash schedule
**Front:** What is a dastur?
**Definition (learn):** A regional schedule or circle of crop rates, not a universal empire price.
**Breakdown:**
- Forms regional crop cash schedules from ten-year data (✅ owner 16 advanced section 16).
- Measured against local productivity and prices (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3025, MED-3026.
**Trap:** Dastur ≠ universal price list.
**Mains hook:** Regional rates explain variation within one imperial system.
**Back:** *"Dastur: the district's rate card."*

### Flashcard MED-3039 — Twelve subas (1580) and the fifteen later
**Front:** How many subas did Akbar create and how did the count change?
**Definition (learn):** Empire divided into twelve subas in 1580; the count is commonly given as fifteen by the end of the reign after Deccan additions.
**Breakdown:**
- Twelve subas in 1580 (✅ owner 16 basic and advanced).
- Later count: fifteen, after Deccan additions (✅ owner 16 advanced section 12).
- Frontier attachment requires record-specific caution (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1580; twelve; fifteen later (owner).
**Links:** MED-3040, MED-3042 (sarkar and pargana).
**Trap:** Twelve subas in 1580; the count changed later (✅ owner 16 advanced Prelims traps).
**Mains hook:** Provincial structure was a hierarchy repeated in provinces.
**Back:** *"Eighty: twelve subas, fifteen later."*

### Flashcard MED-3040 — Provincial checks: subadar, diwan, bakhshi, sadr-qazi, news-writer
**Front:** Which officers in each suba checked one another?
**Definition (learn):** Provincial government repeated the centre's checks: governor (subadar/sipahsalar), provincial diwan, bakhshi, sadr-qazi and news writer (waqia-navis), appointed from the centre.
**Breakdown:**
- Governor did not control revenue independently (✅ owner 16 advanced table row 5).
- Diwan and bakhshi were centrally linked and could check the governor (✅ owner 16 advanced section 12).
- The subedar's real power often exceeded formal checks (✅ owner 16 advanced table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3039, MED-3041, MED-3048 (qazi).
**Trap:** Checks were overlapping counterweights, not separation of powers (✅ owner 16 advanced section 11).
**Mains hook:** Use to show central control through appointed pairs.
**Back:** *"Subadar, diwan, bakhshi, sadr, news: five eyes on one province."*

### Flashcard MED-3041 — Central offices: wakil, wazir/diwan, mir bakhshi, sadr, mir saman
**Front:** What did the main central offices do?
**Definition (learn):** Central departments under Akbar: wakil (regency/general authority), wazir/diwan-i-ala (finance), mir bakhshi (mansab presentation, muster, intelligence), sadr-us-sudur (religious grants, charity), qazi-ul-quzat (judicial law), mir saman (household and stores).
**Breakdown:**
- Wakil: regency power under Bairam; reduced later (✅ owner 16 advanced table).
- Diwan: finance, khalisa, jagir, expenditure; mir bakhshi: mansab, presentation, pay verification (✅ owner 16 advanced).
- Mir bakhshi is not simply a defence minister (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not individually dated in owner.
**Links:** MED-3005 (Bairam), MED-3049 (trap), MED-3040.
**Trap:** Diwan ≠ mir bakhshi; wakil ≠ permanently all-powerful wazir (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Mains hook:** Akbar's checks were centre-appointed counterweights.
**Back:** *"Wakil regent, diwan money, bakhshi men, sadr grants, saman stores."*

### Flashcard MED-3042 — Sarkar, pargana, village: the local hierarchy
**Front:** What is the ascending local administrative ladder?
**Definition (learn):** Village → pargana → sarkar → suba (ascending). Officers: faujdar and amalguzar at sarkar; shiqdar, amil/amin, qanungo, chaudhuri, fotedar and karkuns at pargana; muqaddam, patwari and panchayat in village.
**Breakdown:**
- Sarkar: faujdar (military-police), amalguzar/amil (revenue) and record staff (✅ owner 16 advanced section 12).
