- Pargana: shiqdar, amil/amin, qanungo, chaudhuri (✅ owner 16 advanced).
- Village: muqaddam (headman), patwari, panchayat (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ascending order village → pargana → sarkar → suba (✅ owner 16 advanced section 24, linked to 2021 Prelims Q45).
**Links:** MED-3039, MED-3043, MED-3044.
**Trap:** Formal hierarchy did not erase zamindars, chiefs and customary law (✅ owner 16).
**Mains hook:** The ladder is clean on paper; practice varied with jurisdiction.
**Back:** *"Village, pargana, sarkar, suba: climb up, the paper says."*

### Flashcard MED-3043 — Qanungo and chaudhuri: local record and rural elites
**Front:** Who were the qanungo and the chaudhuri?
**Definition (learn):** Qanungo preserved local revenue and custom records; chaudhuri represented and mediated pargana-level rural elites.
**Breakdown:**
- Qanungo: local knowledge of revenue and custom (✅ owner 16 advanced).
- Chaudhuri: pargana elite mediator (✅ owner 16 advanced).
- Both were indispensable local information and bargaining nodes (✅ owner 16 advanced section 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3042, MED-3026.
**Trap:** Qanungo ≠ chaudhuri; both are local, but the qanungo kept records and the chaudhuri mediated.
**Mains hook:** Local intermediaries made imperial assessment possible.
**Back:** *"Qanungo keeps the ledger, chaudhuri brokers the village."*

### Flashcard MED-3044 — Faujdar: mobile military-police officer
**Front:** What was the faujdar's role?
**Definition (learn):** A mobile military-police and route-order officer whose jurisdiction need not match every sarkar.
**Breakdown:**
- Roads and order, not revenue (✅ owner 16 advanced section 12).
- Jurisdiction could differ from sarkar boundaries (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3042, MED-3023 (negotiated control).
**Trap:** Faujdar ≠ amalguzar.
**Mains hook:** Security and revenue were separate officers.
**Back:** *"Faujdar keeps the road, amil keeps the rent."*

### Flashcard MED-3045 — Ahadis and the army: mansab contingents and the paper-army problem
**Front:** What were ahadis and how did the army work?
**Definition (learn):** Ahadis were individually enrolled cavalry directly associated with the emperor; most of the army came from mansab contingents tied to rank and jagir or cash.
**Breakdown:**
- Mansab contingents: elite command tied to rank (✅ owner 16 advanced table).
- Artillery, matchlocks, infantry, elephants, forts and naval/river forces (✅ owner 16 advanced).
- Muster and dagh could expose paper troops, but corruption persisted (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3031, MED-3033.
**Trap:** Army was not mansab-only; ahadis qualify that claim (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Mains hook:** Technology did not substitute for finance or logistics.
**Back:** *"Ahadis direct, mansabdars contract, dagh checks."*

### Flashcard MED-3046 — Ilahi era and the calendar
**Front:** What was the Ilahi era and how is it different from Tauhid-i-Ilahi?
**Definition (learn):** A calendar introduced in the 1580s and back-reckoned to Akbar's accession, serving fiscal and court ordering.
**Breakdown:**
- Back-reckoned to accession (✅ owner 16 advanced section 20).
- Not the same thing as Tauhid-i-Ilahi religious terminology (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1580s (owner).
**Links:** MED-3060 (Tauhid-i-Ilahi).
**Trap:** Ilahi calendar/era ≠ Tauhid-i-Ilahi (✅ owner 16 advanced Prelims trap).
**Mains hook:** Calendar as a state tool.
**Back:** *"Ilahi is the calendar, Tauhid is the creed."*

### Flashcard MED-3047 — Jharoka darshan: a daily public window
**Front:** What was the jharoka darshan?
**Definition (learn):** A ritual public appearance from a window (jharoka) through which the ruler showed himself daily, creating a personal bond with subjects.
**Breakdown:**
- Akbar's innovation (✅ owner 16 advanced Must-Know facts).
- Ritual sovereignty as a visible hierarchy (✅ owner 16 advanced section 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated in owner.
**Links:** MED-3022, MED-3024.
**Trap:** Jharoka was a ritual of sovereignty, not a modern public audience.
**Mains hook:** Ritual as a tool of imperial legitimacy.
**Back:** *"Jharoka: the window of sovereignty."*

### Flashcard MED-3048 — Qazi, mir adl and legal pluralism
**Front:** How did justice work under Akbar?
**Definition (learn):** Justice was a mix of qazi, mir adl and local or customary forums; royal justice rhetoric did not guarantee impersonal equality.
**Breakdown:**
- Qazi-ul-quzat: judicial and religious law functions (✅ owner 16 advanced table).
- Local and customary forums continued alongside (✅ owner 16 advanced section 20).
- Legal pluralism (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3040, MED-3041, MED-3052 (mahzar).
**Trap:** Royal justice ≠ equal justice.
**Mains hook:** Legal pluralism and imperial authority.
**Back:** *"Qazi, mir adl, custom: many courts, one emperor."*

### Flashcard MED-3049 — Trap: wakil ≠ wazir; diwan ≠ mir bakhshi
**Front:** Which two office pairs do examiners confuse?
**Definition (learn):** Wakil (regency, general authority under Bairam) and wazir (finance) are different; diwan (finance) and mir bakhshi (mansabs, muster) are different.
**Breakdown:**
- Wakil: prestige retained, real power reduced after Bairam (✅ owner 16 advanced).
- Diwan: financial head; mir bakhshi: army and nobility (✅ owner 16 advanced).
- Provincial officers checked one another (✅ owner 16 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3041, MED-3005.
**Trap:** The trap pair itself.
**Mains hook:** Checks and balances, not separation of powers.
**Back:** *"Wakil is not wazir, diwan is not bakhshi."*

### Flashcard MED-3050 — Trap: mansab ≠ jagir ≠ zamindar ≠ land grant
**Front:** What four terms should never be treated as synonyms?
**Definition (learn):** Mansab is rank; jagir is revenue assignment; zamindar is a hereditary or customary right-holder; a land grant is an ownership transfer. None are synonyms.
**Breakdown:**
- Mansab = rank and service (MED-3031).
- Jagir = assignment of revenue (MED-3034).
- Zamindar = diverse village and armed right-holder (✅ owner 16 advanced section 18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3031, MED-3034, MED-3043.
**Trap:** Mansab ≠ land grant; jagir ≠ ownership; zamindar ≠ jagirdar.
**Mains hook:** Clarify categories before evaluating the system.
**Back:** *"Mansab is rank, jagir is revenue, zamindar holds the soil."*

### GROUP — Akbar's Religious Views: Ibadat Khana & Din-i-Ilahi

### Flashcard MED-3051 — Concept: policy before creed (three phases, 1556–1605)
**Front:** How should Akbar's religious policy be periodised?
**Definition (learn):** Policy came before doctrine. Three phases: early accommodation (1556–73), inquiry and breach (1573–80), governing principle and discipleship (1581–1605).
**Breakdown:**
- 1556–73: pilgrim tax (1563), jizyah (1564), no enslavement of rebels' families, Rajput wives allowed worship (✅ owner 17 basic and advanced section 10).
- 1573–80: Ibadat Khana (1575), debates widened after 1578, mahzar (1579) (✅ owner 17 advanced section 10).
- 1581–1605: debates ended (1581/1582); sulh-i-kul and Tauhid-i-Ilahi framed sovereignty (✅ owner 17 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Three phases per owner 17 advanced section 10.
**Links:** MED-3052, MED-3055, MED-3057, MED-3058, MED-3070 (chronology ladder).
**Trap:** Do not narrate religious policy as a single 1582 event; early fiscal and social measures came first (✅ owner 17 advanced chronology trap).
**Mains hook:** Inclusion preceded the Ibadat Khana; that sequence proves the policy was state-building before it was doctrine.
**Back:** *"Sixty-three, sixty-four, seventy-five, seventy-nine, eighty-two: policy first, creed never."*

### Flashcard MED-3052 — Pilgrim tax remitted (1563)
**Front:** What was the pilgrim tax and when did Akbar remit it?
**Definition (learn):** A tax on bathing and pilgrimage at sites such as Prayag and Banaras; Akbar remitted it in 1563.
**Breakdown:**
- Removed a religious disability in the fiscal system (✅ owner 17 basic table).
- Economic impact debatable; symbolic significance greater (✅ owner 17 advanced table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1563 (✅ owner 17 basic and advanced).
**Links:** MED-3051, MED-3053.
**Trap:** Pilgrim tax abolition ≠ jizyah abolition (different taxes, different years).
**Mains hook:** The first measure shows fiscal inclusion before any theological programme.
**Back:** *"Sixty-three: the bathing tax bowed out."*

### Flashcard MED-3053 — Jizyah abolished (1564)
**Front:** What was jizyah and when did Akbar abolish it?
**Definition (learn):** A poll tax on non-Muslims, abolished by Akbar in 1564 after taking power.
**Breakdown:**
- Equal-subject policy, not a revenue priority (✅ owner 17 basic table).
- Removed selected disabilities, not all hierarchy (✅ owner 17 advanced section 13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1564 (✅ owner 17 basic and advanced).
**Links:** MED-3051, MED-3073 (Aurangzeb's 1679 reimposition).
**Trap:** Jizyah abolition ≠ equality of all subjects in service.
**Mains hook:** Inclusion through fiscal change.
**Back:** *"Sixty-four: jizyah dropped, equality by fiscal means."*

### Flashcard MED-3054 — Ban on enslaving families of defeated rebels
**Front:** What did Akbar prohibit about the families of defeated rebels?
**Definition (learn):** Akbar prohibited the enslavement of wives and children of defeated rebels, a humane measure in an otherwise hierarchical empire.
**Breakdown:**
- Early measure listed with pilgrim tax and jizyah (✅ owner 17 basic and advanced section 10).
- Reduced formal religious discrimination (✅ owner 17 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Early reign, within 1556–73 (✅ owner 17 advanced).
**Links:** MED-3051, MED-3053.
**Trap:** This was not a general abolition of slavery or of hierarchy.
**Mains hook:** Early humane measures precede the Ibadat Khana debates.
**Back:** *"Rebel's wife, rebel's child: not sold."*

### Flashcard MED-3055 — Ibadat Khana (1575, Fatehpur Sikri)
**Front:** What was the Ibadat Khana, where and when was it built?
**Definition (learn):** The Hall of Prayer built by Akbar at Fatehpur Sikri in 1575 for religious discussion among theologians, mystics, scholars and courtiers.
**Breakdown:**
- Located near the palace and Anup Talao, around Shaikh Abdullah Niyazi's cell (✅ owner 17 advanced section 11).
- Thursday-night discussions initially with Muslim shaikhs and ulama (✅ owner 17 advanced).
- Architecture or surviving remains cannot be asserted without archaeological proof (⚠️ owner 17 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Built 1575 (✅ Wikipedia: "meeting house built in 1575 CE by Akbar" at Fatehpur Sikri).
**Links:** MED-3056, MED-3057, MED-3058.
**Trap:** Ibadat Khana was a debate institution, not a temple or a mosque; do not label any ruin as the Ibadat Khana without proof.
**Mains hook:** Debate exposed the ulama's narrowness but did not create consensus.
**Back:** *"Seventy-five, Fatehpur, Thursdays: the debate hall."*

### Flashcard MED-3056 — Widening of Ibadat Khana debates (after 1578)
**Front:** Who was admitted to the Ibadat Khana debates after 1578?
**Definition (learn):** After a reported mystical experience in 1578, Akbar admitted Hindu, Jain, Christian and Zoroastrian interlocutors and sceptics.
**Breakdown:**
- Questions covered revelation, prophethood, resurrection and divine unity (✅ owner 17 advanced section 11.2).
- Not a modern parliament: attendance and status were unequal (✅ owner 17 advanced).
- Widening after 1578 is the owner date; Wikipedia's Ibadat Khana article gives no year for the opening (✅ Wikipedia: no date).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c.1578 (owner); Wikipedia not corroborated.
**Links:** MED-3055, MED-3058, MED-3064–3068 (encounters).
**Trap:** Debates were not a sign of equal religious communities.
**Mains hook:** Syncretism began as inquiry, not as a ready creed.
**Back:** *"Seventy-eight: the doors widen, the bitterness follows."*

### Flashcard MED-3057 — Mahzar (1579): juristic arbitration, not infallibility
**Front:** What was the mahzar and how far did it extend Akbar's authority?
**Definition (learn):** An attested statement signed by leading ulama which allowed the emperor to choose among existing juristic opinions for public welfare and order.
**Breakdown:**
- If qualified jurists disagreed, the emperor selected an existing opinion (✅ owner 17 basic table and advanced section 12).
- Ruler-made orders must not contradict an explicit Quranic or hadis command (✅ owner 17 advanced).
- Akbar did not claim prophecy or papal authority (✅ owner 17 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1579 (✅ owner 17 basic and advanced).
**Links:** MED-3055, MED-3072 (trap), MED-3059 (sulh-i-kul).
**Trap:** Mahzar ≠ infallibility decree; it is bounded arbitration (✅ owner 17 trap).
**Mains hook:** Imperial sovereignty over clerical monopoly, without unlimited legislation.
**Back:** *"Seventy-nine: choose among the learned, never against the Book."*

### Flashcard MED-3058 — Closure of Ibadat Khana (1581–82)
**Front:** When did the Ibadat Khana debates end and why?
**Definition (learn):** Public debates practically ceased in 1581 and were finally discontinued in 1582.
**Breakdown:**
- Akbar judged that public contest encouraged participants to seek favour and win, not discover common truth (✅ owner 17 advanced section 11.3).
- Private discussions, missions and translations continued (✅ owner 17 advanced).
- Chandra stresses bitterness as the outcome; the assessment is his interpretation (✅ owner 17 basic table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1581 (practical), 1582 (final).
**Links:** MED-3055, MED-3057.
**Trap:** Closure did not end religious inquiry; Ibadat Khana did not produce instant harmony.
**Mains hook:** Failure as dialogue, success in shaping sulh-i-kul.
**Back:** *"Eighty-one faded, eighty-two closed: contest bred bitterness."*

### Flashcard MED-3059 — Sulh-i-kul: peace with all, a principle of government
**Front:** What was sulh-i-kul and why did it outlast the Ibadat Khana?
**Definition (learn):** "Peace with all" or universal peace: Abu'l Fazl's ideology of justice, paternal kingship and restraint of sectarian conflict.
**Breakdown:**
- A principle of government, not a claim that religions had merged (✅ owner 17 advanced section 13).
- Legitimised a composite nobility (✅ owner 17 basic section 8.2).
- Depended on royal will; no institutional guarantee beyond the reign (✅ owner 17 basic table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Abu'l Fazl's framework; widely associated with the 1580s (⚠️ owner-only dating).
**Links:** MED-3057, MED-3060, MED-3069.
**Trap:** Sulh-i-kul ≠ modern secularism ≠ state indifference to religion (✅ owner 17 advanced anachronism trap).
**Mains hook:** Ruler-centred inclusion, not constitutional equality.
**Back:** *"Sulh-i-kul: peace for all, ruled from the top."*

### Flashcard MED-3060 — Tauhid-i-Ilahi vs Din-i-Ilahi: terms and sources
**Front:** Which term did Abu'l Fazl use and which did Badauni use?
**Definition (learn):** Tauhid-i-Ilahi means "Divine Monotheism"; Din-i-Ilahi is the term more associated with Badauni and Jesuit criticism.
**Breakdown:**
- Abu'l Fazl primarily used Tauhid-i-Ilahi; Badauni used both (✅ owner 17 advanced section 15.1).
- Wikipedia: "called Tawḥīd-i-Ilāhī" and "as it is written by Abu'l-Fazl" (✅ Wikipedia: Din-i-Ilahi).
- Din-i-Ilahi is the later popular label (⚠️ modern usage).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Tauhid-i-Ilahi; Din-i-Ilahi; Badauni, Abu'l Fazl.
**Links:** MED-3061, MED-3063, MED-3046 (Ilahi calendar).
**Trap:** Ilahi calendar ≠ Tauhid-i-Ilahi (MED-3046).
**Mains hook:** Name the term and its source; that is the first step in analysing the fellowship.
**Back:** *"Abu'l Fazl: Tauhid. Badauni: Din. Both: Ilahi."*

### Flashcard MED-3061 — The four degrees of discipleship and court rituals
**Front:** What did the four degrees of devotion require and what rituals are reported?
**Definition (learn):** Four degrees required willingness to sacrifice property, life, honour and religion. Reported practices: Sunday initiation, placing the turban at the emperor's feet, the shast token and greetings "Allah-o-Akbar" / "Jall Jalaluhu".
**Breakdown:**
- Degrees resembled Sufi discipleship and bound a heterogeneous nobility to the throne (✅ owner 17 advanced section 15.2).
- Shast token and greetings are source-dependent (✅ owner 17 advanced).
- Critics called these prostration and greetings a personality cult (⚠️ owner 17 basic counterpoint).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Four degrees; Sunday initiation; shast.
**Links:** MED-3060, MED-3062, MED-3063.
**Trap:** Restricted practices are not the same as a mass religion; do not erase them either.
**Mains hook:** Discipleship as ideological device for elite loyalty.
**Back:** *"Property, life, honour, religion: four pledges, one throne."*

### Flashcard MED-3062 — Birbal and the eighteen nobles of the highest degree
**Front:** Who was the only Hindu in the highest devotional degrees, and how many nobles were there?
**Definition (learn):** Birbal was the only Hindu among the roughly 18 nobles in the highest degrees of Blochmann's reconstruction.
**Breakdown:**
- Birbal was Akbar's close companion (✅ owner 17 basic section 1).
- Blochmann's reconstruction lists about 18 nobles (✅ owner 17 advanced table).
- Source-based reconstruction, not an empire-wide census (✅ owner 17 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** About 18 (Blochmann, per owner).
**Links:** MED-3061, MED-3063.
**Trap:** Membership ≠ mass following; Birbal's membership may reflect personal loyalty.
**Mains hook:** Use membership to refute "mass religion" claims.
**Back:** *"Birbal alone, eighteen grandees: a small circle, not a church."*

### Flashcard MED-3063 — Din-i-Ilahi: no scripture, no priesthood, no congregation
**Front:** What did Din-i-Ilahi lack and how many adherents are reported?
**Definition (learn):** An elite discipleship fellowship with no separate scripture, priesthood, temple or public congregation; no mass conversion programme.
**Breakdown:**
- Wikipedia: "had no sacred scriptures"; "there was no priestly hierarchy" (✅ Wikipedia: Din-i-Ilahi).
- Member counts differ: Wikipedia says "never more than 19 adherents" in one passage and "only 18 members" in the other (✅ Wikipedia: Din-i-Ilahi and Ibadat Khana). ⚠️ Conflict.
- Owner: about 18 nobles, Birbal the only Hindu (✅ owner 17 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Wikipedia: "propounded by emperor Akbar in 1582".
**Links:** MED-3060, MED-3062, MED-3071 (trap).
**Trap:** Not a mass religion; not a complete absence of practices either.
**Mains hook:** A small political-spiritual fellowship with restricted practices.
**Back:** *"No book, no priest, no church: about nineteen souls, maybe eighteen."*

### Flashcard MED-3064 — Translation bureau (Maktab Khana) and the Persian translation programme
**Front:** What translations did Akbar's programme produce and whom did it employ?
**Definition (learn):** Maktab Khana: a translation bureau placing pandits and Persian literati in collaborative work.
**Breakdown:**
- Razmnama (Mahabharata), Ramayana, Atharva Veda, Bhagavad Gita, Panchatantra, Singhasana Battisi, Nal-Damayanti, Rajatarangini, Lilavati; Christian Gospels also translated (✅ owner 17 advanced section 14).
- Aimed to reduce "blind following" but remained an elite encounter (✅ owner 17 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not individually dated in owner.
**Links:** MED-3065, MED-3051, MED-3024 (state knowledge).
**Trap:** The programme was not mass social fusion (✅ owner 17 advanced).
**Mains hook:** Translation as an instrument of imperial intellectual policy.
**Back:** *"Razmnama and Gita: pandits and munshis at one table."*

### Flashcard MED-3065 — Jesuit encounter: Aquaviva and Monserrate
**Front:** Who were Rudolf Aquaviva and Antonio Monserrate and why are they important?
**Definition (learn):** Jesuit missionaries who stayed at Akbar's court (Goa-linked requests) and left valuable evidence.
**Breakdown:**
- Akbar requested learned missionaries from Goa (✅ owner 17 advanced section 14).
- Their letters are missionary and conversion-centred, so read them critically (✅ owner 17 advanced section 16).
- Courtesy, images, Gospel study or Prince Salim's attendance did not prove conversion (✅ owner 17 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated in owner.
**Links:** MED-3064, MED-3069 (churches and grants), MED-3071.
**Trap:** Courtesy ≠ conversion.
**Mains hook:** Outsider observation has its own bias.
**Back:** *"Jesuits saw a door, not a convert."*

### Flashcard MED-3066 — Brahman encounter: Purushottam and Devi
**Front:** Who explained Hindu doctrine at the Ibadat Khana?
**Definition (learn):** Purushottam and Devi explained doctrines to the court in the Brahman/Hindu encounter.
**Breakdown:**
- Pandits joined translation work (✅ owner 17 advanced section 14).
- "One fixed Hinduism" did not speak through equal representatives (✅ owner 17 advanced caveat).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3064, MED-3055.
**Trap:** No single "Hinduism" spoke at the forum.
**Mains hook:** Elite mediation, not community representation.
**Back:** *"Purushottam and Devi: pandits, not a parliament of religions."*

### Flashcard MED-3067 — Jain encounter: Hiravijaya Suri and animal life
**Front:** What is the Jain encounter associated with, and what must not be inferred?
**Definition (learn):** Hiravijaya Suri spent time at court; Jain contact is associated with bounded concern for animal life and slaughter restrictions.
**Breakdown:**
- Hiravijaya Suri at court (✅ owner 17 advanced section 14).
- Bounded restrictions, not empire-wide vegetarianism (✅ owner 17 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated in owner.
**Links:** MED-3066, MED-3069.
**Trap:** Restrictions ≠ permanent empire-wide vegetarianism; no direct Jain conversion.
**Mains hook:** Concern for animal life can coexist with imperial taxation and hierarchy.
**Back:** *"Hiravijaya at court, slaughter curbed: not a vegetarian empire."*

### Flashcard MED-3068 — Zoroastrian encounter: Meherji Rana
**Front:** Which Zoroastrian explained doctrine to Akbar and what symbolism is relevant?
**Definition (learn):** Meherji Rana explained Zoroastrian doctrine; light and fire symbolism were part of a wider field of ideas.
**Breakdown:**
- Explanation of Zoroastrian doctrine at the forum (✅ owner 17 advanced section 14).
- Solar and fire imagery did not derive exclusively from Zoroastrianism (✅ owner 17 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3061 (solar and light symbolism), MED-3066.
**Trap:** Light symbolism is not proof of Zoroastrian conversion.
**Mains hook:** Overlapping Islamic, Indic, Iranian and courtly genealogies.
**Back:** *"Meherji: fire explained, not inherited whole."*

### Flashcard MED-3069 — Religious patronage and welfare establishments
**Front:** What welfare and patronage institutions did Akbar's state set up for each community?
**Definition (learn):** Court-centred provision: Khairpura for Muslims, Dharmapura for Hindus and later Jogipura; grants reached Hindu, Jain and Parsi recipients after 1575/80.
**Breakdown:**
- Khairpura, Dharmapura, Jogipura (✅ owner 17 advanced section 13).
- Jesuits received support for churches (✅ owner 17 advanced).
- Permission for coerced converts to return and temples, churches and synagogues to be built (✅ owner 17 advanced; practice varied).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** After 1575/80 (owner).
**Links:** MED-3059, MED-3065.
**Trap:** Court-centred provision ≠ modern welfare right; orders ≠ uniform local acceptance.
**Mains hook:** Patronage as a way to bind communities to the throne.
**Back:** *"Khairpura, Dharmapura, Jogipura: each faith got its own almshouse."*

### Flashcard MED-3070 — Chronology ladder of Akbar's religious policy
**Front:** Give the ordered dates for the religious measures.
**Definition (learn):** 1563 pilgrim tax; 1564 jizyah; 1575 Ibadat Khana; c.1578 widened debates; 1579 mahzar; 1581 practical end; 1582 final end; 1582 Din-i-Ilahi association; 1605 death.
**Breakdown:**
- Measures before debate and mahzar (✅ owner 17 basic mini-timeline).
- 1582 is not the birth of a new religion (✅ owner 17 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (owner 17 basic mini-timeline).
**Links:** MED-3051, MED-3052, MED-3053, MED-3055, MED-3057, MED-3058.
**Trap:** Do not narrate all religious policy as one 1582 event.
**Mains hook:** Sequence is the proof of policy-led religion.
**Back:** *"Sixty-three, sixty-four, seventy-five, seventy-nine, eighty-two: the ladder."*

### Flashcard MED-3071 — Trap: Din-i-Ilahi was not a mass religion
**Front:** Why is "Akbar created Din-i-Ilahi to convert India" wrong?
**Definition (learn):** Din-i-Ilahi had no coercion and a tiny membership; it was a limited spiritual-political fellowship.
**Breakdown:**
- No coercive conversion (✅ owner 17 basic trap).
- Small elite circle (MED-3062, MED-3063).
- Two-sided trap: do not erase its restricted practices and ethical language (✅ owner 17 advanced section 15.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As above.
**Links:** MED-3060, MED-3063.
**Trap:** The trap is both ways: mass religion claim and total absence of practice.
**Mains hook:** Define the fellowship precisely.
**Back:** *"Not for India, not for nothing: a circle."*

### Flashcard MED-3072 — Trap: mahzar did not make Akbar infallible
**Front:** What is the correct reading of the mahzar?
**Definition (learn):** Clause-by-clause document permitting choice among existing opinions for welfare, bounded by Quranic and hadis commands.
**Breakdown:**
- Akbar was not a pope (✅ owner 17 advanced section 12).
- Historians disagree whether text strengthened autocracy beyond its wording (⚠️ owner 17 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1579.
**Links:** MED-3057.
**Trap:** Mahzar ≠ infallibility; mahzar ≠ abolition of Islam or every qazi.
**Mains hook:** Read the clauses, not the label.
**Back:** *"Choose among, never abolish, never above the Book."*

### Flashcard MED-3073 — Comparison: Akbar's inclusion vs Aurangzeb's jizyah (1679)
**Front:** Why is Aurangzeb's 1679 jizyah a valid comparison but not a moral binary?
**Definition (learn):** Aurangzeb reimposed jizyah in 1679, a contrast owners use, but the political explanation belongs to a different owner.
**Breakdown:**
- Akbar abolished jizyah (1564) (MED-3053).
- Aurangzeb's reimposition in 1679 (⚠️ standard date from owner 17 study link; not fetched).
- Sulh-i-kul depended on sovereign choice (✅ owner 17 advanced counterpoint).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1564 vs 1679 (⚠️ 1679 owner cross-reference).
**Links:** MED-3053, MED-3059.
**Trap:** Do not make it a tolerant-Akbar vs bigoted-Aurangzeb binary (✅ owner 17 advanced).
**Mains hook:** Sovereign choice explains policy shifts.
**Back:** *"Sixty-four dropped it, seventy-nine restored it: policy follows the throne."*

### GROUP — The Deccan and the Mughals (to 1657)

### Flashcard MED-3074 — Concept: connected but frictional Deccan, and the 1657 boundary
**Front:** What is the scope of Mughal–Deccan policy and why does it stop at 1657?
**Definition (learn):** Mughal–Deccan political, military, fiscal and diplomatic relations from the late sixteenth-century opening to the 1657 settlements. Stop at 1657; do not import Aurangzeb's later imperial reign, the 1686–87 annexations or the Mughal–Maratha war.
**Breakdown:**
- Vindhyas were not an absolute wall; traders, Sufis, soldiers and elites moved (✅ owner 18 basic section 2.2 and advanced section 2).
- Connection did not remove friction: plateau edges, supply lines, monsoon and fortified nodes made occupation easier than durable consolidation (✅ owner 18).
- Boundary owners: Topic 09 (Vijayanagara and Bahmani), Topic 23 (Shivaji and 1686–87) (✅ owner 18 basic section 0).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Boundary year 1657 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** MED-3075 (post-Bahmani), MED-3081, MED-3096 (1657 Bijapur), MED-3100 (Vijayanagara trap).
**Trap:** "Occupation = control" fails in the Deccan; 1657 is the end-point, not a war narrative.
**Mains hook:** "Mughal conquest did not produce durable control in the Deccan" is the thesis to test.
**Back:** *"Connected, friction, stop at fifty-seven."*

### Flashcard MED-3075 — Post-Bahmani states and the Khandesh gateway
**Front:** Name the successor states of the Bahmani kingdom and the non-Bahmani gateway state.
**Definition (learn):** Ahmadnagar (Nizam Shahi), Bijapur (Adil Shahi), Berar (Imad Shahi, absorbed by Ahmadnagar), Bidar (Barid Shahi), Golconda (Qutb Shahi). Khandesh (Faruqi) was not a Bahmani successor but the Mughal gateway between Malwa/Gujarat and the Deccan.
**Breakdown:**
- Ahmadnagar, Bijapur and Golconda were the principal powers at the Mughal intervention phase (✅ owner 18 basic section 2.1).
- Bidar remained smaller and contested (✅ owner 18 basic).
- Khandesh–Burhanpur–Asirgarh was the Narmada–Tapti approach (✅ owner 18 basic table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Five successor states; Khandesh the gateway.
**Links:** MED-3074, MED-3076, MED-3081.
**Trap:** Khandesh ≠ one of the five Bahmani successors (✅ owner 18 advanced Prelims fact).
**Mains hook:** Map the field before describing the campaigns.
**Back:** *"Nizam, Adil, Imad, Barid, Qutb: and Faruqi Khandesh at the gate."*

### Flashcard MED-3076 — Khandesh submits (1576) and Burhanpur as the gateway
**Front:** What did Khandesh do in 1576 and why did Burhanpur matter?
**Definition (learn):** Khandesh submitted, paid tribute and handed over Muhammad Husain Mirza, becoming the exposed northern gateway; Burhanpur was its capital and a point of entry to the Deccan on the Agra–Surat route.
**Breakdown:**
- Submission in 1576 (✅ owner 18 basic timeline).
- Burhanpur: command and supply centre, on the Agra–Surat route (✅ owner 18 advanced Prelims fact).
- Submission did not produce immediate conquest (✅ owner 18 advanced section 6.1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1576 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** MED-3075, MED-3077, MED-3081.
**Trap:** Khandesh's submission ≠ annexation; annexation came in 1601.
**Mains hook:** A gateway state is strategic even when it is not conquered.
**Back:** *"Seventy-six: Khandesh bowed, Burhanpur opened the door."*

### Flashcard MED-3077 — Akbar's 1591 missions: coercive signalling
**Front:** What did Akbar's four missions of 1591 demand and what were the results?
**Definition (learn):** Four missions to principal Deccani rulers demanding acceptance of Mughal suzerainty. Khandesh complied; Ahmadnagar rejected it sharply; others offered courtesy and presents without submission.
**Breakdown:**
- Diplomacy as coercive signalling (✅ owner 18 advanced section 6.1).
- Failure of the 1591 offensive pushed Akbar to armed intervention after the Ahmadnagar crisis (✅ owner 18 advanced section 1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1591 (✅ owner 18 basic timeline).
**Links:** MED-3076, MED-3078.
**Trap:** Diplomacy ≠ peaceful equality.
**Mains hook:** Diplomacy and force as a continuum.
**Back:** *"Ninety-one: four envoys, one submission, one refusal."*

### Flashcard MED-3078 — Chand Bibi and the Ahmadnagar crisis (1595–96)
**Front:** How did Chand Bibi's defence end in 1596 and what did it concede?
**Definition (learn):** After Burhan Nizam Shah's death (1595), rival noble coalitions promoted claimants. Chand Bibi backed the infant Bahadur with Habshi support; after a four-month defence, a compromise in 1596 recognised Bahadur under her regency, ceded Berar and accepted Mughal suzerainty.
**Breakdown:**
- Miyan Manju invited Mughal help (✅ owner 18 basic section 6.2).
- Chand Bibi sought Bijapuri and Golconda assistance (✅ owner 18 basic).
- Constrained statecraft, not simple surrender or solitary heroism (⚠️ owner 18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1595 (Burhan's death); 1596 compromise; Berar ceded.
**Links:** MED-3021, MED-3079, MED-3080.
**Trap:** Berar cession ≠ end of conflict; a 1597 combined attempt followed.
**Mains hook:** Chand Bibi as a case of constrained agency.
**Back:** *"Ninety-six: Bahadur kept, Berar given, suzerainty taken."*

### Flashcard MED-3079 — Battle of Sonepat (1597): the Berar theatre, not Haryana's Sonipat
**Front:** Which place-name trap appears in the 1597 Berar battle?
**Definition (learn):** The 1597 engagement in the Berar theatre, printed by Satish Chandra as "Sonepat", is not the north Indian city of Sonipat in Haryana.
**Breakdown:**
- Combined Deccani force (Bijapur, Golconda, Ahmadnagar) sought to recover Berar and was defeated (✅ owner 18 basic section 6.3).
- Mughal army defeated a much larger combined force (✅ owner 18 advanced Prelims fact).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1597 (✅ owner 18).
**Links:** MED-3078, MED-3080.
**Trap:** Sonepat (Berar) ≠ Sonipat (Haryana). Always check the owner's geography.
**Mains hook:** Place names need local geography checks.
**Back:** *"Ninety-seven Sonepat: Berar, not Haryana."*

### Flashcard MED-3080 — Ahmadnagar fort falls and Chand Bibi's death (1600)
**Front:** What happened to Ahmadnagar fort in 1600?
**Definition (learn):** After renewed attacks and factional conflict, Chand Bibi explored a subordinate arrangement for Bahadur, was accused of treachery and killed; the Mughals captured the Ahmadnagar fort.
**Breakdown:**
- Killed by a hostile faction (✅ owner 18 basic timeline).
- Balaghat claimed/occupied (✅ owner 18 basic timeline).
- Possession of the fort ≠ control of the remaining kingdom (✅ owner 18 advanced section 6.3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1600.
**Links:** MED-3078, MED-3081, MED-3021.
**Trap:** Fort capture ≠ countryside control.
**Mains hook:** Fort-level success and state-level incomplete control.
**Back:** *"Sixteen hundred: fort taken, state not."*

### Flashcard MED-3081 — Asirgarh and Khandesh annexed (1601); Murtaza II's remnant
**Front:** What did 1601 bring and what survived?
**Definition (learn):** Asirgarh fell and Khandesh was annexed in 1601; a Nizam Shahi remnant survived under Murtaza II.
**Breakdown:**
- Burhanpur made the annexation more than prestige (✅ owner 18 advanced section 6.3).
- Berar, Balaghat, Khandesh and Ahmadnagar fort were unstable acquisitions at Akbar's death (✅ owner 15 advanced; owner 18).
- Surrender narratives need caution (✅ owner 15 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1601; Murtaza II.
**Links:** MED-3076, MED-3080, MED-3021.
**Trap:** Asirgarh/Khandesh (1601) ≠ Ahmadnagar fort (1600).
**Mains hook:** Deccan frontier unfinished at Akbar's death.
**Back:** *"Oh-one Asirgarh: Khandesh annexed, Murtaza still king."*

### Flashcard MED-3082 — Malik Ambar: Habshi peshwa of the Nizam Shahi state
**Front:** Who was Malik Ambar and what office did he hold?
**Definition (learn):** Ethiopian-born Habshi who passed through enslavement and commercial networks, rose in Deccan service and became Peshwa (prime minister) of the Ahmadnagar Sultanate (✅ Wikipedia: Malik Ambar, Peshwa from 1600 until death).
**Breakdown:**
- Nizam Shahi dynastic legitimacy plus Habshi, Afghan and Deccani soldiers (✅ owner 18 advanced section 7.1).
- Not a Maratha king (✅ owner 18 advanced Prelims fact).
- Death: 14 May 1626 per Wikipedia and owner 18 basic. ⚠️ Owner 18 advanced mini-timeline says 1627; the basic file notes an OCR narrative printing 1627 (conflict flagged).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Born 1548 (✅ Wikipedia); died 14 May 1626 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-3083, MED-3084, MED-3085, MED-3086, MED-3088.
**Trap:** Habshi is not "outsider without local agency" (✅ owner 18 basic table).
**Mains hook:** Malik Ambar as defender and state-builder, with limits.
**Back:** *"Habshi Peshwa, Nizam's pillar, died in twenty-six."*

### Flashcard MED-3083 — Bargir cavalry and deshmukh networks
**Front:** What were bargirs and deshmukhs and why did they matter?
**Definition (learn):** Bargirs/bargis were mounted auxiliary cavalry; deshmukhs were locally rooted landed and revenue power-holders.
**Breakdown:**
- Maratha light cavalry used speed, dispersion, intelligence and cutting of Mughal supplies (✅ owner 18 advanced section 7.2).
- Deshmukhs supplied local intelligence and revenue relations (✅ owner 18 advanced section 7.1).
- Before Shivaji, Marathas were not a single nationalist army or sovereign state (✅ owner 18 basic table).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3082, MED-3089, MED-3098.
**Trap:** Bargir ≠ Maratha state army.
**Mains hook:** Service networks enabled but did not automatically produce the Maratha state.
**Back:** *"Bargirs ride, deshmukhs know, neither is a kingdom."*

### Flashcard MED-3084 — Malik Ambar's revenue reconstruction (later Marathi sources)
**Front:** What revenue measures do later Marathi sources credit to Malik Ambar?
**Definition (learn):** Measurement, village boundaries, rates, and replacement of ijara (revenue farming) by a zabti-type settlement, with chain measurement and progressive rates for new land.
**Breakdown:**
- Later Marathi sources credit him (✅ owner 18 advanced section 7.3).
- Do not claim a uniform one-third assessment or a full Todar Mal copy (⚠️ owner 18).
- The secure claim is that fiscal reconstruction supported military resistance (✅ owner 18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated; later sources.
**Links:** MED-3082, MED-3026 (zabt), MED-3025 (dahsala).
**Trap:** Later Marathi testimony ≠ proof of uniform early-17th-century practice.
**Mains hook:** Revenue as the sinew of resistance.
**Back:** *"Ijara out, zabti in, per later Marathi bakhars."*

### Flashcard MED-3085 — Khirki: the Nizam Shahi centre and its water system
**Front:** What was Khirki and what is associated with it?
**Definition (learn):** A Nizam Shahi centre developed by Malik Ambar and associated with its water system; the place was later occupied and burned by Mughal forces in 1616.
**Breakdown:**
- Developed by Malik Ambar (✅ owner 18 advanced section 7.3).
- Occupied and burned in 1616 (✅ owner 18 advanced section 8.2).
- Later named Aurangabad in the Mughal period (⚠️ standard knowledge, not in owner text).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1616 occupation.
**Links:** MED-3082, MED-3087.
**Trap:** Material remains prove capacity, not every chronicle claim.
**Mains hook:** Urban and logistic capacity.
**Back:** *"Khirki built, Khirki burned: sixteen."*

### Flashcard MED-3086 — Malik Ambar's reversal (1610) and the failed 1611 advance
**Front:** Why did Mughal consolidation collapse by 1610 and what failed in 1611?
**Definition (learn):** After Akbar's death and Khusrau's rebellion, Malik Ambar recovered territory; Bijapur supplied a fort, troops and a marriage alliance. By 1610 most Akbari gains outside secure positions were lost. A 1611 two-pronged advance failed through rivalry and poor coordination.
**Breakdown:**
- Ahmadnagar as buffer for Bijapur (✅ owner 18 advanced section 8.1).
- Numerical strength without unified command and supply discipline (✅ owner 18 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1610; 1611.
**Links:** MED-3082, MED-3087.
**Trap:** Mughal numbers ≠ control.
**Mains hook:** Command and supply as the binding constraint.
**Back:** *"Ten: Akbar's gains slip. Eleven: two prongs, no touch."*

### Flashcard MED-3087 — Khan-i-Khanan and Prince Khurram (1616–21): victories without full annexation
**Front:** What did the 1616, 1617 and 1621 campaigns achieve and what did Jahangir refuse to do?
**Definition (learn):** Khan-i-Khanan defeated the combined Ahmadnagar–Bijapur–Golconda force in 1616 and occupied and burned Khirki; Khurram's pressure in 1617 compelled restoration of Balaghat and delivery of the Ahmadnagar fort key; a 1621 victory compelled restoration, an additional belt near Ahmadnagar and an indemnity. Jahangir did not enlarge Akbar's annexations.
**Breakdown:**
- Khan-i-Khanan won over Habshi and Maratha nobles (✅ owner 18 advanced section 8.2).
- Calling the Adil Shah farzand (son) reflected hierarchy and restraint (✅ owner 18 advanced).
- Deliberate restraint, not impotence (⚠️ owner 18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1616; 1617; 1621.
**Links:** MED-3085, MED-3088, MED-3098.
**Trap:** Field victory ≠ annexation.
**Mains hook:** Jahangir's restraint as a policy choice.
**Back:** *"Sixteen Khirki, seventeen Balaghat, twenty-one indemnity: no annexation."*

### Flashcard MED-3088 — Bhaturi (1624): Malik Ambar's peak
**Front:** What did Malik Ambar achieve at Bhaturi in 1624?
**Definition (learn):** A victory over the Mughal–Adil Shahi forces at Bhaturi (Wikipedia spells it Bhatvadi) that marked Malik Ambar's peak.
**Breakdown:**
- Defeated Mughal–Adil Shahi forces (✅ owner 18 basic timeline).
- His conflicts with Bijapur weakened sustained anti-Mughal cooperation (✅ owner 18 advanced).
- Wikipedia lists the battle by name only and gives no year or outcome (✅ Wikipedia: Malik Ambar).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1624 (owner); not fetch-confirmed.
**Links:** MED-3082, MED-3087.
**Trap:** Peak ≠ permanent success; Ahmadnagar ended in 1633.
**Mains hook:** The peak exposed the limits of coalitions.
**Back:** *"Twenty-four Bhaturi: Ambar's high-water mark."*

### Flashcard MED-3089 — Shahji Bhonsale: from Mughal service to claimant resistance
**Front:** How did Shahji Bhonsale move between Mughal and Deccani service?
**Definition (learn):** Jadhav Rao's killing drove Shahji into Mughal service. Shah Jahan gave him a 5000 mansab and Poona-region jagirs; their transfer to Fath Khan helped trigger his defection. He later sustained a Nizam Shahi claimant with Bijapuri support.
**Breakdown:**
- Jadhav Rao's killing (✅ owner 18 advanced section 9).
- Mansab 5000 and jagirs in the Poona region (✅ owner 18 advanced).
- Claimant resistance from 1633 (✅ owner 18 advanced section 10.2).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 5000 mansab; Poona jagirs.
**Links:** MED-3090, MED-3083, MED-3098.
**Trap:** Shahji's service ≠ Maratha state; he was a service-network figure.
**Mains hook:** Service networks gave later Marathas skill and confidence without making the state inevitable.
**Back:** *"Shahji: Jadhav's blood, Shah Jahan's pay, a claimant's cause."*

### Flashcard MED-3090 — Daulatabad (1633): end of the Nizam Shahi ruling line
**Front:** What ended in 1633 and how did Fath Khan contribute?
**Definition (learn):** Fath Khan, Malik Ambar's son, killed the Nizam Shah in 1632, installed a puppet and recognised Mughal sovereignty via khutba and sikka; Mahabat Khan besieged Daulatabad, which surrendered in 1633; the captive ruler was sent to Gwalior, ending the ruling Nizam Shahi line.
**Breakdown:**
- Fath Khan entered Mughal service (✅ owner 18 advanced section 10.2).
- Dynastic extinction did not end resistance (⚠️ owner 18 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1632 killing; 1633 Daulatabad surrender.
**Links:** MED-3089, MED-3091.
**Trap:** Ruling line ended in 1633; the state's remnant claimants continued.
**Mains hook:** Dynastic extinction as the start of partition.
**Back:** *"Thirty-two Fath Khan; thirty-three Daulatabad: line ends, claim lives."*

### Flashcard MED-3091 — The 1636 ahdnama with Bijapur
**Front:** What did Bijapur concede in the 1636 treaty?
**Definition (learn):** Bijapur recognised Mughal suzerainty, paid an indemnity of twenty lakh rupees, accepted Mughal arbitration in disputes with Golconda, agreed not to interfere with Golconda, and agreed to reduce Shahji to submission.
**Breakdown:**
- In return, Bijapur received a large share of former Ahmadnagar territory, about twenty lakh huns annually in the Part II narrative (✅ owner 18 advanced section 10.3; OCR rupee/hun conversion requires caution).
- Mughal arbitration over Bijapur–Golconda (✅ owner 18 basic section 10.3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1636; twenty lakh rupees indemnity; arbitration.
**Links:** MED-3092, MED-3093, MED-3089.
**Trap:** Ahdnama ≠ annexation.
**Mains hook:** Statesmanship or coercive pause.
**Back:** *"Thirty-six: Bijapur pays twenty lakh, bows, arbitrates, leaves Golconda."*

### Flashcard MED-3092 — The 1636 ahdnama with Golconda
**Front:** What did Golconda concede in 1636 and what did it receive?
**Definition (learn):** Golconda inserted Shah Jahan's name in the khutba, removed the Iranian ruler's name, accepted loyalty and Mughal protection, and redirected the annual payment from Bijapur to the Mughal emperor (two lakh huns in the Part II narrative).
**Breakdown:**
- Khutba and protection (✅ owner 18 basic section 10.3).
- Annual payment redirected (✅ owner 18 basic).
- Protection paired with hierarchy and payment, not equality (✅ owner 18 basic section 4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1636; khutba; two lakh huns.
**Links:** MED-3091, MED-3093, MED-3094.
**Trap:** Khutba ≠ complete administration (✅ owner 18 advanced Prelims trap).
**Mains hook:** Symbolic recognition vs effective control.
**Back:** *"Thirty-six: Shah Jahan's name in the Golconda khutba."*

### Flashcard MED-3093 — Concept: 1636 was coercive suzerainty, not annexation
**Front:** Why is the 1636 settlement a suzerainty and buffer arrangement and not annexation?
**Definition (learn):** The northern Deccan became direct Mughal provinces; Bijapur and Golconda remained autonomous courts under tribute, khutba, protection, arbitration and service conditions.
**Breakdown:**
- Ahmadnagar dynastic extinction and partition (✅ owner 18 basic section 10.4).
- Bijapur and Golconda as subordinate but autonomous courts (✅ owner 18 advanced Prelims).
- Satish Chandra calls the treaties statesmanlike; force created the bargain (✅ owner 18 basic section 10.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1636.
**Links:** MED-3091, MED-3092, MED-3098.
**Trap:** 1636 ≠ annexation treaty (✅ owner 18 advanced trap).
**Mains hook:** Suzerainty vs annexation as the central distinction.
**Back:** *"Thirty-six: rent, khutba, arbitration, no annexation."*

### Flashcard MED-3094 — Aurangzeb's two Deccan viceroyalties (1636–44; 1652–57)
**Front:** When were Aurangzeb's Deccan viceroyalties, and what did each involve?
**Definition (learn):** First viceroyalty 1636–44 implemented the settlement; second 1652–57 faced deficits, weak realisation and pressure for compensation, with diwan Murshid Quli Khan.
**Breakdown:**
- First tenure: implementation and provincial experience, not a plan to annex Bijapur and Golconda (✅ owner 18 basic section 12.1; ⚠️ later programme not imported).
- Second tenure: pressed attacks on Golconda and Bijapur for treasure and territory (✅ owner 18 basic section 12.2).
- Aurangzeb accused Dara of frustrating annexation, but no conclusive proof (✅ owner 18 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1636–44; 1652–57.
**Links:** MED-3091, MED-3095, MED-3096.
**Trap:** Do not read his later emperor-era policy back into the 1636 tenure.
**Mains hook:** Court rivalry cannot replace policy analysis.
**Back:** *"Thirty-six to forty-four: learn. Fifty-two to fifty-seven: press."*

### Flashcard MED-3095 — Mir Jumla and the 1656 Golconda rupture
**Front:** Why did Mir Jumla break with Golconda in 1656, and what did the settlement bring?
**Definition (learn):** Mir Jumla, Golconda's leading noble with Karnataka interests, broke with the Qutb Shahi court and entered the Mughal field. The settlement gave money and the Ramgir district to the Mughals; a dispute followed over his rich Karnataka/Coromandel jagir.
**Breakdown:**
- Aurangzeb sought full annexation; Shah Jahan sought limited compensation and submission (✅ owner 18 basic section 13.2).
- Karnataka textile and indigo mattered (✅ owner 18 basic).
- Mir Jumla was not a passive invitation to conquest (⚠️ owner 18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1656; Ramgir.
**Links:** MED-3094, MED-3096, MED-3099.
**Trap:** Mir Jumla was not a Mughal pawn; his interests shaped the balance.
**Mains hook:** Noble agency and imperial policy.
**Back:** *"Fifty-six: Mir Jumla left Golconda, Ramgir came to the Mughals."*

### Flashcard MED-3096 — Bijapur 1657: the 1636 compact breaks
**Front:** What did the 1657 settlement compel Bijapur to surrender, and what did it stop short of?
**Definition (learn):** After Muhammad Adil Shah's death (1656), arrears and Bijapur's conduct gave a case for war; the 1657 terms compelled Bijapur to surrender former Nizam Shahi lands received in 1636, stopping short of full annexation.
**Breakdown:**
- Shah Jahan authorised annexation if feasible, or a lesser settlement with indemnity and khutba/sikka recognition (✅ owner 18 basic section 13.3).
- Together with Golconda demands, this destroyed confidence in the earlier compact (✅ owner 18 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1656 death; 1657 terms.
**Links:** MED-3091, MED-3094, MED-3095.
**Trap:** 1657 Bijapur ≠ full annexation of the state.
**Mains hook:** The annexation question reopened without solving the cost of direct rule.
**Back:** *"Fifty-seven: Bijapur returns the old Nizam lands, keeps its throne."*

### Flashcard MED-3097 — Actors and factions: no communal flattening
**Front:** Which groups formed the Deccani field and why must labels be used carefully?
**Definition (learn):** Deccanis, afaqis/gharibs (immigrant elites, often Iranian or Khurasani), Habshis (African-origin military and political elites), Afghans, Marathas (deshmukhs, cavalry, intermediaries), Deccani Brahmans and Mughal nobles.
**Breakdown:**
- Afaqi–Deccani, Sunni–Shia and Habshi–Afghan labels describe changing patronage fields (✅ owner 18 basic section 3).
- Mughal forces were also composite (✅ owner 18 basic).
- Alliances crossed ethnic and sectarian lines (✅ owner 18 advanced Prelims).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3082, MED-3083, MED-3089.
**Trap:** "Mughals vs Muslim Deccan states" means no religious complexity: a false equation.
**Mains hook:** Patronage and survival, not timeless communal armies.
**Back:** *"Six groups, many alliances, no timeless communal army."*

### Flashcard MED-3098 — Vocabulary of political control: suzerainty, tribute, protection, arbitration
**Front:** Define suzerainty, tribute, protection, arbitration and occupation as used in Deccan policy.
**Definition (learn):** Suzerainty: acknowledged superior status via service, tribute, khutba or sikka while the subordinate court survives. Tribute: regular or one-time payment, not automatic annexation. Protection: guarantee paired with hierarchy and payment. Arbitration: emperor settles disputes between courts. Occupation: military possession of a fort or tract.
**Breakdown:**
- Durable control: effective revenue, supply, cooperation and law and order; never infer it from a coloured map (✅ owner 18 basic section 4).
- Annexation: direct imperial claim and provincial administration (✅ owner 18 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3093, MED-3091.
**Trap:** Occupation ≠ durable control; suzerainty ≠ annexation.
**Mains hook:** Use the vocabulary to test every treaty.
**Back:** *"Suzerain bows, tribute pays, arbiter rules, occupier sits."*

### Flashcard MED-3099 — Provincial incorporation and the Deccan jama–hasil deficit
**Front:** What structural limits affected Mughal Deccan provinces?
**Definition (learn):** Viceroy, provincial diwan, mansab and jagir assignments, fort garrisons, revenue assessment, recruitment of local elites, and treaty buffers were the instruments; their limits were jama inflation, weak hasil, distance, war and defection.
**Breakdown:**
- Deccan jagirs often had inflated jama and poor hasil (✅ owner 18 basic table).
- Assessed income exceeded realisation; subsidies from Malwa, Gujarat or Surat could be needed (✅ owner 18 basic section 11).
- Territorial expansion did not finance itself (✅ owner 18 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3035, MED-3034, MED-3095.
**Trap:** Territory ≠ revenue.
**Mains hook:** Fiscal-military limits of conquest.
**Back:** *"Jama promised, hasil short: the Deccan ran at a deficit."*

### Flashcard MED-3100 — Vijayanagara and Talikota (1565): not "ended in 1565"
**Front:** What did Talikota (1565) do to Vijayanagara and what should not be written?
**Definition (learn):** Talikota (1565) sacked the Vijayanagara capital, which opened the field for Bijapur and Golconda expansion south. Political survival continued through Aravidu and Nayaka principalities.
**Breakdown:**
- Talikota explains Bijapur and Golconda expansion after 1636 (✅ owner 18 advanced section 15).
- Vijayanagara continued through Nayaka principalities (✅ owner 18 advanced section 15).
- Full Vijayanagara–Portuguese ownership remains Topic 09 (✅ owner 18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1565.
**Links:** MED-3074, MED-3095 (Karnataka), MED-3093.
**Trap:** Vijayanagara did not disappear in 1565; the capital was sacked.
**Mains hook:** Use Talikota as context, not a full Vijayanagara narrative.
**Back:** *"Sixty-five: capital sacked, kingdom not gone."*

### Flashcard MED-3101 — Why the Mughals moved south: five drivers
**Front:** List the five drivers of Mughal southern policy.
**Definition (learn):** Imperial sovereignty; security and corridors (Khandesh, Burhanpur, Asirgarh); Deccani instability; revenue and commerce; Portuguese context.
**Breakdown:**
- Imperial sovereignty: Abu'l Fazl frames one political field (✅ owner 18 basic section 5).
- Security and corridors: Malwa, Gujarat, Surat, plateau (✅ owner 18 basic).
- Revenue and commerce: fertile tracts, ports, Karnataka textiles and indigo (✅ owner 18 basic).
- Portuguese context: cartaz control, pilgrim shipping (✅ owner 18 basic).
- Sectarian peace is a weak justification, not the sole cause (⚠️ owner 18 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3076, MED-3077, MED-3082.
**Trap:** The "civilising mission" reading is not an explanation of conquest.
**Mains hook:** Multi-causal, not one motive.
**Back:** *"Sovereignty, corridors, instability, revenue, Portuguese cartaz."*

### Flashcard MED-3102 — Trap: mobile warfare did not make artillery irrelevant
**Front:** Why is "mobile warfare made artillery irrelevant" wrong?
**Definition (learn):** Sieges and forts remained decisive; mobile Maratha forces attacked the logistics enabling sieges.
**Breakdown:**
- Artillery at Ahmadnagar, Asirgarh and Daulatabad (✅ owner 18 basic section 14).
- Guerrilla is an analytical shorthand, not a uniquely Maratha doctrine (⚠️ owner 18 advanced section 7.2).
- Avoid technological determinism (✅ owner 18 basic section 14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3083, MED-3085.
**Trap:** Mobility did not guarantee decisive victory; artillery did not guarantee consolidation.
**Mains hook:** Technology plus command, finance, alliance, terrain and time.
**Back:** *"Mobile raids cut the rope; the guns still pull the fort."*

### Flashcard MED-3103 — Historiographical debate: Malik Ambar, defender or destabiliser?
**Front:** Give the balanced verdict on Malik Ambar's place in Deccan history.
**Definition (learn):** He preserved Nizam Shahi resistance and mobilised Maratha forces; his rivalry with Bijapur and repeated reopening of war weakened a united front.
**Breakdown:**
- Satish Chandra: valiant defender of Deccani independence, but restless ambition and refusal to honour settlements prolonged war (✅ owner 18 advanced section 1).
- Legacy: confidence and service bridge to later Maratha power, not inevitability (✅ owner 18 advanced section 17).
- Jahangir's 1617 and 1621 restraint: deliberate (⚠️ owner 18).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1610–1626.
**Links:** MED-3082, MED-3087, MED-3089.
**Trap:** Malik Ambar did not permanently save Ahmadnagar (✅ owner 18 advanced trap).
**Mains hook:** Use his career to test the "Maratha rise" narrative.
**Back:** *"Defender, destabiliser, both: resistance without unity."*

### GROUP — Mughal Foreign Policy

### Flashcard MED-3104 — Concept: Mughal foreign policy as court practice, not a ministry
**Front:** What does "foreign policy" mean for the Mughals and what does it not mean?
**Definition (learn):** A changing court practice in which the security of the Indian revenue empire, Timurid dynastic memory, imperial honour, commercial movement and feasible military reach interacted. Not a modern foreign ministry, fixed borders or national-security doctrine.
**Breakdown:**
- Policy was made through emperor, princes, nobles, frontier governors, envoys and merchants (✅ owner 19 basic section 1).
- No permanent resident embassies; repeated missions carried information (✅ owner 19 basic section 6).
- Four courts: Mughal, Uzbek, Safavid, Ottoman (✅ owner 19 basic section 5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3105, MED-3111, MED-3110.
**Trap:** "Foreign policy" is a modern label; avoid nation-state language.
**Mains hook:** Qualified court diplomacy with India-centred security as the core interest.
**Back:** *"Court practice, not ministry: security, memory, honour, trade, reach."*

### Flashcard MED-3105 — The north-west system: Kabul, Ghazni, Qandahar, Hindukush
**Front:** What was the Kabul–Ghazni–Qandahar line and why is "scientific frontier" not a border?
**Definition (learn):** A defensive system of forts, water, roads, garrisons and allies on the north-west; Satish Chandra's language of a defensible frontier, not a modern legal line.
**Breakdown:**
- Kabul: indispensable base; Khyber-Peshawar-Attock: seasonal and contested; Ghazni: corridor node; Qandahar: fortified junction (✅ owner 19 basic section 2).
- Oxus: route and obstacle, fordable, not self-defending (✅ owner 19 advanced section 19).
- Hindukush as a strategic boundary of interest (✅ owner 19 advanced section 7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3106, MED-3113, MED-3115, MED-3116.
**Trap:** Hindukush strategic line ≠ fixed international border; Oxus ≠ defensible frontier (✅ owner 19 advanced Prelims).
**Mains hook:** A system, not a wall: fort + water + road + garrison + intelligence + allies.
**Back:** *"Kabul shields, Ghazni links, Qandahar hinges, Oxus is only a river."*

### Flashcard MED-3106 — Babur: Kabul (1504), Qandahar (1507, 1522), Samarqand (1511)
**Front:** Give the Babur dates that shaped the Timurid inheritance.
**Definition (learn):** Babur captured Kabul in 1504 (his durable base), briefly held Qandahar in 1507, briefly regained Samarqand in 1511 with Safavid support, and secured Qandahar in 1522.
**Breakdown:**
- Samarqand recovery was short-lived (✅ owner 19 basic section 4).
- Timurid genealogy shaped prestige, not a continuous reconquest programme (✅ owner 19 basic section 4).
- Detailed Babur chronology is Topic 12 (✅ owner 19).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1504; 1507; 1511; 1522.
**Links:** MED-3107, MED-3108, MED-3116, MED-3120 (ladder).
**Trap:** Timurid memory ≠ operational reconquest plan.
**Mains hook:** Ancestral prestige vs India-centred security.
**Back:** *"Four Kabul, seven Qandahar, eleven Samarqand, twenty-two Qandahar secured."*

### Flashcard MED-3107 — Humayun and the Safavid bargain: Kabul and Qandahar (1545)
**Front:** What did Humayun recover in 1545 and with whose help?
**Definition (learn):** Humayun recovered Qandahar and Kabul in 1545 with Safavid help in the background; the Safavid bargain was political, not simple sectarian conversion.
**Breakdown:**
- Shah Tahmasp was the patron after exile (✅ owner 19 basic section 4).
- Qandahar remained a negotiable, disputed hinge (✅ owner 19 basic section 4).
- Detailed exile is Topic 13 (✅ owner 19).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1545.
**Links:** MED-3106, MED-3109, MED-3116.
**Trap:** Safavid help ≠ permanent Mughal dependence.
**Mains hook:** The long-term triangle: Safavids as patrons and rivals.
**Back:** *"Forty-five: Humayun takes back Kabul and Qandahar with Tahmasp behind him."*

### Flashcard MED-3108 — Uzbeks: displacers of the Timurids, divided but dangerous
**Front:** Why were the Uzbeks a central danger in Mughal foreign policy?
**Definition (learn):** Uzbek forces displaced the Timurids from Transoxiana, contested Khurasan with Iran and could threaten Balkh, Badakhshan and Kabul.
**Breakdown:**
- Not a single permanently united power; dynastic and tribal divisions changed the danger (✅ owner 19 basic section 5).
- Uzbek encouragement could exploit frontier unrest (✅ owner 19 basic section 7).
- Abdullah Khan (1572–98) and Nazr Muhammad (1628) are key figures (✅ owner 19).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** See MED-3112 and MED-3119.
**Links:** MED-3109, MED-3112, MED-3119.
**Trap:** Uzbek rhetoric of anti-Shia war did not produce a permanent Sunni bloc.
**Mains hook:** Manage division; deter direct threat.
**Back:** *"Uzbeks: pushers of Timurids, rivals of Persia, never one front."*

### Flashcard MED-3109 — Safavid Iran: counterweight, patron and rival
**Front:** Why was Safavid Iran simultaneously ally and rival?
**Definition (learn):** The Safavids were a counterweight to Uzbeks, an old source of aid to Babur and Humayun, a commercial and cultural partner, and a rival claimant to Qandahar.
**Breakdown:**
- "Friendship with Persia" was a strategic keynote, not harmony (✅ owner 19 basic section 5).
- Shah Abbas I (captured Qandahar 1622) and Shah Abbas II (recaptured 1649) (✅ owner 19 advanced).
- Sectarian labels insufficient (✅ owner 19 basic section 5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Shah Abbas I 1622; Shah Abbas II 1649.
**Links:** MED-3110, MED-3118, MED-3124.
**Trap:** Sunni rhetoric ≠ permanent Sunni bloc (✅ owner 19 advanced Prelims).
**Mains hook:** State the sectarian language, then test it against security, balance and trade.
**Back:** *"Persia: counter to Uzbeks, partner in trade, rival for Qandahar."*

### Flashcard MED-3110 — Ottomans: Padshah-i-Islam, prestigious but distant
**Front:** Why did the Mughals not join an Ottoman-Mughal-Uzbek bloc?
**Definition (learn):** The Ottomans controlled West Asia and the eastern Mediterranean, claimed Islamic prestige through titles such as Padshah-i-Islam, fought Safavid Iran, and held naval strength the land-centred Mughals lacked.
**Breakdown:**
- Distance, status rivalry, Uzbek unreliability and Iran's value as counterweight limited schemes (✅ owner 19 basic section 5).
- Shah Jahan's 1636 proposal to Murad IV was aspirational (✅ owner 19 advanced section 10).
- Ottoman embassy to Aurangzeb in 1680 illustrated status diplomacy (✅ owner 19 basic section 14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1636 proposal; 1680 embassy.
**Links:** MED-3120, MED-3127, MED-3128.
**Trap:** Proposed cooperation ≠ operational alliance.
**Mains hook:** Important context, limited operational alliance.
**Back:** *"Padshah-i-Islam, far away: talk without a bloc."*

### Flashcard MED-3111 — Embassy vocabulary: elchi, safir, letters, status and information
**Front:** What are elchi and safir, and what did an embassy carry?
**Definition (learn):** Ambassadors were called elchi or safir; embassies carried letters, news, requests, gifts, verbal instructions and escorts.
**Breakdown:**
- Status language: equal, superior, inferior, elder or younger, "brother" (✅ owner 19 basic section 6).
- Titles, robes, seating and embraces staged rank (✅ owner 19 basic section 6).
- No permanent resident embassies; information lag made delegated command vital (✅ owner 19 basic section 6).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3104, MED-3113.
**Trap:** Embassy ≠ permanent resident mission; gift or courtesy ≠ surrender of sovereignty (✅ owner 19 advanced Prelims).
**Mains hook:** Diplomatic ceremony as evidence of a negotiated relationship.
**Back:** *"Elchi or safir: letters in, news out, no resident seat."*

### Flashcard MED-3112 — Balkh: two Uzbek seizures (1572–73 and 1583)
**Front:** Give the Balkh ladder and explain why 1572–73 and 1583 are not contradictory.
**Definition (learn):** Abdullah Khan Uzbek first seized Balkh in 1572–73; Shahrukh Mirza recovered it; Abdullah reconquered it in 1583; he took Badakhshan in 1585.
**Breakdown:**
- Two seizures separated by a Timurid recovery (✅ owner 19 advanced section 7).
- The buffer pressure increased after the first seizure (✅ owner 19 advanced table).
- Akbar annexed Kabul in 1585 after Mirza Hakim's death (✅ owner 19 advanced section 7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1572–73; 1583; 1585.
**Links:** MED-3108, MED-3113, MED-3114.
**Trap:** 1572–73 ≠ 1583 (✅ owner 19 advanced Prelims).
**Mains hook:** Buffer pressure rising and falling.
**Back:** *"Seventy-two-three seized, eighty-three retaken, eighty-five Badakhshan."*

### Flashcard MED-3113 — Akbar's 1577 refusal: law and religion no ground for war
**Front:** What did Akbar reply when Abdullah Khan urged a joint war on Safavid Iran?
**Definition (learn):** Abdullah urged an anti-Safavid partition using Sunni solidarity and pilgrim difficulties; Akbar replied that differences in law and religion were not sufficient grounds for conquest.
**Breakdown:**
- Strategic logic: strong Iran checked Uzbeks; distant war gained nothing; Akbar would act only if Kabul or Indian possessions were threatened (✅ owner 19 advanced section 7).
- Gujarat offered another route for pilgrims and maritime traffic (✅ owner 19 advanced).
- Not modern internationalism (⚠️ owner 19 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1577.
**Links:** MED-3112, MED-3111, MED-3128.
**Trap:** Akbar's refusal ≠ moral principle of tolerance; it was strategic.
**Mains hook:** Interest-based diplomacy refuses sectarian blocs.
**Back:** *"Seventy-seven: law and religion are not enough to conquer."*

### Flashcard MED-3114 — Mirza Hakim's death (1585) and Kabul annexed
**Front:** What did Akbar do in Kabul in 1585 and why was it significant?
**Definition (learn):** After Mirza Hakim died in 1585, Akbar annexed Kabul and supervised the north-west from Lahore for much of 1585–98.
**Breakdown:**
- Kabul was the anchor of India's outer defence and a trade node, not a dynastic appanage (✅ owner 19 basic section 7).
- Akbar moved toward Attock during the crisis; Raja Birbal died in frontier fighting (✅ owner 19 basic section 7 and owner 19 advanced Prelims).
- Lahore court 1585–98 (✅ owner 15 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1585; 1585–98 Lahore supervision.
**Links:** MED-3018, MED-3112, MED-3115.
**Trap:** Kabul ≠ Balkh (Kabul is the base; Balkh the buffer).
**Mains hook:** Kabul as indispensable base.
**Back:** *"Eighty-five: Hakim dead, Kabul ours."*

### Flashcard MED-3115 — The Hindukush arrangement (Hakim Humam)
**Front:** What understanding did Hakim Humam carry to Abdullah Khan?
**Definition (learn):** A practical understanding that treated the Hindukush as a strategic boundary of interest: Mughals limited immediate ambitions in Balkh and Badakhshan; Uzbeks restrained claims toward Kabul and Qandahar; neither side renounced claims permanently.
**Breakdown:**
- Hakim Humam carried a letter and verbal message (✅ owner 19 advanced section 7).
- Bounded, not a treaty line (✅ owner 19 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated more precisely than the 1580s–1590s (⚠️ owner 19).
**Links:** MED-3105, MED-3114.
**Trap:** Hindukush arrangement ≠ modern boundary; not control of every pass.
**Mains hook:** Mutual restraint, not a settled frontier.
**Back:** *"Hakim Humam: Hindukush as the line of restraint, not the line on a map."*

### Flashcard MED-3116 — Qandahar 1595: the golden key and the transfer
**Front:** How did Akbar acquire Qandahar in 1595?
**Definition (learn):** Akbar had considered Qandahar by 1591 and instructed Khan-i-Khanan to prefer the "golden key" of diplomacy to the sword; by 1595 local Mirzas surrendered the fort, cut off from Iranian support, while Uzbeks pressed nearby.
**Breakdown:**
- 1591 instruction to Abdur Rahim Khan-i-Khanan (✅ owner 19 basic section 8).
- 1595 surrender of local Mirzas (✅ owner 19 advanced).
- "Transfer facilitated by local politics", not "Akbar conquered Persia" (✅ owner 19 basic section 8).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1591 instruction; 1595 acquisition.
**Links:** MED-3105, MED-3118, MED-3019.
**Trap:** Qandahar 1595 was not a conquest of Iran.
**Mains hook:** Diplomacy plus local politics.
**Back:** *"Ninety-one golden key, ninety-five Qandahar in hand."*

### Flashcard MED-3117 — Jahangir and Shah Abbas I: embassy cordiality (1611)
**Front:** What did Shah Abbas's 1611 embassy bring and how did Jahangir respond?
**Definition (learn):** Shah Abbas sent an embassy with Gilan horses, carpets and silks; Jahangir sent Khan Alam in return; Muhammad Hussain Chalabi was appointed royal trade commissioner.
**Breakdown:**
- Reception at Qazvin staged "brotherly" equality (✅ owner 19 advanced section 9).
- Indian merchants (often Multanis, including Hindu and Jain) operated in Iranian cities; totals are not census data (✅ owner 19 advanced).
- Persian envoys raised Qandahar in 1620 (✅ owner 19 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1611; 1620 claim.
**Links:** MED-3118, MED-3129.
**Trap:** Cordiality ≠ preparedness.
**Mains hook:** Diplomatic cordiality cannot substitute for a prepared fort.
**Back:** *"Eleven: horses for Khan Alam, Chalabi in charge of trade."*

### Flashcard MED-3118 — Qandahar lost (1622): garrison numbers and the Khurram factor
**Front:** Why did Qandahar fall in 1622 and what garrison figure is disputed?
**Definition (learn):** Shah Abbas I captured Qandahar in 1622 after the Persian claim persisted, Safavid preparation was superior, the Mughal garrison was limited, relief was slow and Prince Khurram refused or delayed.
**Breakdown:**
- Iqbalnama-i Jahangiri gives 3000 Mughal troops; Jahangir's memoir mentions only 300 or 400 servants (✅ owner 19 advanced section 9). ⚠️ Conflict.
- Wikipedia: "the small garrison of 3,000 men" and governor "Abdul Aziz Khan" (✅ Wikipedia: Siege of Kandahar (1622)); city fell on 22 June (✅ Wikipedia).
- Do not explain 1622 only through a Nur Jahan junta (✅ owner 19 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1622; garrison 3000 (Iqbalnama) vs 300–400 (memoir); Wikipedia 3000.
**Links:** MED-3116, MED-3117, MED-3146 (Nur Jahan junta, Topic 20).
**Trap:** Qandahar's loss ≠ end of Mughal-Safavid diplomacy; it ended only the cordial phase (✅ owner 19 advanced).
**Mains hook:** Diplomacy cannot replace fort, command and relief.
**Back:** *"Twenty-two: Qandahar fell, the garrison numbers quarrel."*

### Flashcard MED-3119 — Nazr Muhammad attacks Kabul (1628)
**Front:** What did Nazr Muhammad do in 1628 and how did the Mughals respond?
**Definition (learn):** Nazr Muhammad attacked Kabul, occupied the city and invested the fort, then withdrew before Mughal reinforcements; Mughals occupied Bamiyan temporarily and used diplomacy to isolate him.
**Breakdown:**
- Yalingtosh raided toward Kabul and Ghazni earlier (✅ owner 19 advanced section 10).
- Mughal defence and artillery checked those efforts (✅ owner 19 advanced).
- Frequent missions 1633–39 discussed anti-Safavid arrangements (✅ owner 19 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1628.
**Links:** MED-3108, MED-3114, MED-3120.
**Trap:** Withdrawal of raiders ≠ Mughal defeat.
**Mains hook:** Relief and diplomacy kept Kabul safe.
**Back:** *"Twenty-eight: Kabul besieged, Nazr gone before relief."*

### Flashcard MED-3120 — Shah Jahan's 1636 proposal to Murad IV
**Front:** What did Shah Jahan propose in 1636 and why did it not become an operational alliance?
**Definition (learn):** Shah Jahan wrote to Ottoman Sultan Murad IV proposing pressure on Iran from India, Turan and Ottoman territory.
**Breakdown:**
- Uzbeks were unreliable; Ottomans too distant for timely action (✅ owner 19 advanced section 10).
- Ottoman superiority claims offended Mughal equality (✅ owner 19 advanced).
- Shah Jahan kept diplomatic flexibility (✅ owner 19 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1636.
**Links:** MED-3110, MED-3121.
**Trap:** Aspiration ≠ alliance.
**Mains hook:** Diplomatic aspiration vs operational reach.
**Back:** *"Thirty-six: a letter to Murad, not an army."*

### Flashcard MED-3121 — Ali Mardan Khan's defection and the recovery of Qandahar (1638)
**Front:** How did the Mughals recover Qandahar in 1638?
**Definition (learn):** The Safavid governor Ali Mardan Khan defected, transferring the fort to Shah Jahan in 1638 through political inducement.
**Breakdown:**
- Not a successful siege of Iran (✅ owner 19 advanced section 11).
- Shah Jahan offered a payment equivalent to the fort's revenue; possession and diplomacy could coexist (✅ owner 19 advanced).
- Shah Safi died in 1642 while preparing a recovery movement (✅ owner 19 advanced section 11).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1638; 1642.
**Links:** MED-3118, MED-3120, MED-3122.
**Trap:** Defection ≠ battlefield recovery.
**Mains hook:** Transferred allegiance as a tool of statecraft.
**Back:** *"Thirty-eight: the governor switched sides, the fort came over."*

### Flashcard MED-3122 — Balkh–Badakhshan campaign (1646–47): Murad, Aurangzeb, withdrawal
**Front:** Summarise the Balkh campaign of 1646–47.
**Definition (learn):** Shah Jahan's attempt to prop up Nazr Muhammad as a friendly ruler at Balkh, which became coercive occupation under Murad; Aurangzeb with Ali Mardan Khan won a battlefield success in 1647; the Mughals withdrew in October 1647.
**Breakdown:**
- 1646 force (per Part II): 50,000 horse, 10,000 foot, musketeers, rocketeers, gunners and Rajputs; attribute the figure to the source (✅ owner 19 advanced section 12).
- Murad demanded Nazr Muhammad's attendance; the Uzbek ruler fled; Mughals had possession without a client (✅ owner 19 advanced).
- Sadullah Khan for administration; Aurangzeb for the military crisis (✅ owner 19 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1645 appeal; 1646 entry; 1647 battle; October 1647 withdrawal.
**Links:** MED-3123, MED-3124, MED-3121.
**Trap:** Balkh was not a total defeat, nor a permanent conquest.
**Mains hook:** Client plan vs direct occupation.
**Back:** *"Forty-six Murad enters, forty-seven Aurangzeb wins, October goes home."*

### Flashcard MED-3123 — Aurangzeb's 1647 battlefield success and what it did not solve
**Front:** How did Aurangzeb defeat the Uzbeks in 1647 and what problems remained?
**Definition (learn):** Aurangzeb did not try to defend the Oxus absolutely; the river was fordable near Balkh, so he used pickets and a mobile main force with artillery. Abdul Aziz crossed and was routed outside Balkh.
**Breakdown:**
- Real military success (✅ owner 19 advanced section 12).
- Did not solve local hostility, lack of a viable client, roaming bands, supplies, winter, forage, noble reluctance or Persian hostility (✅ owner 19 advanced).
- Battlefield success ≠ political sustainability.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1647.
**Links:** MED-3122, MED-3102 (artillery trap), MED-3131 (ladder).
**Trap:** Balkh was not "total Mughal defeat" (✅ owner 19 advanced table).
**Mains hook:** The best verdict: militarily successful, politically and logistically unsustainable.
**Back:** *"Forty-seven: the Oxus ford was not a wall, the win was not a policy."*

### Flashcard MED-3124 — Qandahar lost (1649) and the three failed expeditions
**Front:** Give the failed Qandahar expeditions after Shah Abbas II's 1649 recapture.
**Definition (learn):** Aurangzeb's field success in 1649 did not capture the fort; the second siege in 1652 failed; in 1653 Dara Shukoh's large army with very heavy guns failed to force surrender.
**Breakdown:**
- Shah Abbas II recovered Qandahar in 1649 (✅ owner 19 basic and advanced).
- Why they failed: prepared fort; siege versus field warfare; transport limits; Lahore supply line; scorched-earth pressure; winter; time and cost; competing princely commands (✅ owner 19 advanced section 13).
- Repeated failure damaged prestige more than the loss of "a mass of rocks" (✅ owner 19 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1649; 1652; 1653.
**Links:** MED-3109, MED-3125, MED-3131.
**Trap:** Not "Mughal artillery was weak".
**Mains hook:** Fort, engineering, supply and season, not generic weakness.
**Back:** *"Forty-nine lost, fifty-two failed, fifty-three failed with Dara's guns."*

### Flashcard MED-3125 — Trap: Qandahar failure is not weak artillery
**Front:** Why does the owner reject "Mughal artillery was weak" as an explanation for Qandahar's failures?
**Definition (learn):** Mughal artillery helped win the Balkh field battle; Qandahar's failures came from fortification, engineering, supply, scorched earth and seasonal limits.
**Breakdown:**
- Artillery success at Balkh (✅ owner 19 advanced section 12).
- Long supply line from Lahore (✅ owner 19 advanced section 13).
- Winter siege impracticable (✅ owner 19 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1649–53.
**Links:** MED-3124, MED-3123.
**Trap:** The artillery trap pair.
**Mains hook:** Siege technology in context.
**Back:** *"Guns win fields, forts need the belly of the army."*

### Flashcard MED-3126 — Historiography: Riazul Islam on Balkh (adventurism or realpolitik?)
**Front:** How do historians debate Shah Jahan's Balkh campaign?
**Definition (learn):** Riazul Islam criticised Shah Jahan's adventurism and Timurid obsession; the evidence also shows a rational effort to divide Uzbek power and restore a friendly client.
**Breakdown:**
- Realpolitik reading: buffer and client logic (✅ owner 19 advanced debate 3).
- Adventurism reading: overreach after easy occupation (✅ owner 19 advanced).
- Graded verdict: rational aims enlarged by prestige beyond sustainable reach (✅ owner 19 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1646–47.
**Links:** MED-3122, MED-3123.
**Trap:** Balkh ≠ total defeat ≠ permanent conquest.
**Mains hook:** Use both readings; grade them.
**Back:** *"Riazul says reckless, the client plan says rational: both are partly right."*

### Flashcard MED-3127 — Aurangzeb's later Persian diplomacy: 1664 insult and the 1680 Ottoman embassy
**Front:** What later Persian and Ottoman episodes illustrate status diplomacy?
**Definition (learn):** Aurangzeb abandoned futile Qandahar recovery and resumed diplomacy with Iran. Shah Abbas II's 1664 insult and invasion threat caused preparation in Punjab and Kabul; his death ended the danger. An Ottoman embassy in 1680 illustrated status diplomacy.
**Breakdown:**
- 1664 threat and preparation (✅ owner 19 basic section 14 and advanced timeline).
- 1680 Ottoman embassy (✅ owner 19 basic section 14).
- These are external codas; Aurangzeb's internal policy is Topic 22 (✅ owner 19 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1664; 1680.
**Links:** MED-3110, MED-3109.
**Trap:** Aurangzeb's diplomacy is not a foreign-policy failure in every sense; it resumed.
**Mains hook:** Diplomacy after failed expeditions.
**Back:** *"Sixty-four insult, eighty Ottomans: status still matters."*

### Flashcard MED-3128 — Pilgrimage, Red Sea and Portuguese maritime context
**Front:** How did pilgrimage and Portuguese sea power affect Mughal foreign policy?
**Definition (learn):** Routes through Iran could be disrupted; Akbar pointed to routes via Gujarat and maritime access when answering Abdullah Khan's pilgrimage argument; the Portuguese cartaz system affected Arabian Sea shipping; Akbar used anti-firangi language without a naval alliance.
**Breakdown:**
- Hajj protection had religious and political value but did not determine all relations (✅ owner 19 basic section 15).
- Ottoman attempts in the Red Sea and Persian Gulf (✅ owner 19 basic section 15).
- The Mughals were a land empire without an Ottoman-type navy (✅ owner 19 basic section 15).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3113, MED-3110, MED-3101.
**Trap:** Maritime pressure influenced routes; it did not explain Qandahar policy (✅ owner 19 basic).
**Mains hook:** Bounded conclusion.
**Back:** *"Pilgrims by Gujarat, Portuguese on the sea, Mughals on land."*

### Flashcard MED-3129 — Trade, horses and merchant communities on the routes
**Front:** What goods and people moved through Mughal foreign routes?
