**Definition (learn):** Horses for cavalry; Indian textiles; Persian silks, carpets and luxury goods; gifts; merchants, caravan leaders, pilgrims and information carriers.
**Breakdown:**
- Indian merchant communities operated in Iranian and Central Asian cities (✅ owner 19 basic section 16).
- Merchant presence connected customs and revenue to route security (✅ owner 19 basic).
- Traveller estimates are not census (✅ owner 19 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3117, MED-3116, MED-3128.
**Trap:** "Trade prospered" in a chronicle may record aspiration.
**Mains hook:** Commerce continued despite court rivalry.
**Back:** *"Horses in, silks out, Multanis on every road."*

### Flashcard MED-3130 — Trap: Kabul is not Balkh; Qandahar is not Gandhara
**Front:** What three close distinctions must not be merged?
**Definition (learn):** Kabul (indispensable base) ≠ Balkh (forward buffer); Qandahar (fort-route-commercial hinge) ≠ Balkh (client-buffer experiment); Qandahar ≠ Gandhara.
**Breakdown:**
- Kabul: anchored India's outer defence (✅ owner 19 advanced Prelims).
- Qandahar: fort + water + route + trade (✅ owner 19 advanced section 8).
- Gandhara is an ancient region, not Qandahar (✅ owner 19 basic note).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3114, MED-3116, MED-3122.
**Trap:** The three merged pairs.
**Mains hook:** Use distinctions to avoid vague answers.
**Back:** *"Kabul is the base, Balkh the buffer, Qandahar the hinge; not Gandhara."*

### Flashcard MED-3131 — Chronology ladder of Mughal foreign policy, 1504–1664
**Front:** Give the ordered foreign-policy ladder.
**Definition (learn):** 1504 Kabul; 1507 and 1522 Qandahar; 1511 Samarqand; 1545 Qandahar and Kabul; 1572–73 and 1583 Balkh; 1577 refusal; 1585 Kabul annexed; 1591 golden key; 1595 Qandahar; 1611 embassy; 1622 Qandahar lost; 1628 Kabul attacked; 1636 Murad proposal; 1638 Qandahar recovered; 1645–47 Balkh; 1649 Qandahar lost; 1652–53 failed expeditions; 1664 insult; 1680 Ottoman embassy.
**Breakdown:**
- Akbar's ladder: 1577 → 1585 → 1595 (✅ owner 19 advanced chronology).
- Jahangir: 1611 cordial → 1622 loss (✅ owner 19 advanced).
- Shah Jahan: 1638 → 1646–47 → 1649–53 (✅ owner 19 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As above (owner 19 advanced section 20).
**Links:** MED-3104 to MED-3130.
**Trap:** Sequence matters: diplomacy first, field battle second, siege third.
**Mains hook:** Chronology shows policy oscillating between buffer and occupation.
**Back:** *"Four, eleven, forty-five, seventy-seven, eighty-five, ninety-five, twenty-two, forty-seven, forty-nine: the foreign ladder."*

### GROUP — Jahangir & the Early Seventeenth Century (Nur Jahan)

### Flashcard MED-3132 — Concept: consolidation under pressure (1605–27)
**Front:** What is the exam-safe thesis for Jahangir's reign?
**Definition (learn):** Jahangir (Salim, reign 1605–27) preserved and extended much of Akbar's fiscal-military and composite political order, while succession rivalry, frontier strain and household authority made the reign more contested. Consolidation under pressure, not decline from 1605.
**Breakdown:**
- Five problems: accession; Mewar, Bengal and Kangra consolidation; Nur Jahan and the junta debate; Khurram and Mahabat rebellions; governing and religious persona (✅ owner 20 basic section 1).
- Stop at the 1627 succession bridge (Shah Jahan is Topic 21) (✅ owner 20 basic section 1).
- Continuity with Akbar: composite nobility and broad tolerance (✅ owner 20 advanced section 1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1605–27.
**Links:** MED-3133, MED-3139, MED-3146, MED-3154.
**Trap:** Not "decline from 1605"; not "Nur Jahan's sovereignty" (✅ owner 20 basic trap).
**Mains hook:** Uneven consolidation, not decline.
**Back:** *"Five-oh-five to twenty-seven: pressure, not collapse."*

### Flashcard MED-3133 — Accession as a bargain (1605)
**Front:** How did Jahangir's accession become secure?
**Definition (learn):** A bargain of dynastic claim, noble recognition, rewards and offices, public justice language and continued revenue-military machinery. Neither hereditary law nor a free noble election.
**Breakdown:**
- Salim was the surviving eldest son, but his Allahabad establishment and Abu'l Fazl's killing had damaged trust (✅ owner 20 basic section 3).
- Man Singh and Aziz Koka briefly considered Khusrau; no decisive support (✅ owner 20 basic section 3).
- No general purge at accession (✅ owner 20 basic section 3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1605.
**Links:** MED-3134, MED-3137, MED-3022.
**Trap:** Succession was negotiated; it was not automatic.
**Mains hook:** Accession as political work.
**Back:** *"Five-oh-five: a bargain, not a birthright."*

### Flashcard MED-3134 — Salim's Allahabad establishment and Abu'l Fazl's death (1602)
**Front:** What did Salim do at Allahabad and what happened to Abu'l Fazl?
**Definition (learn):** Salim kept a separate political base at Allahabad (1599–1604), with coins and autonomous appointments; Bir Singh Deo Bundela killed Abu'l Fazl in 1602 in an action connected to Salim.
**Breakdown:**
- Coinage and appointments as an expression of autonomy (✅ owner 20 basic timeline).
- Pre-accession conflict; not the full Topic 15 narrative (✅ owner 20 basic timeline).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Allahabad 1599–1604; 1602.
**Links:** MED-3022, MED-3133.
**Trap:** Abu'l Fazl's death ≠ Akbar's campaign; it belongs to succession politics.
**Mains hook:** Succession rivalry before 1605.
**Back:** *"Ninety-nine to four at Allahabad; two Bundela blade for Fazl."*

### Flashcard MED-3135 — The twelve orders (Jahangirnama)
**Front:** What were the twelve orders and how should their implementation be judged?
**Definition (learn):** Early regulations recorded in the Jahangirnama, covering unofficial cesses, merchant property, inheritance, sarais, forcible seizure of houses or peasant land, hospitals and mutilation as punishment.
**Breakdown:**
- Three evidence levels: textual proclamation; administrative transmission; local implementation (✅ owner 20 basic section 4).
- Benevolent programme in text does not prove local practice (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Early reign (not precisely dated in owner).
**Links:** MED-3136, MED-3153.
**Trap:** Orders ≠ implementation.
**Mains hook:** Source and practice, separated.
**Back:** *"Twelve orders: declared, sent, maybe obeyed."*

### Flashcard MED-3136 — Zanjir-i-adl: the chain of justice
**Front:** What was the zanjir-i-adl and what did it signify?
**Definition (learn):** A chain with bells set at the Agra riverfront or fort, projecting direct royal accessibility to petitioners.
**Breakdown:**
- Symbol and petition route in a ruler-centred monarchy (✅ owner 20 basic section 4).
- Not an independent judiciary or equality before impersonal law (✅ owner 20 basic).
- One enquiry into an outrage linked to Muqarrab Khan led to punishment and reduction of mansab (✅ owner 20 basic). ⚠️ one case is an intervention, not uniform justice.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Early reign.
**Links:** MED-3135, MED-3048 (legal pluralism).
**Trap:** Chain of justice ≠ judicial review.
**Mains hook:** Ideal and intervention, not uniform justice.
**Back:** *"Bells at Agra: the king listens, the courts do not."*

### Flashcard MED-3137 — Khusrau's revolt (1606) and his blinding and death (1621)
**Front:** What happened in Khusrau's 1606 revolt and afterwards?
**Definition (learn):** Six months after accession Khusrau escaped with a small following toward Lahore; Dilawar Khan kept Lahore closed; Jahangir defeated him near Bhairowal and captured him as he tried to reach Afghanistan.
**Breakdown:**
- Severe punishment of followers and penalties on persons who blessed or helped the rebel (✅ owner 20 basic section 5).
- Blinded after an alleged conspiracy; died in 1621 at Burhanpur in Khurram's custody (✅ owner 20 basic and advanced).
- Causal frame: unsettled accession + princely claim + mobile followers + Punjab routes + exemplary punishment (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1606; Bhairowal; 1621 Burhanpur.
**Links:** MED-3138, MED-3133, MED-3149.
**Trap:** Khusrau's revolt is not a Hindu-Muslim or Sikh-Muslim war (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Mains hook:** Succession politics explain the revolt more than religion.
**Back:** *"Six: Khusrau runs to Lahore, Bhairowal catches him, twenty-one ends him."*

### Flashcard MED-3138 — Guru Arjan's death (1606): four separate dimensions
**Front:** Why is the 1606 Guru Arjan episode hard to summarise and what four dimensions must be kept apart?
**Definition (learn):** Jahangir's memoir treats Guru Arjan as politically culpable for blessing or assisting Khusrau; Sikh traditions and Jesuit testimony emphasise martyrdom, fine and torture.
**Breakdown:**
- Political suspicion after rebellion (✅ owner 20 basic section 5).
- Jahangir's hostile religious language (✅ owner 20 basic).
- Fine, confiscation and punishment; disagreement over whether execution was direct or a heavy fine (✅ owner 20 basic).
- Sikh community memory and later consequences; not an instant militarised Sikh conflict (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1606.
**Links:** MED-3137, MED-3151.
**Trap:** Do not reassign later Sikh-history demands to the 1606 episode (✅ owner 20 advanced section 16).
**Mains hook:** Separate the four dimensions before judging.
**Back:** *"Six: Arjan fined, tortured or killed: four causes, four answers."*

### Flashcard MED-3139 — Mewar settlement (1615): suzerainty with honour
**Front:** What were the terms of the 1615 Mewar settlement?
**Definition (learn):** The 1615 settlement recognised Mughal suzerainty; Rana Amar Singh was not required to attend personally; a son or brother was to serve; Karan Singh was received with honour and mansab; Mewar kept its ruling house; Chittor's fortifications were restricted.
**Breakdown:**
- Mughal pressure, Mewar's exhaustion, Sisodia honour and unequal service = durable accommodation (✅ owner 20 basic section 6).
- Not annexation (✅ owner 20 advanced trap).
- Jahangir operated from Ajmer; Khurram renewed pressure (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1613–15; settlement 1615.
**Links:** MED-3017, MED-3132, MED-3141.
**Trap:** Mewar = suzerainty plus honour-sensitive accommodation, not annexation.
**Mains hook:** Conciliation over conquest, but unequal.
**Back:** *"Fifteen: Amar Singh stays home, Karan Singh serves with honour."*

### Flashcard MED-3140 — Bengal and Islam Khan (1608): Dacca as provincial centre
**Front:** What did Islam Khan achieve in Bengal after 1608?
**Definition (learn):** Jahangir appointed Islam Khan in 1608; he shifted the provincial centre to Dacca, won over zamindars, defeated the Barah-Bhuiyan confederacy associated with Musa Khan and broke Usman Khan's resistance.
**Breakdown:**
- Jessore, Sylhet, Cachar and Kamrup entered the Mughal field; an attack on the Ahoms failed (✅ owner 20 basic section 6).
- Defeated chiefs were conciliated; Afghans entered the nobility in larger numbers (✅ owner 20 basic).
- Layered consolidation, not one-date conquest of Bengal and Assam (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1608; Dacca.
**Links:** MED-3015 (Bengal under Akbar), MED-3141.
**Trap:** Akbar weakened Afghans in Bengal; Jahangir's reign completed the eastern field only in part.
**Mains hook:** Campaign plus alliance plus headquarters plus service.
**Back:** *"Eight: Dacca made the capital, Bhuiyans bowed, Ahoms bit back."*

### Flashcard MED-3141 — Kangra fort (1620) and Jahangir's 1622 visit
**Front:** How was Kangra secured and what symbolic act followed?
**Definition (learn):** In 1620 a campaign under Raja Bikramajit Baghela secured the Kangra fort; a Mughal commander and faujdar were placed there; Jahangir visited in 1622, had the khutba read and ordered a mosque.
**Breakdown:**
- A 1615 attempt had failed (✅ owner 20 basic section 6).
- Chamba's submission followed (✅ owner 20 basic).
- Fort-level success, not effortless control of every Himalayan polity (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1620; 1622 visit.
**Links:** MED-3139, MED-3140.
**Trap:** Kangra proves fort success, not full administrative control.
**Mains hook:** Symbolic sovereignty through religious-political ritual.
**Back:** *"Twenty: Kangra fell, Jahangir read the khutba in 1622."*

### Flashcard MED-3142 — William Hawkins and the Swally action (1612)
**Front:** Who was Hawkins and what changed bargaining conditions in 1612?
**Definition (learn):** William Hawkins represented the English East India Company at Jahangir's court seeking commercial access; English naval success against the Portuguese near Swally (Suvali) in 1612 altered bargaining conditions on the Gujarat coast.
**Breakdown:**
- Personal access did not produce unlimited privileges (✅ owner 20 basic section 7).
- Did not transfer sovereignty or predetermine British conquest (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1612 (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Links:** MED-3143, MED-3101 (Portuguese context).
**Trap:** Hawkins ≠ treaty; Swally ≠ conquest.
**Mains hook:** Commercial encounter within Mughal sovereignty.
**Back:** *"Twelve Swally: Hawkins had access, not territory."*

### Flashcard MED-3143 — Sir Thomas Roe's embassy (1615–19) and the limited grants
**Front:** What did Sir Thomas Roe represent and what did his mission secure?
**Definition (learn):** Roe was James I's ambassador at Jahangir's court; his mission produced limited trading facilities and confirmation of the English position, especially at Surat, on Mughal terms.
**Breakdown:**
- Roe did not gain territorial sovereignty, an empire-wide monopoly, private territorial state or immunity from local authority (✅ owner 20 basic section 7).
- Wikipedia gives the Mughal stay as "until 1619" and "1615 to 1618" in different places; "permission and protection" for a factory at Surat; "no major trading privileges were conceded" (✅ Wikipedia: Sir Thomas Roe). ⚠️ The owner's 1618 farman date is not confirmed by the fetched page.
- Roe's journal is an interested European source (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1615–19 (owner); Wikipedia internal conflict noted.
**Links:** MED-3142, MED-3152, MED-3153.
**Trap:** Roe ≠ territory; trading facilities ≠ sovereignty.
**Mains hook:** Commercial diplomacy within Mughal sovereignty.
**Back:** *"Fifteen to nineteen: Roe's journal, Surat's permission, no empire."*

### Flashcard MED-3144 — Nur Jahan: biography, marriage (1611) and the Persian migrant household
**Front:** Who was Nur Jahan, when did she marry Jahangir, and what was her family?
**Definition (learn):** Born Mehr-un-Nisa (Mihr-un-Nisa), daughter of the migrant family of Mirza Ghiyas Beg; first married Ali Quli Istajlu (Sher Afghan); married Jahangir on 25 May 1611 (✅ Wikipedia: Nur Jahan).
**Breakdown:**
- Household: father Ghiyas Beg (Itimad-ud-Daulah), brother Asaf Khan, daughter Ladli Begum who married Shahryar (✅ owner 20 basic section 8).
- Asaf Khan's daughter Arjumand Banu (Mumtaz Mahal) married Khurram (✅ owner 20 basic table).
- Later chroniclers' claim that Jahangir engineered Sher Afghan's death is not accepted as secure history (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Marriage 25 May 1611 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-3145, MED-3146, MED-3149.
**Trap:** Kinship ≠ identical interest.
**Mains hook:** Household kinship as channel of access.
**Back:** *"Eleven: Mehr-un-Nisa becomes Nur Jahan, the migrant family rises."*

### Flashcard MED-3145 — Nur Jahan's forms of authority: farmans, coins, household
**Front:** What evidence associates Nur Jahan with authority?
**Definition (learn):** Command of the household; reception of petitioners; influence over patronage; farmans issued in her name; coins with Badshah Begum legends; hunting and travel; architectural and charitable patronage; active politics after 1622.
**Breakdown:**
- Coins struck in her name (✅ Wikipedia: Nur Jahan, coinage privileges). The Wikipedia page gives a Padshah Begum tenure "10 June 1620 – 28 October 1627" in the infobox (✅ Wikipedia).
- Farmans and coins show formal public authority; they do not prove that she made every decision (✅ owner 20 basic section 8).
- Gender interpretation must be specific (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Padshah Begum title 1620–27 (infobox, Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-3144, MED-3146, MED-3147.
**Trap:** Coins and farmans ≠ total rule; her career did not abolish patriarchy.
**Mains hook:** Exceptional authority within a patriarchal household.
**Back:** *"Farmans and coins in her name: power shown, not monopolised."*

### Flashcard MED-3146 — Beni Prasad's "Nur Jahan junta" thesis
**Front:** What was Beni Prasad's junta thesis?
**Definition (learn):** A stable junta of Nur Jahan, Itimad-ud-Daulah, Asaf Khan and Khurram dominant after 1611, whose favour was the route to promotion, dividing the court into two camps; Nur Jahan later turned from Khurram to Shahryar.
**Breakdown:**
- Treated as a disputed model, not a fact (⚠️ owner 20 basic section 9).
- Set aside by Satish Chandra and Nurul Hasan (✅ owner 20 advanced section 1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1611–20 model.
**Links:** MED-3147, MED-3148, MED-3118.
**Trap:** The "fixed junta" is a historiographical label, not a securely established faction (✅ owner 20 advanced Prelims).
**Mains hook:** State the thesis fairly before critiquing it.
**Back:** *"Beni's junta: four names, two camps, set aside."*

### Flashcard MED-3147 — Nurul Hasan and Satish Chandra's critique; Irfan Habib's offices
**Front:** What evidence rebuts the fixed-junta model and what supports family influence?
**Definition (learn):** Promotions remained spread among several noble families; Mahabat Khan and others kept favour outside the bloc; no secure contemporary proof of a stable Nur Jahan–Khurram alliance for 1611–20; Khurram's rise followed his Mewar and Deccan performance.
**Breakdown:**
- Irfan Habib's evidence confirms extensive family offices and governorships (✅ owner 20 basic section 9).
- Family concentration did not prove two permanent camps (⚠️ owner 20 basic).
- Alignments cut across Persian versus old nobility (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1611–20.
**Links:** MED-3146, MED-3148.
**Trap:** Family influence ≠ junta.
**Mains hook:** Set aside the junta, not Nur Jahan's power.
**Back:** *"Spread offices, no fixed camp: the junta model fails."*

### Flashcard MED-3148 — Nur Jahan's periodisation: 1611–21 and after 1622
**Front:** How should Nur Jahan's power be periodised?
**Definition (learn):** 1611–21: strong household influence and family advancement, but fluid alignments and continuing Jahangirian decisions. After 1622: Itimad-ud-Daulah's death, Jahangir's illness, Qandahar, Khurram's rebellion and Mahabat's challenge pushed her into active crisis politics.
**Breakdown:**
- Itimad-ud-Daulah's death is late 1621 or early 1622 in the owner's narrative (✅ owner 20 basic timeline).
- Active crisis politics mainly after 1622 (✅ owner 20 advanced section 1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1611–21; after 1622.
**Links:** MED-3146, MED-3149, MED-3150.
**Trap:** Do not date her crisis politics to 1611.
**Mains hook:** Periodisation avoids a flat "she ruled" claim.
**Back:** *"Eleven to twenty-one: household rising; twenty-two on: crisis."*

### Flashcard MED-3149 — Khurram's rebellion (1622–25/26): causes and outcome
**Front:** What caused Khurram's rebellion and how did it end?
**Definition (learn):** Multi-causal rebellion: succession anxiety, Deccan base, the Qandahar command dispute, jagir and resource bargaining, Shahryar–Ladli marriage, emperor–prince correspondence and shifting noble support.
**Breakdown:**
- Defeated at Bilochpur in 1623; moved through eastern India and the Deccan; defeated by Mahabat Khan (✅ owner 20 basic section 10).
- Submitted and sent Dara and Aurangzeb as hostages; Balaghat assigned to him (✅ owner 20 basic).
- Khusrau died in Khurram's custody in 1621 (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1622–25/26; Bilochpur 1623.
**Links:** MED-3137, MED-3150, MED-3155.
**Trap:** "Nur Jahan caused the rebellion" is monocausal (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Mains hook:** Princely armies and succession anxiety.
**Back:** *"Twenty-three Bilochpur, Dara and Aurangzeb as hostages: Khurram submits."*

### Flashcard MED-3150 — Mahabat Khan's coup (1626) at the Jhelum
**Front:** What happened at the Jhelum in 1626 and why did it collapse?
**Definition (learn):** Mahabat Khan, with a trusted Rajput force, seized Jahangir while the camp was crossing the Jhelum; Nur Jahan's first armed effort failed; she entered the captive camp and helped detach nobles from Mahabat.
**Breakdown:**
- Wikipedia: Mahabat led loyal Rajput soldiers to Punjab in 1626; the royal camp on the Jhelum was attacked and the emperor taken; Nur Jahan's forces later freed Jahangir (✅ Wikipedia: Mahabat Khan).
- Mahabat held the emperor's person but not treasury, administration or broad nobility (✅ owner 20 basic).
- Structural lesson: capture of the embodied sovereign ≠ control of the empire (⚠️ owner 20 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1626; Jhelum.
**Links:** MED-3149, MED-3148, MED-3155.
**Trap:** Custody of emperor ≠ control of state.
**Mains hook:** Vulnerability of personal monarchy.
**Back:** *"Twenty-six at the Jhelum: the emperor taken, the state not."*

### Flashcard MED-3151 — Religious policy: continuity with contextual coercion
**Front:** What did Jahangir keep from Akbar's sulh-i-kul, and what coercive episodes occurred?
**Definition (learn):** Continuity: no jizya restored; prohibition of forcible conversion; festivals; support for temples and brahmans; Christians kept worship; meetings with Vedantin Jadrup and Muslim saints. Coercion: Guru Arjan; jihad language in Mewar; Kangra's Islamic ritual; destruction of a Varaha image at Pushkar; a 1617 anti-Jain order (not uniformly implemented); imprisonment of Shaikh Ahmad Sirhindi.
**Breakdown:**
- Continuity list (✅ owner 20 basic section 12).
- Coercive episodes (✅ owner 20 basic section 12).
- Neither "perfect tolerance" nor "bigotry" is adequate (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1606 (Arjan); 1617 (Jain order); Sirhindi imprisonment (no date in owner).
**Links:** MED-3138, MED-3139, MED-3059.
**Trap:** Declaration ≠ implementation ≠ reversal.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish declaration, context, locality, implementation and reversal.
**Back:** *"No jizya, many festivals, a few burnings: declared tolerance, practised unevenly."*

### Flashcard MED-3152 — Jahangir's painting and naturalism (Ustad Mansur)
**Front:** What role did painting and natural observation play in Jahangir's court?
**Definition (learn):** Jahangir was a connoisseur of painting, gardens, architecture, animals and plants; his memoir records natural observation; Ustad Mansur is associated with precise studies of flora and fauna.
**Breakdown:**
- Art as self-fashioning, connoisseurship, natural history, household patronage (✅ owner 20 basic section 13).
- Itimad-ud-Daulah's tomb links household memory, white marble and inlay (✅ owner 20 basic).
- A painting is representation, not a photograph (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3153, MED-3143 (Roe as observer).
**Trap:** Art ≠ literal event record.
**Mains hook:** Use art as evidence of imperial image, not of ground truth.
**Back:** *"Mansur paints the flora, Jahangir watches the fauna, the court admires."*

### Flashcard MED-3153 — Tuzuk-i-Jahangiri / Jahangirnama: the royal memoir and its source ladder
**Front:** What can the Jahangirnama establish and what can it not?
**Definition (learn):** Indispensable royal memoir: imperial chronology, persona, observations, stated orders and justifications; not automatic proof of implementation; authorship and continuation layers differ.
**Breakdown:**
- Jahangir wrote much of it; later portions involved continuation or redaction (✅ owner 20 basic section 14).
- Other sources: Mutamad Khan's Iqbalnama, farmans, paintings, tombs, Roe and Hawkins, Sikh and Jesuit evidence (✅ owner 20 basic table).
- Method: claim → source → what it proves → corroboration → what it cannot prove (✅ owner 20 basic).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Not dated.
**Links:** MED-3135, MED-3143, MED-3118 (Iqbalnama garrison conflict).
**Trap:** Memoir ≠ neutral record.
**Mains hook:** Source criticism as the answer's spine.
**Back:** *"The memoir speaks, the rest test."*

### Flashcard MED-3154 — Jahangir's death (1627) and the succession bridge
**Front:** When and where did Jahangir die and what did it set up?
**Definition (learn):** Jahangir died in 1627 near Lahore; Asaf Khan secured Khurram's succession and Nur Jahan retired.
**Breakdown:**
- Wikipedia gives 28 October 1627 and says the death sparked a succession war (✅ Wikipedia: Nur Jahan).
- The reign's end is a bridge to Shah Jahan (Topic 21) (✅ owner 20 basic table).
- Nur Jahan's Padshah Begum tenure is shown ending 28 October 1627 (✅ Wikipedia infobox).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 28 October 1627 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** MED-3132, MED-3145, MED-3149.
**Trap:** The 1627 death ≠ the 1657–58 war of succession.
**Mains hook:** Bridge to the next reign, not its narrative.
**Back:** *"Twenty-seven, Lahore, Asaf's hand: Khurram's turn."*

### Flashcard MED-3155 — Trap: Khurram's rebellion is not the 1657–58 war of succession
**Front:** Why must Khurram's 1622–26 rebellion not be confused with the 1657–58 war?
**Definition (learn):** Khurram's rebellion (1622–25/early 1626) was a princely rebellion in Jahangir's reign; the 1657–58 succession war belongs to Shah Jahan's reign (Topic 21).
**Breakdown:**
- Mahabat's coup in 1626 is a separate event (✅ owner 20 basic Prelims).
- Jahangir's death 1627 is a bridge only (✅ owner 20 basic).
- Shah Jahan's accession and ruling class are Topic 21 (✅ owner 20 basic section 1).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1622–26; 1657–58.
**Links:** MED-3149, MED-3150, MED-3154.
**Trap:** Dates and names.
**Mains hook:** Keep the timelines apart.
**Back:** *"Twenty-two to twenty-six was Khurram's revolt, fifty-seven to fifty-eight was another war."*

## MEDIEVAL INDIAN HISTORY — Part 4: Shah Jahan, Aurangzeb, Marathas, Mughal Society and Decline

> Scope: flashcards for Medieval-Indian-History owners 21–25 (basic and advanced): Shah Jahan and the mansabdari evolution; Aurangzeb's religious policy and the Rajputs; the Marathas, Shivaji and the Deccan with the jagirdari crisis; Mughal society, economy and culture; and the decline of the Mughal empire.
> Currency: 2026-11-01. Owner-derived facts carry `(✅ owner NN)`. Facts checked against a fetched Wikipedia page carry `(✅ Wikipedia: <article>)` with the fetch date 2026-11-01. Memory-only facts and owner-internal problems carry ⚠️.
> Owner-vs-source conflicts are flagged inside the card, not silently resolved. Owner files record no verified direct CSE Prelims/GS-I PYQ routes for topics 21, 22, 23 and 25; the owner 24 PYQ ledger lists 4 Prelims GS-I demands and 1 Mains GS-I demand with the official key unavailable locally, so no answers are inferred from them.

### GROUP — Shah Jahan and the Evolution of the Mughal Ruling Class and Mansabdari System (owner 21)

### Flashcard MED-4001 — Mansabdari as a ranked service elite (concept)
**Front:** What was the Mughal ruling class, and what held the mansabdari system together?
**Definition (learn):** A ranked imperial service elite, not a fixed race, religion or hereditary peerage. Membership depended on mansab (rank), office, jagir assignment, court access, military service, kinship and demonstrated competence.
**Breakdown:**
- Two numbers define a mansab: zat (personal rank) and sawar (cavalry obligation). Dual rank was in place by Akbar's 40th regnal year, 1595–96 (✅ owner 21).
- Enforcement instruments: dagh (branding of horses) and chehra (descriptive muster roll).
- Fiscal link: the state paid mansabdars largely through jagirs (revenue assignments), so rank growth depended on revenue growth.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 500 zat and above: 123 (1595) → 518 (1656) (✅ owner 21, citing Athar Ali's prosopography). Jama index 100 (1595–96) → about 176 (1656–57) (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4002, MED-4013, MED-4016, MED-4018, MED-4024, MED-4130 (be-jagiri), MED-4086 (jagirdari mechanisms).
**Trap:** A mansab is not a post or an estate. Zat is status and pay grade, not troop count (MED-4016).
**Mains hook:** "Shah Jahan's mansabdari system worked but its fiscal base was eroding." Qualify with the zat/sawar/jagir logic, not with a single moral verdict on the emperor.
**Back:** *"Rank outran revenue: the service elite grew faster than its paying land."*

### Flashcard MED-4002 — Shah Jahan's accession: 1627 death, 1628 formal accession
**Front:** When did Shah Jahan succeed, and what is the trap in the dates?
**Definition (learn):** Jahangir died in 1627; Khurram's succession was secured through Asaf Khan, the diwan, leading nobles and the army. The formal accession is conventionally dated 1628.
**Breakdown:**
- Succession was settled by a military-political coalition, not by automatic primogeniture (⚠️ owner interpretation).
- Rival princes and claimants were eliminated (✅ owner 21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Jahangir's death 1627; formal accession 1628 (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4027 (1657–58 succession war), MED-4004 (Khan Jahan Lodi), MED-4003 (Jujhar Singh).
**Trap:** Do not write "1627 accession". 1627 is the death and succession transition; 1628 is the formal enthronement.
**Mains hook:** The 1627–28 transition shows that Mughal succession rested on coalition and military resources; it also frames the 1657–58 war as a repeat of that logic.
**Back:** *"1627 the father died, 1628 the son sat on the throne."*

### Flashcard MED-4003 — Jujhar Singh Bundela: submission, flight and Orchha
**Front:** What happened to Jujhar Singh Bundela under Shah Jahan?
**Definition (learn):** A Bundela chief who submitted in 1628–29 after imperial pressure, then later attacked a Gond ruler, was pursued and died while fleeing.
**Breakdown:**
- Submission came with indemnity and partial jagir confiscation; he then served in the Deccan (✅ owner 21).
- Later attack on Gond ruler Prem Narayan and seizure of wealth led to another campaign; he died while fleeing; territory was redistributed and family members were coerced (✅ owner 21).
- Owner notes the destruction of Bir Singh Deo's Orchha temple and mosque construction gave the victory an Islamic idiom (✅ owner 21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Submission 1628–29 (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4002, MED-4004, MED-4012.
**Trap:** Do not map-colour all frontier chiefs as uniformly annexed. Ask what tribute, route, fort or service was actually secured (⚠️ owner 21).
**Mains hook:** Use Jujhar Singh to show imperial extraction plus local reordering of authority, not neutral administration or a pure religious war (⚠️ owner interpretation).
**Back:** *"Submit, serve, rebel, flee: a Bundela cycle of pressure."*

### Flashcard MED-4004 — Khan Jahan Lodi's revolt (1629–31)
**Front:** Why did Khan Jahan Lodi rebel, and what did his defeat achieve?
**Definition (learn):** An Afghan noble who rebelled in 1629–31 out of mistrust and Deccan connections; his defeat strengthened Shah Jahan's early authority.
**Breakdown:**
- Causes: mistrust, Deccan connections and the monarchy-noble relationship (✅ owner 21).
- Outcome: defeat and suppression, a first demonstration of imperial control (✅ owner 21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Revolted and was defeated 1629–31 (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4002, MED-4007 (Deccan), MED-4019.
**Trap:** Not a Deccan war in itself; the full Deccan sequence belongs to topic 18 (✅ owner 21 boundary).
**Mains hook:** Early Shah Jahan rebellions show the monarchy-noble contract under strain, not an automatic military-political stability.
**Back:** *"Afghan noble, Deccan ties, defeat by 1631."*

### Flashcard MED-4005 — Mumtaz Mahal's death: 1631 (standard) versus 1630 (OCR variant)
**Front:** When did Mumtaz Mahal die, and why does one source print a different year?
**Definition (learn):** Mumtaz Mahal, Shah Jahan's queen, died in Burhanpur. Her death is the dynastic trigger for the Taj Mahal project.
**Breakdown:**
- Standard date: 17 June 1631 at Burhanpur (✅ Wikipedia: Mumtaz Mahal).
- Shah Jahan began planning the mausoleum while still at Burhanpur; her body moved to Agra in December 1631 (✅ Wikipedia: Mumtaz Mahal).
- One assigned Part II OCR narrative prints 1630 (✅ owner 21, source variant).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 17 June 1631 Burhanpur (✅ Wikipedia: Mumtaz Mahal). Taj Mahal's construction period is given as 22 years in the Wikipedia Mumtaz Mahal article.
**Links:** MED-4009, MED-4109 (Taj Mahal), MED-4024.
**Trap:** The 1630 variant is an OCR/print variant, not the standard date. Use 1631 in answers.
**Mains hook:** Use Mumtaz's death to show how dynastic grief fed monumental patronage; do not claim the tomb alone shows fiscal ruin (⚠️ owner 21).
**Back:** *"Standard 1631, OCR says 1630: trust the 1631."*

### Flashcard MED-4006 — Capture of the Portuguese Hugli (1632)
**Front:** What did the Mughal capture of Hugli in 1632 signify?
**Definition (learn):** The Mughal seizure of the Portuguese settlement at Hugli in 1632, a bounded Bengal and coastal policy event.
**Breakdown:**
- A coastal-policy action against a European settlement, not a conquest of Bengal as a whole (✅ owner 21 boundary).
- Shows the Mughal willingness to use land power against European maritime coercion (✅ owner 24, trade-policy link).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1632 (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4122 (European fortification and Mughal trade policy), MED-4002.
**Trap:** Do not present it as the start of British or Portuguese colonial rule. Post-Plassey state formation is outside this owner.
**Mains hook:** European companies entered Indian Ocean commerce as guests of an open, customs-light state, not as its masters in the 1630s (✅ owner 24).
**Back:** *"1632, Hugli taken, Portugal checked, Bengal not conquered."*

### Flashcard MED-4007 — Daulatabad (1633) and the 1636 Deccan settlement
**Front:** What did the fall of Daulatabad and the 1636 settlement do to the Deccan balance?
**Definition (learn):** Daulatabad fell in 1633 and the Nizam Shahi dynasty of Ahmadnagar ended. In 1636 Bijapur and Golconda accepted an unequal relationship with the Mughals, and Aurangzeb became Deccan viceroy.
**Breakdown:**
- 1633: Nizam Shahi dynasty ended (✅ owner 21, Topic 18 core).
- 1636: settlements with Bijapur and Golconda; Aurangzeb became viceroy (✅ owner 21).
- Late operations in 1656–57 reopened the settlement (✅ owner 21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1633 Daulatabad; 1636 settlements; 1656–57 late Deccan operations (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4011, MED-4029, MED-4059.
**Trap:** Shah Jahan did not annex the whole Deccan; treaty detail belongs to topic 18 (⚠️ owner 21).
**Mains hook:** The 1636 settlement was a bounded Mughal supremacy that later Deccan operations reopened, which shows why Mughal Deccan policy was cyclical.
**Back:** *"1633 Ahmadnagar falls, 1636 Bijapur and Golconda bow."*

### Flashcard MED-4008 — The Peacock Throne (completed, first used 1635)
**Front:** When was the Peacock Throne first used, and what was its political function?
**Definition (learn):** The Peacock Throne was a ceremonial throne completed over seven years and first sat upon by Shah Jahan in 1635.
**Breakdown:**
- Political function: materialise hierarchy and imperial wealth (✅ owner 21).
- Later loss: Nadir Shah took it in 1739 (✅ owner 25; see MED-4136).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Completed over seven years; first used 1635 (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4009, MED-4026, MED-4136.
**Trap:** The Peacock Throne was not Akbar's throne; its ceremonial use belongs to Shah Jahan (✅ owner 21).
**Mains hook:** Use it to argue that imperial magnificence was political economy, not mere display.
**Back:** *"1635 the peacock, Shah Jahan's seat of order and wealth."*

### Flashcard MED-4009 — Shahjahanabad begun (1638)
**Front:** When was Shahjahanabad begun, and what did it mean for the Mughal capital?
**Definition (learn):** The new imperial capital at Delhi, begun in 1638; it took about a decade in the assigned narrative.
**Breakdown:**
- Urban ideology: Red Fort, Jama Masjid and the capital (✅ owner 21).
- Political meaning: ordered urban space for imperial hierarchy (⚠️ owner 21 interpretation).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Begun 1638; about a decade (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4008, MED-4109, MED-4026.
**Trap:** Shahjahanabad is not Fatehpur Sikri; Akbar's capital was a different project (⚠️ memory).
**Mains hook:** Shahjahanabad combined form, labour and order; it is not evidence of imperial collapse (⚠️).
**Back:** *"1638 new capital, Delhi reborn as Shahjahanabad."*

### Flashcard MED-4010 — Balkh-Badakhshan (1646–47): field success, political unsustainability
**Front:** What did the Balkh campaign of 1646–47 show about Mughal capacity?
**Definition (learn):** A Mughal campaign into Central Asia that gained field success but could not be sustained politically or logistically.
**Breakdown:**
- Field success coexisted with political and logistical unsustainability (✅ owner 21).
- Lesson: long-distance supply, siege capacity and durable control were limited (⚠️ owner 21).
- Balkh-Badakhshan jagirs also carried a one-fifth muster rule (see MED-4022).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1646–47 (✅ owner 21). Owner gives the Balkh-Badakhshan jama series as about 862 crore dams excluding Balkh-Badakhshan in 1647–48 (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4011, MED-4022, MED-4024.
**Trap:** Balkh is a bounded topic-19 context here; its diplomacy belongs to topic 19 (✅ owner 21 boundary).
**Mains hook:** Use Balkh and Qandahar together as evidence that capacity was real but overextended.
**Back:** *"Balkh won in the field, lost in logistics."*

### Flashcard MED-4011 — Qandahar lost (1649) and the three failed recoveries
**Front:** What happened to Qandahar in 1649–53, and who tried to recover it?
**Definition (learn):** Qandahar was lost in 1649; three recovery attempts failed between 1649 and 1653.
**Breakdown:**
- Aurangzeb failed twice; Dara failed once (✅ owner 21).
- The failures reveal the limits of supply, siege capacity and durable control (⚠️ owner 21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Lost 1649; three attempts failed 1649–53 (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4010, MED-4029 (Aurangzeb), MED-4027 (Dara).
**Trap:** Do not credit Aurangzeb alone with the failure; the attempts were shared with Dara (✅ owner 21).
**Mains hook:** Qandahar exposes the cost of overextension in Central Asia and the link to later succession conflict.
**Back:** *"Lost 1649, three tries failed to 1653."*

### Flashcard MED-4012 — Chittor walls and Mewar tension (1654)
**Front:** What did the pulling down of Chittor's walls in 1654 signal?
**Definition (learn):** The Mughal state pulled down Chittor's walls and sequestered some Mewar parganas in 1654, marking late Rajput tension before the succession crisis.
**Breakdown:**
- Sign of Mughal control of a historic Rajput fortress (✅ owner 21).
- Mewar tension rose later into the Marwar-Mewar crisis of 1678–81 (see MED-4043).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1654 (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4043, MED-4044, MED-4048.
**Trap:** Do not conflate the 1654 Chittor action with the 1679 jizyah or temple policy; those belong to Aurangzeb (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Shows that Rajput service and Rajput resentment coexisted under Shah Jahan.
**Back:** *"1654 Chittor's walls down, Mewar's trust cracks."*

### Flashcard MED-4013 — Ruling-class composition 1595–1656 (Athar Ali series)
**Front:** How did the composition of the 500-zat-and-above elite change between 1595 and 1656?
**Definition (learn):** A historian-made prosopographic series, taken from Athar Ali, counting mansabdars of 500 zat and above by group.
**Breakdown:**
- Princes: 4 → 8. Iranis: 27 → 139. Turanis: 46 → 123. Afghans: 4 → 34. Indian Muslims: 14 → 59. Rajputs: 20 → 87. Marathas: 0 → 12. Other Hindus: 2 → 7 (✅ owner 21, table for 1595, 1621, 1647–48 and 1656).
- Total: 123 → 242 → 443 → 518, elite numbers grew about 4.2 times (✅ owner 21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Total 500 zat and above: 123 (1595) and 518 (1656) (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4014, MED-4015, MED-4024.
**Trap:** The percentages are historian-made categories. They do not prove fixed ethnic or political blocs (✅ owner 21).
**Mains hook:** Use the series to argue for expansion and inclusion, but qualify that recruitment did not erase hierarchy or faction.
**Back:** *"123 became 518; Iranis and Turanis grew, but never became blocs."*
**Owner-internal check:** ⚠️ The group rows do not sum to the printed totals (for example 1595 rows sum to 117, not 123). Use the totals and the trend, not exact shares.

### Flashcard MED-4014 — Iranis and Turanis: share trap and the origin label
**Front:** What do Iranis and Turanis denote, and what share did they hold?
**Definition (learn):** Iranis are the Persian-origin nobles and Turanis the Central Asian (Turanian) nobles in the Mughal service. Both are origin labels for service networks, not political parties.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner gives their combined share falling from about 62.6% (1595) to 52.12% (1656) while absolute numbers grew (✅ owner 21).
- Iranis held many central offices and governorships (✅ owner 21).
- They were largely immigrants or descendants incorporated into service, required to prove competence (⚠️ owner 21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Combined share about 62.6% (1595) → 52.12% (1656) (✅ owner 21, owner-internal arithmetic not reproducible from the printed group rows; see MED-4013).
**Links:** MED-4013, MED-4126 (nobles born in India), MED-4149.
**Trap:** Do not call Iranis "foreign agents". Do not read the share as a fixed party vote.
**Mains hook:** Origin labels were service networks; faction followed patronage, kinship and region (⚠️ owner 21).
**Back:** *"Iranis and Turanis: origin tags for service, not a party."*

### Flashcard MED-4015 — Afghans, Indian Muslims, Rajputs, Marathas in the service elite
**Front:** How did Afghans, Indian Muslims, Rajputs and Marathas fare in the ruling elite under Shah Jahan?
**Definition (learn):** Four service groups whose inclusion widened the imperial elite but kept hierarchy and distinction.
**Breakdown:**
- Afghans: 4 → 34; gained under Jahangir and Shah Jahan but were less often placed in some central posts; older distrust mattered (✅ owner 21).
- Indian Muslims/Shaikhzadas: 14 → 59; Sadullah Khan became Shah Jahan's celebrated wazir (✅ owner 21).
- Rajputs: 20 → 87; enduring military-political partners; served in Deccan, Balkh and Qandahar campaigns (✅ owner 21).
- Marathas: 0 → 12; early but limited inclusion; a stable Maratha alliance was not achieved (✅ owner 21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Rajputs 87 and Marathas 12 at 500+ zat in 1656 (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4013, MED-4028 (Sadullah Khan), MED-4048 (Rajput alliance).
**Trap:** Continued inclusion does not mean equality or an end to later Rajput crisis (✅ owner 21 and 22).
**Mains hook:** Use the groups to show composite recruitment in a hierarchical service state.
**Back:** *"Afghans gained, Muslims advanced, Rajputs stayed, Marathas barely began."*

### Flashcard MED-4016 — Zat: personal rank and pay grade
**Front:** What is zat in a mansab, and what is the common mistake?
**Definition (learn):** Zat is the personal rank, status and salary grade of a mansabdar.
**Breakdown:**
- Zat fixes status and the pay grade (✅ owner 21).
- Zat is not troop count alone (✅ owner 21).
- Under Akbar's dual rank, zat and sawar were stated as two numbers from about 1595 (✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dual rank from about 1595 (✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar; ✅ owner 21, Akbar's 40th regnal year, 1595–96).
**Links:** MED-4001, MED-4017, MED-4021.
**Trap:** Du-aspa sih-aspa raised sawar obligation, not zat (see MED-4021). Zat was normally equal to or higher than sawar (✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar).
**Mains hook:** Zat measured standing; sawar measured the army cost. Mixing them is the main error in mansab answers.
**Back:** *"Zat is status and pay, not the soldiers."*

### Flashcard MED-4017 — Sawar: cavalry obligation and its pay claim
**Front:** What is sawar and how does it differ from zat?
**Definition (learn):** The cavalry rank, which fixed the number of horsemen a mansabdar was obliged to maintain and the pay claim for them.
**Breakdown:**
- Sawar is a cavalry obligation and pay claim, not identical to zat (✅ owner 21).
- Sawar payment figures were contract rates for hiring and maintaining cavalry, not a uniform wage paid to each trooper (✅ owner 21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Contract rate examples in MED-4023.
**Links:** MED-4016, MED-4022, MED-4023.
**Trap:** Sawar payment was not handed directly to each trooper; actual trooper pay varied with horse and quality (✅ owner 21).
**Mains hook:** Sawar is where the state's military-fiscal bargain was negotiated, which is why its adjustments were the first to be trimmed.
**Back:** *"Sawar: the horse count you owe and the contract rate you are paid."*

### Flashcard MED-4018 — Jagir: transferable revenue assignment, not land ownership
**Front:** What is a jagir, and what is it not?
**Definition (learn):** A jagir is a transferable assignment of revenue claim, given to a mansabdar in lieu of salary.
**Breakdown:**
- Transferable assignment of revenue claim (✅ owner 21).
- Not private ownership of soil (✅ owner 21; see also MED-4099 and MED-4100).
- Jagirs were graded by realised income through the month-scale (MED-4023).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Deccan jagirs often worth only 3–4 months (see MED-4025).
**Links:** MED-4001, MED-4019, MED-4100, MED-4130.
**Trap:** Jagir (assigned revenue) vs zamindar (locally rooted hereditary claimant) is a standard pair; see MED-4100.
**Mains hook:** The jagir system tied imperial service to revenue realisation; when realisation fell, service failed.
**Back:** *"Jagir: a revenue assignment you may lose, not a field you own."*

### Flashcard MED-4019 — Jama versus hasil
**Front:** What is the difference between jama and hasil?
**Definition (learn):** Jama is the assessed revenue on paper; hasil is the revenue actually realised.
**Breakdown:**
- Jama is the assessment (✅ owner 21).
- Hasil is the realised collection (✅ owner 21).
- Jama–hasil gap: paper assessment exceeded collection, the core of the jagirdari crisis (✅ owner 23 and 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Jama 516.251 crore dams (1595–96) and about 912 crore dams (1656–57) (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4024, MED-4086, MED-4130.
**Trap:** Do not treat a jama figure as realised income. Owner 21 uses jama index as a claim measure, not an income figure.
**Mains hook:** Any fiscal answer that quotes jama without hasil overstates the state's income.
**Back:** *"Jama is the bill, hasil is the cash."*

### Flashcard MED-4020 — Dagh and chehra: branding and the descriptive roll
**Front:** What were dagh and chehra, and did they stop evasion?
**Definition (learn):** Dagh is the branding of horses; chehra is the descriptive roll of troopers. Both were muster-control devices.
**Breakdown:**
- Dagh: branding of horses (✅ owner 21).
- Chehra: descriptive roll recording a horseman's age, stature, features, origin and names; horses registered separately (✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar).
- Two brands used: the imperial mark on one side, the mansabdar's own on the other (✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Abu'l-Fazl says the roll came first and branding was added only when evasion continued (✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar).
**Links:** MED-4017, MED-4021, MED-4022.
**Trap:** A roll records men; it is not proof of constant readiness. Dagh and chehra did not eliminate fraud; evasion persisted (✅ owner 21).
**Mains hook:** Evasion at muster shows the limits of paper control in a service state.
**Back:** *"Brand the horse, roll the man, and still expect fraud."*

### Flashcard MED-4021 — Du-aspa sih-aspa: doubled duty without raised zat
**Front:** What is du-aspa sih-aspa, and when did it first appear?
**Definition (learn):** A device that doubled the sawar obligation and pay for a specified part of the sawar rank, without raising the zat.
**Breakdown:**
- "Two-horse, three-horse": a component inside the sawar rank that never exceeded it (✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar).
- Pay and obligation for such a trooper were exactly double those of an ordinary one (✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar).
- First recorded instance: Jahangir's tenth regnal year, when 1,700 of Mahabat Khan's sawars received it (✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar; ✅ owner 21 says Jahangir).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Jahangir's 10th regnal year (✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar).
**Links:** MED-4016, MED-4017, MED-4022.
**Trap:** Du-aspa does not raise zat. Owner states the trap as "did not raise zat" (✅ owner 21).
**Mains hook:** Du-aspa was a military-obligation lever, but Gommans reads it as "a matter of accountancy" (✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar).
**Back:** *"Double the horses, not the rank."*

### Flashcard MED-4022 — Muster fractions: one-third, one-fourth, one-fifth
**Front:** What were the Shah Jahan muster rules for effective sawar?
**Definition (learn):** Effective contingents below nominal rank, regularised under Shah Jahan.
**Breakdown:**
- One-third: a mansabdar posted in the province where his jagir lay might muster about one-third of nominal sawar rank (✅ owner 21; ✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar).
- One-fourth: where the jagir lay elsewhere in Hindustan (✅ owner 21; ✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar).
- One-fifth: Balkh-Badakhshan and perhaps Deccan service (owner 21), and on distant expeditions and when paid in cash (✅ Wikipedia: Mansabdar).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Worked example: a 2000/2000 mansab with 1000 du-aspa sih-aspa and an outside-province jagir yields 750 (250 ordinary plus 500 doubled) (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4021, MED-4023, MED-4010.
**Trap:** The one-fifth rule is attributed differently. Owner says Balkh-Badakhshan and "perhaps Deccan"; Wikipedia says distant expeditions and cash-paid holders. Flag as ⚠️ conflict; do not state one as settled.
**Mains hook:** Muster ratios show that rank was formally kept while effective obligation was lowered, a managed adjustment and not proof that rank meant nothing (⚠️ owner 21).
**Back:** *"Third at home, fourth away, fifth on the frontier."*

### Flashcard MED-4023 — The month-scale and the sawar rate ladder
**Front:** What is the month-scale, and what sawar rates did it produce?
**Definition (learn):** A system grading jagirs by realised income relative to assessed jama, so that salary was scaled to the jagir's actual yield.
**Breakdown:**
- Ladder: 12-month → 10-month → 8-month → 6-month → 4-month (✅ owner 21).
- Only princes and a few top grandees got ten-month jagirs; many got eight-month; North India generally not below four (✅ owner 21).
- Illustrative sawar contract rates: 12-month Rs 40; 8-month Rs 30; 6-month Rs 25 per sawar per month (✅ owner 21).
- The reduction was not exactly proportional because remount obligations also declined (✅ owner 21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Rs 40 / Rs 30 / Rs 25 per sawar per month (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4017, MED-4024, MED-4025, MED-4086.
**Trap:** The month-scale was a salary-reduction and accounting adjustment, not a bonus system (✅ owner 21 and owner 21 advanced trap).
**Mains hook:** The month-scale aligned paper rank with expected revenue, and so shows the fiscal squeeze in one mechanism.
**Back:** *"Twelve months, eight, six: pay follows the yield."*

### Flashcard MED-4024 — Jama index against mansab growth (1595–1657)
**Front:** How did revenue growth compare with mansab growth under Shah Jahan?
**Definition (learn):** A fiscal comparison: the number of 500-zat-and-above mansabdars rose about 4.2 times while the jama index rose only to about 176.
**Breakdown:**
- Series (crore dams): 1595–96 about 516.251 (index 100); 1627 about 630 (index 122); 1647–48 about 880 (about 862 excluding Balkh-Badakhshan); 1656–57 about 912 (index 176) (✅ owner 21).
- Owner's causal chain: more rank and salary claims + uneven hasil + limited productive jagirs + costly service theatres → salary cuts + reduced muster ratios + month-scale (⚠️ owner 21).
- The system still mobilised large armies and financed administration (⚠️ owner 21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-internal check: 880 crore over 516.251 is about 170, not the printed 162 index for 1647–48. Flag as ⚠️ owner arithmetic.
**Links:** MED-4013, MED-4019, MED-4023, MED-4086.
**Trap:** The "squeeze" is about the direction of growth, not an absolute collapse. The acute jagirdari crisis belongs to topic 23 (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Use the ratio of rank growth to revenue growth to explain strain without asserting inevitable collapse.
**Back:** *"Ranks 4.2 times, revenue index 1.76: the gap is the crisis."*

### Flashcard MED-4025 — Deccan jagirs worth only three to four months
**Front:** Why were Deccan jagirs often only three or four months in value?
**Definition (learn):** Because the Deccan jama was especially inflated, realised jagir income was often a fraction of paper value.
**Breakdown:**
- Deccan jagirs could fall to three or four months (✅ owner 21).
- A structural grievance feeding disaffection, not a privilege (✅ owner 21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 3–4 months (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4023, MED-4086, MED-4067 (Sambhaji), MED-4130.
**Trap:** Deccan mansabdars were not the best paid; their jagirs were often worth much less (✅ owner 21 trap).
**Mains hook:** Inflated Deccan jama is one reason southern postings became fiscally toxic.
**Back:** *"Deccan jama inflated, Deccan jagir shrunk to three months."*

### Flashcard MED-4026 — Building expenditure: Rs 289 lakh over twenty-eight years (Moosvi)
**Front:** How large was Shah Jahan's building expenditure, and what does it show?
**Definition (learn):** A figure quoted by Satish Chandra from Shireen Moosvi's calculation: Shah Jahan's buildings and gardens cost a little over Rs 289 lakh over twenty-eight years.
**Breakdown:**
- Construction was a significant khalisa expenditure but not so excessive as to cripple imperial or military finance (✅ owner 21).
- Expenditure organised labour, materials, patronage and ceremonial power (⚠️ owner 21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Rs 289 lakh over 28 years (✅ owner 21, citing Satish Chandra quoting Shireen Moosvi).
**Links:** MED-4008, MED-4009, MED-4109.
**Trap:** Monuments were not costless, but expenditure alone cannot be declared the cause of fiscal crisis (✅ owner 21).
**Mains hook:** Use the Moosvi figure to qualify any "architecture ruined the treasury" claim.
**Back:** *"Rs 289 lakh over 28 years: costly, not ruinous."*

### Flashcard MED-4027 — The 1657–58 war of succession: endpoint of Shah Jahan
**Front:** How did the 1657–58 succession war end Shah Jahan's reign?
**Definition (learn):** After Shah Jahan's illness in September 1657, Dara, Shuja, Aurangzeb and Murad contested succession; Aurangzeb prevailed and confined his father in Agra.
**Breakdown:**
- No accepted primogeniture; nomination, military resources, provincial bases and noble/Rajput connections decided the outcome (✅ owner 21).
- Dara received extraordinary favour and the designation of heir, which increased his brothers' fears (✅ owner 21).
- Shuja and Murad crowned themselves; Aurangzeb allied with Murad; Dharmat and Samugarh decided the main military sequence (✅ owner 21).
- Religious language mattered, but nobles aligned through interest and survival, not a liberal-versus-orthodox referendum (⚠️ owner 21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Illness September 1657 (✅ owner 21); Samugarh 1658 (✅ owner 22 advanced).
**Links:** MED-4002, MED-4030, MED-4011.
**Trap:** Do not make the conflict a simple Dara versus Aurangzeb ideology contest (⚠️ owner 21).
**Mains hook:** The war shows how succession without primogeniture made resources and noble networks decisive.
**Back:** *"1657 the father falls ill, 1658 the brother wins, the father is confined."*

### Flashcard MED-4028 — Sadullah Khan and the wazir
**Front:** Who was Sadullah Khan, and why does he matter for Shah Jahan's ruling class?
**Definition (learn):** Sadullah Khan was a Shaikhzada, an Indian Muslim service figure who became Shah Jahan's celebrated wazir.
**Breakdown:**
- Indian Muslims/Shaikhzadas advanced under Shah Jahan (✅ owner 21).
- He represents the administrative route into the elite, not a Turani or Irani blocs leader (⚠️ owner 21).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Named as Shah Jahan's celebrated wazir (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** MED-4015, MED-4013.
**Trap:** Do not treat a wazir as a sole policy author; wazirs competed for patronage (⚠️ owner 25 logic).
**Mains hook:** The wazir's rise shows how composite recruitment widened the administrative base.
**Back:** *"Sadullah: Shaikhzada, wazir, the composite elite in one name."*

### GROUP — Aurangzeb: Religious Policy, North India and the Rajputs (owner 22)

### Flashcard MED-4029 — Aurangzeb's religious policy as differentiated incorporation (concept)
**Front:** How should Aurangzeb's religious and Rajput policy be characterised?
**Definition (learn):** Differentiated incorporation: the state kept recruiting Hindus, Rajputs and others into service while imposing discriminatory measures (jizyah, temple orders, appointment and customs orders) that sharpened religious hierarchy.
**Breakdown:**
- Orthodoxy altered the public legal-symbolic language and produced coercive measures (✅ owner 22).
- The emperor remained a Timurid sovereign using political discretion, composite recruitment and ruler-made regulations (✅ owner 22).
- Neither a communal monocause nor a religion-is-irrelevant apologetic fits the evidence (⚠️ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Reign phases: early pragmatic continuity; selective puritan/court symbolic regulation; sharper 1669–79 legal-symbolic and coercive differentiation; later wartime rule with uneven enforcement (✅ owner 22, analytical phases).
**Links:** MED-4031, MED-4040, MED-4048, MED-4052.
**Trap:** Continued Hindu service does not erase discrimination; discrimination does not prove that recruitment stopped (⚠️ owner 22).
**Mains hook:** Argue that orthodoxy shaped choices and symbols, while political context selected targets and timing, and uneven administration shaped reach.
**Back:** *"Orthodox symbols, political targets, uneven reach."*

### Flashcard MED-4030 — Dara Shukoh's execution and Aurangzeb's accession (1658–59)
**Front:** What did Aurangzeb do after winning the 1657–58 war?
**Definition (learn):** After defeating rivals he executed Dara after a juristic process and consolidated his accession, 1658–59.
**Breakdown:**
- Aurangzeb defeated rivals and executed Dara after a juristic process (✅ owner 22).
- Topic 22 begins with the result of the 1657–58 war, which topic 21 owns (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1658–59 (✅ owner 22). Samugarh 1658: Aurangzeb defeated Dara at Samugarh (✅ owner 22 advanced).
**Links:** MED-4027, MED-4029, MED-4011.
**Trap:** The execution used a juristic process; do not describe it as a simple battlefield death (✅ owner 22 phrasing).
**Mains hook:** The execution shows legal idiom used for succession, not only military finality.
**Back:** *"Samugarh 1658, Dara judged and executed."*

### Flashcard MED-4031 — Sharia, zawabit and the state's legal idiom
**Front:** What were zawabit, and how did they relate to sharia?
**Definition (learn):** Zawabit were ruler-made administrative regulations, distinct from sharia opinions.
**Breakdown:**
- Aurangzeb was a pious Hanafi Muslim who sought greater conformity with Islamic law and patronised jurists, qazis and sadrs (✅ owner 22).
- Zawabit: the emperor selected among opinions, appointed officials, allocated revenue and made political exceptions (✅ owner 22).
- The state was neither an ulama-run theocracy nor a modern secular state. Chain: legal opinion → imperial selection/order → provincial administration → implementation, negotiation or non-enforcement (⚠️ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No single date; reign-wide practice (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4032, MED-4033, MED-4029.
**Trap:** Zawabit are not sharia. Sharia was invoked; zawabit and political necessity still decided outcomes (✅ owner 22 advanced).
**Mains hook:** Sovereignty mixed sharia language with imperial regulation; that is the core of the "orthodox but statesman" verdict.
**Back:** *"Sharia gave the words, zawabit gave the orders."*

### Flashcard MED-4032 — Fatawa-i-Alamgiri: a legal compendium, not a constitution
**Front:** What was the Fatawa-i-Alamgiri, and what was it not?
**Definition (learn):** An imperially sponsored collective compendium of Hanafi jurisprudence.
**Breakdown:**
- It proves the political prestige of legal learning (✅ owner 22).
- It was not a modern written constitution (✅ owner 22).
- It was not a single criminal code automatically applied everywhere, nor proof that every official consulted it (✅ owner 22).
- It did not replace imperial regulations and political judgement (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Imperially sponsored compendium (✅ owner 22). No date given in the owner file; do not add one from memory.
**Links:** MED-4031, MED-4033.
**Trap:** Fatawa = legal compendium, not constitution (✅ owner 22 traps).
**Mains hook:** Use the compendium to show legal prestige without automatic enforcement.
**Back:** *"A library of law, not a constitution."*

### Flashcard MED-4033 — Muhtasib, court symbolism and the public idiom
**Front:** What puritan and court-symbolic measures are attributed to Aurangzeb, and how should each be tested?
**Definition (learn):** Reported measures include action against intoxicants, gambling, prostitution and practices considered un-Islamic; appointment of muhtasibs; changes to court ceremonies such as jharoka darshan; regulation of Nauroz, music and coin formulae; mosque repair; and patronage of Islamic officials.
**Breakdown:**
- Test each claim: exact date; court practice or social order; order vs chronicle vs implementation; which province; any exception or reversal (✅ owner 22).
- Court symbolism could change the public idiom of sovereignty even without uniform village enforcement (⚠️ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Muhtasib appointments and court ceremony changes are reported measures, not fixed dates (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4031, MED-4113 (music trap), MED-4035.
**Trap:** Popular lists telescope decades; do not present a reign-wide label as settled (✅ owner 22).
**Mains hook:** Distinguish public idiom, enforcement and local practice before judging the emperor.
**Back:** *"Test the claim: date, place, order, enforcement."*

### Flashcard MED-4034 — Jai Singh's death (1667)
**Front:** Why does Jai Singh's death in 1667 matter for Aurangzeb's Rajput policy?
**Definition (learn):** Jai Singh, the Amber ruler and Mughal mediator, died in 1667; an important Rajput mediator disappeared from the Mughal scene.
**Breakdown:**
- He shaped the 1665 Shivaji settlement (✅ owner 22 advanced).
- His death removed a key Rajput adviser and Deccan strategist (✅ owner 22 advanced mini-timeline).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1667 (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4062 (Purandar), MED-4048.
**Trap:** Jai Singh's death is not the cause of the 1679 break; it weakened mediation (⚠️ owner 22).
**Mains hook:** Loss of Rajput mediators raised the cost of later breaches.
**Back:** *"1667, the Amber mediator goes; trust thins."*

### Flashcard MED-4035 — Temple policy: evidence categories and the 1669 cases
**Front:** How should Mughal temple policy be tested, and which 1669 cases are cited?
**Definition (learn):** Formal law protected old temples of zimmis while opposing new construction or political challenge; actual practice did not follow one rule.
**Breakdown:**
- Evidence categories: (1) general or provincial orders against new or unauthorised temples; (2) specific destruction or conversion at Kashi, Mathura and elsewhere; (3) wartime punishment in Marwar-Mewar; (4) defacement or bricking up rather than identical destruction; (5) grants, protection or non-interference; (6) restoration and complaint decisions (✅ owner 22).
- In 1669 an order targeted schools and temples associated with objectionable teaching; Kashi Vishvanath and the Keshava Rai temple at Mathura became major cases (✅ owner 22).
- During the 1679–80 Rajput war, old temples were destroyed in Jodhpur and Udaipur areas (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1669 order; 1679–80 war destruction (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4029, MED-4036, MED-4042, MED-4052.
**Trap:** Avoid "all temples destroyed throughout India" and "only new or rebellious temples affected" (⚠️ owner 22).
**Mains hook:** Method: dated site → source → stated context → actual action → regional reach → counter-evidence (⚠️ owner 22).
**Back:** *"Date, site, source, act, reach, counter-proof."*

### Flashcard MED-4036 — The Jat rising near Mathura and Agra (c. 1669)
**Front:** What was the Jat rising of c. 1669, and why is it not one event?
**Definition (learn):** A rising of Jat cultivators and zamindars in the Mathura–Agra region, associated with Gokula, c. 1669.
**Breakdown:**
- Emerged from local revenue, zamindari, official coercion and religious-political conditions (✅ owner 22).
- Later resistance under Rajaram and Churaman shows recurrence, not one event (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 1669 (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4035, MED-4144 (Jat state formation under Churaman), MED-4050.
**Trap:** Do not flatten the Jat rising into a single communal revolt (✅ owner 22).
**Mains hook:** Use the Jat case to show local state-society conflict turning armed.
**Back:** *"1669 Gokula's Jats, not one event but a recurring line."*

### Flashcard MED-4037 — The Satnami rising near Narnaul (1672)
**Front:** What was the 1672 Satnami rising, and how was it suppressed?
**Definition (learn):** A local clash near Narnaul that escalated into mobilisation by the Satnami community, which included cultivators and artisans.
**Breakdown:**
- Local clash, then mobilisation; imperial force suppressed it (✅ owner 22).
- Social base: cultivators and artisans, not a single class (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1672, Narnaul (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4036, MED-4038, MED-4050.
**Trap:** Satnamis are not Sikhs; the owner treats them as a separate community movement (⚠️ owner 22).
**Mains hook:** Use Satnamis to show the variety of north Indian rebellions.
**Back:** *"1672 Narnaul clash, Satnami army, imperial suppression."*

### Flashcard MED-4038 — Guru Tegh Bahadur executed at Delhi (1675)
**Front:** What do the sources say about Guru Tegh Bahadur's execution?
**Definition (learn):** The Sikh Guru was arrested and executed at Delhi in November 1675, after travel in eastern India and return to Punjab in 1671.
**Breakdown:**
- Became Guru in 1664 (✅ owner 22).
- Travelled in eastern India; returned to Punjab 1671 (✅ owner 22).
- Source controls: no contemporary Persian narrative is transparent; later Persian sources present law-and-order claims; Sikh tradition centres on defence of religious freedom and martyrdom (✅ owner 22).
- Chandra regards the punishment as unjustified (⚠️ owner 22 reports Chandra's view).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Executed November 1675 (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4047 (Khalsa 1699), MED-4029.
**Trap:** The 1675 execution did not mechanically cause every later Sikh institution or battle (⚠️ owner 22).
**Mains hook:** A defensible answer holds imperial security framing, religious coercion and Sikh martyr memory together.
**Back:** *"Guru Tegh Bahadur, Delhi, November 1675: martyr and suspect text."*

### Flashcard MED-4039 — Jaswant Singh's death at Jamrud (1678)
**Front:** How did Jaswant Singh's death in 1678 open the Marwar crisis?
**Definition (learn):** Jaswant Singh, Rathor ruler of Marwar, died at Jamrud in 1678 without an acknowledged surviving adult male heir.
**Breakdown:**
- Widows later gave birth to sons; Ajit Singh became the surviving claimant (✅ owner 22).
- Marwar was taken into khalisa amid disputed succession, debt, withheld revenue and disorder (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1678, Jamrud (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4042, MED-4043, MED-4046.
**Trap:** Khalisa control had precedents in disputed successions; it did not by itself prove permanent annexation (✅ owner 22).
**Mains hook:** The Marwar crisis began as a succession problem and became a legitimacy crisis.
**Back:** *"1678 Jamrud, no adult heir, Marwar goes khalisa."*

### Flashcard MED-4040 — Jizyah reimposed (1679)
**Front:** What was jizyah, and why did Aurangzeb reimpose it in 1679?
**Definition (learn):** Jizya is a poll tax marking differentiated non-Muslim subjecthood in classical Islamic law, not land revenue or a tax on every Hindu.
**Breakdown:**
- Reimposed 1679 "in lieu of military service" per the fetched Jizya article (✅ Wikipedia: Jizya).
- Reasons owned: personal and legal orthodoxy; public assertion of Islamic rulership; alliance with theologians; fiscal form; political differentiation under pressure (✅ owner 22).
- Effects: visible reversal of Akbarian policy; alienation among sections of Hindus; new collector-subject contact and corruption; symbolic support to critics; no proof that jizyah alone caused decline (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1679 (✅ Wikipedia: Jizya; ✅ owner 22). Owner: Akbar's policy was the visible reversal point; Wikipedia says Akbar was an exception to jizya collection (✅ Wikipedia: Jizya).
**Links:** MED-4041, MED-4053, MED-4045.
**Trap:** Do not quote a jizyah rate unless the unit, category and source are secure (✅ owner 22).
**Mains hook:** Was jizyah a fiscal measure, a religious symbol or a political signal? Owner gives all three; answer with evidence and qualification.
**Back:** *"1679 the poll tax returns: symbol more than fix."*

### Flashcard MED-4041 — Jizyah suspended in the south (1704) and the abolition conflict (1712)
**Front:** When was jizyah suspended in the Deccan, and when was it abolished?
**Definition (learn):** Aurangzeb's 1704 suspension in the Deccan during war, and the later abolition.
**Breakdown:**
- 1704: jizyah suspended in the south for the duration of war (✅ owner 22); the Wikipedia Jizya article cites the 1704 suspension in the Deccan, with revolts behind periodic suspensions (✅ Wikipedia: Jizya).
- 1712: owner 22 says jizyah was formally abolished under Jahandar Shah at Asad Khan's instance (✅ owner 22).
- Owner 25 advanced says the Sayyid brothers abolished jizyah (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1704 suspension (✅ owner 22; ✅ Wikipedia: Jizya). 1712 abolition (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4040, MED-4131, MED-4134.
**Trap:** ⚠️ Owner conflict. Owner 22 gives 1712 under Jahandar Shah; owner 25 gives the Sayyids. The fetched Wikipedia Sayyid brothers article does not mention jizyah, and the fetched Jizya article gave no 1712 date. Do not state the abolitionist as settled; check Chandra's Part II before answering.
**Mains hook:** Use the abolition only after confirming who ordered it; otherwise say jizyah was later scrapped in the early eighteenth century (⚠️).
**Back:** *"1704 suspended in the south; 1712 scrapped; who scrapped it is the trap."*

### Flashcard MED-4042 — Marwar taken into khalisa and the succession dispute
**Front:** Why was Marwar taken into khalisa, and what escalated the crisis?
**Definition (learn):** Khalisa is the crown-controlled land/revenue category. Marwar was taken into khalisa after Jaswant Singh's death amid disputed succession.
**Breakdown:**
- Khalisa administration during a minority or disputed succession had precedents (✅ owner 22).
- Escalation: officials behaved as conquerors and searched property; Aurangzeb recognised Inder Singh for the tika; rival claims of the ranis and Rathor sardars were mishandled; temple demolition teams and the 1679 jizyah intensified fear (✅ owner 22).
- Durgadas and Rathor leaders carried Ajit Singh away; the Rathors restored Ajit's tika at Jodhpur (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1678–79 crisis (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4039, MED-4043, MED-4044, MED-4048.
**Trap:** Religion sharpened the legitimacy crisis but was not the sole trigger; the immediate problem was succession, watan, debt, honour and imperial overreach (⚠️ owner 22).
**Mains hook:** The Marwar crisis exposes the limits of Mughal centralisation in dealing with watan, succession and honour.
**Back:** *"Khalisa, Inder Singh, tika restored: a succession case that turned into a war."*

### Flashcard MED-4043 — Inder Singh's tika and Durgadas Rathor's resistance
**Front:** Who were Inder Singh and Durgadas Rathor in the Marwar crisis?
**Definition (learn):** Inder Singh was recognised by Aurangzeb for the Jodhpur tika; Durgadas Rathor led the Rathor resistance that carried Ajit Singh away.
**Breakdown:**
- Aurangzeb's choice of Inder Singh alienated the Rathor sardars (✅ owner 22).
- Durgadas and Rathor leaders carried Ajit Singh away and rejected imperial arrangements (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Crisis 1678–79 (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4042, MED-4044, MED-4046.
**Trap:** Durgadas Rathor is the Rathor resistance leader; do not confuse with Rana Raj Singh of Mewar (⚠️ memory-safe distinction).
**Mains hook:** The Rathor split shows that succession rights and honour mattered more than a formal religion-only dispute.
**Back:** *"Inder Singh got the tika; Durgadas took Ajit away."*

### Flashcard MED-4044 — Rana Raj Singh of Mewar and the Marwar alliance
**Front:** Why did Rana Raj Singh of Mewar support the Rathor resistance?
**Definition (learn):** Mewar's ruler supported Rathor resistance because Mughal occupation of Marwar and interference in Rajput succession threatened Mewar's strategic and political position.
**Breakdown:**
- He was not simply acting through a close family relation to Ajit (✅ owner 22).
- Terrain and Rathor-Mewar cooperation slowed Mughal operations (✅ owner 22).
- Mewar reached a settlement after Rana Raj Singh's death (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Mewar war from 1679 (✅ owner 22 advanced).
**Links:** MED-4042, MED-4045, MED-4048.
**Trap:** The breach with Marwar-Mewar was not a breach with all Rajputs; Amber, Bikaner, Bundi and Kota remained in service (✅ owner 22).
**Mains hook:** Rajput resistance is explained by interference in succession and strategic threat, not only by religion (⚠️ owner 22).
**Back:** *"Mewar backs Marwar: interference in succession is the trigger."*

### Flashcard MED-4045 — Prince Akbar's revolt (January 1681)
**Front:** What was the 1681 rebellion of Prince Akbar, and how did it end?
**Definition (learn):** Prince Akbar, sent to command in the Rajput war, rebelled in January 1681 with Rathor support; Aurangzeb split the coalition and Akbar fled toward the Deccan.
**Breakdown:**
- Aurangzeb used letters, intelligence and political manoeuvre to split the coalition (✅ owner 22).
- Owner 25 gives the rebellion in alliance with Durgadas and the Rathors (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** January 1681 (✅ owner 22; ✅ owner 25).
**Links:** MED-4044, MED-4054 (Shivaji and Sambhaji), MED-4066.
**Trap:** The failed revolt is a 1681 event; do not confuse it with the 1698 recognition of Ajit Singh.
**Mains hook:** The revolt shows that Mughal princes could become competing centres of Rajput and Deccan politics.
**Back:** *"January 1681, Akbar revolts, Aurangzeb divides, Akbar flees."*

### Flashcard MED-4046 — Ajit Singh recognised (1698) but Jodhpur not fully restored
**Front:** What did Ajit Singh receive in 1698, and what remained unresolved?
**Definition (learn):** Ajit Singh of Marwar received recognition in 1698, but Jodhpur was not fully restored.
**Breakdown:**
- Recognition of Ajit Singh (✅ owner 22; ✅ owner 25 advanced: "recognised as ruler of Marwar, but capital not fully restored").
- Rathor war continued intermittently (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1698 (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4039, MED-4043, MED-4045.
**Trap:** Recognition is not restoration; a recognised ruler without the capital is a partial settlement (✅ owner 22).
**Mains hook:** Partial settlement shows the cost of the 1678–81 crisis.
**Back:** *"1698 Ajit recognised, Jodhpur still unfinished."*

### Flashcard MED-4047 — The Khalsa founded (1699)
**Front:** When was the Khalsa founded, and what is its place in this owner?
**Definition (learn):** Guru Gobind Singh founded the Khalsa in 1699; owner 22 treats it as a bounded consequence context.
**Breakdown:**
- Founded by Guru Gobind Singh (✅ owner 22).
- Detailed Sikh institutional history belongs to a dedicated owner; topic 22 owns the Aurangzeb-period state-policy link (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1699 (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4038, MED-4145 (misls and Dal Khalsa).
**Trap:** The 1675 execution did not mechanically produce the Khalsa (⚠️ owner 22).
**Mains hook:** Khalsa formation is a consequence of Sikh community formation and frontier conditions, not one Mughal act.
**Back:** *"1699 Khalsa founded, the 1675 martyrdom remembered."*

### Flashcard MED-4048 — The Rajput alliance as an exchange
**Front:** What was the Rajput alliance, and what did it require?
**Definition (learn):** An exchange, not equality: Rajput rulers gave service in return for mansab, jagir, honour and succession recognition.
**Breakdown:**
- Chain: watan authority + imperial mansab/jagir + honour/status + military service + succession recognition (⚠️ owner 22).
- Aurangzeb initially worked with Jai Singh, Jaswant Singh and other Rajput rulers; Jai Singh shaped the 1665 Shivaji settlement; Jaswant retained major service (✅ owner 22).
- It required credible Mughal guarantees (⚠️ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Alliance baseline before 1678 (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4015, MED-4034, MED-4049, MED-4042.
**Trap:** Growing reservations in promotions and late-1670s Deccan use began before the breach (✅ owner 22).
**Mains hook:** The alliance broke when guarantees failed in succession, not when religion alone was invoked.
**Back:** *"Watan, mansab, honour, service, succession: a contract."*

### Flashcard MED-4049 — Rajput houses that stayed in Mughal service after 1679
**Front:** Which Rajput houses remained in Mughal service after 1679?
**Definition (learn):** Many Rajput houses stayed in Mughal service after the 1679 breach.
**Breakdown:**
- Amber, Bikaner, Bundi and Kota remained in service (✅ owner 22).
- Continued inclusion does not prove equality or erase jizyah or temple coercion (⚠️ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Named houses (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4048, MED-4044, MED-4015.
**Trap:** The correct category is differentiated incorporation, not full equality (⚠️ owner 22).
**Mains hook:** The breach was with Marwar and Mewar, not with the whole Rajput world.
**Back:** *"Amber, Bikaner, Bundi, Kota stayed: the breach was local."*

### Flashcard MED-4050 — Bundela chiefs: Champat Rai and Chhatrasal
**Front:** What do Champat Rai and Chhatrasal show about Bundelkhand rebellion?
**Definition (learn):** Bundela chiefs whose rebellions belonged to a Bundelkhand setting of local chiefship, Mughal extraction, service and revolt.
**Breakdown:**
- Champat Rai and later Chhatrasal (✅ owner 22).
- Chronology and causes cannot be collapsed into jizyah alone (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No dates in owner file; do not add. Flag as ⚠️ if dates are needed.
**Links:** MED-4003 (Jujhar Singh), MED-4036.
**Trap:** Do not treat them as a single Bundela movement with a single cause (✅ owner 22).
