**Mains hook:** Local chiefship plus extraction explains recurring Bundela revolt better than religion alone.
**Back:** *"Bundelkhand chiefs: local power, extraction, revolt."*

### Flashcard MED-4051 — Sources: Waqa-i-Ajmer and the Jodhpur Hukumat-ri-Bahi
**Front:** Which regional sources revised the Marwar-Mewar story, and what do they show?
**Definition (learn):** Waqa-i-Ajmer and the Jodhpur Hukumat-ri-Bahi are regional and local records of the Marwar-Mewar crisis.
**Breakdown:**
- Waqa-i-Ajmer: Marwar-Mewar administration and events; official or local perspective (✅ owner 22).
- Jodhpur Hukumat-ri-Bahi: Rathor court and government evidence; dynastic or local perspective (✅ owner 22).
- They foreground succession, debt, khalisa precedents and political miscalculation (✅ owner 22 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner gives no dates for the two sources; do not add (⚠️).
**Links:** MED-4042, MED-4052.
**Trap:** A regional record is not the whole empire; one locality is not empire-wide proof (✅ owner 22).
**Mains hook:** Using Rajasthani sources shifts the explanation from pure religion to succession and debt.
**Back:** *"Waqa-i-Ajmer and Hukumat-ri-Bahi: the Marwar story from inside."*

### Flashcard MED-4052 — Historiography of Aurangzeb: monocause, statecraft and balanced views
**Front:** What are the three historiographical positions on Aurangzeb's religious policy?
**Definition (learn):** Three frames used to judge the policy.
**Breakdown:**
- Communal monocause: notices discrimination but explains too much through one identity (✅ owner 22).
- Statecraft-only revision: notices succession, fiscal and strategic motives but can understate religious meaning and coercion (✅ owner 22).
- Balanced view: orthodoxy shaped choices and symbols; political contexts selected targets and timing; uneven administration shaped reach (✅ owner 22).
- Satish Chandra calls Marwar-Mewar policy clumsy and blundering; Aurangzeb was constrained and responsible (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Chandra quote "clumsy and blundering" (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4029, MED-4042, MED-4149.
**Trap:** Do not present the balanced view as neutral without evidence; each position has its blind spot.
**Mains hook:** The question "victim of circumstances or maker of circumstances" is answered by the balanced view (⚠️ owner 22 Mains angle).
**Back:** *"Communal explains too much, statecraft too little, balanced tests both."*

### Flashcard MED-4053 — Jizyah is a poll tax, not land revenue (trap pair)
**Front:** What is jizyah, and what is it commonly confused with?
**Definition (learn):** A poll tax marking differentiated non-Muslim subjecthood in classical Islamic law.
**Breakdown:**
- Not land revenue, not customs duty, not a tax on every Hindu without exception (✅ owner 22).
- Exempt categories in classical practice included women, children, indigent persons, some dependants and those unable to pay; administration and remissions varied (✅ owner 22).
- Revenue was placed in a separate treasury for needy Muslims and ulama (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Avoid quoting rates unless the unit, category and source are secure (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** MED-4040, MED-4041.
**Trap:** Jizyah is a poll tax and a political signal, not land revenue (✅ owner 22 traps).
**Mains hook:** The fiscal yield was contested; the symbolic cost was large.
**Back:** *"Poll tax, not land tax; symbol as much as coin."*

### GROUP — The Marathas, Shivaji, Aurangzeb's Deccan and the Jagirdari Crisis (owner 23)

### Flashcard MED-4054 — Maratha state formation: terrain, service and fiscal extraction (concept)
**Front:** How did the Maratha state form, and what did geography contribute?
**Definition (learn):** A sovereign regional state built on local military, fort, agrarian and coastal resources, converting terrain into power through leadership, fiscal extraction, diplomacy and rival-state weakness.
**Breakdown:**
- Sahyadri hill ranges, passes and forts; Maval uplands and mobile infantry; Konkan creeks, ports and maritime communities (✅ owner 23).
- Deshmukhs, patils, village bodies and hereditary landed rights; Marathi language and devotional networks (✅ owner 23).
- Service mobility among Ahmadnagar, Bijapur, Golconda and the Mughals (✅ owner 23).
- Geography enabled but did not cause the state (⚠️ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Coronation 1674; death of Shivaji 1680 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MED-4055, MED-4057, MED-4064, MED-4086.
**Trap:** Do not treat the Maratha state as a modern nation-state or an ethnically pure administration (⚠️ owner 23).
**Mains hook:** Terrain was an opportunity; organisation, leadership, fiscal extraction and diplomacy converted it into power.
**Back:** *"Hills gave the chance; forts, revenue and diplomacy gave the state."*

### Flashcard MED-4055 — Shahji Bhonsle: mobile service politics
**Front:** Who was Shahji Bhonsle, and why does his career matter?
**Definition (learn):** Shivaji's father, who served shifting Ahmadnagar, Mughal and Bijapur interests.
**Breakdown:**
- Supported a Nizam Shahi claimant; controlled a Poona-region base; later held southern assignments (✅ owner 23).
- His career shows mobile service politics, not a continuous hereditary anti-Mughal programme (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No dates in owner for his career; do not add (⚠️).
**Links:** MED-4054, MED-4056, MED-4059.
**Trap:** Do not call Shahji an anti-Mughal nationalist (✅ owner 23 caution).
**Mains hook:** Use Shahji to show that regional power came from service mobility.
**Back:** *"Shahji served all sides: mobility, not one banner."*

### Flashcard MED-4056 — Jijabai and Dadaji Kondadev: the Poona jagir's early management
**Front:** What role did Jijabai and Dadaji Kondadev play in Shivaji's early base?
**Definition (learn):** Shivaji's mother Jijabai and administrator Dadaji Kondadev are linked to the management of the Poona jagir.
**Breakdown:**
- Early management of the Poona jagir (✅ owner 23).
- Later narratives must be checked for chronology and hagiographic purpose (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No dates in owner; do not add (⚠️).
**Links:** MED-4055, MED-4057.
**Trap:** Hagiography inflates founder-figure roles; test against chronology (✅ owner 23).
**Mains hook:** Use administrators to explain how a jagir became a political base.
**Back:** *"Jijabai and Dadaji ran the Poona base; test the legend."*

### Flashcard MED-4057 — Torna, Rajgad and the Poona-Maval forts (1640s)
**Front:** Which forts did Shivaji acquire in the 1640s, and why do they matter?
**Definition (learn):** Torna, Kondana and other Poona-Maval forts, with Murumbdev (Rajgad) as a major base, acquired in the 1640s.
**Breakdown:**
- Acquisition in the 1640s (✅ owner 23).
- Murumbdev/Rajgad became a major base (✅ owner 23).
- Owner advanced gives the period as 1645–47 for Rajgad, Kondana and Torna (✅ owner 23 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1640s; 1645–47 (✅ owner 23 both files).
**Links:** MED-4054, MED-4058, MED-4088.
**Trap:** Rajgad is spelled Rajgarh in one owner source; the place is the same (✅ owner 23 advanced note).
**Mains hook:** Early fort capture is the material base of later sovereignty.
**Back:** *"1640s: Torna, Kondana, Rajgad, the first Maratha forts."*

### Flashcard MED-4058 — Javli (1656): Maval highlands and the Konkan route
**Front:** Why was the conquest of Javli in 1656 important?
**Definition (learn):** The conquest of Javli opened the Maval–Satara–Konkan movement, consolidating the Maval highlands.
**Breakdown:**
- Control of Javli and the More chiefs' territory strengthened Shivaji in the Maval highlands (✅ owner 23).
- Opened routes toward Satara and the Konkan (✅ owner 23).
- Use Javli as geographical-institutional consolidation, not proof of inevitable statehood (⚠️ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1656 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MED-4057, MED-4054.
**Trap:** Do not present Javli as a romantic inevitable step (⚠️ owner 23).
**Mains hook:** Javli shows how control of communication routes built a state.
**Back:** *"1656 Javli opens Maval, Satara and the Konkan."*

### Flashcard MED-4059 — Afzal Khan killed at Pratapgad (10 November 1659)
**Front:** What happened at Pratapgad in 1659, and what followed?
**Definition (learn):** Afzal Khan, a Bijapuri general, was killed at Pratapgad in a meeting in a hut at the foot of the fort; Bijapuri pressure then broke and Maratha gains followed.
**Breakdown:**
- Afzal Khan's 1659 expedition ended with his death at Pratapgad (✅ owner 23).
- Rapid Maratha gains followed (✅ owner 23).
- The meeting: 10 November 1659 in a hut at the foothills of Pratapgad (✅ Wikipedia: Shivaji).
- Wikipedia says the manner of death is not known with certainty (✅ Wikipedia: Shivaji).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 10 November 1659 (✅ Wikipedia: Shivaji).
**Links:** MED-4054, MED-4062.
**Trap:** The exact details of the killing are contested; state only the fact of death at the meeting (✅ Wikipedia: Shivaji).
**Mains hook:** Pratapgad broke Bijapur's northern pressure and enlarged Shivaji's room for manoeuvre.
**Back:** *"1659 Pratapgad, Afzal Khan dies, Bijapur breaks."*

### Flashcard MED-4060 — Shaista Khan in Poona and the 1663 night raid
**Front:** What did the 1663 night raid on Shaista Khan do to Mughal prestige?
**Definition (learn):** Shaista Khan, the Mughal viceroy, occupied Poona; Shivaji's 1663 night raid damaged Mughal prestige.
**Breakdown:**
- Shaista Khan occupied Poona (✅ owner 23).
- Shivaji's 1663 night raid damaged prestige (✅ owner 23).
- Owner timeline: Panhala crisis and Shaista Khan in Poona, 1660–63 (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1660–63 (✅ owner 23); 1663 raid (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MED-4061, MED-4062.
**Trap:** Do not treat the raid as a conquest; it was a prestige blow (⚠️ owner 23).
**Mains hook:** Prestige losses weakened Mughal claims in the Deccan.
**Back:** *"1663 night raid: Shaista Khan humiliated in Poona."*

### Flashcard MED-4061 — The first (1664) and second (1670) Surat sacks
**Front:** What did the sacks of Surat in 1664 and 1670 provide, and what did they not?
**Definition (learn):** Shivaji sacked the port city of Surat in 1664 and again in 1670.
**Breakdown:**
- Surat supplied wealth and political theatre (✅ owner 23).
- It did not mean permanent Maratha rule over the port (✅ owner 23).
- Sack years: 1664 and 1670 (✅ Wikipedia: Shivaji).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1664 first sack; 1670 second sack (✅ owner 23; ✅ Wikipedia: Shivaji).
**Links:** MED-4060, MED-4062, MED-4122.
**Trap:** Surat was plundered, not annexed (✅ owner 23).
**Mains hook:** Surat shows the Maratha raid economy as a source of wealth without territorial rule.
**Back:** *"1664 and 1670: loot Surat, keep no port."*

### Flashcard MED-4062 — The Treaty of Purandar (1665): forts and mansab
**Front:** What were the terms of the Treaty of Purandar, and what did Shivaji keep?
**Definition (learn):** A 1665 treaty between Jai Singh I and Shivaji after Jai Singh besieged Purandar.
**Breakdown:**
- Shivaji surrendered 23 forts and kept 12; the 12 retained were worth an income of one lakh huns (✅ Wikipedia: Treaty of Purandar (1665)).
- Total forts implied 35, which is a derived sum, not stated (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 23 says 23 of 35).
- Sambhaji received a mansab of 5000 (✅ Wikipedia: Treaty of Purandar (1665); ✅ owner 23).
- Shivaji was to cooperate in the Deccan (✅ owner 23).
- Signed 11 June 1665 (✅ Wikipedia: Treaty of Purandar (1665)).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 11 June 1665; 23 surrendered; 12 retained; Sambhaji mansab 5000 (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 23). Owner-internal: the Wikipedia infobox image caption gives 12 June 1665 for a meeting a day before the treaty, so signing dates differ slightly in one source.
**Links:** MED-4061, MED-4063, MED-4065, MED-4088.
**Trap:** Purandar made Shivaji a subordinate ally for a political project; it did not erase his independent base (⚠️ owner 23). Do not write 25 or 35 forts as settled without the sources.
**Mains hook:** Purandar was a missed accommodation that Agra later undid, showing settlement was possible but not guaranteed.
**Back:** *"1665 Purandar: 23 forts given, 12 kept, son gets 5000."*

### Flashcard MED-4063 — Shivaji at Agra (1666): prestige crisis and escape
**Front:** What happened at Agra in 1666, and how did Shivaji escape?
**Definition (learn):** Shivaji attended Aurangzeb's court at Agra in 1666, was detained, and escaped.
**Breakdown:**
- Court placement, rank expectation and detention created a prestige crisis (✅ owner 23).
- Escape on 17 August 1666, by a basket, per the Wikipedia account; Stewart Gordon sees no contemporary evidence for the basket story (✅ Wikipedia: Shivaji).
- Satish Chandra treats Aurangzeb's failure to value Shivaji as a major political mistake (⚠️ owner 23 reports Chandra's view).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1666; 17 August 1666 escape (✅ Wikipedia: Shivaji).
**Links:** MED-4062, MED-4029, MED-4086.
**Trap:** The basket story is a legend claim, not contemporary evidence (✅ Wikipedia: Shivaji).
**Mains hook:** Agra shows how a prestige mismatch turned a subordinate ally into a rival.
**Back:** *"1666 Agra: detained, escaped, the alliance died."*

### Flashcard MED-4064 — The 1674 coronation at Raigad
**Front:** What did the 1674 coronation at Raigad achieve?
**Definition (learn):** Shivaji's coronation, officiated by Gaga Bhatta, raised him above competing Maratha chiefs and asserted independent kingship.
**Breakdown:**
- Raised Shivaji above competing chiefs; asserted independent kingship (✅ owner 23).
- Gaga Bhatta validated Kshatriya status (✅ owner 23).
- Regularised ritual, titles and era; improved treaty standing with Deccan rulers (✅ owner 23).
- Two dates: 6 June 1674 and a second coronation on 24 September 1674 (✅ Wikipedia: Shivaji).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 6 June 1674 (✅ Wikipedia: Shivaji); 24 September 1674 second rite (✅ Wikipedia: Shivaji).
**Links:** MED-4054, MED-4065, MED-4089.
**Trap:** Do not present the coronation as creating modern democracy, nationalism or an exclusively Hindu administration (✅ owner 23). Legitimacy had to be made, not merely declared (✅ owner 23).
**Mains hook:** A second rite shows that legitimacy needed repeated making, a point for the qualified-regional-state verdict.
**Back:** *"1674 Raigad: Gaga Bhatta, Kshatriya claim, two rites."*

### Flashcard MED-4065 — Annaji Datto's revenue assessment (1679)
**Front:** What was Annaji Datto's 1679 assessment, and how far can it be quantified?
**Definition (learn):** A measurement-based revenue assessment under Shivaji, completed in 1679 by Annaji Datto.
**Breakdown:**
- Measurement and classification were used; village headmen and officials collected (✅ owner 23).
- The demand is associated with a substantial share, often stated around two-fifths plus cesses, but incidence and comparison are debated (✅ owner 23).
- Do not present one fraction as uniform realised extraction in every village (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1679 (✅ owner 23; ✅ owner 23 advanced).
**Links:** MED-4079, MED-4080, MED-4081.
**Trap:** ⚠️ "Two-fifths" is an owner-reported share and is not confirmed by fetched sources; do not state it as uniform.
**Mains hook:** Revenue reform curbed deshmukh exactions and produced a measurement-based system, but the exact incidence is not reconstructible.
**Back:** *"1679 Annaji's assessment: measured, claimed, not uniform."*

### Flashcard MED-4066 — Bijapur (1686) and Golconda (1687) annexed
**Front:** What did Aurangzeb gain and lose by annexing Bijapur and Golconda?
**Definition (learn):** Annexation of the two Deccan sultanates in 1686 and 1687.
**Breakdown:**
- Annexation removed independent states but also ended buffers and negotiating partners (✅ owner 23).
- Added vast territory, forts and officials; absorbed unemployed Deccani nobles and soldiers (✅ owner 23).
- Exposed longer communication lines; left local power networks unsettled (✅ owner 23).
- Conquest was not durable pacification (⚠️ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1686 Bijapur; 1687 Golconda (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MED-4029, MED-4086, MED-4149.
**Trap:** Bijapur-Golconda annexation did not automatically strengthen Mughal finance; it added claims and strain (✅ owner 23 advanced).
**Mains hook:** Aurangzeb won Bijapur and Golconda but did not win the Deccan; qualify with Maratha resistance and jagirdari strain.
**Back:** *"1686 and 1687: buffers gone, liabilities added."*

### Flashcard MED-4067 — Sambhaji's capture and execution (1689)
**Front:** Why did Sambhaji's execution in 1689 not end Maratha resistance?
**Definition (learn):** Sambhaji, Shivaji's successor after the 1680 succession, was captured and executed in 1689.
**Breakdown:**
- Sambhaji defeated rivals after Shivaji's death, continued war and sheltered Prince Akbar for a time (✅ owner 23).
- He faced Mughal, Sidi and Portuguese pressures (✅ owner 23).
- Execution removed a recognised opponent with whom negotiation might have been possible (⚠️ owner 23).
- Resistance became decentralised and more diffuse (✅ owner 23 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1689 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MED-4068, MED-4069, MED-4088.
**Trap:** Sambhaji's execution did not end resistance; it made it harder to negotiate (✅ owner 23 traps).
**Mains hook:** Sambhaji's death is a turning point because it decentralised the Maratha war.
**Back:** *"1689 Sambhaji killed: resistance scattered, not ended."*

### Flashcard MED-4068 — Rajaram at Gingee: a distant royal centre
**Front:** Where did Rajaram go after 1689, and when did the Mughals take Gingee?
**Definition (learn):** After Sambhaji's capture, Rajaram escaped to Gingee, creating a distant royal centre while commanders operated across Maharashtra.
**Breakdown:**
- Rajaram escaped to Gingee (✅ owner 23).
- The long Mughal siege ended in 1698, but Rajaram had escaped (✅ owner 23).
- Rajaram died in 1700 (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1689 escape; 1698 Gingee fell; 1700 Rajaram died (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MED-4067, MED-4069.
**Trap:** Gingee's fall did not end the royal cause (✅ owner 23).
**Mains hook:** A distant royal centre plus decentralised commanders made resistance resilient.
**Back:** *"Rajaram to Gingee 1689, Gingee falls 1698, Rajaram dies 1700."*

### Flashcard MED-4069 — Tarabai's regency (after 1700)
**Front:** What role did Tarabai play after Rajaram's death?
**Definition (learn):** After Rajaram's death in 1700, Tarabai directed resistance in the name of her son Shivaji II.
**Breakdown:**
- Maratha command became decentralised but politically connected through the royal cause, forts, local chiefs and revenue collection (✅ owner 23).
- Decentralisation increased resilience but also strengthened autonomous commanders and later confederate tendencies (⚠️ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1700 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MED-4068, MED-4147 (Shahu and the Peshwas).
**Trap:** Tarabai was not a neutral regent; she was a party in the post-1707 struggle with Shahu (✅ owner 25).
**Mains hook:** The royal cause survived through commanders, showing the confederate direction.
**Back:** *"1700 Tarabai for Shivaji II: the cause outlives the king."*

### Flashcard MED-4070 — Ashtapradhan: the council of eight (cabinet trap)
**Front:** What was the Ashtapradhan, and was it a cabinet?
**Definition (learn):** A council of eight ministers, formalised by Shivaji at his 1674 coronation.
**Breakdown:**
- Eight named offices: Peshwa, Amatya/Mazumdar, Sachiv, Mantri/Waqia-navis, Sumant/Dabir, Senapati, Nyayadhish and Panditrao (✅ owner 23; ✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
- Ministers served the ruler, could hold military as well as civil duties (✅ owner 23).
- Each minister was placed in charge of an administrative department (✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
- Sambhaji reduced the council's powers (✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Formalised at the 1674 coronation (✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
**Links:** MED-4071 to MED-4078, MED-4064.
**Trap:** ⚠️ Source conflict. The owner says the Ashtapradhan "was not a modern collectively responsible cabinet" and that ministers answered to the ruler. Wikipedia calls it "the cabinet of the Maratha Empire" in its lead. Use the owner's qualification: a council, not a responsible cabinet.
**Mains hook:** The council combined administration with ruler-centred command; avoid calling it a parliamentary cabinet.
**Back:** *"Eight ministers, one king: a council, not a cabinet."*

### Flashcard MED-4071 — Peshwa: general administration and finance coordination
**Front:** What was the Peshwa's office in the Ashtapradhan?
**Definition (learn):** The prime minister (Pant Pradhan), responsible for general administration and finance coordination.
**Breakdown:**
- General administration and finance coordination (✅ owner 23).
- First appointee: Moropant Trimbak Pingle (✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No appointment year in owner file.
**Links:** MED-4070, MED-4147 (the Peshwa's rise).
**Trap:** The Peshwa of the 1700s is a later office with a different power; this is the Shivaji-era minister (⚠️ owner 25 link).
**Mains hook:** The Peshwa's later rise is the link to the eighteenth-century Maratha confederacy (⚠️).
**Back:** *"Peshwa: the chief minister who coordinated the state."*

### Flashcard MED-4072 — Amatya/Mazumdar: accounts and finance
**Front:** What did the Amatya (Mazumdar) do in the Ashtapradhan?
**Definition (learn):** The finance minister responsible for accounts.
**Breakdown:**
- Accounts and finance (✅ owner 23).
- Renamed Amatya in 1674; given to Ramchandra Pant Amatya (✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Renamed 1674 (✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
**Links:** MED-4070, MED-4079.
**Trap:** The name Mazumdar and Amatya refer to the same post in different sources (✅ owner 23 and Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** Finance was a separate office, but still a ruler-appointed one.
**Back:** *"Amatya: the accounts minister."*

### Flashcard MED-4073 — Sachiv: correspondence and records
**Front:** What did the Sachiv do, and who held the post first?
**Definition (learn):** The secretary who handled correspondence and records and drafted royal edicts.
**Breakdown:**
- Correspondence and records (✅ owner 23).
- Drafted royal edicts (✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
- Annaji Datto Sachiv was appointed in 1662 (✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1662 (✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
**Links:** MED-4070, MED-4065 (Annaji Datto's later assessment).
**Trap:** Annaji Datto's 1679 assessment and his 1662 secretaryship are two different roles (✅ owner 23 and Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** The secretariat is where Shivaji's administrative records took shape.
**Back:** *"Sachiv: edicts and records, Annaji 1662."*

### Flashcard MED-4074 — Mantri/Waqia-navis: court chronicle and intelligence
**Front:** What did the Mantri (Waqia-navis) do?
**Definition (learn):** The office for court and intelligence chronicle functions, interior affairs and espionage.
**Breakdown:**
- Court and intelligence chronicle functions (✅ owner 23).
- Interior affairs, intelligence and espionage (✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No date in owner file.
**Links:** MED-4070, MED-4060.
**Trap:** Waqia-navis is an intelligence and chronicle role, not a military one.
**Mains hook:** Intelligence was part of Maratha mobility and fort control.
**Back:** *"Mantri: the court chronicle and the spy network."*

### Flashcard MED-4075 — Sumant/Dabir: foreign affairs and diplomacy
**Front:** What did the Sumant (Dabir) do?
**Definition (learn):** The minister for foreign affairs and relations with other sovereigns.
**Breakdown:**
- External and diplomatic affairs (✅ owner 23).
- Managing relations with other sovereigns (✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No date in owner file.
**Links:** MED-4070, MED-4062, MED-4088.
**Trap:** Sumant and Dabir are the same post in the owner's table (✅ owner 23 and Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** Diplomacy was central to Purandar, Agra and the chauth settlements.
**Back:** *"Sumant: foreign affairs, treaties and rivals."*

### Flashcard MED-4076 — Senapati: military command
**Front:** What did the Senapati do?
**Definition (learn):** The commander-in-chief of the forces (Sar-i-Naubat in Wikipedia's variant).
**Breakdown:**
- Military command (✅ owner 23).
- Sar-i-Naubat / Senapati; first holder Netaji Palkar (✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No year for the first holder in the owner file.
**Links:** MED-4070, MED-4083.
**Trap:** The Senapati was one office among eight, not the Maratha army's sole commander (⚠️ owner 23).
**Mains hook:** Military command was embedded in a council, which supports the "not a modern cabinet" view.
**Back:** *"Senapati: the army, one minister among eight."*

### Flashcard MED-4077 — Nyayadhish: justice
**Front:** What did the Nyayadhish decide?
**Definition (learn):** The office responsible for justice, deciding civil and criminal matters.
**Breakdown:**
- Justice (✅ owner 23).
- Civil and criminal matters (✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
- Niraji Ravaji held the post (✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No year in owner file.
**Links:** MED-4070.
**Trap:** The Nyayadhish is not the Peshwa's judicial office; keep the eight distinct (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Justice inside the council shows the ruler's judicial control.
**Back:** *"Nyayadhish: justice, not appeal to a cabinet."*

### Flashcard MED-4078 — Panditrao: religious grants and duties
**Front:** What did the Panditrao do?
**Definition (learn):** The office for religious grants and internal religious duties, a high-priest role in the Wikipedia account.
**Breakdown:**
- Religious grants and duties (✅ owner 23).
- High-priest style role in internal religious matters (✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
- Raghunath Panditrao held the post (✅ Wikipedia: Ashtapradhan).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No year in owner file.
**Links:** MED-4070, MED-4089.
**Trap:** Religious office is not a priestly cabinet decision; the ruler's control is the frame (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Religious grants show the Sanskritic kingship idiom in administration.
**Back:** *"Panditrao: religious grants, Sanskrit legitimacy."*

### Flashcard MED-4079 — Revenue reform: curbing deshmukhs without abolishing rights
**Front:** How far did Shivaji reform revenue and local power?
**Definition (learn):** Shivaji curbed illegal exactions and over-mighty deshmukhs and mirasdars, but did not abolish hereditary local rights or landed intermediaries.
**Breakdown:**
- Curbed illegal exactions; ended some revenue farming (✅ owner 23 advanced).
- Did not abolish hereditary rights, mokasa, jagir or landed intermediaries (✅ owner 23).
- Central supervision coexisted with negotiated local power (⚠️ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Revenue measurement and 1679 assessment (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MED-4065, MED-4070, MED-4080.
**Trap:** Do not state that Shivaji abolished the deshmukhs (✅ owner 23 trap).
**Mains hook:** Negotiated power, not centralisation by decree, is the qualifier.
**Back:** *"Curb, not abolish: deshmukhs stayed, exactions ended."*

### Flashcard MED-4080 — Chauth: one-fourth, from protection to political claim
**Front:** What was chauth, and how is it defined?
**Definition (learn):** A regular tax or tribute, nominally 25% of revenue or produce, imposed by the Maratha state from the early eighteenth century (Wikipedia). Owner says one-fourth claim initially linked to protection and non-raiding arrangements.
**Breakdown:**
- One-fourth of revenue or produce (✅ Wikipedia: Chauth; ✅ owner 23).
- Initially linked to protection and non-raiding arrangements in neighbouring territory, later a wider political claim (✅ owner 23).
- Sarkar: protection money; Ranade: payment for armed security comparable to Wellesley's subsidiary alliances (✅ Wikipedia: Chauth).
- Mughal grant: in 1719 Rafi-ud-Darajat granted chauth and sardeshmukhi rights over six Deccan provinces to Shahu (✅ Wikipedia: Chauth).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 25% nominal (✅ Wikipedia: Chauth); 1719 grant (✅ Wikipedia: Chauth).
**Links:** MED-4081, MED-4082, MED-4090, MED-4147.
**Trap:** Chauth was not a religious tax; it was a Maratha fiscal levy (✅ owner 25 advanced).
**Mains hook:** Chauth shows the shift from raid-protection to revenue claim, which underlies the confederacy.
**Back:** *"Chauth: a quarter, bought as protection, claimed as right."*

### Flashcard MED-4081 — Sardeshmukhi: the additional ten per cent
**Front:** What was sardeshmukhi, and how did it differ from chauth?
**Definition (learn):** An additional 10% claim based on the asserted superior deshmukh right.
**Breakdown:**
- Ten-per-cent claim on the asserted superior deshmukh right (✅ owner 23).
- An additional 10% levy on top of chauth (✅ Wikipedia: Chauth).
- Granted in 1719 with chauth to Shahu over six provinces (✅ Wikipedia: Chauth).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 10%; 1719 grant (✅ Wikipedia: Chauth).
**Links:** MED-4080, MED-4082.
**Trap:** Sardeshmukhi is not a separate sovereignty; it did not equal full territorial sovereignty (✅ owner 23).
**Mains hook:** The 10% claim was the deshmukh-rights claim, which shows the Maratha claim to local superiority.
**Back:** *"Sardeshmukhi: one-tenth, the deshmukh's right."*

### Flashcard MED-4082 — Chauth versus sardeshmukhi (comparison pair)
**Front:** How do chauth and sardeshmukhi differ?
**Definition (learn):** Two distinct Maratha claims that were not the same as territorial sovereignty.
**Breakdown:**
- Chauth: one-fourth, linked to protection and non-raiding, later a wider political claim (✅ owner 23).
- Sardeshmukhi: additional ten per cent based on asserted superior deshmukh right (✅ owner 23).
- In the later war, kamaishdars and local agents made chauth a parallel fiscal network (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 25% and 10% (✅ Wikipedia: Chauth).
**Links:** MED-4080, MED-4081, MED-4090.
**Trap:** Chauth and sardeshmukhi were distinct; treating them as one tax is a standard error (✅ owner 23 trap).
**Mains hook:** Distinguish the two claims before judging the Maratha fiscal network.
**Back:** *"Chauth a quarter, sardeshmukhi a tenth; not the same claim."*

### Flashcard MED-4083 — Mavali infantry, light cavalry and ganimi kava
**Front:** What was Shivaji's army, and what is ganimi kava?
**Definition (learn):** Mavali infantry, light cavalry and forts, with rapid movement, night attacks and dispersed operations. Ganimi kava is enemy-style irregular warfare adapted to terrain and intelligence.
**Breakdown:**
- Mavali infantry, light cavalry, forts, intelligence, rapid movement, night attacks and disciplined command (✅ owner 23).
- Regular troops were often cash-paid; plunder and local assignments also existed (✅ owner 23).
- Ganimi kava is not a single timeless guerrilla doctrine (✅ owner 23).
- Maratha forces also fought sieges and battles (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No dates; mechanisms only (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MED-4054, MED-4085, MED-4084.
**Trap:** Ganimi kava is not "guerrilla warfare" as a fixed doctrine (✅ owner 23 trap).
**Mains hook:** Mughal difficulty came from long communications, numerous forts, seasonal terrain and dispersed opponents (✅ owner 23).
**Back:** *"Mavali feet, ganimi tactics, forts as bases, not a doctrine."*

### Flashcard MED-4084 — The navy: littoral force and Sindhudurg
**Front:** What kind of navy did Shivaji build, and why?
**Definition (learn):** A regional littoral force of gunboats, grabs/gallivats and trading craft, recruited from Koli, Bhandari and Muslim seafarers, with coastal forts such as Sindhudurg.
**Breakdown:**
- Gunboats, grabs, gallivats and trading craft (✅ owner 23).
- Recruited Koli, Bhandari and Muslim seafarers (✅ owner 23).
- Aims: creek and coast defence; checking the Sidis of Janjira; protecting or taxing trade; coastal supply; limiting Portuguese and English leverage (✅ owner 23).
- Not a modern blue-water navy (⚠️ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sindhudurg coastal fort (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MED-4083, MED-4089.
**Trap:** Navy was littoral, not blue-water; it did not command the Indian Ocean (✅ owner 23 trap).
**Mains hook:** The navy's Muslim and Koli crews undercut a narrow communal description of the state (✅ owner 23 advanced).
**Back:** *"Littoral navy, Koli crews, Sindhudurg, not the Indian Ocean."*

### Flashcard MED-4085 — The Mughal fort-siege dilemma (1700–05 onward)
**Front:** Why did Aurangzeb's fort-siege cycle bring declining strategic returns?
**Definition (learn):** From about 1700 Aurangzeb personally moved against forts; the cycle of capture, garrison, supply and return made each gain costly.
**Breakdown:**
- Marathas could surrender a fort, recover it after Mughal movement, cut supplies or negotiate payments (✅ owner 23).
- Chain: capture → garrison → supply → move onward → Maratha return → recapture cost (✅ owner 23).
- Owner advanced: the emperor personally dragged the army from fort to fort, 1700–05 (✅ owner 23 advanced).
- Military persistence remained formidable, but strategic returns declined (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1700–05 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MED-4086, MED-4083, MED-4067.
**Trap:** Persistence is not success; the cycle lowered returns, not military capacity alone (✅ owner 23).
**Mains hook:** Use the siege cycle to show capacity with overextension.
**Back:** *"Take, garrison, supply, lose it back: the fort-trap."*

### Flashcard MED-4086 — Jagirdari crisis: the ten mechanisms (Deccan form)
**Front:** What were the ten mechanisms of the jagirdari crisis in the Deccan?
**Definition (learn):** The owner's list of mechanisms that together produced the acute Deccan jagirdari crisis.
**Breakdown:**
- 1. be-jagiri: shortage of assignable productive jagirs (✅ owner 23).
- 2. jama-hasil gap (✅ owner 23).
- 3. paibaqi pressure: land awaiting assignment or unsettled did not guarantee income (✅ owner 23).
- 4. war disruption: raiding, forts and uncertain control reduced collection (✅ owner 23).
- 5. parallel chauth: Maratha agents claimed revenue in Mughal jagirs (✅ owner 23).
- 6. private bargains: jagirdars paid or negotiated to protect collection (✅ owner 23).
- 7. expanded nobility: Mughal, Deccani and Maratha entrants increased claims (✅ owner 23).
- 8. transfers and corruption: short tenure encouraged extraction (✅ owner 23).
- 9. thin contingents: poor income weakened troop maintenance (✅ owner 23).
- 10. zamindar and local power: collection required cooperation that war eroded (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Topic 21 supplies the early claim-resource gap; topic 23 owns the acute Deccan form (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MED-4001, MED-4019, MED-4024, MED-4087, MED-4130.
**Trap:** The crisis was not only "too many mansabdars, too few jagirs" (✅ owner 23 advanced).
**Mains hook:** A combined social, administrative, military and fiscal crisis; cite at least three mechanisms.
**Back:** *"Be-jagiri, gap, paibaqi, war, chauth, bargains, entrants, transfers, thin troops, zamindars."*

### Flashcard MED-4087 — Jagirdari feedback loop and the debate
**Front:** What is the jagirdari feedback loop, and what do the debaters say?
**Definition (learn):** A feedback loop in which a bad jagir or low hasil forces harsh collection and resistance, lowering hasil further.
**Breakdown:**
- Loop: bad jagir or low hasil → weak contingent and harsh collection → local resistance or Maratha bargain → lower hasil → noble dissatisfaction and faction → more transfers and coercion (✅ owner 23).
- Debate points: "too many mansabdars, too few jagirs" identifies one real mechanism; a purely fiscal account misses war, ecology, zamindars and administration; a pure Deccan-war account misses pressures visible before 1681; the state did not cease to function before 1707 (⚠️ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Pressures before 1681 and state function before 1707 (⚠️ owner 23).
**Links:** MED-4086, MED-4130, MED-4149.
**Trap:** Crisis is not total collapse under Aurangzeb (✅ owner 23 advanced).
**Mains hook:** Argue for the multi-cause crisis and qualify with function until 1707.
**Back:** *"Bad jagir, harsh collection, lower hasil, more faction: the loop."*

### Flashcard MED-4088 — Why Mughal-Maratha settlement failed
**Front:** Why did the settlement moments at Purandar, Agra, Sambhaji and Shahu fail?
**Definition (learn):** A list of failure causes at each possible settlement.
**Breakdown:**
- Conflicting rank and sovereignty claims; distrust after Agra; repeated changes in Deccan strategy (✅ owner 23).
- Mughal insistence on subordination; Maratha value of forts and chauth (✅ owner 23).
- Rival Deccan and European and Sidi actors; succession and court politics; gains from continued war for local commanders (✅ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Purandar 1665, Agra 1666, Sambhaji 1680–89, Shahu later (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MED-4062, MED-4063, MED-4067, MED-4147.
**Trap:** A settlement was possible, not inevitable; avoid claiming one decision doomed the empire (✅ owner 23).
**Mains hook:** Settlement failures show conflicting sovereignty claims, not a single personal error.
**Back:** *"Rank, distrust, subordination, forts, rivals, succession, war profit."*

### Flashcard MED-4089 — Shivaji's state character: Sanskritic kingship and Deccani practice
**Front:** Was Shivaji's state a Hindu state in a fixed sense?
**Definition (learn):** The state used Sanskritic kingship, protection language and Hindu symbols, while its institutions borrowed from Deccani-Persianate practice.
**Breakdown:**
- Institutions borrowed from Deccani-Persianate practice (✅ owner 23).
- Muslims served in army, navy and administration (✅ owner 23).
- War was directed against Hindu and Muslim opponents; revenue and fort control structured conflict (✅ owner 23).
- Local peasants and chiefs experienced protection and extraction (✅ owner 23).
- "Hindu state" is an important legitimacy idiom only if not turned into an ethnically pure administration (⚠️ owner 23).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4064, MED-4084, MED-4078.
**Trap:** Do not call Shivaji's state modern nationalist or institutionally pure (✅ owner 23 trap).
**Mains hook:** "Hindu state" as legitimacy idiom, not as administrative purity.
**Back:** *"Sanskrit idiom, Deccani machinery, mixed service."*

### Flashcard MED-4090 — Parallel chauth in the late war: kamaishdars and agents
**Front:** How did chauth become a parallel fiscal network in the Deccan?
**Definition (learn):** During the later war, Maratha commanders, local headmen and kamaishdars collected chauth in parts of the Deccan in parallel to the Mughal revenue system.
**Breakdown:**
- Maratha commanders, local headmen and kamaishdars set up parallel chauth (✅ owner 23 advanced).
- Mughal jagirdars often could not collect even paper income; some made private pacts to pay chauth (✅ owner 23 advanced).
- Chauth was an organised claim collected through district agents, not mere bandit plunder (✅ owner 23 advanced trap).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Late war, 1690s onward (⚠️ owner 23, "1690s decentralised commanders expanded war and chauth collection").
**Links:** MED-4080, MED-4086, MED-4082.
**Trap:** Chauth was not merely bandit plunder (✅ owner 23 advanced).
**Mains hook:** Parallel chauth eroded Mughal revenue authority.
**Back:** *"Agents, pacts and parallel chauth: the empire's revenue leaks."*

### GROUP — Mughal Society, Economy and Culture (owner 24)

### Flashcard MED-4091 — Composite economy and culture under Mughal peace (concept)
**Front:** How did political integration shape Mughal society, economy and culture?
**Definition (learn):** Mughal political integration encouraged roads, sarais, a stable silver currency, urban life, trade and handicraft growth, while culture became a composite and institutional synthesis.
**Breakdown:**
- Trade and manufacture expanded in the seventeenth century; India was a major textile producer (✅ owner 24).
- Nobles, merchants and royal family members participated in trade (✅ owner 24).
- Rural society was stratified; zamindars were armed and locally rooted (✅ owner 24).
- Culture was institutional: painting as karkhana, architecture as collective craftsmanship, literature through Persian-regional exchange (✅ owner 24 advanced).
- Technology lagged because elite science and artisanal production were weakly connected (✅ owner 24 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Seventeenth century as the high point (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MED-4092, MED-4120, MED-4125, MED-4151.
**Trap:** Commercial expansion is not modern industrialisation (✅ owner 24 advanced trap).
**Mains hook:** "The seventeenth century was a second classical age" is the frame; qualify it with hierarchy, revenue pressure and technology limits.
**Back:** *"Peace, roads, silver, workshops: a composite culture with limits."*

### Flashcard MED-4092 — Mughal peace: silver currency, sarais and roads
**Front:** What infrastructure supported Mughal trade and urban growth?
**Definition (learn):** Roads, sarais, ferries, stable silver coinage and river and coastal shipping, forming a multimodal system.
**Breakdown:**
- Stable silver currency; silver rupee, copper dam and gold mohur in circulation (✅ owner 24).
- Banjaras and carriers moved bulk grain; pack bullocks, ox-carts, river craft and coastal shipping (✅ owner 24).
- Roads were political-commercial corridors, not all-weather highways; monsoon, tolls and insecurity mattered (⚠️ owner 24).
- Barter and payments in kind persisted; coin scarcity was real (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner descriptions.
**Links:** MED-4091, MED-4103, MED-4104.
**Trap:** Bullion imports supported minting but did not eliminate barter (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** Money and roads show integration; do not claim all-weather national markets.
**Back:** *"Silver rupee, sarai, banjara: the circuit."*

### Flashcard MED-4093 — Virji Vohra of Surat
**Front:** Who was Virji Vohra, and what does he show?
**Definition (learn):** A merchant of Surat cited as an example of the scale of Indian private commercial capital.
**Breakdown:**
- Named with Abdul Ghaffur as major merchant capital (✅ owner 24).
- Shows that Indian merchant capital remained powerful alongside European companies (✅ owner 24).
- Merchant wealth was regionally concentrated (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No dates in owner file (⚠️).
**Links:** MED-4094, MED-4122, MED-4150.
**Trap:** Do not treat the single merchant as typical of all Indian commerce (⚠️ owner 24 limit).
**Mains hook:** Indigenous capital is evidence against the claim that Europeans dominated Indian trade immediately (✅ owner 24).
**Back:** *"Virji Vohra: Surat merchant capital, not a European agent."*

### Flashcard MED-4094 — Abdul Ghaffur, Surat sea merchant and ship-owner
**Front:** Who was Abdul Ghaffur, and why is he cited?
**Definition (learn):** A Surat ship-owner and sea merchant cited as a major late-seventeenth or early-eighteenth-century figure.
**Breakdown:**
- Ship-owner and sea merchant (✅ owner 24).
- Surat's annual turnover estimate suggested the European share was a fraction (✅ owner 24 advanced; modern research cited by Chandra).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Late seventeenth or early eighteenth century (✅ owner 24 advanced).
**Links:** MED-4093, MED-4091.
**Trap:** Do not state a Surat turnover figure; the owner gives only a fractional European share (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** Shipping by Indian merchants is the counterweight to "European domination" narratives.
**Back:** *"Abdul Ghaffur: the ship-owner who sailed on Indian capital."*

### Flashcard MED-4095 — Khud-kasht and riyayati: resident cultivators
**Front:** What were khud-kasht and riyayati cultivators?
**Definition (learn):** Resident cultivators with stronger local occupancy and often lower customary dues.
**Breakdown:**
- Resident owner-cultivators with stronger occupancy position than pahi-kasht (✅ owner 24).
- Not an equal class; caste, cattle and credit varied (✅ owner 24).
- Rights could erode under pressure or indebtedness (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4096, MED-4097, MED-4098.
**Trap:** Khud-kasht is not a landlord class; it is a resident-cultivator category (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** The rural hierarchy shows peasants were not one class.
**Back:** *"Khud-kasht: the resident cultivator with the stronger hold."*

### Flashcard MED-4096 — Raiyati and muzarian: ordinary cultivators and tenants
**Front:** What were raiyati and muzarian cultivators?
**Definition (learn):** The ordinary cultivator or tenant category, with terminology and burden varying regionally.
**Breakdown:**
- Ordinary cultivator or tenant category (✅ owner 24).
- Terminology and burden varied by region (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4095, MED-4097.
**Trap:** Raiyati and muzarian are ideal-type labels; local usage varied (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** Use the category with its regional caveats.
**Back:** *"Raiyati: the ordinary cultivator, burden varies by place."*

### Flashcard MED-4097 — Pahi-kasht: the incoming cultivator
**Front:** Who were pahi-kasht cultivators?
**Definition (learn):** Non-resident or incoming cultivators who could receive concessions but had weaker local embeddedness.
**Breakdown:**
- Non-resident or incoming cultivator (✅ owner 24).
- Could receive concessions; weaker local embeddedness (✅ owner 24).
- Peasant migration and flight were bargaining strategies as well as signs of distress (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4095, MED-4096.
**Trap:** Khud-kasht and pahi-kasht are not the same as raiyati; each is a distinct rights category (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** Migration as a bargaining tool, not only distress.
**Back:** *"Pahi-kasht: the outsider who arrives with concessions and weaker roots."*

### Flashcard MED-4098 — Mirasidar: hereditary occupancy holder in the south
**Front:** What is a mirasidar?
**Definition (learn):** A hereditary occupancy-right holder in land, especially in south India, based on long-standing cultivation rights rather than revenue assignment.
**Breakdown:**
- Hereditary occupancy-right holder; south India (✅ owner 24).
- Based on long-standing cultivation rights, not revenue assignment (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4018 (jagir), MED-4099, MED-4101.
**Trap:** A mirasidar is not a jagirdar; the jagirdar holds an assigned revenue claim (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** Mirasidari shows that occupancy rights existed outside imperial assignment.
**Back:** *"Mirasidar: hereditary occupancy, not a revenue assignment."*

### Flashcard MED-4099 — Zamindars: armed, local and negotiable
**Front:** What powers did zamindars hold?
**Definition (learn):** Hereditary revenue claimants and chiefs with local roots, ranging from village claimants to large rajas.
**Breakdown:**
- Hereditary revenue claims and perquisites; forts (garhis) and armed retainers (✅ owner 24).
- Caste, clan and village authority; assistance in collection, policing and cultivation (✅ owner 24).
- Rights in land without modern absolute ownership; capacity for negotiation, resistance or imperial service (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4100, MED-4042, MED-4086.
**Trap:** Zamindars were not merely tax clerks (✅ owner 24 trap).
**Mains hook:** Zamindars were both collaborators and constraints on imperial rule (✅ owner 24).
**Back:** *"Zamindar: fort, followers, family, negotiation."*

### Flashcard MED-4100 — Jagirdar versus zamindar (comparison pair)
**Front:** How do a jagirdar and a zamindar differ?
**Definition (learn):** The jagirdar is an imperial assignee of a transferable revenue claim; the zamindar is a locally rooted hereditary claimant.
**Breakdown:**
- Jagirdar: imperial assignee; rank and service relationship; assignment usually transferable; not owner of all soil in the jagir (✅ owner 24).
- Zamindar: locally rooted; social, political and agrarian roots; rights often hereditary but contestable; not merely a salaried tax clerk (✅ owner 24).
- The same person could enter imperial service while keeping local or watan interests (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4018, MED-4099.
**Trap:** The 2019 Prelims routing ledger asks about jagirdar and zamindar differences; the key is unavailable locally (✅ owner 24 PYQ ledger). Answer on the statement level above.
**Mains hook:** Jagirdar-zamindar conflict was central to the jagirdari crisis.
**Back:** *"Jagirdar: transferable, imperial. Zamindar: hereditary, local."*

### Flashcard MED-4101 — Aurang: the warehouse, not a military rank
**Front:** What was an aurang in Mughal trade?
**Definition (learn):** A warehouse or depot, especially for textiles awaiting trade or export.
**Breakdown:**
- Warehouse and depot, especially for textiles (✅ owner 24).
- Commercial infrastructure linking production to export (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PYQ 2020 Prelims GS-I Q21 lists "Aurang Banian Mirasidar roles"; the key is unavailable locally (✅ owner 24 PYQ ledger).
**Links:** MED-4102, MED-4098, MED-4092.
**Trap:** Aurang was a warehouse; it was not a military rank (✅ owner 24 trap).
**Mains hook:** Terms like aurang show that Mughal commerce had its own institutions.
**Back:** *"Aurang: the godown of cloth."*

### Flashcard MED-4102 — Banian/dalal: the merchant-broker
**Front:** What was a banian in Mughal trade?
**Definition (learn):** An Indian merchant-broker who handled local purchases, credit and market access for European traders.
**Breakdown:**
- Broker and intermediary (✅ owner 24).
- Handled local purchases, credit and market access for Europeans (✅ owner 24).
- Also written as banyan or bania; dalal is a related broker term (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PYQ 2020 Prelims GS-I Q21 routing; key unavailable locally (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MED-4101, MED-4103, MED-4122.
**Trap:** A banian is not a caste label only; it is a merchant-broker (✅ owner 24 trap).
**Mains hook:** Brokers linked European and Indian commercial worlds.
**Back:** *"Banian: broker, not a caste label."*

### Flashcard MED-4103 — Hundi: the credit and remittance instrument
**Front:** What was a hundi?
**Definition (learn):** A credit and remittance instrument used in Mughal commerce, not simply a modern cheque.
**Breakdown:**
- Credit and remittance instrument (✅ owner 24).
- Not simply a modern cheque (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4104, MED-4092.
**Trap:** Do not call a hundi a bill of exchange in a modern sense without qualification (⚠️ memory).
**Mains hook:** Credit instruments show monetised trade without modern banking.
**Back:** *"Hundi: remittance and credit, not a cheque."*

### Flashcard MED-4104 — Sarraf/shroff: money changer, assayer and banker
**Front:** What did a sarraf (shroff) do?
**Definition (learn):** Money changer, assayer and banker.
**Breakdown:**
- Money changer, assayer and banker (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4103, MED-4092.
**Trap:** Sarraf and banian are different roles: money changer versus broker (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** Money-changers managed the coin scarcity and multi-metal circulation.
**Back:** *"Sarraf: money changer, assayer, banker."*

### Flashcard MED-4105 — Tavernier and the Golconda diamond trade
**Front:** What did Jean-Baptiste Tavernier record, and how reliable is he?
**Definition (learn):** A French jeweller-traveller who made six visits to India c. 1631–1668 and described Indian diamond mines and the Golconda/Kollur trade.
**Breakdown:**
- Six visits c. 1631–1668 (✅ owner 24 basic).
- Described Indian diamond mines and the Golconda/Kollur trade circuits (✅ owner 24).
- Traveller focus privileges luxury trade over ordinary production (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 1631–1668; Golconda and Kollur (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MED-4106, MED-4122.
**Trap:** Tavernier is a traveller source with a luxury lens; do not extend his diamond focus to all trade (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** Use Tavernier as named evidence for high-value trade, with its limits.
**Back:** *"Tavernier: six visits, Golconda diamonds, a luxury lens."*
**PYQ note:** Prelims GS-I 2018 Q73 routes "foreign traveller who discussed diamonds and diamond mines"; the key is unavailable locally (✅ owner 24 PYQ ledger).

### Flashcard MED-4106 — Bernier and the private-property critique
**Front:** What did Bernier argue about property and the economy, and how is it contested?
**Definition (learn):** François Bernier argued that insecure assignments and the lack of private landed property weakened economic incentives.
**Breakdown:**
- Bernier's argument: insecure assignment and no private landed property (✅ owner 24).
- Contested by Irfan Habib and Satish Chandra, who note strong merchant wealth and layered landed claims (⚠️ owner 24).
- Chandra also criticises Bernier on nobles being foreigners and on middle strata (✅ owner 24 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4105, MED-4100, MED-4126.
**Trap:** Do not accept Bernier literally; it is a European mercantilist lens (✅ owner 24 trap).
**Mains hook:** Use Bernier as a historiographical debate, not a settled truth.
**Back:** *"Bernier's lens: European, mercantilist, contested."*

### Flashcard MED-4107 — The Hamzanama and the imperial painting workshop (karkhana)
**Front:** When did Akbar's Hamzanama project start, and what did it institutionalise?
**Definition (learn):** The illustrated Hamza Nama project under Akbar, which organised painting in an imperial karkhana.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner gives the start as c. 1567 (✅ owner 24 basic; c. 1567–82 in the advanced file, ✅ owner 24 advanced).
- Fetched Wikipedia: commissioned about 1562, finished about 1577 over fourteen years (✅ Wikipedia: Akbar Hamzanama).
- A large group of painters; about thirty main artists and over a hundred people overall (✅ Wikipedia: Akbar Hamzanama).
- Named painters: Mir Sayyid Ali oversaw first; Abd al-Samad took over; Daswant named in one caption (✅ Wikipedia: Akbar Hamzanama).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner c. 1567 versus Wikipedia c. 1562–1577. Flag ⚠️ conflict.
**Links:** MED-4108, MED-4091, MED-4015.
**Trap:** The start date conflicts between owner (c. 1567) and Wikipedia (c. 1562). Do not state either as settled without checking Seyller (2002) (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Karkhana painting was collaborative and state-backed, not an isolated genius.
**Back:** *"Hamzanama: karkhana, collaborative, c. 1562–77 by Wikipedia."*

### Flashcard MED-4108 — Jahangir's naturalism and Ustad Mansur
**Front:** What was distinctive about Jahangir's painting?
**Definition (learn):** Portrait, animal, bird, flower and natural studies, with Ustad Mansur an important painter.
**Breakdown:**
- Portraiture, naturalism, animals and birds, flowers (✅ owner 24).
- Ustad Mansur is important (✅ owner 24).
- Contrast with Akbar: narrative scale versus closer observation (✅ owner 24 advanced).
- Still largely elite and courtly in audience (✅ owner 24 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1605–27 (✅ owner 24 mini-timeline).
**Links:** MED-4107, MED-4113.
**Trap:** Jahangir's taste is not Akbar's; the narrative karkhana is Akbar's contribution (✅ owner 24 advanced).
**Mains hook:** Use Mansur to show Jahangiri naturalism as a stage in Mughal painting.
**Back:** *"Jahangir: portraits, animals, flowers; Mansur at the brush."*

### Flashcard MED-4109 — Shah Jahan's architecture: Taj Mahal, Red Fort and Jama Masjid
**Front:** What defined Shah Jahan's architectural style?
**Definition (learn):** Scale, symmetry, marble and inlay: the mature Shahjahani synthesis, with the Taj Mahal as culmination.
**Breakdown:**
- Taj Mahal, Red Fort and Jama Masjid (✅ owner 24).
- Marble, pietra dura, symmetry and imperial urbanism (✅ owner 24).
- Taj Mahal presented as a culmination of Mughal-Indian architectural evolution (✅ owner 24).
- Monumentality depended on extraction and court patronage (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1628–58 phase (✅ owner 24 mini-timeline).
**Links:** MED-4110, MED-4005, MED-4026, MED-4009.
**Trap:** The Taj Mahal was not an isolated foreign design (✅ owner 24 trap).
**Mains hook:** Architecture expressed imperial confidence and composite craftsmanship, while requiring extraction.
**Back:** *"Taj, Red Fort, Jama Masjid: marble symmetry as imperial message."*

### Flashcard MED-4110 — Itimad-ud-Daula: marble and pietra dura before the Taj
**Front:** What did Itimad-ud-Daula's tomb prefigure?
**Definition (learn):** A tomb that used marble and pietra dura before the Taj Mahal.
**Breakdown:**
- Used marble and pietra dura before the Taj Mahal (✅ owner 24).
- Marks the Jahangiri marble and inlay transition (✅ owner 24 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No date in owner file; do not add (⚠️).
**Links:** MED-4109.
**Trap:** Itimad-ud-Daula precedes the Taj; do not treat it as Shah Jahan's work (⚠️ memory-safe, owner 24 sequence).
**Mains hook:** Use the sequence to show a building-form evolution.
**Back:** *"Itimad-ud-Daula: marble and inlay, first."*

### Flashcard MED-4111 — Buland Darwaza (1573): commemorating Gujarat
**Front:** What did the Buland Darwaza commemorate, and when was it begun?
**Definition (learn):** A gateway at Fatehpur Sikri begun in 1573 to commemorate the Gujarat victory.
**Breakdown:**
- Begun 1573 (✅ owner 24).
- Commemorates the Gujarat victory (✅ owner 24).
- Part of Akbar's experimental Fatehpur Sikri (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1573 (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MED-4112, MED-4109.
**Trap:** Do not confuse Buland Darwaza with the Taj Mahal's gate (⚠️ memory-safe).
**Mains hook:** Architecture as commemoration of conquest.
**Back:** *"1573 Buland Darwaza: Gujarat remembered in stone."*

### Flashcard MED-4112 — Fatehpur Sikri: Akbar's architectural experiment
**Front:** What was Fatehpur Sikri's role in Mughal architecture?
**Definition (learn):** Akbar's experimental architecture, including trabeate and arched synthesis, from which Shah Jahan's marble style developed.
**Breakdown:**
- Akbarian experimentation and trabeate-arched synthesis (✅ owner 24).
- Mughal architecture developed from Fatehpur Sikri to Shah Jahan's marble style (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond the Buland Darwaza date.
**Links:** MED-4111, MED-4109.
**Trap:** Fatehpur Sikri is Akbar's experiment, not Shahjahanabad (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Trace a single line of experimentation to the Taj.
**Back:** *"Fatehpur Sikri: Akbar's trial, Shah Jahan's marble."*

### Flashcard MED-4113 — "Aurangzeb banned music" (trap)
**Front:** Did Aurangzeb abolish music?
**Definition (learn):** No. He banished singing from court, but instruments and patronage by nobles and royal women continued.
**Breakdown:**
- Singing banished from court; instruments and patronage by nobles and women continued (✅ owner 24).
- The "burial of music" anecdote is a moral story, not proof that music ended (✅ owner 24 synthesis).
- Later growth in provincial courts (✅ owner 24 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4033, MED-4108, MED-4151.
**Trap:** Aurangzeb did not abolish music (✅ owner 24 trap).
**Mains hook:** Music's survival shows cultural continuity beyond one emperor's taste.
**Back:** *"Court silence, not burial: music survived."*

### Flashcard MED-4114 — Yoga Vasistha and Dara Shukoh (translation trap)
**Front:** What translation did Dara Shukoh sponsor, and what do the sources say?
**Definition (learn):** The Sanskrit philosophical text Yogavasistha, attributed to Valmiki, was translated into Persian; the owner dates a translation under Dara Shukoh's patronage to the mid-seventeenth century.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: translated into Persian under Dara Shukoh's patronage in the mid-seventeenth century (✅ owner 24).
- Fetched Wikipedia: translated several times, ordered by Akbar, Jahangir and Dara Shukoh; one translation by Nizam al-Din Panipati, called Jug-Basisht, in the late sixteenth century (✅ Wikipedia: Yoga Vasistha).
- Wikipedia gives no date for a Dara Shukoh translation (✅ Wikipedia: Yoga Vasistha).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner: mid-seventeenth century. Wikipedia: late sixteenth century (Panipati) and Dara Shukoh as one of three patrons. ⚠️ conflict.
**Links:** MED-4115, MED-4117.
**Trap:** Do not state Dara Shukoh's translation date (1653 or similar) without checking; the fetched source does not confirm it (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** The Persian Yogavasistha is an example of Sanskrit–Persian convergence under several patrons over time.
**Back:** *"Yogavasistha into Persian: several patrons, several dates."*
**PYQ note:** Prelims GS-I 2022 Q94 routes "Yogavasistha Persian translation during Mughal reign"; the key is unavailable locally (✅ owner 24 PYQ ledger).

### Flashcard MED-4115 — Tulsidas and Rahim: regional and devotional literature
**Front:** What do Tulsidas and Rahim show about regional literary creativity?
**Definition (learn):** Regional literary figures whose work moved cultural creativity beyond Persian court culture into vernaculars.
**Breakdown:**
- Tulsidas and Rahim named as regional literary florescence (✅ owner 24).
- Linguistic democratisation alongside imperial patronage (✅ owner 24).
- Regional traditions differed in theology and audience (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None in owner file.
**Links:** MED-4116, MED-4117.
**Trap:** Regional literature is not the same as Persian court literature (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** Vernacular literature shows cultural creativity beyond the court.
**Back:** *"Tulsidas and Rahim: the vernacular turn."*

### Flashcard MED-4116 — Eknath and Tukaram: Marathi devotional literature
**Front:** What do Eknath and Tukaram add to the Mughal-period literary picture?
**Definition (learn):** Marathi devotional figures in the regional literary florescence.
**Breakdown:**
- Named as regional literary figures (✅ owner 24).
- Bhakti and Sufi traditions created devotional and social languages without erasing doctrinal, caste or institutional difference (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None in owner file.
**Links:** MED-4115, MED-4089.
**Trap:** Bhakti languages did not erase caste or doctrinal difference (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** Marathi devotion and Maratha state formation developed in the same region.
**Back:** *"Eknath and Tukaram: Marathi devotion, not erased caste."*

### Flashcard MED-4117 — Persian chronicles: Akbarnama, Ain-i Akbari, Tuzuk, Muntakhab
**Front:** Which Persian sources form the textual spine for Mughal society and culture?
**Definition (learn):** Court chronicles and memoirs: Akbarnama, Ain-i Akbari, Muntakhab-ut-Tawarikh and Tuzuk-i Jahangiri, with Padshahnama and later court histories.
**Breakdown:**
- Akbarnama, Ain-i Akbari, Muntakhab-ut-Tawarikh, Tuzuk-i Jahangiri (✅ owner 24).
- Padshahnama and later court histories (✅ owner 24 synthesis).
- Each genre serves a different purpose: patronage, court position and self-fashioning (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None in owner file.
**Links:** MED-4118, MED-4119, MED-4113.
**Trap:** Court texts protect patrons or blame rivals; do not read them as neutral (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** Persian sources refract the age through patronage, genre and silence (✅ owner 24).
**Back:** *"Akbarnama, Ain, Tuzuk, Muntakhab: four genres, four biases."*

### Flashcard MED-4118 — Gulbadan Begum and Badauni: alternative voices
**Front:** Why do Gulbadan Begum and Badauni matter as sources?
**Definition (learn):** Alternative sources that offer angles to official praise.
**Breakdown:**
- Gulbadan Begum and Badauni offer alternatives to official praise (✅ owner 24).
- Each has its own bias and partiality (✅ owner 24).
- Badauni's evidence on nobles' evasion of muster is cited in the owner 21 advanced file (✅ owner 21 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None in owner file.
**Links:** MED-4117, MED-4020.
**Trap:** An alternative source is still partial; cross-verify (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** Source criticism needs several genres.
**Back:** *"Gulbadan and Badauni: the other voices, still biased."*

### Flashcard MED-4119 — PYQ 2020 Mains GS-I: Persian literary sources and the spirit of the age
**Front:** What does the 2020 GS-I Mains demand on Persian sources require?
**Definition (learn):** A 15-mark, 250-word question on Persian literary sources of medieval India and the spirit of the age.
**Breakdown:**
- Directive: comment (✅ owner 24 PYQ ledger).
- Routing: GS-I Q12, 15 marks, 250 words (✅ owner 24 PYQ ledger).
- Answer frame: context, core dimensions, evidence, counterpoint, concise conclusion (✅ owner 24 PYQ ledger).
- Persian sources "reflect" the age only after being treated as refracting it (✅ owner 24 section 9.13).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2020, GS-I, Q12 (✅ owner 24 PYQ ledger). Mains has no objective key.
**Links:** MED-4117, MED-4118, MED-4091.
**Trap:** Do not write a description of Persian chronicles only; write the refraction argument.
**Mains hook:** Persian sources refract, and corroboration with regional texts and material evidence is required.
**Back:** *"2020 Mains: Persian sources refract the age; corroborate."*

### Flashcard MED-4120 — Textile regions: Gujarat, Coromandel and Bengal
**Front:** Which regions were the major textile zones?
**Definition (learn):** Gujarat, Coromandel and Bengal were the main zones of cotton and silk textile production, with product and quality specialisation.
**Breakdown:**
- Gujarat, Coromandel and Bengal (✅ owner 24).
- Product and quality specialisation (✅ owner 24).
- Production forms: household and village; artisan workshop; merchant advance; urban master-craftsman; imperial or noble karkhana; dispersed production via brokers and warehouses (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None in owner file.
**Links:** MED-4091, MED-4121, MED-4101.
**Trap:** Textile manufacture was commercially dynamic without being modern industrialisation (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** Use textile regions to show the early-modern production system.
**Back:** *"Gujarat, Coromandel, Bengal: the cloth belt."*

### Flashcard MED-4121 — Karkhana: an imperial workshop, not a modern factory
**Front:** What was a karkhana, and why is the factory analogy a trap?
**Definition (learn):** An imperial or noble workshop producing goods or arts; not a mechanised modern factory.
**Breakdown:**
- Imperial or noble karkhana (✅ owner 24).
- Not a modern mechanised factory; merchant advances could link producers to distant markets while creating dependence (✅ owner 24).
- Karkhana under Akbar organised painting (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4107, MED-4120.
**Trap:** Karkhana is not a factory (✅ owner 24 trap).
**Mains hook:** Karkhana describes organised craft, not industrialisation.
**Back:** *"Karkhana: workshop for the court, not a factory."*

### Flashcard MED-4122 — Mughal trade policy and European companies
**Front:** How did the Mughal state treat European companies and trade?
**Definition (learn):** The state kept ports open and customs comparatively low, granted farmans without surrendering sovereignty, limited European fortification, and seized factories and agents on land when companies used maritime coercion.
**Breakdown:**
- Ports open; customs comparatively low (✅ owner 24).
- Granted farmans and facilities; opposed or limited fortification (✅ owner 24).
- Seized factories and agents on land when companies used maritime coercion (✅ owner 24).
- Chandra: the state did not block trade; it protected it and often encouraged merchants (✅ owner 24 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Seventeenth century (✅ owner 24).
**Links:** MED-4006, MED-4093, MED-4105.
**Trap:** Do not project the post-Plassey colonial state back into the Jahangir or Shah Jahan era (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** Mughal policy shaped European entry; Europeans were guests before they were masters.
**Back:** *"Open ports, low customs, no forts, seize on coercion."*

### Flashcard MED-4123 — Caste, mobility and slavery
**Front:** How did caste, mobility and slavery structure Mughal society?
**Definition (learn):** Society ordered by rank, occupation, caste or jati, lineage, religious community, gender and locality, with mobility through service and wealth and a slave-servitude system.
**Breakdown:**
- Mobility through mansab and military service; clerical and revenue skill; merchant wealth; migration; religious institutions; patronage, marriage and conversion (✅ owner 24).
- Mobility did not erase hierarchy (✅ owner 24).
- Slavery: domestic, military, court and labour forms existed; enslavement followed war, purchase, debt, birth or trafficking (✅ owner 24).
- Manumission was praised but did not remove the institution (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4124, MED-4015, MED-4013.
**Trap:** Slavery should not be collapsed into Atlantic plantation slavery or benign household service (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** Mobility and hierarchy coexisted; state service let people rise without ending rank.
**Back:** *"Mobility through service, hierarchy kept, slavery persisted."*

### Flashcard MED-4124 — Women: Nur Jahan, Jahanara and the limits of agency
**Front:** What do Nur Jahan and Jahanara show about women's roles?
**Definition (learn):** Elite women's agency examples, which prove agency at particular levels, not general equality.
**Breakdown:**
- Women worked in sowing, transplanting, harvesting, animal care, spinning, food processing and domestic service (✅ owner 24).
- Elite women managed estates, trade, ships, charity, architecture and household patronage (✅ owner 24).
- Purdah varied by status, region and occupation (✅ owner 24).
- Inheritance rights existed in law; access and enforcement were unequal (✅ owner 24).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4123, MED-4113.
**Trap:** Elite visibility should not be mistaken for general female autonomy (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** Use Nur Jahan and Jahanara to show elite agency with a caveat.
**Back:** *"Nur Jahan, Jahanara: agency at the top, inequality below."*

### Flashcard MED-4125 — Technology lag and selective adoption (Chandra)
**Front:** What limited Mughal technology, and what is the balanced claim?
**Definition (learn):** Capabilities in irrigation, shipbuilding, textile skill, metal casting, architecture, surveying, medicine and astronomy, with selective adoption of clocks, telescopes and European knowledge.
**Breakdown:**
- Satish Chandra's limitation: weak institutional connection between elite theoretical learning and artisanal experimentation (✅ owner 24).
- Limited mechanisation and printing diffusion (✅ owner 24).
- Lag areas per advanced file: cannon casting, water pumps, screws and springs, telescopes and printing (✅ owner 24 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4091, MED-4120.
**Trap:** Avoid "technologically stagnant" and "proto-industrial take-off" extremes (✅ owner 24).
**Mains hook:** The gap was institutional connection, not skill alone.
**Back:** *"Skill high, link weak, printing and mechanisation limited."*

### Flashcard MED-4126 — Nobles born outside India (Bernier claim)
**Front:** How many high nobles were born outside India by late Aurangzeb's reign?
**Definition (learn):** Chandra's counter to Bernier: by Aurangzeb's later reign less than 10% of nobles of 1000 zat and above were born outside India.
**Breakdown:**
- Less than 10% of nobles of 1000 zat and above born outside India (✅ owner 24 advanced).
- Immigrants assimilated into Indo-Mughal culture (✅ owner 24 advanced).
- Chandra rejects Bernier's claim that nobles were simply foreigners (✅ owner 24 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Late Aurangzeb (✅ owner 24 advanced).
**Links:** MED-4014, MED-4106.
**Trap:** This figure is an owner-reported claim; do not state it as a census (⚠️).
**Mains hook:** Use it to qualify the foreign-ruling-class thesis.
**Back:** *"Under 10% foreign-born at 1000 zat: the foreignness claim fails."*

### GROUP — Decline of the Mughal Empire and the Eighteenth Century (owner 25)

### Flashcard MED-4127 — The decline as a convergence of structural crises (concept)
**Front:** How should the decline of the Mughal empire be explained?
**Definition (learn):** A multi-causal, structural account: jagirdari malfunction, Deccan military exhaustion, noble factionalism, rural resistance, frontier exposure and regional autonomy, not a single ruler's personality or religious policy.
**Breakdown:**
- Satish Chandra's distinctive thesis is the jagirdari crisis (✅ owner 25).
- Decline is convergent: administrative, fiscal, social, military and political strains (✅ owner 25 advanced).
- The empire was still at the height of its power and prestige during much of Aurangzeb's reign (✅ owner 25).
- Weak rulers are symptoms, not sufficient causes (⚠️ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Decline is a contraction of central fiscal-military authority; the dynasty lingered nominally to 1857 (✅ owner 25 trap).
**Links:** MED-4128, MED-4130, MED-4149.
**Trap:** Do not reduce the decline to Aurangzeb's religion or to weak successors alone (✅ owner 25 trap).
**Mains hook:** "A structural crisis, not merely weak rulers" (✅ owner 25 Mains angle).
**Back:** *"Many strains, one convergence; no single culprit."*

### Flashcard MED-4128 — Aurangzeb's responsibility: Sarkar's bigotry thesis versus Chandra's structure
**Front:** How do Jadunath Sarkar and Satish Chandra differ on Aurangzeb's responsibility?
**Definition (learn):** Two frames for the decline: a personalised Aurangzeb-centred overexpansion and bigotry argument, and a structural jagirdari argument.
**Breakdown:**
- Sarkar: Aurangzeb-centred, highlights Deccan overreach and political and religious choices (✅ owner 25).
- Chandra: structural approach; Aurangzeb's choices mattered, but he did not personally create every fiscal, agrarian or succession problem (✅ owner 25).
- Inherited structures: earlier mansab growth, jama-hasil mismatch, local power and no primogeniture (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond reign-wide frames (✅ owner 25).
**Links:** MED-4127, MED-4129, MED-4052.
**Trap:** Sarkar's "bigotry" thesis is not universally accepted; neither is Chandra's approach universal (⚠️ owner 25).
**Mains hook:** Argue that Aurangzeb was a maker of his own problems and also the heir of inherited ones (✅ owner 22 advanced Chandra verdict).
**Back:** *"Sarkar blames the man, Chandra blames the machine."*

### Flashcard MED-4129 — The Deccan wars (1687–1707): the "ulcer"
**Front:** Why are the Deccan wars (1687–1707) central to the decline debate?
**Definition (learn):** Twenty years of southern war that tied down the emperor and army, lengthened communications and expanded garrisons.
**Breakdown:**
- Drained the treasury and tied down the army (✅ owner 25).
- Swelled the nobility with defeated Deccani and Maratha entrants (✅ owner 25).
- Emperor's absence from the north loosened control there (✅ owner 25).
- Annexation removed the Bijapur and Golconda buffers (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1687–1707 (✅ owner 25); Bijapur 1686 and Golconda 1687 (✅ owner 23).
**Links:** MED-4066, MED-4085, MED-4128.
**Trap:** Deccan was not the only cause; north Indian problems pre-existed (✅ owner 25 evidence limitation).
**Mains hook:** The Deccan war is the best concrete example of military-fiscal overstretch.
**Back:** *"Twenty years in the south, the ulcer in the empire."*

### Flashcard MED-4130 — Be-jagiri: jagirlessness and the jama-hasil gap
**Front:** What is be-jagiri, and how does it link to the jama-hasil gap?
**Definition (learn):** Be-jagiri means jagirlessness: a shortage of assignable productive jagirs for a swelling nobility.
**Breakdown:**
- Shortage of assignable productive jagirs (✅ owner 23 and 25).
- Jama-hasil gap: paper assessment exceeded realised collection (✅ owner 25).
- New entrants and corruption weakened the service system (✅ owner 25).
- Be-jagiri produced fierce factionalism and predatory extraction (✅ owner 25 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4019, MED-4086, MED-4087, MED-4132.
**Trap:** Jagirdari crisis is a structural thesis, not the claim that no jagirs existed (✅ owner 25).
**Mains hook:** "The empire's fiscal skeleton collapsed before its political shell" (✅ owner 25 thesis option).
**Back:** *"Be-jagiri: no jagirs for the many."*

### Flashcard MED-4131 — Bahadur Shah I (1707–12): won succession, lacked time
**Front:** What did Bahadur Shah I achieve in 1707–12?
**Definition (learn):** He won the succession war after Aurangzeb and attempted conciliation with Rajputs, Marathas and Sikhs, but lacked time and resources.
**Breakdown:**
- Won succession (✅ owner 25).
- Conciliation with Rajputs, Marathas and Sikhs without durable settlements (✅ owner 25).
- Died 1712 (✅ owner 25 mini-timeline dates 1707–12).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1707–12 (✅ owner 25).
**Links:** MED-4127, MED-4132, MED-4041 (jizyah 1712).
**Trap:** Bahadur Shah's conciliation was not a full settlement (✅ owner 25).
**Mains hook:** Post-1707 emperors faced noble coalitions; "weak rulers" alone do not explain the decline.
**Back:** *"1707–12: won the throne, lacked the time."*

### Flashcard MED-4132 — Jahandar Shah (1712–13) and Zulfiqar Khan
**Front:** Who backed Jahandar Shah, and what did his short reign show?
**Definition (learn):** A short reign that depended heavily on Zulfiqar Khan and exposed noble coalition politics.
**Breakdown:**
- Depended heavily on Zulfiqar Khan (✅ owner 25).
- Exposed noble coalition politics (✅ owner 25).
- Owner 22 places the jizyah abolition under Jahandar Shah at Asad Khan's instance in 1712 (✅ owner 22).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1712–13 (✅ owner 25).
**Links:** MED-4041, MED-4133.
**Trap:** Jizyah abolition attribution is an owner-internal conflict (see MED-4041).
**Mains hook:** The short reign shows the wizarat's power over emperors.
**Back:** *"Jahandar Shah: Zulfiqar's emperor, short and manipulated."*

### Flashcard MED-4133 — Farrukhsiyar (1713–19): raised by the Sayyids
**Front:** How did Farrukhsiyar come to the throne, and how did his reign end?
**Definition (learn):** Farrukhsiyar was raised to the throne by the Sayyid brothers and was deposed and killed in 1719 after conflict with them.
**Breakdown:**
- Raised by Sayyid brothers, 1713–19 (✅ owner 25).
- Deposed, blinded and murdered in 1719 (✅ Wikipedia: Sayyid brothers).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Deposition 1719 (✅ owner 25; ✅ Wikipedia: Sayyid brothers).
**Links:** MED-4134, MED-4132.
**Trap:** Farrukhsiyar's fate was violent; do not state that he was simply removed (✅ Wikipedia: Sayyid brothers).
**Mains hook:** Kingmaking by nobles showed the central collapse of authority.
**Back:** *"Farrukhsiyar: raised 1713, blinded, murdered 1719."*

### Flashcard MED-4134 — The Sayyid brothers: king-makers 1713–20
**Front:** Who were the Sayyid brothers, and what did they do?
**Definition (learn):** Abdullah Khan and Husain Ali Khan, king-makers who made and unmade emperors between 1713 and 1720.
**Breakdown:**
- Abdullah Khan (Qutb-ul-Mulk, the Vizier) and Husain Ali Khan (✅ Wikipedia: Sayyid brothers).
- Deposed Farrukhsiyar in 1719 (✅ owner 25; ✅ Wikipedia: Sayyid brothers).
- Husain Ali killed on 9 October 1720; Abdullah defeated and imprisoned in November 1720 (✅ Wikipedia: Sayyid brothers).
- Hussain Ali rejected the tribute agreements with the Marathas; he declared hostility and raided Maratha territory up to Satara's suburbs (✅ Wikipedia: Sayyid brothers).
