- The owner's advanced file says the Sayyids abolished jizyah and bargained with the Marathas over chauth and sardeshmukhi (✅ owner 25). The fetched Wikipedia article does not mention jizyah.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1713–20; 1719 deposition; 9 October 1720 (✅ Wikipedia: Sayyid brothers).
**Links:** MED-4133, MED-4135, MED-4041, MED-4080.
**Trap:** The Sayyids were central king-makers, not provincial rebels (✅ owner 25 trap). Owner 25 says the Sayyids abolished jizyah; owner 22 and Wikipedia do not support that as a Sayyid act. Flag ⚠️.
**Mains hook:** Sayyid kingmaking proves the decline of central authority, yet their fall did not restore a settled monarchy (✅ owner 25).
**Back:** *"Two brothers made emperors, 1713 to 1720, and unmade one."*

### Flashcard MED-4135 — Muhammad Shah (1719–48): regional states consolidate
**Front:** What happened during Muhammad Shah's long reign (1719–48)?
**Definition (learn):** A long reign in which the Sayyids were defeated in 1720, the Nizam left the court, regional autonomy deepened and Nadir Shah struck in 1739.
**Breakdown:**
- Sayyids defeated in 1720 (✅ owner 25).
- Nizam left the court; regional autonomy deepened (✅ owner 25).
- Nadir Shah struck in 1739 (✅ owner 25; see MED-4136).
- Delhi remained culturally significant (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1719–48 (✅ owner 25).
**Links:** MED-4134, MED-4136, MED-4141, MED-4143.
**Trap:** Muhammad Shah was not a weak emperor only; his court remained a centre of culture (✅ owner 25 section 9.12).
**Mains hook:** Regional states consolidated under a nominal emperor in this reign.
**Back:** *"Muhammad Shah: 1719–48, the regional tide rises."*

### Flashcard MED-4136 — Nadir Shah's invasion and sack of Delhi (1739)
**Front:** What did Nadir Shah take in 1739, and when?
**Definition (learn):** Nadir Shah defeated the Mughal forces at Karnal, entered Delhi and sacked the city, taking the Peacock Throne and the Koh-i-Noor.
**Breakdown:**
- Battle of Karnal on 24 February 1739 (✅ Wikipedia: Nader Shah's invasion of India).
- Sack of Delhi in March 1739; the massacre on 22 March; the city sacked for several days (✅ Wikipedia: Nader Shah's invasion of India).
- Muhammad Shah surrendered and entered Delhi with Nadir; handed over the treasury keys and the Peacock Throne (✅ Wikipedia: Nader Shah's invasion of India).
- Koh-i-Noor and Darya-i-Noor taken; the Koh-i-Noor is now in the British collection and Darya-i-Noor in Iranian collection (✅ Wikipedia: Nader Shah's invasion of India).
- Nadir left on 16 May 1739 (✅ Wikipedia: Nader Shah's invasion of India).
- Owner says he exploited weak frontier intelligence, factional delay and fiscal-military deterioration (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 24 February 1739 Karnal; March 1739 sack; 16 May 1739 departure (✅ Wikipedia: Nader Shah's invasion of India).
**Links:** MED-4137, MED-4008, MED-4135.
**Trap:** Nadir's raid was an event, not a structural cause (✅ owner 25 trap).
**Mains hook:** External shock exposed weakness but does not explain why the centre weakened.
**Back:** *"Karnal, 24 February 1739; Delhi sacked; Peacock and Koh-i-Noor gone."*

### Flashcard MED-4137 — Nadir Shah did not annex or rule India (trap)
**Front:** Did Nadir Shah conquer and rule India?
**Definition (learn):** No. He raided Delhi, took wealth and the Peacock Throne, and left.
**Breakdown:**
- Nadir took the lands west of the Indus, ceded back the eastern territories to Muhammad Shah (✅ Wikipedia: Nader Shah's invasion of India).
- Delhi was returned to the Mughal emperor (✅ Wikipedia: Nader Shah's invasion of India).
- Owner: he raided, looted Delhi and left (✅ owner 25 trap).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 16 May 1739 departure (✅ Wikipedia: Nader Shah's invasion of India).
**Links:** MED-4136.
**Trap:** Do not state that Nadir annexed India; the fetched source gives an occupation of Delhi and the lands west of the Indus (✅ Wikipedia).
**Mains hook:** A raid can be a devastating shock without being rule.
**Back:** *"Nadir raided, took, returned: no annexation."*

### Flashcard MED-4138 — Ahmad Shah Durrani's invasions (1748–67, or to 1769–71)
**Front:** When did Ahmad Shah Durrani (Abdali) invade India, and how many times?
**Definition (learn):** The Afghan ruler of the Durrani empire who invaded India repeatedly, contesting Punjab and north India.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner: repeated invasions 1748–1767 (✅ owner 25).
- Fetched Wikipedia: began 1748, "a series of invasions into India that would span the next 24 years" (✅ Wikipedia: Ahmad Shah Durrani).
- Wikipedia lists a tenth and eleventh invasion in 1769–71 and an attempted twelfth (✅ Wikipedia: Ahmad Shah Durrani).
- Fourth invasion 1756–57 with the occupation and sack of Delhi in 1757; fifth 1759–61 with Panipat (✅ Wikipedia: Ahmad Shah Durrani).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1748 start; 1757 Delhi sacking; 1761 Panipat (✅ Wikipedia: Ahmad Shah Durrani; ✅ owner 25).
**Links:** MED-4140, MED-4145, MED-4146.
**Trap:** The owner's end date of 1767 conflicts with Wikipedia's 1769–71 later invasions. Flag ⚠️ and state "1748 onward, 24 years" if unsure.
**Mains hook:** Invasions exposed frontier weakness, with Punjab and Delhi as the targets.
**Back:** *"1748 start, 1757 Delhi, 1761 Panipat, to 1769–71 by Wikipedia."*

### Flashcard MED-4139 — Alamgir II and Imad-ul-Mulk (1754–59): the emperor killed
**Front:** What happened under Alamgir II, and who dominated?
**Definition (learn):** Alamgir II reigned 1754–59; Imad-ul-Mulk dominated and the emperor was killed.
**Breakdown:**
- Imad-ul-Mulk dominated (✅ owner 25).
- Emperor killed (✅ owner 25).
- Shah Jahan III (1759–60) was a brief puppet phase (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1754–59 (✅ owner 25).
**Links:** MED-4138, MED-4148, MED-4127.
**Trap:** The emperor's death shows the wizarat's coercion, not a legal succession (✅ owner 25).
**Mains hook:** Imperial killing by a minister signals the empty throne.
**Back:** *"Alamgir II: Imad-ul-Mulk's puppet, then his end."*

### Flashcard MED-4140 — The Third Battle of Panipat (14 January 1761)
**Front:** What was the Third Battle of Panipat, who fought, and what was the Mughal role?
**Definition (learn):** The 1761 battle in which the Afghan Durrani army defeated the Marathas, checking Peshwa-led north Indian supremacy.
**Breakdown:**
- Date: 14 January 1761 (✅ Wikipedia: Third Battle of Panipat).
- Ahmad Shah Durrani led the Afghan side; Sadashivrao Bhau led the Maratha army, killed in action (✅ Wikipedia: Third Battle of Panipat).
- Mughal emperor not the principal belligerent; elements of the Mughal empire fought on the Afghan side (✅ Wikipedia: Third Battle of Panipat; ✅ owner 25).
- Abdali had the Indian chiefs recognise Shah Alam II as emperor (✅ Wikipedia: Third Battle of Panipat).
- Maratha difficulties: extended supply, uncertain alliances, large non-combatant camp, Abdali–Rohilla coordination (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 14 January 1761 (✅ Wikipedia: Third Battle of Panipat).
**Links:** MED-4138, MED-4146, MED-4147, MED-4148.
**Trap:** Panipat III was not "Mughals versus Marathas"; it was Marathas versus Abdali. Owner says the emperor was a bystander; Wikipedia says some Mughal elements fought on the Afghan side. Both are flagged as ✅ with the nuance.
**Mains hook:** Panipat checked the Peshwa's north Indian bid but did not end Maratha power or make British paramountcy automatic (⚠️ owner 25).
**Back:** *"14 January 1761: Marathas lose to Abdali; the Mughal is a bystander."*

### Flashcard MED-4141 — Bengal: Murshid Quli Khan and revenue autonomy
**Front:** Who founded Bengal's successor state, and how did it work?
**Definition (learn):** Bengal under Murshid Quli Khan became a successor state with revenue reorganisation and court autonomy while remitting tribute.
**Breakdown:**
- Founder: Murshid Quli Khan (✅ owner 25).
- Mechanism: revenue reorganisation, provincial control, court autonomy (✅ owner 25 section 9.6).
- Continuity: remitted tribute; used Mughal offices and titles (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No founding date in owner file; do not add (⚠️).
**Links:** MED-4135, MED-4142, MED-4143.
**Trap:** Successor states were not declared independent; they stayed nominally loyal (✅ owner 25 trap).
**Mains hook:** Bengal's revenue regime shows devolution, not anarchy.
**Back:** *"Bengal: Murshid Quli, revenue first, tribute still sent."*

### Flashcard MED-4142 — Awadh: Saadat Khan Burhan-ul-Mulk
**Front:** Who founded Awadh's autonomy, and what was its base?
**Definition (learn):** Awadh in the Ganga plain, consolidated by Saadat Khan Burhan-ul-Mulk through a military-fiscal base and zamindar management.
**Breakdown:**
- Founder: Saadat Khan Burhan-ul-Mulk (✅ owner 25).
- Military-fiscal base; zamindar management; control of the Ganga plain (✅ owner 25).
- Nominal imperial service and a Persianate court (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None in owner file.
**Links:** MED-4141, MED-4135.
**Trap:** Awadh was not an independent republic; it kept Mughal forms (✅ owner 25).
**Mains hook:** Awadh shows regional state-building through zamindar and military management.
**Back:** *"Awadh: Saadat Khan, Ganga plain, Persian court, nominal loyalty."*

### Flashcard MED-4143 — Hyderabad: Nizam-ul-Mulk Asaf Jah
**Front:** Who founded Hyderabad's autonomy, and how?
**Definition (learn):** Nizam-ul-Mulk Asaf Jah built a Deccan military and provincial base after leaving Delhi politics.
**Breakdown:**
- Founder Nizam-ul-Mulk (Asaf Jah) (✅ owner 25).
- Left Delhi politics; built a Deccan military and provincial base (✅ owner 25).
- Mughal mansab, administration and legitimacy vocabulary kept (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None in owner file.
**Links:** MED-4135, MED-4141, MED-4066.
**Trap:** The Nizam kept Mughal forms; he did not issue a modern declaration of independence (✅ owner 25).
**Mains hook:** Hyderabad is the Deccan side of successor-state formation.
**Back:** *"Hyderabad: Asaf Jah, Deccan base, Mughal vocabulary."*

### Flashcard MED-4144 — Jats: Churaman, Badan Singh, Suraj Mal and Bharatpur
**Front:** How did Jat power form in the 1700s?
**Definition (learn):** A Bharatpur-centred power built by Churaman and later Badan Singh and Suraj Mal from zamindari, forts, cultivation and control of routes near Agra and Delhi.
**Breakdown:**
- Churaman, then Badan Singh and Suraj Mal (✅ owner 25).
- Zamindari, forts, cultivation, control of routes (✅ owner 25).
- Jat state formation was not peasant protest alone (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None in owner file.
**Links:** MED-4036, MED-4146.
**Trap:** Do not reduce Jat power to peasant revolt or religious conflict (✅ owner 25).
**Mains hook:** Jat formation shows zamindari power turning into a state.
**Back:** *"Churaman, Badan Singh, Suraj Mal: Bharatpur from forts and roads."*

### Flashcard MED-4145 — Sikh misls and the Dal Khalsa
**Front:** How did Sikh power grow after Guru Gobind Singh and Banda Bahadur?
**Definition (learn):** After repression and reorganisation, Sikh power grew through misls and the Dal Khalsa in the Punjab, drawing on the armed community, local bases and frontier conditions.
**Breakdown:**
- Repression and reorganisation followed Guru Gobind Singh and Banda Bahadur (✅ owner 25).
- Growth of misls and Dal Khalsa in Punjab (✅ owner 25).
- Local bases and frontier conditions (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None in owner file.
**Links:** MED-4047, MED-4138.
**Trap:** Full Sikh Empire history lies beyond this topic (✅ owner 25).
**Mains hook:** Sikh misl power shows the frontier's political reorganisation.
**Back:** *"Misls and Dal Khalsa: Punjab's armed community."*

### Flashcard MED-4146 — Rohillas in Rohilkhand
**Front:** What was the Rohilla role in north India?
**Definition (learn):** Afghan and Rohilla chiefs established power in Rohilkhand amid Mughal provincial weakness, through migration and service networks.
**Breakdown:**
- Afghan and Rohilla chiefs in Rohilkhand (✅ owner 25).
- Migration and service networks (✅ owner 25).
- One element in a multipolar north Indian balance (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None in owner file.
**Links:** MED-4138, MED-4140, MED-4144.
**Trap:** Rohillas were not invaders simply replacing the empire (✅ owner 25).
**Mains hook:** Use the Rohillas to show the multipolar balance after 1707.
**Back:** *"Rohillas: multipolar, not simply invaders."*

### Flashcard MED-4147 — The Maratha bid: Shahu, the Peshwas and the 1719 grant
**Front:** How did the Maratha bid for supremacy develop after 1707?
**Definition (learn):** After 1707, Shahu's release, conflict with Tarabai and the rise of the Peshwas reshaped Maratha politics, with recognition of chauth and sardeshmukhi claims.
**Breakdown:**
- Shahu's release, conflict with Tarabai, rise of the Peshwas (✅ owner 25).
- Recognition of chauth and sardeshmukhi claims (✅ owner 25).
- The Mughal emperor Rafi-ud-Darajat granted Shahu chauth and sardeshmukhi over six Deccan provinces in 1719 (✅ Wikipedia: Chauth).
- Holkar, Scindia, Gaekwad and Bhonsle houses (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1719 grant (✅ Wikipedia: Chauth).
**Links:** MED-4069, MED-4080, MED-4081, MED-4088.
**Trap:** Maratha expansion often sought revenue and recognition, not uniform direct rule (✅ owner 25).
**Mains hook:** Confederacy widened reach but complicated central command.
**Back:** *"1719: chauth and sardeshmukhi granted; Peshwas rise; confederacy follows."*

### Flashcard MED-4148 — Shah Alam II and the Plassey boundary
**Front:** What boundary dates matter for the 1757–61 period?
**Definition (learn):** Plassey (1757) is a boundary date; Shah Alam II's reign begins 1759–60, before Panipat, but Buxar, Diwani and Company relations belong to Modern History.
**Breakdown:**
- Shah Alam II from 1759/60 (✅ owner 25).
- Buxar (1764) and Diwani (1765) belong to Modern History Topic 01 onward (✅ owner 25).
- Plassey (1757) may appear as a boundary date (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1757 Plassey; 1759/60 Shah Alam II; 1764 Buxar; 1765 Diwani (✅ owner 25).
**Links:** MED-4139, MED-4140.
**Trap:** Do not project Company conquest backward into this topic (✅ owner 25 boundary).
**Mains hook:** Mughal decline made space for the Company but did not make British supremacy inevitable (✅ owner 25).
**Back:** *"Plassey 1757 is the marker; Buxar and Diwani are Modern."*

### Flashcard MED-4149 — Historiographical ladder on decline
**Front:** What are the historiographical emphases on the Mughal decline?
**Definition (learn):** Overlapping emphases, not a simple supersession ladder.
**Breakdown:**
- Weak successors: notices court instability; makes symptom into cause (✅ owner 25).
- Sarkar's Aurangzeb-centred view: highlights overreach; can personalise structure (✅ owner 25).
- Chandra's jagirdari crisis: links fiscal-service mechanism, nobility and war; must include agrarian and local power (✅ owner 25).
- Agrarian crisis and resistance; regional reordering; colonial inevitability (teleological) (✅ owner 25).
- Best synthesis: inherited institutional strain + Aurangzeb-period choices + succession conflict + local fiscal resistance + regional state formation + external shocks (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4127, MED-4128, MED-4087.
**Trap:** Do not present these as a ladder that supersedes one another (✅ owner 25 advanced).
**Mains hook:** A 20-mark answer should array the debate with a graded verdict.
**Back:** *"Weak rulers, Sarkar, Chandra, agrarian, regions, colonial: all partial."*

### Flashcard MED-4150 — Economic continuity amid political fragmentation
**Front:** What economic continuities survived imperial contraction?
**Definition (learn):** Political fragmentation did not mean uniform economic collapse.
**Breakdown:**
- Agriculture and cultivation expansion in several regions; internal trade, merchant credit and textile production (✅ owner 25).
- Regional courts and urban consumption; Mughal revenue and administrative practices; Persianate and regional cultural patronage (✅ owner 25).
- Changes: warfare, multiple levies, insecure routes; shifting capitals; regionalised revenue and military markets; Company influence at some ports (✅ owner 25).
- Bengal's commercial strength coexisted with Delhi's political contraction (✅ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4091, MED-4122, MED-4141.
**Trap:** "Chaos and darkness" is a trap; Part II stresses regional state formation and continuing economic vitality (✅ owner 25 advanced).
**Mains hook:** Use Bengal to show regional economic vitality amid central contraction.
**Back:** *"Fields, trade, courts: the economy outlived Delhi's contraction."*

### Flashcard MED-4151 — Culture after imperial contraction: artists move to regional courts
**Front:** Where did artists, musicians and literati go after the centre contracted?
**Definition (learn):** They moved to Awadh, Hyderabad, Rajasthan, Punjab, Bengal and Maratha courts; Muhammad Shah's Delhi remained culturally significant.
**Breakdown:**
- Movement to Awadh, Hyderabad, Rajasthan, Punjab, Bengal and Maratha courts (✅ owner 25).
- Cultural decentralisation preserved Mughal forms while generating regional styles (✅ owner 25).
- Aurangzeb's reduced personal patronage dispersed artists to Rajasthan and Pahari centres (✅ owner 24 advanced).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None.
**Links:** MED-4113, MED-4108, MED-4150.
**Trap:** Cultural decentralisation was not decay alone (✅ owner 25).
**Mains hook:** Decentralisation created new patronage networks, not only loss.
**Back:** *"Artists scattered: Awadh, Hyderabad, Rajasthan, Pahari, Bengal."*

### Flashcard MED-4152 — The Ottoman sultan's support and Mughal prestige (1680 claim)
**Front:** What does the owner claim about Mughal prestige in 1680, and how should it be used?
**Definition (learn):** The owner states that the Ottoman sultan sought Aurangzeb's support in 1680, to show the empire's height during much of his reign.
**Breakdown:**
- Owner claim: Ottoman sultan sought support in 1680 (✅ owner 25; the owner does not cite a primary source for this claim).
- Use the claim to show prestige, not as a settled diplomatic fact (⚠️ owner 25).
- Prestige is compatible with structural strain (⚠️ owner 25).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1680 (owner-only, ⚠️ unverified).
**Links:** MED-4127, MED-4129.
**Trap:** Prestige and power are not the same; a sultan's request does not prove control (⚠️ owner 25).
**Mains hook:** Use prestige and strain together to avoid a one-sided decline thesis.
**Back:** *"1680: prestige is not control; check the claim."*
